# Weather Book

---

# Chapter 1  
## The Ocean of Air

Weather begins with something we rarely notice: the enormous ocean of air above us.

Air looks empty, but it is matter. It has weight, takes up space, stores heat, carries water, and pushes against every surface it touches. Every breeze, cloud, cold front, thunderstorm, and hurricane is air responding to differences in heat, moisture, and pressure.

To understand weather, start with four ideas:

**The Sun supplies the energy. Earth’s surface heats unevenly. Warm and cold air have different densities. Air moves in response.**

Almost everything else in meteorology grows from those four facts.

---

## 1.1 What Is the Atmosphere?

The **atmosphere** is the mixture of gases surrounding Earth and held in place by gravity. It makes life possible by supplying breathable air, moderating temperature, transporting water, and shielding the surface from much of the Sun’s harmful ultraviolet radiation. [Center for Science Education](https://scied.ucar.edu/learning-zone/atmosphere/what-is-atmosphere?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

Dry air near sea level consists mainly of:

| Gas | Approximate share |
|---|---:|
| Nitrogen | 78% |
| Oxygen | 21% |
| Argon | 0.93% |
| Carbon dioxide | About 0.04% |
| Other gases | Trace amounts |

But dry air is only part of the story. The atmosphere also contains a constantly changing amount of **water vapor**, plus tiny suspended particles such as sea salt, dust, smoke, pollen, and pollution.

Water vapor may make up only a small portion of the atmosphere—commonly from near zero to a few percent—but it has an enormous influence on weather. It forms clouds and precipitation, carries heat, and supplies energy to storms. [NOAA Satellite Information](https://www.nesdis.noaa.gov/news/peeling-back-the-layers-of-the-atmosphere?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

### Illustration 1: What air contains

*A magnified cube of apparently empty air containing nitrogen and oxygen molecules, a few water-vapor molecules, and suspended particles of dust, salt, smoke, and pollen.*

---

## 1.2 The Atmosphere Has Layers

The atmosphere does not end at a sharp boundary. It becomes thinner and thinner until it merges gradually with space. Scientists divide it into five main layers based largely on how temperature changes with altitude. [NOAA](https://www.noaa.gov/jetstream/atmosphere/layers-of-atmosphere?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

### The troposphere

The **troposphere** is the lowest layer and the one that matters most to weather watchers. It begins at the ground and extends roughly:

- 8 kilometers, or 5 miles, above the poles
- 17 kilometers, or 11 miles, above the tropics

Its upper boundary is called the **tropopause**.

Nearly all familiar weather occurs in the troposphere. Clouds form there. Rain and snow fall through it. Air masses collide within it. Thunderstorms may climb nearly to its upper boundary.

The troposphere is heated mainly **from below**. Sunlight passes through the air and warms the land and ocean. The surface then warms the air touching it. This is why air in the troposphere generally becomes colder with increasing altitude.

### The stratosphere

Above the troposphere lies the **stratosphere**, reaching to roughly 50 kilometers, or 31 miles.

Unlike the troposphere, temperature generally rises with altitude in the stratosphere because ozone absorbs ultraviolet radiation. The stratosphere is comparatively stable, so it contains far less vertical mixing and ordinary weather.

### The mesosphere, thermosphere, and exosphere

Above the stratosphere are the:

- **Mesosphere**, where many meteors burn up
- **Thermosphere**, which includes much of the region where auroras occur
- **Exosphere**, the extremely thin outermost layer that fades into space

These upper layers are fascinating, but weather remains mostly a tropospheric affair. The atmosphere is vast; weather does its best work in the basement.

### Illustration 2: Layers of the atmosphere

```text
            SPACE
              │
       EXOSPHERE
              │
      THERMOSPHERE       Auroras, many satellites
              │
       MESOSPHERE        Meteors
              │
       STRATOSPHERE      Ozone layer
              │
        TROPOPAUSE
              │
       TROPOSPHERE       Clouds, storms, rain, snow
              │
          EARTH
```

---

## 1.3 Air Has Weight

You are living beneath thousands of kilometers of atmosphere. Gravity pulls all that air toward Earth, and the weight of the air above produces **atmospheric pressure**.

At sea level, standard atmospheric pressure is approximately:

- **1,013.25 hectopascals**
- **1,013.25 millibars**
- **29.92 inches of mercury**
- **14.7 pounds per square inch**

The units differ, but they describe the same thing.

You do not feel crushed because the air and fluids inside your body push outward while the atmosphere pushes inward. The forces are largely balanced.

Atmospheric pressure decreases rapidly with altitude because there is less air above you. Roughly half of the atmosphere’s mass lies below about 5.5 kilometers, or 3.4 miles. Pressure and air density continue decreasing as altitude increases. [Center for Science Education](https://scied.ucar.edu/kids/sky-wonders/what-is-atmosphere?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

### A useful mental model

Imagine a stack of blankets.

The blanket on the bottom bears the weight of every blanket above it. The upper blanket bears almost no weight. Air behaves similarly: molecules near the surface are compressed by the atmosphere above them, while molecules high in the atmosphere are spread farther apart.

### Illustration 3: Pressure with altitude

```text
HIGH ALTITUDE        •       •          •
                     widely spaced molecules
                     low density, low pressure

MID-ALTITUDE         •   •    •   •
                     moderate density

SEA LEVEL            •••••••••••••••
                     closely packed molecules
                     high density, high pressure
```

---

## 1.4 Temperature: The Motion of Molecules

Temperature measures the average kinetic energy—the motion—of particles in a substance.

In warmer air, molecules move faster. In colder air, they move more slowly.

When air is warmed and allowed to expand, its molecules spread apart. The air becomes less dense than the surrounding cooler air and tends to rise. When air cools, it generally becomes denser and tends to sink.

This creates **convection**, one of the fundamental engines of weather.

```text
        Cooling air
          sinks
            ↓
     ┌─────────────┐
     │             │
     │    ↺        │
     │             │
     └─────────────┘
            ↑
        Warm air
          rises
```

The atmosphere is rarely heated evenly. Dark soil, pale sand, forests, cities, lakes, and oceans absorb and release heat at different rates. The equator receives more direct solar energy than the poles. Day alternates with night, and seasons alter the angle and duration of sunlight.

These unequal temperatures produce differences in density and pressure. Those differences set air in motion.

That moving air is **wind**.

---

## 1.5 Density: Why Warm Air Rises

People often say, “Heat rises.” More precisely, **warm air rises when it is less dense than the air around it**.

Consider two invisible boxes of equal size:

- The cold-air box contains more tightly packed molecules.
- The warm-air box contains fewer, more widely spaced molecules.

The cold box therefore contains more mass and weighs more. If both are placed in the atmosphere, the lighter warm air is buoyed upward by the denser surrounding air.

The same principle explains why a hot-air balloon rises. Heating the air inside the balloon lowers its density relative to the surrounding atmosphere.

However, warm air does not rise forever. As it rises, it enters regions of lower pressure, expands, and cools. Eventually it may reach a level where it is no warmer or less dense than the surrounding air.

This struggle between rising and sinking air determines whether the atmosphere is:

- **Stable**, resisting vertical movement
- **Unstable**, encouraging rising currents, clouds, and sometimes storms

We will return to atmospheric stability in the chapter on clouds and thunderstorms.

---

## 1.6 The Sun Powers Weather

The Sun supplies nearly all the energy driving Earth’s weather.

But the Sun does not heat the atmosphere uniformly. Several factors create unequal heating:

### Sun angle

Near the equator, sunlight strikes the surface more directly and concentrates its energy over a smaller area. Near the poles, the same energy arrives at a lower angle and spreads across a larger area.

### Length of daylight

Long summer days allow more time for heating. Short winter days allow less.

### Surface type

Different surfaces absorb and release energy differently.

- Water heats and cools relatively slowly.
- Dry land heats and cools more quickly.
- Snow and ice reflect much of the sunlight reaching them.
- Dark pavement absorbs more sunlight than light-colored surfaces.
- Vegetation cools the nearby air through shade and evaporation.

### Clouds and atmospheric particles

Clouds can reflect incoming sunlight, reducing daytime heating. They can also absorb and re-emit infrared energy, slowing nighttime cooling.

Because heating is uneven, Earth is continually trying to redistribute energy. Air and ocean currents carry heat from warmer regions toward cooler ones.

Weather is part of that planetary heat-transfer system.

### Illustration 4: Unequal solar heating

*A curved Earth shown in cross-section. Nearly vertical sunlight reaches the tropics, while slanted rays spread over a larger area near the poles. Rising warm air appears over the tropics and sinking cold air toward higher latitudes.*

---

## 1.7 Water in the Air

Water exists in the atmosphere in three forms:

- **Gas:** invisible water vapor
- **Liquid:** droplets in clouds, fog, rain, and drizzle
- **Solid:** ice crystals, snow, sleet, and hail

Water continuously moves between Earth’s surface and the atmosphere through the **water cycle**.

```text
                     CLOUDS
              condensation / deposition
                       ↓
                 precipitation
                       ↓
OCEANS • LAKES • SOIL • PLANTS
                       ↑
          evaporation / transpiration
```

When liquid water evaporates, it absorbs energy from its surroundings. That energy is stored invisibly within the water vapor as **latent heat**.

Later, when the vapor condenses into liquid droplets, the stored heat is released into the atmosphere.

This exchange is crucial. A developing thunderstorm can release enormous amounts of latent heat as water vapor condenses. That heat warms the rising air, helping it remain buoyant and continue climbing.

Water is not merely carried by weather. Water helps power it.

---

## 1.8 Humidity and Dew Point

**Humidity** describes the amount of water vapor in the air, but several different measurements are used.

### Relative humidity

**Relative humidity** compares the amount of water vapor currently in the air with the maximum amount the air could hold at that temperature.

It is expressed as a percentage.

Relative humidity changes when:

- Water vapor is added or removed
- Air temperature changes

This second point causes confusion. Suppose the actual amount of water vapor stays constant. If the temperature falls, relative humidity rises. If the temperature rises, relative humidity falls.

That is why relative humidity is often highest around sunrise, when the air is coolest, even though the air may not contain more moisture than it did the previous afternoon.

### Dew point

The **dew point** is the temperature to which air must cool for it to become saturated.

Dew point is usually a better everyday measure of how moist the air actually feels because it does not swing merely because the temperature changes.

A rough comfort guide:

| Dew point | General sensation |
|---|---|
| Below 50°F / 10°C | Dry or comfortable |
| 50–60°F / 10–16°C | Comfortable |
| 60–65°F / 16–18°C | Noticeably humid |
| 65–70°F / 18–21°C | Muggy |
| Above 70°F / 21°C | Very humid |
| Above 75°F / 24°C | Oppressive for many people |

These categories describe human comfort, not strict meteorological boundaries. People acclimated to different climates may judge them differently.

When air cools to its dew point, water vapor can condense onto surfaces as dew or into the air as fog or cloud droplets—provided suitable microscopic particles are present. Clouds commonly form when rising air expands and cools enough for condensation to occur. [Center for Science Education](https://scied.ucar.edu/learning-zone/clouds/how-clouds-form?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

---

## 1.9 Pressure Differences Create Wind

Air does not move simply because pressure is “high” or “low.” It moves because pressure differs from one place to another.

The force pushing air from higher pressure toward lower pressure is called the **pressure-gradient force**.

The greater the pressure difference over a given distance, the stronger the pressure gradient and the faster the wind can become.

On a weather map, pressure is often shown using lines called **isobars**.

```text
Widely spaced isobars:      1020       1016       1012
                            generally lighter winds

Closely spaced isobars:     1020 1016 1012 1008
                            generally stronger winds
```

Air initially accelerates toward lower pressure, but Earth’s rotation, friction, terrain, and other forces alter its path.

At the surface:

- Air generally spreads outward from high-pressure areas.
- Air generally flows inward toward low-pressure areas.
- Rising air near low pressure encourages cloud formation.
- Sinking air near high pressure often discourages clouds.

These are useful rules, not guarantees. Moisture, stability, topography, and the structure of the atmosphere all matter. [Center for Science Education](https://scied.ucar.edu/learning-zone/how-weather-works/highs-and-lows-air-pressure?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

---

## 1.10 A Simple Weather Machine

The basic chain of weather can now be assembled:

```text
SUNLIGHT
    ↓
Uneven heating of land, water, and atmosphere
    ↓
Temperature and density differences
    ↓
Pressure differences
    ↓
Air begins to move
    ↓
Warm air rises and cools
    ↓
Water vapor may condense
    ↓
Clouds and precipitation may form
```

Real weather is far more complicated because Earth rotates, continents and oceans interrupt airflow, mountains redirect winds, water changes phase, and patterns interact across many scales.

But this simple machine is always operating underneath the complexity.

---

# Field Observation  
## Watch the Ground Heat the Air

Choose a mostly sunny day and compare two nearby surfaces—for example:

- Grass and asphalt
- Shade and direct sunlight
- Dry soil and water
- A rooftop and a garden

Stand near each surface without touching it. Notice the temperature of the air immediately above it. Look for:

- Shimmering air above hot pavement
- Small leaves or insects carried upward
- Developing fair-weather clouds later in the day
- Stronger breezes as daytime heating increases

For a more precise observation, place two thermometers at the same height above different surfaces. Keep both out of direct sunlight or shield them identically. Record the temperature every ten minutes for one hour.

You are watching uneven heating create the first step in a weather system.

---

# Try It  
## Make a Convection Chamber

You will need:

- A clear glass baking dish or transparent container
- Warm water
- Cold water
- Two small cups
- Food coloring

Place one cup of warm colored water beneath one end of the clear dish and one cup of cold colored water beneath the other. Fill the dish carefully with room-temperature water. Watch how water warmed from below rises while cooler water sinks.

The experiment uses water rather than air, but the density-driven circulation is similar.

Use sensible temperatures; boiling water would turn a charming weather lesson into a much less charming emergency-room anecdote.

---

# Chapter Summary

The atmosphere is a mixture of gases, water vapor, and suspended particles held around Earth by gravity.

Most weather occurs in the troposphere, the lowest atmospheric layer.

Air has weight and produces atmospheric pressure. Pressure and density decrease with altitude.

Uneven solar heating creates differences in temperature and air density. Those differences help create variations in atmospheric pressure.

Air moves in response to pressure differences, producing wind.

Warm, buoyant air can rise, expand, and cool. If it cools to its dew point, water vapor may condense into clouds.

The phase changes of water transfer energy through the atmosphere and help power weather systems.

Together, sunlight, air, water, and Earth’s rotation create the continuously changing system we call weather.

---

## Weather-Watcher’s Vocabulary

**Atmosphere:** The layer of gases surrounding Earth.  
**Troposphere:** The lowest atmospheric layer, where nearly all weather occurs.  
**Air pressure:** The force produced by the weight of the atmosphere.  
**Density:** The amount of mass contained within a given volume.  
**Convection:** Circulation caused by differences in temperature and density.  
**Water vapor:** Water in its invisible gaseous state.  
**Humidity:** A measure of water vapor in the air.  
**Relative humidity:** Current moisture compared with the maximum possible at the same temperature.  
**Dew point:** The temperature at which air becomes saturated when cooled.  
**Latent heat:** Energy absorbed or released when water changes phase.  
**Pressure gradient:** A change in atmospheric pressure across a distance.  
**Isobar:** A line connecting places with equal atmospheric pressure.

**Next: Chapter 2 — Temperature, Heat, and the Restless Atmosphere**

# Chapter 2  
## Temperature, Heat, and the Restless Atmosphere

A weather report may say the temperature is 72°F, but that number alone does not tell you how the atmosphere behaves.

Weather depends not only on **temperature**, but on how heat moves, how quickly surfaces warm and cool, how air expands, and how temperature changes with height.

The atmosphere is restless because heat is always being transferred from one place to another.

---

## 2.1 Temperature Is Not Heat

The words **temperature** and **heat** are often used as though they mean the same thing. They do not.

**Temperature** describes the average motion of particles in a substance.

**Heat** is energy transferred from a warmer object or region to a cooler one.

A small cup of boiling water has a higher temperature than a warm swimming pool. But the pool contains far more total thermal energy because it contains vastly more water.

That distinction matters in weather because oceans, soil, forests, pavement, and air store and release different amounts of heat.

### Illustration 1: Temperature versus total heat

*A steaming cup beside a large warm lake. The cup is labeled “higher temperature,” while the lake is labeled “more total thermal energy.”*

---

## 2.2 The Three Main Ways Heat Moves

Heat moves through the environment in three basic ways:

- **Radiation**
- **Conduction**
- **Convection**

Weather depends on all three.

---

## 2.3 Radiation: Energy Across Space

**Radiation** transfers energy through electromagnetic waves.

The Sun warms Earth mainly through radiation. Sunlight crosses roughly 150 million kilometers of space before reaching the atmosphere and surface.

The Sun emits most of its energy as relatively shortwave radiation, including:

- Visible light
- Ultraviolet radiation
- Near-infrared radiation

Earth absorbs some of that energy and later emits energy back upward as longer-wave infrared radiation.

### The surface does most of the warming

The atmosphere absorbs some incoming solar energy, but much sunlight passes through the air and warms the ground and ocean.

The warm surface then transfers heat to the lower atmosphere.

This is why the lowest part of the atmosphere is generally heated from below.

```text
                SUN
                 ↓
       Shortwave solar energy
                 ↓
        LAND AND OCEAN WARM
                 ↑
      Longwave infrared energy
                 ↑
        LOWER ATMOSPHERE WARMS
```

---

## 2.4 Reflection and Absorption

Not all sunlight reaching Earth is absorbed.

Some is reflected back toward space by:

- Clouds
- Snow
- Ice
- Light-colored ground
- Atmospheric particles

The fraction of incoming radiation a surface reflects is called its **albedo**.

A surface with high albedo reflects a large portion of sunlight. A surface with low albedo absorbs more.

Examples:

| Surface | General behavior |
|---|---|
| Fresh snow | Reflects strongly |
| Thick clouds | Reflect strongly |
| Desert sand | Reflects moderately |
| Forest | Absorbs strongly |
| Ocean | Often absorbs strongly |
| Dark pavement | Absorbs strongly |

This is one reason snowy ground can remain cold even under bright sunlight, while black asphalt becomes painfully hot.

### Illustration 2: High and low albedo

*Two sunlight beams strike snow and pavement. Most light bounces away from the snow, while most is absorbed by the pavement.*

---

## 2.5 Conduction: Heat by Contact

**Conduction** is the transfer of heat through direct molecular contact.

A sun-warmed sidewalk heats the thin layer of air touching it. Molecules in the surface move more rapidly and transfer energy to nearby air molecules.

Air is not a very good conductor, so conduction usually affects only a shallow layer near the surface.

This thin layer matters, however, because it is where convection often begins.

At night, the process can reverse. The ground loses heat and cools the air touching it. On calm, clear nights, this can create a very cold layer near the surface.

That is why frost can form on grass even when a thermometer several feet above the ground reads slightly above freezing.

---

## 2.6 Convection: Heat Carried by Motion

**Convection** occurs when a fluid such as air or water moves and carries heat with it.

When air near the ground warms:

1. It expands.
2. Its density decreases.
3. It becomes buoyant.
4. It rises.

Cooler, denser air then moves in to replace it.

This creates a circulating current called a **convection cell**.

```text
              COOLER AIR
          sinks       sinks
             ↘       ↙
               ↺   ↻
                 ↑
              WARM AIR
                rises
         HEATED GROUND BELOW
```

Small convection cells may produce gentle thermals over a field. Larger ones can build towering clouds and thunderstorms.

---

## 2.7 Advection: Heat Moving Sideways

Meteorologists use the word **advection** for the horizontal transport of air properties by wind.

Warm-air advection occurs when wind carries warmer air into a region.

Cold-air advection occurs when wind carries colder air into a region.

Advection can change local weather quickly, even if the amount of sunlight remains the same.

For example:

- A south wind may bring warm, humid air northward.
- A northwest wind may bring cold, dry air behind a front.
- An ocean breeze may cool a hot coastline.
- A downslope wind may warm rapidly as it descends.

Convection moves heat mainly vertically. Advection moves it mainly horizontally. The atmosphere enjoys doing both at once, because apparently one complication was not enough.

---

## 2.8 Why Land and Water Heat Differently

Land and water respond very differently to solar heating.

Land generally:

- Heats quickly
- Cools quickly
- Transfers heat through only a shallow layer
- Can reach large temperature extremes

Water generally:

- Heats slowly
- Cools slowly
- Mixes heat through greater depth
- Uses energy for evaporation
- Experiences smaller temperature changes

Several properties explain this difference.

### Water mixes

Warm water near the surface can mix with cooler water below, spreading the heat.

Solid ground cannot circulate in the same way.

### Water is transparent

Sunlight can penetrate below the water’s surface, distributing energy through a deeper layer.

Most solar energy absorbed by land remains near the top.

### Water has a high heat capacity

Water requires a large amount of energy to change temperature.

### Evaporation removes heat

Some solar energy over water is used to convert liquid water into vapor rather than raise the water temperature.

---

## 2.9 Sea Breezes and Land Breezes

The different heating rates of land and water create local winds.

### Daytime sea breeze

During a sunny day:

1. Land warms faster than water.
2. Air over land heats and rises.
3. Surface pressure becomes relatively lower over land.
4. Cooler air moves inland from the water.

This is a **sea breeze**.

```text
DAY

        Rising warm air
              ↑
   LAND       │       WATER
    HOT       │        COOL
      ← upper return flow
      surface wind from water →
```

Sea breezes can lower coastal temperatures and help trigger afternoon clouds or thunderstorms where the incoming air converges with inland air.

### Nighttime land breeze

At night:

1. Land cools faster than water.
2. Air over the water remains relatively warmer.
3. Air rises over the water.
4. Cooler surface air flows from land toward water.

This is a **land breeze**.

```text
NIGHT

   LAND                 WATER
   COOL                  WARM
     surface wind toward water →
                           ↑
                      Rising air
```

Land breezes are often weaker than daytime sea breezes because nighttime temperature differences are usually smaller.

---

## 2.10 The Daily Temperature Cycle

The coldest and warmest parts of the day do not occur exactly at sunrise and noon.

### Why the coldest time is after sunrise

The ground loses heat through the night. Even after the Sun rises, outgoing heat may briefly exceed incoming solar energy.

The temperature usually continues falling until incoming energy becomes greater than outgoing energy.

Therefore, the daily minimum often occurs near or shortly after sunrise.

### Why the warmest time is after noon

The Sun is highest around solar noon, but the surface continues gaining more energy than it loses for several hours afterward.

The maximum temperature commonly occurs during mid- to-late afternoon.

```text
TEMPERATURE
    ^
    |                    maximum
    |                  /\
    |                /   \
    |              /      \
    |      minimum          \
    |_____/____________________> TIME
        sunrise   noon   afternoon
```

Cloud cover, wind, humidity, terrain, and passing weather systems can shift this pattern.

---

## 2.11 The Seasonal Temperature Cycle

Earth’s seasons are caused primarily by the tilt of its axis, not by changes in distance from the Sun.

Earth’s axis is tilted about 23.5 degrees relative to its orbit.

During summer in a hemisphere:

- The Sun appears higher in the sky.
- Sunlight strikes more directly.
- Days are longer.
- More solar energy reaches the surface.

During winter:

- The Sun stays lower.
- Sunlight arrives at a shallower angle.
- Days are shorter.
- Less solar energy reaches the surface.

### Illustration 3: Earth’s tilt and the seasons

*Four Earth positions around the Sun, with the axis pointing in the same direction through the orbit. One hemisphere tilts toward the Sun during summer and away during winter.*

### Seasonal lag

The warmest and coldest periods usually occur after the solstices.

This delay is called **seasonal lag**.

Land, water, and air take time to warm and cool. In much of the Northern Hemisphere, July is often hotter than June even though the summer solstice occurs in June.

Coastal regions usually have a longer seasonal lag than inland regions because oceans heat and cool slowly.

---

## 2.12 Measuring Air Temperature Correctly

A thermometer exposed to direct sunlight may measure the temperature of the heated instrument rather than the true air temperature.

Official air temperatures are measured:

- In shade
- Over a natural surface when possible
- With good ventilation
- At a standard height above the ground
- Inside a protective instrument shelter or radiation shield

A thermometer placed against a brick wall, on a sunny porch, or inside a parked car can produce wildly misleading readings.

### Illustration 4: Proper thermometer placement

*A thermometer mounted inside a ventilated white shield over grass, several feet above the ground, away from buildings and pavement.*

---

## 2.13 Temperature Scales

Three temperature scales are commonly encountered.

### Fahrenheit

Used mainly in the United States.

- Water freezes at 32°F
- Water boils at 212°F at standard sea-level pressure

### Celsius

Used by most of the world and in much scientific work.

- Water freezes at 0°C
- Water boils at 100°C at standard sea-level pressure

### Kelvin

Used in scientific calculations.

- 0 K is absolute zero
- Water freezes at about 273.15 K
- Water boils at about 373.15 K

Kelvin temperatures do not use the degree symbol.

### Converting between Fahrenheit and Celsius

```text
°C = (°F − 32) × 5/9

°F = (°C × 9/5) + 32
```

Useful reference points:

| Fahrenheit | Celsius |
|---:|---:|
| 0°F | −17.8°C |
| 32°F | 0°C |
| 50°F | 10°C |
| 68°F | 20°C |
| 86°F | 30°C |
| 100°F | 37.8°C |

---

## 2.14 Temperature Changes with Altitude

In the troposphere, temperature usually decreases with height.

The average rate is about:

**6.5°C per kilometer**, or roughly **3.6°F per 1,000 feet**

This average is called the **environmental lapse rate**.

Actual lapse rates vary greatly. Temperature may decrease rapidly, decrease slowly, remain nearly constant, or even increase with height.

---

## 2.15 Why Rising Air Cools

When a parcel of air rises, it enters an environment with lower pressure.

The parcel expands because there is less surrounding pressure squeezing it.

Expansion requires energy. The air parcel uses some of its internal energy to expand, so its temperature drops.

This cooling occurs even if the air parcel does not exchange heat with its surroundings.

A temperature change caused by expansion or compression without heat exchange is called an **adiabatic temperature change**.

### Rising air

- Pressure decreases
- Air expands
- Temperature falls

### Sinking air

- Pressure increases
- Air compresses
- Temperature rises

```text
       RISING AIR
            ↑
      expands and cools

      compresses and warms
            ↓
       SINKING AIR
```

This is one of the most important principles in meteorology.

---

## 2.16 Dry and Moist Adiabatic Cooling

Unsaturated rising air cools at approximately:

**9.8°C per kilometer**, or about **5.4°F per 1,000 feet**

This is called the **dry adiabatic lapse rate**.

Here, “dry” means unsaturated, not necessarily lacking all water vapor.

Once the air cools enough for condensation to begin, cloud droplets form. Condensation releases latent heat, partially offsetting the cooling.

Saturated air therefore cools more slowly, often around:

**4–7°C per kilometer**, depending on temperature and moisture.

This is called the **moist adiabatic lapse rate**.

```text
RISING AIR PARCEL

Below cloud base:
cools rapidly
dry adiabatic rate

At cloud base:
condensation begins

Inside cloud:
latent heat released
cooling slows
moist adiabatic rate
```

---

## 2.17 Atmospheric Stability

**Stability** describes whether the atmosphere resists or encourages vertical motion.

### Stable air

Stable air discourages rising motion.

If an air parcel is lifted and becomes colder and denser than the surrounding air, it tends to sink back toward its starting level.

Stable conditions often produce:

- Smooth air
- Layered clouds
- Fog
- Haze
- Light, steady precipitation
- Trapped pollution

### Unstable air

Unstable air encourages rising motion.

If a lifted parcel remains warmer and less dense than the surrounding air, it continues rising.

Unstable conditions often produce:

- Turbulence
- Puffy cumulus clouds
- Showers
- Thunderstorms
- Strong vertical currents

### Neutral air

In neutral conditions, a displaced air parcel has nearly the same temperature as its surroundings and neither strongly rises nor sinks.

---

## 2.18 A Simple Stability Test

Imagine lifting a bubble of air upward.

Then compare its temperature with the surrounding atmosphere.

- If the parcel is **warmer**, it keeps rising.
- If it is **colder**, it sinks.
- If it is about the **same temperature**, it remains near that level.

```text
PARCEL WARMER THAN ENVIRONMENT
          ↑ rises

PARCEL COLDER THAN ENVIRONMENT
          ↓ sinks

PARCEL SAME TEMPERATURE
          — neutral
```

The actual atmosphere may contain layers with different stability, which is why clouds can grow through one layer and then flatten beneath another.

---

## 2.19 Temperature Inversions

A **temperature inversion** occurs when temperature increases with height instead of decreasing.

Inversions act like lids because warmer air above suppresses rising motion from below.

They can trap:

- Fog
- Smoke
- Pollution
- Cold air
- Moisture

### Radiation inversion

On a clear, calm night, the ground cools rapidly. Air touching the ground becomes colder than the air above it.

This creates a shallow inversion near the surface.

### Valley inversion

Cold, dense air drains downhill and collects in valleys. Higher slopes may remain warmer.

This is why valley floors can experience frost while nearby hillsides do not.

### Subsidence inversion

In a high-pressure system, sinking air compresses and warms. This can create a warm layer above cooler surface air.

### Illustration 5: A valley inversion

*Cold air pooled in a valley beneath warmer air, with smoke spreading sideways instead of rising.*

---

## 2.20 Urban Heat Islands

Cities are often warmer than nearby rural areas, especially at night.

This pattern is called the **urban heat island effect**.

Cities tend to have:

- Dark pavement and roofs
- Less vegetation
- Less evaporative cooling
- Heat released by vehicles, buildings, and machinery
- Structures that slow nighttime heat loss
- Materials that store heat during the day

Urban heat islands are usually strongest on clear, calm nights.

They can affect:

- Energy use
- Heat stress
- Air quality
- Thunderstorm development
- Local wind patterns

Trees, reflective roofs, parks, and lighter-colored surfaces can reduce urban heating.

---

## 2.21 Wind Chill and Heat Index

The measured air temperature does not always describe how conditions feel to the human body.

### Wind chill

Wind removes the thin layer of warmed air next to exposed skin.

In cold weather, stronger wind increases heat loss and makes conditions feel colder.

Wind chill applies to people and animals. It does not make inanimate objects colder than the actual air temperature, though it can make them cool faster.

### Heat index

High humidity slows the evaporation of sweat.

Because evaporation is one of the body’s main cooling mechanisms, humid air can make hot weather feel much hotter.

The **heat index** combines temperature and relative humidity to estimate this effect.

Both wind chill and heat index describe human physiological stress, not a change in the actual air temperature.

---

## 2.22 Clouds and Temperature

Clouds affect temperature differently during day and night.

### During the day

Clouds reflect some incoming sunlight.

This often keeps daytime temperatures cooler.

### At night

Clouds absorb and re-emit infrared radiation from Earth’s surface.

This often slows nighttime cooling.

Therefore:

- Clear days may heat strongly.
- Clear nights may cool strongly.
- Cloudy days often have lower maximum temperatures.
- Cloudy nights often have higher minimum temperatures.

Cloud cover tends to reduce the daily temperature range.

---

## 2.23 Snow Cover and Temperature

Snow strongly affects local temperature.

Fresh snow:

- Reflects much of the incoming sunlight
- Insulates the ground
- Cools the air near the surface
- Encourages strong nighttime cooling
- Can strengthen surface inversions

A fresh snowpack beneath clear skies can produce extremely low nighttime temperatures, especially when winds are light and the air is dry.

---

## 2.24 Why Deserts Have Large Temperature Swings

Deserts often experience very hot days and cool nights.

Several factors contribute:

- Dry soil heats quickly.
- Sparse vegetation provides little shade.
- Low humidity allows rapid nighttime cooling.
- Few clouds allow strong daytime heating and strong nighttime heat loss.
- Limited water means less energy is used for evaporation.

Humid regions usually have smaller daily temperature ranges because water vapor and clouds slow nighttime cooling, while evaporation limits daytime heating.

---

# Field Observation  
## Track a Day’s Temperature

Choose a day with relatively calm weather.

Record the air temperature every hour from morning through evening.

Also note:

- Cloud cover
- Wind
- Sun or shade
- Surface conditions
- Humidity or dew point, if available

Create a simple graph with time along the bottom and temperature along the side.

Look for:

- The morning minimum
- The afternoon maximum
- Sudden changes caused by clouds
- Cooling after sunset
- Temperature changes when wind direction shifts

A single day’s graph can reveal the balance between incoming and outgoing energy.

---

# Try It  
## Compare Land and Water Heating

You will need:

- Two identical containers
- Dry soil or sand
- Water
- Two thermometers
- A sunny location or lamp

Fill one container with soil and the other with water.

Place a thermometer at the same depth in each.

Record the temperature every five minutes while both receive equal light.

Then move both into shade and continue recording.

You should find that:

- Soil warms faster.
- Soil reaches a higher temperature.
- Soil cools faster.
- Water changes temperature more slowly.

This small experiment explains sea breezes, coastal climates, and much of the seasonal behavior of oceans and continents.

---

# Try It  
## Find a Temperature Inversion

On a clear, calm morning, compare temperatures in two nearby locations:

- A low valley or hollow
- A nearby hill or slope

The lower site may be colder, even though it is at a lower elevation.

Look for:

- Frost in low spots
- Fog pooled in valleys
- Smoke spreading horizontally
- Warmer air on elevated ground

Do not wander into traffic, trespass onto someone’s property, or climb a radio tower in the name of meteorology. The atmosphere will still be there tomorrow.

---

# Chapter Summary

Temperature measures the average motion of particles, while heat is energy transferred from warmer areas to cooler ones.

Heat moves by radiation, conduction, convection, and horizontal advection.

Earth’s surface absorbs solar radiation and warms the atmosphere from below.

Land heats and cools faster than water, producing coastal temperature differences and local winds.

Air rising into lower pressure expands and cools. Air sinking into higher pressure compresses and warms.

Unsaturated air cools faster than saturated air because condensation releases latent heat.

Atmospheric stability determines whether vertical motion is suppressed or encouraged.

Temperature inversions trap cold air, moisture, smoke, and pollution near the surface.

Clouds, snow, humidity, wind, terrain, cities, and surface type all influence local temperature.

Understanding heat transfer makes it possible to explain many everyday weather patterns before even looking at a forecast map.

---

## Weather-Watcher’s Vocabulary

**Heat:** Energy transferred because of a temperature difference.  
**Radiation:** Energy transferred by electromagnetic waves.  
**Conduction:** Heat transfer through direct contact.  
**Convection:** Heat transfer through vertical fluid motion.  
**Advection:** Horizontal transport by wind.  
**Albedo:** The fraction of incoming radiation reflected by a surface.  
**Lapse rate:** The rate at which temperature changes with altitude.  
**Adiabatic cooling:** Cooling caused by expansion without heat exchange.  
**Adiabatic warming:** Warming caused by compression without heat exchange.  
**Stability:** The atmosphere’s tendency to resist or encourage vertical motion.  
**Inversion:** A layer in which temperature increases with height.  
**Seasonal lag:** The delay between maximum solar heating and the warmest part of the season.  
**Urban heat island:** A city area that is warmer than nearby rural surroundings.  
**Wind chill:** The apparent cold caused by wind increasing heat loss from skin.  
**Heat index:** The apparent heat produced by the combination of temperature and humidity.

**Next: Chapter 3 — Water Vapor, Humidity, Clouds, and the Birth of Rain**

# Chapter 3  
## Water Vapor, Humidity, Clouds, and the Birth of Rain

Every cloud begins invisibly.

Before water becomes a droplet, an ice crystal, a raindrop, or a snowflake, it exists in the atmosphere as **water vapor**—an invisible gas mixed with the air.

Clouds form when air is cooled enough for some of that vapor to change into liquid water or ice. Rain begins when those tiny cloud particles grow large enough to fall.

That sounds simple. Naturally, the atmosphere adds several layers of mischief.

---

## 3.1 Water Is Always Moving

Water continually circulates between Earth’s surface and atmosphere.

This movement is called the **water cycle**.

The main processes are:

- Evaporation
- Transpiration
- Condensation
- Deposition
- Precipitation
- Runoff
- Infiltration

Water may spend days in the atmosphere, years in a lake, centuries in deep groundwater, or thousands of years locked in ice.

### Illustration 1: The water cycle

```text
                      CLOUDS
          condensation and deposition
                       ↓
                precipitation
              rain • snow • hail
                       ↓
       LAND ─────────────── OCEAN
        ↓                      ↑
     runoff                evaporation
        ↓                      ↑
   rivers and lakes      water vapor
        ↑
 transpiration from plants
```

The water cycle is not a neat circular conveyor belt. Water may move through many different paths and storage places before returning to the atmosphere.

---

## 3.2 Evaporation

**Evaporation** is the change of liquid water into water vapor.

At the surface of a liquid, some molecules move fast enough to escape into the air.

Evaporation occurs from:

- Oceans
- Lakes
- Rivers
- Wet soil
- Puddles
- Leaves
- Skin
- Snow and ice, under some conditions

The rate of evaporation increases when:

- Temperature rises
- Wind increases
- Air becomes drier
- More water surface is exposed

A shallow puddle evaporates faster than the same amount of water in a narrow bottle because more of its surface is exposed.

Wind speeds evaporation by carrying away the moist air immediately above the water and replacing it with drier air.

---

## 3.3 Transpiration

Plants also add water vapor to the atmosphere.

They absorb water through their roots and release much of it through tiny pores in their leaves. This process is called **transpiration**.

Evaporation from surfaces and transpiration from plants are often combined under the term **evapotranspiration**.

Forests, crops, wetlands, and grasslands can release enormous amounts of water vapor into the air, influencing humidity, cloud formation, and rainfall.

### Illustration 2: Evapotranspiration

*A tree draws water upward from the soil. Tiny arrows show vapor leaving the leaves while nearby sunlight warms a pond and causes evaporation.*

---

## 3.4 Water Vapor Is Invisible

Water vapor cannot be seen.

The white plume rising from a kettle is not water vapor itself. It is made of countless tiny liquid droplets formed when invisible vapor cools and condenses.

Likewise:

- Clouds are not water vapor.
- Fog is not water vapor.
- Your visible breath on a cold morning is not water vapor.

All are collections of tiny droplets or ice crystals suspended in air.

This distinction is useful because it explains why warm, humid air can look perfectly clear even when it contains a large amount of water vapor.

---

## 3.5 How Much Water Vapor Can Air Contain?

Warm air can contain more water vapor before becoming saturated than cold air can.

This is often simplified as “warm air holds more moisture.” That phrase is convenient, though slightly misleading.

Air is not a container gripping water molecules. Instead, higher temperatures allow more water molecules to remain in the vapor phase before condensation begins.

The maximum possible amount rises rapidly with temperature.

For example, saturated air at 86°F, or 30°C, can contain far more water vapor than saturated air at 32°F, or 0°C.

This is why tropical air can become extremely humid and why cooling warm air can quickly produce clouds, fog, or dew.

---

## 3.6 Saturation

Air is **saturated** when water vapor and liquid water are in balance at a given temperature.

At saturation:

- Relative humidity is about 100 percent
- Evaporation and condensation may occur at equal rates
- Further cooling tends to produce condensation
- Adding more water vapor can also produce condensation

Saturation does not guarantee rain. It only means conditions allow water vapor to begin condensing if suitable particles are present.

---

## 3.7 Relative Humidity

**Relative humidity** compares the amount of water vapor currently present with the amount required for saturation at the same temperature.

It is expressed as a percentage.

Suppose the actual amount of water vapor remains unchanged.

If the air cools:

- The amount needed for saturation decreases
- Relative humidity rises

If the air warms:

- The amount needed for saturation increases
- Relative humidity falls

### Example

At sunrise, the air temperature is 60°F and the relative humidity is 95 percent.

By afternoon, the temperature rises to 82°F. No moisture is added or removed, but the relative humidity may fall to 45 percent.

The air did not become drier in terms of actual water vapor. It became warmer.

This is why relative humidity alone can be deceptive.

---

## 3.8 Dew Point

The **dew point** is the temperature to which air must cool for saturation to occur.

Unlike relative humidity, dew point directly reflects the actual amount of water vapor in the air.

Higher dew point means more moisture.

Lower dew point means less moisture.

A rough guide:

| Dew point | How the air often feels |
|---|---|
| Below 40°F / 4°C | Dry |
| 40–50°F / 4–10°C | Comfortable |
| 50–60°F / 10–16°C | Mildly humid |
| 60–65°F / 16–18°C | Noticeably humid |
| 65–70°F / 18–21°C | Muggy |
| 70–75°F / 21–24°C | Very humid |
| Above 75°F / 24°C | Oppressive |

Dew point is especially useful for comparing air masses.

A summer afternoon with a dew point of 45°F feels much drier than one with a dew point of 75°F, even if both have the same temperature.

---

## 3.9 Dew and Frost

When the ground cools at night, nearby air may cool to its dew point.

If the surface temperature remains above freezing, water vapor condenses into liquid droplets called **dew**.

If the surface is below freezing, water vapor may change directly into ice crystals. This is called **frost deposition**.

Frost can form even when the official air temperature is slightly above 32°F because surfaces such as grass, roofs, and car tops may cool more than the air measured several feet above the ground.

### Illustration 3: Dew and frost formation

```text
CLEAR NIGHT

Ground loses heat upward
          ↑
Surface cools
          ↓
Nearby air reaches saturation

Above freezing → DEW
Below freezing → FROST
```

---

## 3.10 Condensation Requires Tiny Surfaces

Water droplets do not usually form from nothing in perfectly clean air.

They form around microscopic particles called **cloud condensation nuclei**.

These particles may include:

- Sea salt
- Dust
- Smoke
- Sulfates
- Organic particles
- Pollution

Ice crystals may form around special particles called **ice nuclei**.

Without these particles, air can sometimes become slightly supersaturated before condensation begins.

The atmosphere is full of tiny particles, so cloud formation usually has plenty of raw material to work with. Even the sky needs a little dust to get things started.

---

## 3.11 How Air Reaches Saturation

Air can become saturated in several ways.

### Cooling the air

This is the most common route.

Air may cool by:

- Rising and expanding
- Contact with a cold surface
- Mixing with colder air
- Losing heat at night
- Moving over snow, ice, or cold water

### Adding water vapor

Air can also approach saturation when moisture is added through:

- Evaporation
- Transpiration
- Rain falling into dry air
- Moist air flowing into a region

Often, cooling and moisture addition happen together.

---

## 3.12 Why Rising Air Forms Clouds

When air rises, pressure decreases.

The air expands and cools.

If it cools to its dew point, condensation begins. Tiny droplets or ice crystals form, creating a cloud.

The altitude where condensation begins is called the **lifting condensation level**, or LCL.

This often corresponds roughly to the base of a cumulus cloud.

### Illustration 4: Rising air and cloud formation

```text
             CLOUD
       droplets begin forming
              ☁
              ↑
      air reaches dew point
              ↑
     rising air expands and cools
              ↑
          warm moist air
              ↑
          heated ground
```

The flatter the cloud base, the more uniform the lifting condensation level across the area.

---

## 3.13 Four Main Ways Air Is Lifted

Clouds often form because air is forced upward.

There are four major lifting mechanisms.

### Convection

The Sun heats the ground, which warms the air above it.

Warm, buoyant air rises in bubbles or columns called **thermals**.

This often produces puffy cumulus clouds.

### Frontal lifting

A front forms where different air masses meet.

Denser cold air can wedge beneath warm air, forcing the warm air upward.

This may produce broad cloud bands and precipitation.

### Orographic lifting

Air is forced upward over hills or mountains.

As it rises, it cools and may form clouds and precipitation on the windward side.

The descending air on the leeward side warms and dries.

### Convergence

When air flows together near the surface, it has nowhere to go but upward.

Convergence commonly occurs near low-pressure systems, sea-breeze boundaries, and tropical weather systems.

### Illustration 5: Four lifting mechanisms

*A four-panel diagram shows surface heating, a cold front lifting warm air, wind crossing a mountain, and two surface winds meeting and rising.*

---

## 3.14 Cloud Droplets Are Extremely Small

A typical cloud droplet is about 10 to 20 micrometers across.

That is tiny—far smaller than a raindrop.

A raindrop may be roughly one hundred times wider and contain about a million times more water than a small cloud droplet.

Cloud droplets fall very slowly. Even weak rising air can keep them suspended.

That is why a cloud can remain overhead rather than immediately falling out of the sky like an airborne bathtub.

---

## 3.15 Why Clouds Do Not Always Produce Rain

A cloud may contain millions of droplets without producing precipitation.

For rain to fall, droplets must become large enough that gravity overcomes the upward motion and air resistance keeping them suspended.

This growth occurs mainly through two processes:

- Collision and coalescence
- The ice-crystal process

---

## 3.16 Collision and Coalescence

In warm clouds, all or most of the cloud is above freezing.

Some droplets become slightly larger than others.

The larger droplets fall faster, collide with smaller droplets, and merge with them.

This is called **collision and coalescence**.

```text
small droplets   •  •  •  •
                       ↓
larger droplet      ●
                    ↓
collisions        ● + •
                    ↓
growing drop       ◉
                    ↓
                  RAIN
```

The process works best when clouds contain droplets of different sizes and have enough vertical depth for repeated collisions.

It is especially important in tropical clouds.

---

## 3.17 The Ice-Crystal Process

Many clouds extend into temperatures below freezing.

Cloud droplets can remain liquid even below 32°F. These are called **supercooled droplets**.

In a mixed cloud containing both ice crystals and supercooled droplets, water vapor tends to deposit onto the ice crystals.

The ice crystals grow while nearby droplets shrink.

This is often called the **Bergeron process** or **ice-crystal process**.

As ice crystals grow, they may:

- Fall as snow
- Join together into snowflakes
- Melt into rain
- Collect supercooled droplets
- Become sleet or hail under certain conditions

The ice-crystal process produces much of the precipitation in middle latitudes, even when the precipitation reaches the ground as rain.

---

## 3.18 Snowflakes

Snowflakes begin as ice crystals.

Their shapes depend mainly on temperature and humidity within the cloud.

Common forms include:

- Plates
- Columns
- Needles
- Dendrites
- Bullet rosettes
- Irregular crystals

The classic six-branched snowflake is a dendrite.

Snow crystals have sixfold symmetry because of the molecular structure of ice.

As crystals fall, they pass through changing temperature and humidity layers. Their final shape records part of that journey.

### Illustration 6: Basic snow crystal forms

*A field-guide plate showing a plate, column, needle, dendrite, capped column, and irregular crystal, each labeled.*

---

## 3.19 Rain, Drizzle, and Virga

### Rain

**Rain** consists of liquid drops generally larger than about 0.5 millimeters.

Large raindrops are not shaped like teardrops. Air resistance flattens their bottoms, making them resemble small hamburger buns.

Very large drops become unstable and break apart.

### Drizzle

**Drizzle** consists of very small, closely spaced droplets falling from low clouds.

It usually falls more slowly and uniformly than rain.

### Virga

Sometimes precipitation falls from a cloud but evaporates before reaching the ground.

This is called **virga**.

Virga often appears as wispy streaks beneath a cloud.

Evaporation cools the air, which can create strong downward-moving air called a downdraft.

---

## 3.20 Snow, Sleet, and Freezing Rain

Winter precipitation depends on the temperature of the entire atmosphere between the cloud and the ground.

### Snow

Snow reaches the ground when the air remains cold enough that ice crystals do not completely melt.

The surface temperature can sometimes be slightly above freezing if the lower atmosphere is cold enough or the snowfall is heavy.

### Sleet

Sleet consists of ice pellets.

Snow melts while falling through a warm layer, then refreezes in a deeper cold layer before reaching the ground.

### Freezing rain

Freezing rain begins as snow high in the cloud.

The snow melts into rain in a warm layer, then falls through a shallow layer of subfreezing air near the ground.

The drops do not have enough time to refreeze before landing. Instead, they freeze on contact with roads, trees, wires, and other surfaces.

### Illustration 7: Winter precipitation profiles

```text
SNOW
cold all the way down

SLEET
cold → warm melting layer → deep cold layer

FREEZING RAIN
cold → warm melting layer → shallow cold layer

RAIN
warm air reaches the ground
```

A difference of only a few degrees—or a few hundred feet in the depth of a cold layer—can completely change the precipitation type.

---

## 3.21 Hail

Hail forms in strong thunderstorms with powerful updrafts.

A developing hailstone may be carried above the freezing level, where supercooled droplets freeze onto it.

It may then move repeatedly through different parts of the storm, adding layers of clear or cloudy ice.

Eventually, it becomes too heavy for the updraft to support and falls.

Hail size depends on:

- Updraft strength
- Storm depth
- Amount of supercooled water
- Time spent inside the storm
- Melting before reaching the ground

Hail is different from sleet. Sleet is a winter precipitation process. Hail grows inside thunderstorms, often during warm weather.

---

## 3.22 Fog Is a Cloud at Ground Level

Fog forms when air near the surface becomes saturated.

Meteorologically, fog and cloud are essentially the same thing. The difference is where they form.

Common fog types include:

- Radiation fog
- Advection fog
- Upslope fog
- Steam fog
- Valley fog
- Precipitation fog

---

## 3.23 Radiation Fog

**Radiation fog** forms on clear, calm nights when the ground loses heat rapidly.

The ground cools the air directly above it. If that air reaches its dew point, fog forms.

Radiation fog is most common:

- Near sunrise
- In low-lying areas
- After recent rain
- Under clear skies
- With light winds

A slight breeze may help mix the cooled air and deepen the fog. Strong wind usually prevents it from forming.

Radiation fog often burns off after sunrise as the surface warms.

---

## 3.24 Advection Fog

**Advection fog** forms when warm, moist air moves over a colder surface.

Examples include:

- Moist air moving over cold ocean water
- Warm air flowing across snow-covered ground
- Coastal fog moving inland

Unlike radiation fog, advection fog can occur with moderate wind and may persist through the day.

---

## 3.25 Steam Fog

**Steam fog** forms when cold air moves over warmer water.

Water evaporates into the cold air immediately above the surface, quickly raising its moisture content to saturation.

The fog appears as wisps rising from the water.

It is sometimes called **sea smoke** or **evaporation fog**.

This is common over lakes and rivers during cold autumn or winter mornings.

---

## 3.26 Upslope and Valley Fog

### Upslope fog

Moist air moving uphill expands and cools.

If it reaches saturation, fog or low cloud forms along the slope.

### Valley fog

Cold, dense air drains into valleys and collects there.

If the air becomes saturated, fog can fill the valley while higher ground remains clear.

This pattern is especially common on calm nights.

---

## 3.27 Cloud Cover and Sky Condition

Meteorologists describe cloud cover by the fraction of the sky covered.

Common terms include:

| Term | Approximate coverage |
|---|---:|
| Clear | Little or no cloud |
| Few | 1/8 to 2/8 |
| Scattered | 3/8 to 4/8 |
| Broken | 5/8 to 7/8 |
| Overcast | 8/8 |

“Broken” and “overcast” are especially important in aviation because they represent a ceiling when clouds are low enough.

Cloud cover can be difficult to estimate because clouds overlap and perspective makes the sky dome appear distorted.

---

## 3.28 Cloud Base and Cloud Ceiling

The **cloud base** is the lowest altitude of a cloud.

The **ceiling** is the height of the lowest broken or overcast cloud layer, or the vertical visibility in obscured conditions.

Pilots and aviation forecasters pay close attention to ceilings because low clouds can restrict visibility and instrument approaches.

A rough estimate of cumulus cloud-base height can be made from the difference between air temperature and dew point.

In Fahrenheit:

```text
Cloud base in feet ≈
(temperature − dew point) ÷ 4.4 × 1,000
```

In Celsius:

```text
Cloud base in meters ≈
(temperature − dew point) × 125
```

This is only an approximation and works best for surface-heated convective clouds.

### Example

Temperature: 82°F  
Dew point: 64°F

Difference: 18°F

```text
18 ÷ 4.4 × 1,000 ≈ 4,100 feet
```

The cumulus cloud base may be near 4,100 feet above the surface.

---

## 3.29 Why Clouds Are White, Gray, or Dark

Cloud droplets scatter sunlight in many directions.

Because they scatter visible wavelengths fairly evenly, clouds often appear white.

Thicker clouds appear gray or dark because:

- Less sunlight penetrates through them
- Light is scattered repeatedly
- The cloud base is shaded
- The observer sees the cloud against a brighter surrounding sky

A dark cloud is not necessarily packed with dirty water or grim intentions. It is mainly thick enough to block more light.

Cloud edges appear bright because sunlight reaches them more directly.

---

## 3.30 Why Clouds Float

Cloud droplets fall under gravity, but they fall extremely slowly.

A small droplet may descend only a few centimeters per second.

Even weak rising air can keep it suspended.

Clouds persist because droplets are continually forming, evaporating, rising, falling, and being carried by air currents.

A cloud is not a fixed object. It is a visible region where atmospheric conditions allow droplets or ice crystals to exist.

Watching a cloud is more like watching a waterfall than watching a boulder.

---

# Field Observation  
## Measure Temperature and Dew Point

On three different days, record:

- Air temperature
- Dew point
- Relative humidity
- Cloud cover
- Whether the air feels dry, comfortable, humid, or oppressive

Try to include:

- A dry day
- A humid day
- A rainy or foggy day

Compare your physical impression with the dew point.

You may find that dew point matches comfort more consistently than relative humidity.

---

# Field Observation  
## Watch a Cloud Form

Choose a warm day with scattered cumulus clouds.

Watch one small cloud for ten minutes.

Notice whether:

- The base stays relatively flat
- The top grows upward
- The edges evaporate
- The cloud merges with another
- The entire cloud disappears

The cloud is revealing where air is rising, cooling, condensing, mixing, and sinking.

Sketch its shape once every two minutes. You will quickly discover that clouds are terrible at holding still for portraits.

---

# Try It  
## Make a Cloud in a Jar

You will need:

- A clear glass jar
- Warm water
- A metal lid or small plate
- Ice
- An adult to help if needed

Pour a small amount of warm water into the jar and swirl it around.

Place the lid or plate on top.

Put several ice cubes on the lid.

Warm water increases evaporation inside the jar. The cold lid cools the moist air near the top. Tiny droplets form, creating a faint cloud or fog.

A small amount of aerosol spray is sometimes used in demonstrations to provide condensation nuclei, but it is not necessary for a basic version and is best avoided indoors.

---

# Try It  
## Make Dew

Place two metal cans or glasses outside or on a table.

Fill one with room-temperature water.

Fill the other with ice water.

Watch the outside surfaces.

Water droplets should form on the colder container.

The water did not leak through the glass. It condensed from water vapor in the surrounding air.

---

# Chapter Summary

Water vapor is invisible and continually enters the atmosphere through evaporation and transpiration.

Warm air can contain more water vapor before saturation than cold air.

Relative humidity depends strongly on temperature, while dew point more directly describes the actual moisture content.

Clouds form when air cools to saturation and water vapor condenses or deposits onto microscopic particles.

Air commonly rises through convection, frontal lifting, terrain, or convergence.

Cloud droplets are tiny and remain suspended because they fall slowly and are supported by rising air.

Rain develops through collision and coalescence or through ice-crystal growth.

Winter precipitation type depends on the temperature profile through the entire atmosphere, not merely the temperature at the ground.

Fog is simply a cloud forming at the surface.

Clouds are constantly changing regions of condensation, evaporation, and airflow rather than permanent objects.

---

## Weather-Watcher’s Vocabulary

**Evaporation:** The change of liquid water into water vapor.  
**Transpiration:** The release of water vapor from plants.  
**Evapotranspiration:** Combined evaporation and transpiration.  
**Saturation:** The state in which air contains the equilibrium amount of water vapor at a given temperature.  
**Relative humidity:** Current water vapor compared with the saturation amount at the same temperature.  
**Dew point:** The temperature at which air becomes saturated when cooled.  
**Condensation:** The change of water vapor into liquid water.  
**Deposition:** The direct change of water vapor into ice.  
**Condensation nucleus:** A microscopic particle on which water droplets form.  
**Lifting condensation level:** The altitude where rising air first becomes saturated.  
**Supercooled water:** Liquid water existing below freezing.  
**Coalescence:** The joining of cloud droplets into larger drops.  
**Virga:** Precipitation that evaporates before reaching the ground.  
**Fog:** A cloud at ground level.  
**Ceiling:** The height of the lowest broken or overcast cloud layer.

**Next: Chapter 4 — The Cloud Families: Reading the Sky**

<!-- End of source chapter -->

---

# Chapter 4  
## The Cloud Families: Reading the Sky

Clouds are the atmosphere made visible.

They reveal where air is rising, sinking, cooling, spreading, or colliding. Their height, shape, texture, and rate of growth can offer clues about what the atmosphere is doing now—and what it may do next.

You do not need instruments to begin reading the sky. You need only a clear view, a little patience, and the willingness to look up before walking directly into a mailbox.

---

## 4.1 How Clouds Are Named

Most cloud names are built from a small group of Latin roots.

| Root | Meaning |
|---|---|
| **cirro-** | high, wispy |
| **alto-** | middle level |
| **stratus** | layer or sheet |
| **cumulus** | heap or pile |
| **nimbus / nimbo-** | precipitation-bearing |

These roots combine to describe a cloud’s altitude and form.

For example:

- **Cirrostratus**: a high, thin layer
- **Altocumulus**: middle-level heaps
- **Nimbostratus**: a widespread rain-bearing layer
- **Cumulonimbus**: a towering heap that produces precipitation and thunderstorms

Cloud naming is wonderfully logical right up until the atmosphere starts mixing categories together, which it does with great enthusiasm.

---

## 4.2 The Four Main Cloud Families

Clouds are often grouped into four broad families:

- High clouds
- Middle clouds
- Low clouds
- Clouds with strong vertical development

Typical cloud heights vary with latitude, season, and local conditions.

In general:

| Cloud family | Approximate base height |
|---|---:|
| High | 20,000–45,000 ft / 6–14 km |
| Middle | 6,500–20,000 ft / 2–6 km |
| Low | Surface–6,500 ft / surface–2 km |
| Vertical development | Low bases, tops reaching middle or high levels |

In colder polar regions, cloud layers are generally lower. In the tropics, they can extend much higher.

### Illustration 1: Cloud families by altitude

```text
             HIGH CLOUDS
      Cirrus • Cirrostratus • Cirrocumulus
       20,000–45,000 ft / 6–14 km

            MIDDLE CLOUDS
        Altostratus • Altocumulus
        6,500–20,000 ft / 2–6 km

              LOW CLOUDS
       Stratus • Stratocumulus
       Surface–6,500 ft / 0–2 km

       VERTICAL DEVELOPMENT
      Cumulus • Cumulonimbus
       Low bases, very high tops
```

---

# High Clouds

High clouds are composed mainly of ice crystals because temperatures at their altitude are well below freezing.

They are often thin, white, and delicate. They usually do not produce precipitation that reaches the ground, though falling ice crystals may form streaks called virga.

High clouds can be among the earliest visible signs of an approaching weather system.

---

## 4.3 Cirrus

**Cirrus** clouds are thin, wispy, fibrous clouds found high in the atmosphere.

They may resemble:

- Feathers
- Strands of hair
- Brushstrokes
- Curved hooks
- Horse tails

They form when ice crystals are carried and stretched by strong winds aloft.

### What cirrus may indicate

A few isolated cirrus clouds often mean fair weather.

Increasing cirrus that gradually thickens and spreads across the sky may signal an approaching warm front or large storm system, especially when the clouds arrive from one direction in an organized progression.

Cirrus can appear many hours before surface weather changes.

### Illustration 2: Cirrus

*Thin white streaks crossing a deep blue sky, with fibers curving at their ends.*

### Field marks

- Very high
- Thin and fibrous
- White, even when the lower sky is gray
- Does not cast noticeable shade
- Often moves differently from lower clouds

---

## 4.4 Cirrostratus

**Cirrostratus** is a thin, widespread veil of ice crystals covering much or all of the sky.

The Sun or Moon remains visible through it, though the sky may look milky.

Its most famous feature is the **halo**.

A common halo forms as light passes through hexagonal ice crystals. The ring often appears about 22 degrees from the Sun or Moon.

An easy way to estimate 22 degrees is to extend your arm and spread your thumb and little finger. The span is roughly comparable, though hand sizes vary.

### What cirrostratus may indicate

Cirrostratus often appears ahead of a warm front or broad low-pressure system.

When it thickens steadily and is followed by lower clouds, precipitation may arrive within roughly a day. This is a clue, not a countdown clock. Weather remains stubbornly unwilling to sign contracts.

### Illustration 3: Cirrostratus halo

*A pale sheet covers the sky, with a bright circular halo around the Sun.*

### Field marks

- Thin, uniform veil
- Covers a broad area
- Sun or Moon visible
- Halo may be present
- Few distinct cloud edges

---

## 4.5 Cirrocumulus

**Cirrocumulus** consists of small, white ripples, grains, or patches arranged in rows.

It may create the classic **mackerel sky**, named for its resemblance to fish scales.

The individual cloud elements are very small because they are high and far away.

### What cirrocumulus may indicate

Cirrocumulus can form where there is instability or wave motion high in the atmosphere.

It does not usually produce significant surface weather by itself, but it may accompany changing conditions aloft.

### Illustration 4: Cirrocumulus

*Hundreds of tiny white cloudlets arranged in parallel ripples across part of the sky.*

### Field marks

- High and white
- Small, grain-like cloudlets
- Little or no shading
- Often arranged in bands or waves
- Cloud pieces appear smaller than a finger held at arm’s length

---

# Middle Clouds

Middle clouds are composed of water droplets, ice crystals, or a mixture of both.

They often play an important role in developing frontal systems and can signal increasing moisture and lift in the middle atmosphere.

---

## 4.6 Altostratus

**Altostratus** is a broad gray or blue-gray sheet that covers much or all of the sky.

The Sun may appear as a dim, blurry disk, sometimes described as looking through frosted glass.

Altostratus is thicker than cirrostratus and usually does not produce a halo.

### What altostratus may indicate

Altostratus often forms ahead of widespread rain or snow.

It commonly appears as high clouds lower and thicken during the approach of a warm front.

Light precipitation may fall from it, especially as it deepens toward nimbostratus.

### Illustration 5: Altostratus

*A smooth gray-blue layer fills the sky. The Sun appears as a faint bright spot without sharp edges.*

### Field marks

- Broad, mostly featureless layer
- Gray or blue-gray
- Sun faintly visible or completely hidden
- Little contrast between cloud elements
- Often precedes steady precipitation

---

## 4.7 Altocumulus

**Altocumulus** clouds appear as rounded patches, rolls, or clumps at middle altitude.

They are larger than cirrocumulus elements and often show some gray shading.

They may occur in rows, waves, or broad fields.

### How to distinguish altocumulus from cirrocumulus

Hold your hand at arm’s length.

- Cirrocumulus elements are usually smaller than your little fingernail.
- Altocumulus elements are often about the size of your thumb or larger.
- Stratocumulus elements are usually larger still.

This is only a rough guide, but it works surprisingly well.

### What altocumulus may indicate

Ordinary altocumulus often brings no immediate precipitation.

However, certain forms can reveal instability.

**Altocumulus castellanus** has small turret-like tops. If it appears in the morning on a warm, humid day, it can suggest elevated instability and the possibility of thunderstorms later.

### Illustration 6: Altocumulus

*Rounded gray-and-white cloud patches arranged in rows, each with visible shading.*

### Field marks

- Middle altitude
- Rounded masses or rolls
- White and gray
- Some shading
- Elements larger than those of cirrocumulus

---

## 4.8 Altocumulus Lenticularis

**Lenticular clouds** are smooth, lens-shaped clouds that form in standing waves downwind of mountains or hills.

They may appear stacked like plates.

Because they remain nearly stationary while air flows through them, they can look eerily solid.

They are sometimes mistaken for flying saucers, which is understandable. The sky occasionally enjoys cosplay.

### What lenticular clouds indicate

They reveal strong, organized airflow over terrain and may indicate severe turbulence aloft.

Pilots treat them with respect.

### Illustration 7: Lenticular clouds

*Smooth oval clouds stacked above a mountain ridge, with sharp edges and a polished appearance.*

---

# Low Clouds

Low clouds consist mainly of liquid droplets, though ice may be present in cold weather.

They often produce gray skies, drizzle, mist, or light precipitation.

---

## 4.9 Stratus

**Stratus** is a low, uniform cloud layer that resembles fog lifted above the ground.

It often covers the entire sky and can create dull, damp conditions.

Stratus may form when:

- Moist air cools near the surface
- Fog lifts
- Gentle wind pushes air uphill
- Warm air moves over a cold surface
- Moisture becomes trapped beneath an inversion

### Weather associated with stratus

Stratus may produce:

- Mist
- Drizzle
- Light snow grains
- Reduced visibility

It rarely produces heavy precipitation.

### Illustration 8: Stratus

*A flat gray ceiling hangs low over a landscape, obscuring hilltops.*

### Field marks

- Very low
- Uniform gray layer
- Little visible texture
- May touch hills or buildings
- Often accompanied by mist or drizzle

---

## 4.10 Stratocumulus

**Stratocumulus** consists of low, broad rolls, lumps, or patches that cover much of the sky.

Unlike stratus, it has visible structure and texture.

The cloud elements are often large, with darker undersides and lighter edges.

### Weather associated with stratocumulus

Stratocumulus usually produces little precipitation, though drizzle or light showers are possible.

It commonly forms:

- Behind a cold front
- Beneath an inversion
- Over cool ocean water
- When cumulus clouds spread horizontally

### Illustration 9: Stratocumulus

*Large connected rolls of gray cloud fill the sky, with occasional blue gaps.*

### Field marks

- Low
- Large rounded elements
- Broad coverage
- Noticeable shading
- Usually limited vertical growth

---

## 4.11 Nimbostratus

**Nimbostratus** is a thick, dark, widespread cloud layer that produces prolonged rain or snow.

It often develops as altostratus thickens and lowers.

The cloud base may be difficult to identify because precipitation and ragged lower clouds obscure it.

### Weather associated with nimbostratus

Typical conditions include:

- Continuous rain or snow
- Low visibility
- Little or no thunder
- Large geographic coverage
- Several hours of precipitation

Nimbostratus is the cloud of a day that has fully committed to being wet.

### Illustration 10: Nimbostratus

*A dark, nearly featureless sky produces steady rain over a wide landscape. Ragged low clouds move beneath the main layer.*

### Field marks

- Thick and dark
- Covers most or all of the sky
- Sun completely hidden
- Steady precipitation
- Ragged clouds may form below it

---

# Clouds with Vertical Development

These clouds begin low but grow upward through multiple atmospheric levels.

Their vertical extent reveals the strength of rising air.

---

## 4.12 Fair-Weather Cumulus

**Cumulus humilis** is the familiar fair-weather cumulus cloud.

It appears as a small, bright heap with:

- A flat base
- Rounded top
- Crisp edges
- Limited vertical growth

The flat base marks the level where rising air reaches saturation.

The cloud top marks the level where rising air loses buoyancy or mixes with drier surroundings.

### What fair-weather cumulus indicates

These clouds usually form during daytime heating and often fade near sunset.

They suggest shallow convection and generally stable conditions above the cloud tops.

### Illustration 11: Fair-weather cumulus

*A small white cotton-like cloud with a flat gray base and rounded top.*

### Field marks

- Low, flat base
- Bright white top
- Modest height
- Large blue gaps
- Little change in size

---

## 4.13 Towering Cumulus

**Cumulus congestus**, or towering cumulus, forms when strong rising air allows a cumulus cloud to grow rapidly upward.

It has a cauliflower-like appearance with sharply defined turrets.

Its height may be greater than its width.

### What towering cumulus indicates

Towering cumulus can produce:

- Heavy showers
- Gusty winds
- Turbulence
- Early-stage thunderstorm development

If the cloud continues growing and develops ice in its upper portion, it may become cumulonimbus.

### Illustration 12: Towering cumulus

*A tall white cloud rises in stacked cauliflower towers above a dark, flat base.*

### Field marks

- Strong vertical growth
- Crisp bubbling top
- Darkening base
- Rapid change over minutes
- May produce showers

---

## 4.14 Cumulonimbus

**Cumulonimbus** is the thunderstorm cloud.

It can extend from a low base to near the top of the troposphere.

A mature cumulonimbus may contain:

- Powerful updrafts
- Downdrafts
- Heavy rain
- Lightning
- Hail
- Strong winds
- Tornadoes
- Ice crystals near the top

Its upper region often spreads outward into an **anvil** when rising air reaches the stable tropopause.

### Illustration 13: Cumulonimbus anatomy

```text
                     ANVIL
              ─────────────────
                     ice
                       ↑
               strong updraft
                       ↑
              towering cloud
            rain and hail core
                       ↓
                  downdraft
                       ↓
               gust front
──────────────────────────────── ground
```

### Field marks

- Huge vertical extent
- Dark base
- Heavy precipitation shaft
- Anvil-shaped top
- Lightning or thunder
- Rapid growth and movement

A cumulonimbus does not need to be directly overhead to be dangerous. Lightning can strike well outside the visible rain shaft.

---

## 4.15 The Anvil

The anvil forms when a thunderstorm updraft reaches the tropopause.

The air can no longer rise easily, so it spreads horizontally.

Upper-level winds may stretch the anvil far downwind.

A long, fibrous anvil indicates strong winds aloft and a mature storm.

Sometimes a dome briefly rises above the anvil. This is called an **overshooting top**.

It marks an exceptionally strong updraft punching into the stable layer above.

An overshooting top is often associated with severe weather.

---

## 4.16 Mammatus

**Mammatus** clouds are pouch-like bulges hanging beneath a cloud layer, often below a thunderstorm anvil.

They form through complex interactions involving sinking air, ice, evaporation, and turbulence.

### What mammatus indicates
