Mammatus is often associated with strong thunderstorms nearby, but it does not by itself mean a tornado is forming.

It may appear after the worst part of a storm has passed.

### Illustration 14: Mammatus

*Rounded pouch-like clouds hang beneath a dark anvil, glowing orange in late sunlight.*

---

## 4.17 Shelf Clouds

A **shelf cloud** is a low, horizontal cloud attached to the leading edge of a thunderstorm.

It forms above the gust front as cool storm outflow lifts warm, moist air.

Shelf clouds can look dramatic and threatening.

### What a shelf cloud indicates

A shelf cloud often means strong wind is approaching.

It may accompany:

- Sudden temperature drop
- Strong straight-line wind
- Blowing dust
- Heavy rain
- Frequent lightning

Shelf clouds can be mistaken for tornadoes because of their low, dark appearance, but they are usually broad and horizontal.

### Illustration 15: Shelf cloud

*A long, low wedge-shaped cloud advances ahead of heavy rain.*

---

## 4.18 Wall Clouds

A **wall cloud** is a localized lowering beneath the rain-free base of a strong thunderstorm.

It forms near the storm’s main updraft.

Wall clouds are usually smaller and more isolated than shelf clouds.

### Why wall clouds matter

A rotating wall cloud can precede tornado formation.

Warning signs include:

- Persistent rotation
- Rapid upward motion
- Strong inflow winds
- A tail-like cloud feeding into the lowering
- Increasing organization

Not every wall cloud produces a tornado, but a rotating wall cloud deserves immediate attention.

### Illustration 16: Wall cloud versus shelf cloud

```text
SHELF CLOUD
broad, horizontal, leading edge of storm
often associated with outflow wind

WALL CLOUD
localized lowering beneath storm base
often associated with inflow and rotation
```

---

## 4.19 Scud Clouds

**Scud** clouds are ragged, low fragments that form beneath storm clouds or in humid air.

They may move rapidly and look alarming.

Scud can resemble funnels, especially when viewed briefly through trees or buildings.

The key difference is rotation.

A true funnel is attached to a rotating cloud base and often has organized motion. Scud usually changes shape quickly and moves irregularly without persistent rotation.

Do not stand outside studying rotation during a severe thunderstorm. Curiosity is excellent. Becoming conductive is less so.

---

## 4.20 Funnel Clouds and Tornadoes

A **funnel cloud** is a rotating, cone-shaped condensation cloud extending downward from a thunderstorm but not visibly reaching the ground.

A **tornado** is a violently rotating column of air in contact with both the cloud base and the ground.

A tornado may be present even when the visible funnel does not extend all the way down. Dust or debris at the surface can reveal ground contact.

Funnel appearance varies:

- Narrow rope
- Cone
- Cylinder
- Wedge
- Irregular or partly hidden by rain

Cloud shape alone does not indicate tornado strength.

---

## 4.21 Pileus Clouds

A **pileus** is a smooth cap cloud that forms above a rapidly growing cumulus tower.

It develops when the rising cloud pushes moist air upward, causing it to cool and condense.

Pileus clouds are usually short-lived because the growing cumulus quickly rises through them.

### What pileus indicates

A pileus cloud is evidence of a strong updraft and rapid vertical development.

It may signal that a towering cumulus is becoming a thunderstorm.

### Illustration 17: Pileus

*A thin smooth cap sits over the top of a rapidly rising cauliflower-shaped cloud.*

---

## 4.22 Virga and Fallstreaks

**Virga** is precipitation that falls from a cloud but evaporates or sublimates before reaching the ground.

It appears as streaks extending beneath a cloud.

High-cloud fallstreaks are often made of ice crystals. Lower-cloud virga may contain evaporating rain.

Virga can produce strong downdrafts because evaporation cools the falling air, making it denser.

In dry climates, this can lead to a **dry microburst**—a powerful burst of wind with little rain at the surface.

---

## 4.23 Contrails

A **contrail**, short for condensation trail, forms behind an aircraft when hot, moist exhaust mixes with very cold air at high altitude.

Ice crystals form in the wake.

Contrails may:

- Disappear within seconds
- Persist for hours
- Spread into thin cirrus-like sheets

Persistence depends mainly on humidity at flight altitude.

A quickly fading contrail indicates relatively dry air aloft. A persistent, spreading contrail suggests ice-saturated air.

Contrails are human-made clouds, which feels a little arrogant, but there we are.

---

## 4.24 Cloud Streets

**Cloud streets** are long parallel rows of cumulus clouds aligned with the low-level wind.

They form in horizontal rolls of rising and sinking air.

Clouds develop over the rising portions, while clear gaps form over the sinking portions.

Cloud streets are common:

- Behind cold fronts
- Over oceans
- During cold-air outbreaks
- On breezy days with shallow convection

### Illustration 18: Cloud streets

*Parallel rows of small cumulus clouds extend toward the horizon.*

---

## 4.25 Wave Clouds

Air flowing over mountains or stable layers may begin oscillating in waves.

Clouds form where the air rises and cools, then evaporate where it sinks and warms.

This produces:

- Lenticular clouds
- Parallel cloud bands
- Repeating gaps
- Stationary cloud patterns despite strong wind

Wave clouds reveal that the atmosphere can behave much like water flowing over submerged rocks.

---

## 4.26 Clouds and Approaching Fronts

A classic warm-front cloud sequence often develops like this:

```text
CIRRUS
thin wisps

      ↓ thickening

CIRROSTRATUS
halo-producing veil

      ↓ lowering

ALTOSTRATUS
gray middle-level sheet

      ↓ thickening

NIMBOSTRATUS
steady rain or snow
```

This progression occurs because warm air is gradually lifted over a broad wedge of colder air.

The sequence may unfold over many hours.

Cold fronts often produce a different pattern:

- Cumulus or towering cumulus develops
- A narrow band of showers or thunderstorms forms
- Winds shift
- Cooler, drier air arrives
- Stratocumulus or clearing follows

Real fronts do not always follow the textbook pattern, but the cloud sequence is a useful starting point.

---

## 4.27 Clouds and Wind Direction Aloft

Clouds at different heights may move in different directions.

This reveals **wind shear**, a change in wind speed or direction with height.

For example:

- Low clouds move north
- Middle clouds move northeast
- High clouds race east

Such differences are common near fronts and storms.

Strong wind shear is important because it can organize thunderstorms and support rotation.

Watching multiple cloud layers is one of the simplest ways to see the three-dimensional atmosphere at work.

---

## 4.28 Estimating Cloud Motion

Choose a stationary object such as:

- A roof edge
- Utility pole
- Tree branch
- Building corner

Watch how long a cloud feature takes to cross behind it.

Repeat with different cloud layers.

You can compare relative speed and direction, though perspective makes precise estimates difficult.

Clouds near the horizon may appear to move slowly even when winds are strong because they are far away.

---

## 4.29 Cloud Optical Effects

Clouds and ice crystals can create beautiful optical phenomena.

### Halos

Rings around the Sun or Moon caused mainly by refraction through ice crystals.

### Sundogs

Bright spots appearing to the left and right of the Sun, often on a halo.

### Sun pillars

Vertical columns of light above or below the Sun caused by reflection from flat ice crystals.

### Iridescence

Pastel colors near cloud edges caused by diffraction through similarly sized droplets or crystals.

### Coronas

Small colored rings around the Sun or Moon, usually formed by water droplets.

### Crepuscular rays

Sunbeams separated by cloud shadows, appearing to spread outward through haze or dust.

### Illustration 19: Common cloud optics

*A field-guide plate showing a halo, sundogs, sun pillar, iridescent cloud, and crepuscular rays.*

Never stare directly at the Sun while examining these effects. Block it with a building, tree, or your hand.

---

## 4.30 A Practical Sky-Reading Method

When you look at a cloud, ask five questions.

### 1. How high is it?

Does it appear:

- High and delicate?
- Middle-level and patchy?
- Low and heavy?
- Vertically towering?

### 2. What shape is it?

Is it:

- Layered?
- Puffy?
- Fibrous?
- Smooth?
- Ragged?
- Turreted?

### 3. Is it growing or fading?

Sharp, rising edges suggest active growth.

Soft, fraying edges suggest evaporation.

### 4. What is the cloud doing to light?

Can you see the Sun?

Does the cloud cast a dark shadow?

Is there a halo?

### 5. Is the pattern changing?

Increasing and lowering clouds often indicate worsening weather.

Clouds breaking and lifting often indicate improvement.

The trend is usually more useful than a single cloud identification.

---

# Field Guide: Ten Common Clouds

| Cloud | Appearance | Common implication |
|---|---|---|
| Cirrus | Thin, wispy fibers | Fair weather or approaching system |
| Cirrostratus | High milky veil | Moisture increasing aloft |
| Cirrocumulus | Tiny ripples | High-level instability or waves |
| Altostratus | Gray middle layer | Widespread precipitation may approach |
| Altocumulus | Rounded middle patches | Variable; instability possible |
| Stratus | Low uniform sheet | Mist, drizzle, stable air |
| Stratocumulus | Low rolls or lumps | Mostly dry, shallow mixing |
| Nimbostratus | Thick dark layer | Long-lasting rain or snow |
| Cumulus | Puffy heap, flat base | Fair weather if growth stays limited |
| Cumulonimbus | Tower and anvil | Thunderstorm and severe hazards |

---

# Field Observation  
## Keep a Cloud Log

For seven days, record the sky at roughly the same time.

Write down:

- Cloud type
- Amount of sky covered
- Direction of movement
- Whether clouds are growing or shrinking
- Temperature
- Wind
- Weather six hours later

Do not worry about perfect identification.

The goal is to connect changing cloud patterns with later weather.

After a week, look for repeated sequences.

You may begin noticing that the sky has been giving away the plot before the forecast app catches up.

---

# Try It  
## Estimate Cloud Cover

Divide the sky mentally into eight equal sections.

Estimate how many sections are covered by cloud.

Use these categories:

```text
0/8      clear
1–2/8    few
3–4/8    scattered
5–7/8    broken
8/8      overcast
```

Repeat the estimate every fifteen minutes for one hour.

Notice how quickly cloud cover can change, especially on a windy or convective day.

---

# Try It  
## Identify Cloud Height by Shadows

Compare the brightness and shadowing of cloud elements.

High clouds:

- Usually bright white
- Little internal shadow
- Fine texture

Middle clouds:

- Larger elements
- Some gray shading
- Moderate texture

Low clouds:

- Stronger dark areas
- Large elements
- Obvious underside

This is not foolproof, but it helps when altitude is difficult to judge.

---

# Weather Safety  
## When Clouds Mean “Go Inside”

Seek substantial shelter when you observe:

- Thunder or lightning
- A rapidly growing cumulonimbus
- A shelf cloud approaching
- A rotating wall cloud
- A funnel cloud
- Strong wind lifting dust or debris
- A severe-weather warning
- A sudden greenish or unusually dark storm sky combined with other warning signs

Do not shelter under a tree, picnic cover, porch roof, or open garage doorway during lightning.

A sturdy enclosed building or hard-topped vehicle is safer.

Remember: if you can hear thunder, lightning is close enough to strike.

---

# Chapter Summary

Clouds are classified mainly by height, shape, and whether they produce precipitation.

High clouds include cirrus, cirrostratus, and cirrocumulus.

Middle clouds include altostratus and altocumulus.

Low clouds include stratus, stratocumulus, and nimbostratus.

Cumulus clouds reveal convection. Limited growth usually means fair weather, while rapid vertical growth can lead to thunderstorms.

Cumulonimbus clouds contain strong updrafts, downdrafts, lightning, heavy precipitation, and sometimes severe weather.

Special cloud features such as anvils, overshooting tops, shelf clouds, wall clouds, mammatus, and pileus reveal specific storm processes.

Cloud trends are usually more informative than a single cloud viewed in isolation.

Learning to read the sky turns clouds from scenery into evidence.

---

## Weather-Watcher’s Vocabulary

**Cirrus:** A high, thin cloud composed mainly of ice crystals.  
**Cirrostratus:** A high cloud veil that may produce halos.  
**Cirrocumulus:** Small high-level cloud ripples or grains.  
**Altostratus:** A broad middle-level gray cloud sheet.  
**Altocumulus:** Rounded middle-level cloud patches or rolls.  
**Stratus:** A low, uniform cloud layer.  
**Stratocumulus:** A low layer made of broad rolls or lumps.  
**Nimbostratus:** A thick layered cloud producing widespread precipitation.  
**Cumulus:** A heaped cloud formed by rising air.  
**Cumulonimbus:** A deep thunderstorm cloud.  
**Anvil:** The spreading upper portion of a thunderstorm.  
**Overshooting top:** A dome rising above a thunderstorm anvil.  
**Mammatus:** Pouch-like formations beneath a cloud layer.  
**Shelf cloud:** A broad cloud attached to a storm’s leading outflow.  
**Wall cloud:** A localized lowering beneath a thunderstorm updraft.  
**Pileus:** A smooth cap cloud above a rapidly growing cumulus.  
**Lenticular cloud:** A smooth lens-shaped wave cloud.  
**Contrail:** An aircraft-produced ice-cloud trail.  
**Virga:** Precipitation that evaporates before reaching the ground.  
**Wind shear:** A change in wind speed or direction with height.

**Next: Chapter 5 — Air Pressure, Wind, and the Forces That Move the Atmosphere**

<!-- End of source chapter -->

---

# Chapter 5  
## Air Pressure, Wind, and the Forces That Move the Atmosphere

Wind is air in motion, but it does not move randomly.

It responds to differences in pressure, Earth’s rotation, friction with the surface, terrain, temperature contrasts, and the structure of the atmosphere above.

A breeze slipping through trees and a jet stream racing across a continent are governed by the same basic forces. The difference is scale—and a frankly indecent amount of speed.

---

## 5.1 Atmospheric Pressure

Atmospheric pressure is the force produced by the weight of the air above a surface.

At sea level, average pressure is approximately:

- 1,013.25 hectopascals
- 1,013.25 millibars
- 29.92 inches of mercury

Meteorologists commonly use **hectopascals**, abbreviated hPa, and **millibars**, abbreviated mb. The two units are numerically equal.

```text
1 hPa = 1 mb
```

Pressure is not constant. It changes with:

- Altitude
- Temperature
- Humidity
- Air movement
- Nearby weather systems
- Time of day

Even small pressure differences can move enormous quantities of air.

---

## 5.2 Measuring Pressure

A **barometer** measures atmospheric pressure.

Two main types are commonly used.

### Mercury barometer

A mercury barometer uses a column of mercury inside a glass tube.

Higher atmospheric pressure supports a taller mercury column. Lower pressure supports a shorter one.

The traditional unit **inches of mercury**, abbreviated inHg, comes from this design.

### Aneroid barometer

An aneroid barometer uses a sealed, flexible metal chamber.

As outside pressure changes, the chamber expands or contracts. Levers translate that movement into a reading.

Most household barometers and many aircraft instruments use aneroid mechanisms.

### Electronic sensors

Modern weather stations, phones, aircraft, and research instruments often use electronic pressure sensors.

These can detect very small changes and continuously record trends.

### Illustration 1: Three barometer types

*A field-guide plate showing a mercury tube, a round aneroid dial, and a compact digital sensor.*

---

## 5.3 Station Pressure and Sea-Level Pressure

Pressure decreases with altitude.

A weather station on a mountain naturally measures lower pressure than one at sea level, even when both are beneath the same general weather system.

To compare stations fairly, meteorologists calculate what the pressure would be if each station were located at sea level.

This adjusted value is called **sea-level pressure**.

Without this adjustment, weather maps would mostly show mountains as permanent low-pressure systems, which would be accurate in one technical sense and useless in every practical one.

### Station pressure

The actual pressure measured at the station’s elevation.

### Sea-level pressure

The pressure adjusted to a common sea-level reference.

Weather maps typically use sea-level pressure.

---

## 5.4 High and Low Pressure

A **high-pressure system** is an area where pressure is greater than in the surrounding region.

A **low-pressure system** is an area where pressure is lower than nearby values.

Pressure is always relative. A reading is not automatically “high” or “low” by itself; it depends on the surrounding pattern.

### High pressure commonly features

- Sinking air
- Surface air spreading outward
- Fewer clouds
- Lighter winds near the center
- Stable conditions
- Dry weather

### Low pressure commonly features

- Rising air
- Surface air flowing inward
- Clouds
- Precipitation
- Stronger wind
- Changing weather

These are tendencies, not guarantees. A high can bring fog, cold air, heat, or pollution. A low can pass with little rain if moisture is scarce.

---

## 5.5 Isobars

Lines connecting equal pressure on a weather map are called **isobars**.

They reveal the shape and strength of pressure systems.

```text
        1024
      1020  1020
    1016      1016
         H
```

A closed set of isobars surrounding higher values marks a high-pressure center.

```text
        1008
      1004  1004
    1000      1000
         L
```

A closed set surrounding lower values marks a low-pressure center.

### Closely spaced isobars

Indicate a strong pressure gradient and usually stronger wind.

### Widely spaced isobars

Indicate a weak pressure gradient and usually lighter wind.

### Illustration 2: Isobar spacing

```text
LIGHTER WIND

1024        1020        1016

STRONGER WIND

1024  1020  1016  1012
```

---

## 5.6 The Pressure-Gradient Force

The **pressure-gradient force** pushes air from higher pressure toward lower pressure.

It is the force that starts the wind moving.

The strength of the force depends on how much pressure changes over a given distance.

A large change over a short distance produces a strong pressure gradient.

A small change over a long distance produces a weak gradient.

```text
HIGH PRESSURE  →  →  →  LOW PRESSURE
```

If this were the only force acting, wind would blow directly from high pressure to low pressure.

But Earth rotates, which complicates matters in a rather elegant way.

---

## 5.7 The Coriolis Effect

Because Earth rotates, moving air appears to curve relative to the surface.

This apparent turning is called the **Coriolis effect**.

In the Northern Hemisphere, moving air is deflected to the right.

In the Southern Hemisphere, it is deflected to the left.

The Coriolis effect:

- Is weakest near the equator
- Increases toward the poles
- Becomes more important as wind speed increases
- Affects large-scale motion much more than small-scale motion

It does not initiate wind. Pressure differences begin the motion; Coriolis changes its direction.

### Illustration 3: Hemispheric deflection

```text
NORTHERN HEMISPHERE
moving air curves right

SOUTHERN HEMISPHERE
moving air curves left
```

The Coriolis effect does not control the direction water drains from an ordinary sink or toilet. At that scale, basin shape, plumbing, and initial motion dominate.

The atmosphere has enough mysteries without recruiting bathroom fixtures.

---

## 5.8 Geostrophic Wind

High above the surface, friction is weak.

There, the pressure-gradient force may become balanced by the Coriolis effect.

The resulting airflow is called **geostrophic wind**.

Instead of blowing directly across isobars from high to low pressure, geostrophic wind flows approximately parallel to them.

In the Northern Hemisphere:

- Lower pressure lies to the left of the wind
- Higher pressure lies to the right

In the Southern Hemisphere, the relationship is reversed.

```text
NORTHERN HEMISPHERE

HIGH PRESSURE
────────────── isobars
wind → → → → →
──────────────
LOW PRESSURE
```

Geostrophic flow is an idealized balance, but it is a useful approximation for large-scale winds away from the surface and outside sharply curved systems.

---

## 5.9 Gradient Wind

Around curved pressure systems, another balance develops.

The wind following curved isobars is called **gradient wind**.

Curvature matters because air moving in a circle requires an inward-directed force.

As a result, wind speed differs around highs and lows even when pressure gradients are similar.

The exact balance can become mathematically involved, but the practical point is simple:

**Curved airflow does not behave exactly like straight airflow.**

That difference matters around cyclones, anticyclones, hurricanes, and upper-level weather systems.

---

## 5.10 Friction Near the Surface

Near the ground, wind encounters friction from:

- Trees
- Buildings
- Hills
- Crops
- Water waves
- Rough terrain

Friction slows the wind.

When wind slows, the Coriolis effect weakens because Coriolis depends partly on wind speed.

The pressure-gradient force then turns the wind more directly toward lower pressure.

This is why surface wind crosses isobars at an angle rather than flowing perfectly parallel to them.

### Over smooth water

Friction is relatively weak, so surface wind crosses isobars at a shallow angle.

### Over rough land

Friction is stronger, so wind crosses isobars more sharply toward lower pressure.

### Illustration 4: Surface and upper-level wind

```text
ALOFT
wind flows nearly parallel to isobars

SURFACE
wind crosses isobars toward lower pressure
```

---

## 5.11 Circulation Around Highs and Lows

In the Northern Hemisphere:

### Around a low

Air spirals inward and counterclockwise.

### Around a high

Air spirals outward and clockwise.

In the Southern Hemisphere, both directions reverse.

```text
NORTHERN HEMISPHERE

LOW PRESSURE
counterclockwise and inward

HIGH PRESSURE
clockwise and outward
```

The inward flow around a low creates **convergence** near the surface. Air is forced upward.

The outward flow around a high creates **divergence** near the surface. Air from above sinks to replace it.

---

## 5.12 Convergence and Divergence

### Convergence

Convergence occurs when air flows together.

Near the surface, converging air usually rises.

Rising air expands and cools, which may lead to:

- Clouds
- Rain
- Thunderstorms
- Deepening low pressure

### Divergence

Divergence occurs when air spreads apart.

At the surface, divergence is often associated with sinking air from above.

Sinking air compresses and warms, which tends to suppress cloud development.

But upper-level divergence can strengthen rising air below.

This is one reason meteorologists study pressure patterns at several altitudes rather than focusing only on the surface.

### Illustration 5: Surface and upper-air motion

```text
DEVELOPING LOW

upper air spreads apart
← ←       → →
       ↑
air rises
       ↑
surface air converges
→ →   L   ← ←
```

---

## 5.13 Cyclones and Anticyclones

A broad region of rotating low pressure is called a **cyclone**.

A broad region of rotating high pressure is called an **anticyclone**.

The word cyclone does not automatically mean hurricane.

Cyclone is a general term that includes:

- Midlatitude low-pressure systems
- Tropical cyclones
- Some smaller rotating weather systems

An anticyclone is simply a high-pressure circulation.

### Midlatitude cyclone

Usually linked to fronts, widespread cloud bands, and changing weather.

### Tropical cyclone

A warm-core system fueled by warm ocean water and thunderstorms.

### Anticyclone

Often associated with sinking air and fair weather, though it can also trap heat, cold, fog, or pollution.

---

## 5.14 Pressure Tendency

The trend in pressure can be more useful than the current reading.

### Falling pressure

May indicate:

- An approaching low
- An approaching front
- Increasing wind
- Deteriorating weather

### Rising pressure

May indicate:

- A departing low
- An approaching high
- Improving weather
- Cooler or drier air behind a front

### Steady pressure

May indicate:

- Little immediate change
- Location near the center of a stable system
- A slow-moving pattern

Pressure does not predict weather alone. The speed of change, season, location, wind direction, and cloud trend all matter.

---

## 5.15 The Diurnal Pressure Cycle

Atmospheric pressure normally rises and falls slightly during the day, especially in the tropics.

This regular pattern is caused by atmospheric tides driven mainly by solar heating.

There are often two daily pressure maxima and two minima.

In middle latitudes, larger weather systems can hide the pattern, but precision barometers may still detect it.

A tiny pressure wobble does not necessarily mean a storm is approaching. Sometimes the atmosphere is merely keeping its appointment.

---

## 5.16 Wind Direction

Wind direction is named for the direction the wind comes from.

A north wind blows from north to south.

A southwest wind blows from southwest to northeast.

This convention matters because wind often reveals the source region of an air mass.

For example:

- North wind may bring colder air
- South wind may bring warmer air
- Onshore wind may bring moist marine air
- Offshore wind may bring drier continental air

The effect depends on geography.

A south wind means something different in Texas than it does in coastal Chile.

---

## 5.17 Wind Speed

Wind speed may be reported in:

- Miles per hour
- Kilometers per hour
- Knots
- Meters per second

One knot equals one nautical mile per hour.

```text
1 knot ≈ 1.15 mph
1 knot ≈ 1.85 km/h
```

Aviation and marine forecasts commonly use knots.

Meteorological research often uses meters per second.

---

## 5.18 Measuring Wind

### Wind vane

A wind vane shows wind direction.

The broad tail turns downwind while the pointer faces into the wind.

### Cup anemometer

Rotating cups measure wind speed.

The faster the cups turn, the stronger the wind.

### Propeller anemometer

A propeller faces into the wind and spins according to wind speed.

### Sonic anemometer

Sonic instruments measure how wind changes the travel time of sound pulses.

They have no moving parts and can measure rapid changes in wind.

### Illustration 6: Common wind instruments

*A weather vane, cup anemometer, propeller sensor, and sonic anemometer shown side by side.*

---

## 5.19 Sustained Wind and Gusts

A **sustained wind** is an average over a specified period.

A **gust** is a brief increase above the sustained speed.

For example:

```text
Wind: 20 mph
Gusts: 35 mph
```

This means the wind averages near 20 mph but occasionally peaks around 35 mph.

Gusts are caused by:

- Turbulent mixing
- Showers and thunderstorms
- Strong winds mixing down from aloft
- Terrain effects
- Passing fronts
- Buildings and trees disrupting flow

A single gust can cause more damage than the average wind suggests.

---

## 5.20 Wind Shear

**Wind shear** is a change in wind speed or direction over distance.

### Vertical wind shear

Wind changes with altitude.

### Horizontal wind shear

Wind changes across a horizontal distance.

Wind shear can:

- Organize thunderstorms
- Create aircraft turbulence
- Produce rotating storm updrafts
- Help form jet streams
- Intensify weather fronts
- Damage structures during strong gusts

Strong vertical shear can separate a thunderstorm’s updraft from its downdraft, allowing the storm to survive longer and become more organized.

---

## 5.21 Turbulence

Turbulence is irregular, swirling air motion.

It may be caused by:

- Surface heating
- Buildings and terrain
- Wind shear
- Thunderstorms
- Jet streams
- Mountain waves
- Fronts

### Mechanical turbulence

Created when wind flows around obstacles.

### Thermal turbulence

Created by rising warm air.

### Clear-air turbulence

Occurs without visible clouds, often near strong jet-stream shear.

Turbulence may be uncomfortable, but aircraft are designed to tolerate far more than the average passenger’s clenched jaw would suggest.

---

## 5.22 Boundary-Layer Wind

The lowest part of the atmosphere directly influenced by the surface is called the **planetary boundary layer**.

Its depth changes through the day.

### Daytime

Surface heating creates turbulence and mixes air vertically.

The boundary layer deepens.

Stronger wind from aloft may mix downward, producing afternoon gustiness.

### Nighttime

The ground cools and a stable layer forms near the surface.

Vertical mixing weakens.

Surface wind may become light while stronger wind continues just above the ground.

This can produce a **low-level jet**, especially over broad plains.

---

## 5.23 Local Wind Systems

Not all winds come from large pressure systems.

Local temperature and terrain differences create smaller circulations.

Common examples include:

- Sea breeze
- Land breeze
- Valley breeze
- Mountain breeze
- Slope wind
- Urban breeze
- Lake breeze

---

## 5.24 Valley and Mountain Breezes

### Valley breeze

During the day, mountain slopes warm.

Air along the slopes heats and rises, producing an upslope or valley breeze.

### Mountain breeze

At night, slopes cool rapidly.

Cold, dense air drains downhill into valleys.

```text
DAY
warm air moves upslope

NIGHT
cold air drains downslope
```

These circulations affect:

- Fog
- Frost
- Wildfire behavior
- Cloud formation
- Air pollution
- Local temperature

---

## 5.25 Katabatic and Anabatic Winds

An **anabatic wind** flows uphill, usually because the slope is heated.

A **katabatic wind** flows downhill because cold, dense air sinks under gravity.

Katabatic winds can become extremely strong over ice sheets, mountains, and high plateaus.

Examples include powerful downslope winds in Antarctica and Greenland.

---

## 5.26 Downslope Warming

When air descends a mountain, pressure increases.

The air compresses and warms.

If it lost moisture while rising on the windward side, it may arrive on the leeward side much warmer and drier.

This creates warm downslope winds known by regional names such as:

- Chinook
- Foehn
- Santa Ana
- Zonda

These winds can cause:

- Rapid warming
- Very low humidity
- Snowmelt
- Dangerous wildfire conditions
- Strong gusts

### Illustration 7: Mountain airflow

```text
WINDWARD SIDE

moist air rises
cools
clouds and rain form

       MOUNTAIN

LEEWARD SIDE

air descends
compresses
warms and dries
```

---

## 5.27 The Rain Shadow

Mountains can create a **rain shadow**.

Moist air rises on the windward side, cools, and produces precipitation.

The air then descends on the leeward side, warms, and becomes drier.

As a result, one side of a mountain range may be forested while the other is desert or semiarid grassland.

Rain shadows shape entire regional climates.

---

## 5.28 The Beaufort Wind Scale

Before accurate anemometers were common, observers estimated wind speed by its visible effects.

The **Beaufort scale** remains useful for rough field estimates.

| Beaufort force | Description | Approximate effect |
|---:|---|---|
| 0 | Calm | Smoke rises vertically |
| 1 | Light air | Smoke drifts |
| 2 | Light breeze | Leaves rustle |
| 3 | Gentle breeze | Small twigs move |
| 4 | Moderate breeze | Dust and loose paper lift |
| 5 | Fresh breeze | Small trees sway |
| 6 | Strong breeze | Large branches move |
| 7 | Near gale | Whole trees move |
| 8 | Gale | Twigs break |
| 9 | Strong gale | Minor structural damage |
| 10 | Storm | Trees may uproot |
| 11 | Violent storm | Widespread damage |
| 12 | Hurricane force | Severe and extensive damage |

The exact wind-speed ranges vary by unit and official table, but the visible effects remain a practical guide.

---

## 5.29 Wind and Trees

Trees reveal wind differently depending on species, season, and moisture.

A useful rough guide:

- Leaves trembling: light breeze
- Small branches moving: moderate breeze
- Large branches moving: strong breeze
- Entire trees swaying: near gale
- Twigs breaking: gale
- Large limbs breaking: damaging wind

Trees are useful indicators, but they are also potential hazards.

Do not estimate wind speed from beneath the one dropping branches. That would be impressively empirical and deeply stupid.

---

## 5.30 Wind Over Water

Water surfaces also reveal wind strength.

### Calm

Mirror-like surface.

### Light breeze

Small ripples.

### Moderate breeze

Frequent small waves and scattered whitecaps.

### Strong breeze

Many whitecaps and blowing spray.

### Gale

Large waves, streaks of foam, and difficult visibility.

Sailors used these clues long before modern instruments.

---

## 5.31 Jet Streams

A **jet stream** is a narrow band of very strong wind high in the atmosphere.

Jet streams commonly occur near the tropopause where large horizontal temperature differences exist.

They generally flow from west to east, though they meander in broad waves.

Major jet streams include:

- Polar jet
- Subtropical jet

Wind speeds can exceed 150 mph, or 240 km/h.

### Illustration 8: Meandering jet stream

*A ribbon of fast upper-level wind curves north and south across a continent.*

---

## 5.32 Why Jet Streams Form

Warm air columns are thicker than cold air columns.

As a result, pressure surfaces slope across regions with strong temperature contrasts.

This creates strong upper-level pressure gradients.

The Coriolis effect turns the resulting flow until powerful winds develop roughly parallel to the temperature boundary.

Jet streams are therefore closely connected to:

- Fronts
- Midlatitude storms
- Seasonal temperature contrasts
- Polar and tropical air masses

---

## 5.33 Ridges and Troughs

Jet streams form large waves.

### Ridge

A northward bulge in upper-level flow in the Northern Hemisphere.

Ridges are often associated with:

- Warmer conditions
- Sinking air
- Drier weather
- Persistent heat

### Trough

A southward dip in upper-level flow.

Troughs are often associated with:

- Cooler conditions
- Rising air
- Clouds
- Storm systems

```text
RIDGE
      /\
     /  \

TROUGH
     \  /
      \/
```

These relationships are broad tendencies. Surface weather depends on the exact position and movement of the pattern.

---

## 5.34 Rossby Waves

The large north-south bends in upper-level westerly winds are called **Rossby waves**.

They form because of Earth’s rotation, the variation of the Coriolis effect with latitude, and the distribution of heat around the planet.

Rossby waves can move slowly and strongly influence weather for days or weeks.

When the pattern becomes highly amplified, it can contribute to:

- Heat waves
- Cold outbreaks
- Flooding
- Drought
- Persistent storms

---

## 5.35 Shortwaves

Smaller disturbances embedded within large upper-level waves are called **shortwaves**.

They can enhance rising motion and help trigger:

- Cloud development
- Rain
- Snow
- Thunderstorms
- Surface low-pressure development

A weather map may show a broad trough, but a smaller shortwave inside it may determine where the most active weather occurs.

The atmosphere is very fond of hiding important details inside bigger details.

---

## 5.36 The Polar Vortex

The **polar vortex** is a broad region of low pressure and cold air surrounding the poles high in the atmosphere.

There is both a tropospheric and stratospheric polar circulation.

It is not a single storm that suddenly escapes the Arctic.

When the circulation weakens, stretches, or shifts, lobes of cold air may move farther south.

The phrase is often used dramatically in news reports, but polar circulation is a normal part of Earth’s atmosphere.

---

## 5.37 Blocking Patterns

Sometimes upper-level systems become nearly stationary.

This is called **blocking**.

A blocking pattern can redirect or stall normal weather systems.

It may cause prolonged:

- Heat
- Cold
- Rain
- Drought
- Wildfire conditions

Common forms include:

- Omega block
- Rex block
- Cutoff high
- Cutoff low

An omega block resembles the Greek letter Ω, with a high-pressure ridge between two lows.

---

## 5.38 Wind Around Buildings

Buildings distort airflow.

Wind may:

- Accelerate around corners
- Funnel between tall structures
- Create eddies behind walls
- Produce strong downdrafts near towers
- Become turbulent at roof level

This explains why a city sidewalk can suddenly feel much windier than the official observation from a nearby airport.

Wind between buildings is not necessarily stronger over the entire region. It is locally squeezed and redirected.

---

## 5.39 Wind in Forests

Forests slow wind near the ground.

Above the canopy, wind may remain much stronger.

Near forest edges, turbulence can increase as airflow suddenly encounters rough vegetation.

This matters for:

- Fire behavior
- Agriculture
- Wildlife
- Wind-energy planning
- Outdoor safety

A forest can provide shelter from steady wind but becomes dangerous during high winds because branches and trees may fail.

---

## 5.40 Wind and Fire

Wind is one of the most important factors controlling wildfire behavior.

It can:

- Supply oxygen
- Push flames toward new fuel
- Carry embers far ahead
- Dry vegetation
- Change direction rapidly near terrain
- Create dangerous fire-generated winds

Strong downslope winds are especially hazardous because they are often warm, dry, and gusty.

Weather conditions that seem merely unpleasant in town may be explosive in dry vegetation.

---

## 5.41 Wind and the Human Body

Wind increases heat transfer from the skin.

In cold weather, this produces wind chill.

In hot weather, wind may improve comfort by increasing evaporation—unless the air is extremely hot, in which case it can add heat to the body.

Strong wind also affects balance.

People may have difficulty walking when gusts become strong, especially on:

- Bridges
- Exposed ridges
- Icy surfaces
- Beaches
- Rooftops

Wind also turns ordinary objects into projectiles with alarming creativity.

---

# Field Observation  
## Read the Wind Without Instruments

Choose an open location and observe:

- Smoke
- Leaves
- Flags
- Grass
- Tree branches
- Clouds
- Water, if nearby

Estimate:

- Wind direction
- Sustained speed
- Gustiness
- Whether wind is steady or variable

Then compare your estimate with a nearby official weather observation.

Repeat on several days.

Over time, you will become surprisingly good at judging wind by its effects.

---

# Try It  
## Build a Simple Wind Vane

You will need:

- A drinking straw
- An index card
- A pin
- A pencil with an eraser
- Modeling clay
- A paper plate
- A compass

Cut the card into an arrowhead and tail.

Attach them to opposite ends of the straw.

Push a pin through the center of the straw and into the pencil eraser.

Stand the pencil upright in clay.

Mark north, south, east, and west on the plate using a compass.

Place the vane outdoors in an open area.

The arrow should point toward the direction the wind comes from.

---

# Try It  
## Make a Pressure Trend Log

Record barometric pressure at the same times each day for one week.

Also record:

- Cloud cover
- Wind direction
- Wind speed
- Precipitation
- Temperature

Look for patterns.

Did pressure fall before rain?

Did wind shift when pressure began rising?

Did skies clear behind the lowest pressure?

A single week may not reveal every relationship, but it will turn a number on a screen into part of a living weather pattern.

---

# Field Exercise  
## Read an Isobar Map

On a surface weather map:

1. Locate the nearest high and low.
2. Find where isobars are closest together.
3. Predict where wind is strongest.
4. Estimate the broad wind direction.
5. Look for fronts near the low.
6. Compare your prediction with observed wind.

Remember that surface wind crosses isobars slightly toward lower pressure.

The map is not merely a picture. It is a diagram of forces.

---

# Weather Safety  
## Strong Wind

During a high-wind warning:

- Move indoors
- Stay away from windows
- Secure loose outdoor objects
- Avoid wooded areas
- Avoid high-profile vehicles when possible
- Expect power outages
- Never approach downed power lines

After a storm, assume fallen wires are energized.

A branch resting on a line may also be energized.

Wind often causes danger indirectly through debris, falling trees, vehicles, waves, and electrical damage.

---

# Chapter Summary

Atmospheric pressure is produced by the weight of the air.

Pressure differences create the pressure-gradient force, which starts air moving.

Earth’s rotation produces the Coriolis effect, turning large-scale airflow.

Friction slows surface wind and causes it to cross isobars toward lower pressure.

In the Northern Hemisphere, surface air spirals inward counterclockwise around lows and outward clockwise around highs.

Convergence encourages rising air, while divergence is associated with spreading or sinking air.

Closely spaced isobars usually indicate stronger wind.

Wind direction is named for the direction from which the wind comes.

Local winds develop through uneven heating and terrain effects.

Jet streams are narrow bands of strong upper-level wind that guide major weather systems.

Ridges, troughs, Rossby waves, shortwaves, and blocks help determine whether weather changes quickly or remains stuck in place.

Pressure and wind reveal the invisible architecture of the atmosphere.

---

## Weather-Watcher’s Vocabulary

**Barometer:** An instrument that measures atmospheric pressure.  
**Station pressure:** The actual pressure measured at a station’s elevation.  
**Sea-level pressure:** Pressure adjusted to sea level for comparison.  
**Isobar:** A line connecting equal pressure.  
**Pressure-gradient force:** The force that pushes air from higher toward lower pressure.  
**Coriolis effect:** The apparent turning of moving air caused by Earth’s rotation.  
**Geostrophic wind:** Upper-level wind flowing approximately parallel to isobars.  
**Gradient wind:** Wind following curved pressure patterns.  
**Convergence:** Airflow coming together.  
**Divergence:** Airflow spreading apart.  
**Cyclone:** A rotating low-pressure circulation.  
**Anticyclone:** A rotating high-pressure circulation.  
**Wind shear:** A change in wind speed or direction over distance.  
**Boundary layer:** The lowest atmospheric layer directly influenced by the surface.  
**Katabatic wind:** A cold, dense downslope wind.  
**Anabatic wind:** An upslope wind produced by surface heating.  
**Rain shadow:** A dry region on the leeward side of mountains.  
**Jet stream:** A narrow band of strong upper-level wind.  
**Ridge:** A northward bulge of upper-level high pressure in the Northern Hemisphere.  
**Trough:** A southward dip of upper-level low pressure in the Northern Hemisphere.  
**Rossby wave:** A large atmospheric wave in the upper-level westerlies.  
**Blocking pattern:** A persistent upper-level pattern that slows or redirects weather systems.

**Next: Chapter 6 — Air Masses and Fronts: Where Weather Systems Meet**

# Chapter 6  
## Air Masses and Fronts: Where Weather Systems Meet

Most dramatic weather does not happen inside a perfectly uniform body of air.

It happens where different kinds of air meet.

A warm, humid air mass may slide beneath cold, dry air. Arctic air may push south across a plain. Tropical moisture may surge north ahead of a low-pressure system. Along the boundaries between them, air rises, clouds form, winds shift, and weather changes.

These vast bodies of air are called **air masses**. Their boundaries are called **fronts**.

A front is not a wall. It is a sloping transition zone, often hundreds of miles long and several miles deep.

---

## 6.1 What Is an Air Mass?

An **air mass** is a large body of air with relatively uniform temperature and moisture characteristics.

Air masses may cover:

- Several states
- Large parts of a country
- Entire seas
- Much of a continent

They form when air remains over a broad, fairly uniform surface long enough to take on that surface’s properties.

Such areas are called **source regions**.

Good source regions include:

- Ice sheets
- Snow-covered plains
- Deserts
- Tropical oceans
- Cool northern oceans
- Broad continental interiors

Mountainous areas and regions with constantly changing weather are poor source regions because the surface is too varied or the air does not remain still long enough.

### Illustration 1: Air-mass source regions

*A globe centered on North America shows arrows from Arctic ice, northern Canada, the Pacific Ocean, the Gulf of Mexico, and the desert Southwest.*

---

## 6.2 How Air Masses Are Classified

Air-mass names describe moisture and temperature.

The first letter indicates moisture:

| Symbol | Meaning |
|---|---|
| **c** | Continental: generally dry |
| **m** | Maritime: generally moist |

The second letter indicates temperature source:

| Symbol | Meaning |
|---|---|
| **A** | Arctic |
| **P** | Polar |
| **T** | Tropical |
| **E** | Equatorial |

Common air masses include:

- **cA** — continental Arctic
- **cP** — continental Polar
- **mP** — maritime Polar
- **cT** — continental Tropical
- **mT** — maritime Tropical

Equatorial air masses are sometimes included in broader tropical classifications.

---

## 6.3 Continental Arctic Air

**Continental Arctic**, or cA, air is extremely cold and very dry.

It forms over:

- Arctic ice
- Northern Canada
- Siberia
- Snow-covered high-latitude land

Typical characteristics:

- Very low temperatures
- Very low dew points
- Strong surface pressure
- Stable air near the source
- Clear skies or ice fog

When cA air moves over warmer ground or water, it becomes unstable from below.

This can produce:

- Snow showers
- Lake-effect snow
- Steam fog
- Strong gusts
- Rapid temperature drops

A true Arctic outbreak is not merely “a bit chilly.” It is the atmosphere arriving with paperwork.

---

## 6.4 Continental Polar Air

**Continental Polar**, or cP, air is cold and dry.

It forms over broad high-latitude continents, especially:

- Central and northern Canada
- Alaska
- Interior Eurasia

Compared with cA air, cP air is usually less extreme.

In winter it may bring:

- Cold temperatures
- Low humidity
- Clear skies
- Gusty northwest winds

In summer, modified cP air may bring:

- Pleasantly cool conditions
- Low dew points
- Good visibility
- Stable weather

This is often the crisp air that follows a cold front.

---

## 6.5 Maritime Polar Air

**Maritime Polar**, or mP, air is cool and moist.

It forms over cold oceans, including:

- North Pacific
- North Atlantic
- Southern oceans

Its weather depends heavily on the temperature of the surface it moves over.

When mP air moves over warmer land or water, it becomes unstable and may produce:

- Showers
- Gusty winds
- Low clouds
- Snow squalls
- Thunderstorms in some seasons

When it moves over colder surfaces, it becomes more stable and may produce:

- Fog
- Drizzle
- Low stratus

---

## 6.6 Continental Tropical Air

**Continental Tropical**, or cT, air is hot and dry.

It forms over deserts and dry subtropical land.

In North America, important source regions include:

- Northern Mexico
- Desert Southwest
- High plains during summer

Typical characteristics:

- High temperatures
- Low humidity
- Clear skies
- Deep dry boundary layer
- Strong daytime heating

When cT air spreads into populated regions, it can intensify heat waves and wildfire danger.

If moisture arrives above or below it, the resulting instability can help fuel severe thunderstorms.

---

## 6.7 Maritime Tropical Air

**Maritime Tropical**, or mT, air is warm and humid.

It forms over warm oceans, such as:

- Gulf of Mexico
- Caribbean Sea
- Tropical Atlantic
- Tropical Pacific

It often brings:

- High dew points
- Warm nights
- Haze
- Showers
- Thunderstorms
- Heavy rainfall

In the United States, Gulf and Atlantic mT air is a major source of moisture for severe storms, hurricanes, and summer humidity.

It is the air mass that makes stepping outside feel like entering a warm dishwasher.

---

## 6.8 Air Masses Change as They Travel

An air mass does not remain unchanged forever.

As it moves over new surfaces, it is **modified**.

For example:

### Cold air over warm water

The air warms from below and gains moisture.

This increases instability and can produce clouds and snow showers.

### Warm air over cold water

The lowest layer cools.

This increases stability and can produce fog or low stratus.

### Dry air over moist land

Evaporation and transpiration add water vapor.

### Moist air over dry land

Mixing and evaporation may lower humidity.

The farther an air mass travels from its source region, the less purely it retains its original identity.

---

## 6.9 Air-Mass Boundaries

A boundary between contrasting air masses is called a **front**.

Across a front, you may find changes in:

- Temperature
- Dew point
- Wind direction
- Pressure
- Cloud type
- Visibility
- Precipitation

A front may be narrow or broad.

Some fronts produce violent storms. Others pass almost unnoticed.

The strength of a front depends on how different the air masses are and how strongly the atmosphere forces them together.

---

## 6.10 Fronts Are Sloped

Cold air is denser than warm air.

When the two meet, cold air tends to remain near the ground while warm air rises above it.

This causes frontal surfaces to slope.

```text
          WARM AIR
             /
            /
           /
----------/---------------- ground
       COLD AIR
```

The slope determines how rapidly warm air rises and what kinds of clouds and precipitation form.

Warm fronts usually have gentle slopes.

Cold fronts usually have steeper slopes.

---

# Cold Fronts

## 6.11 What Is a Cold Front?

A **cold front** forms when advancing cold air replaces warmer air.

On weather maps, it is shown as a blue line with triangles pointing in the direction of movement.

```text
────▲────▲────▲────
```

Because cold air is dense, it wedges beneath the warm air and forces it upward.

The lifting may be rapid, especially when the temperature contrast is strong.

### Illustration 2: Cold-front cross section

```text
                 towering clouds
                      ☁
                     ↑
WARM AIR            /|
                   / |
------------------/--|---------- ground
       COLD AIR  →   |
```

---

## 6.12 Weather Ahead of a Cold Front

Before a cold front arrives, conditions may include:

- Warm temperatures
- Rising humidity
- Falling pressure
- South or southwest wind
- Increasing cumulus clouds
- Haze
- Thunderstorms developing along the front

If the atmosphere is stable, cloud development may remain limited.

If it is unstable, the front can trigger a narrow line of strong storms.

---

## 6.13 Weather During Cold-Front Passage

As the front passes, you may notice:

- Wind shift
- Sudden gusts
- Brief heavy rain
- Thunderstorms
- Rapid temperature drop
- Pressure reaching a minimum
- Sharp dew-point drop
- Reduced visibility

The active weather is often concentrated near the front.

A fast cold front may pass within an hour or two.

---

## 6.14 Weather Behind a Cold Front

After passage:

- Pressure rises
- Wind often turns west or northwest
- Temperature falls
- Humidity decreases
- Visibility improves
- Clouds may break
- Stratocumulus or showers may linger

In winter, unstable cold air behind the front can produce snow showers or squalls.

---

## 6.15 Anafronts and Katafronts

Not all cold fronts behave the same way.

### Anafront

Warm air rises along and behind the frontal surface.

Clouds and precipitation may extend well behind the surface front.

### Katafront

Dry air descends behind the front, limiting clouds and pushing precipitation ahead of the boundary.

These distinctions help explain why some cold fronts bring broad rain shields while others produce narrow storm lines followed by rapid clearing.

---

## 6.16 Dry Cold Fronts

A cold front can pass with little or no rain if moisture is scarce.

Signs may include:

- Abrupt wind shift
- Blowing dust
- Temperature drop
- Lower humidity
- Rising pressure
- Clearer visibility

Dry fronts are especially important in wildfire-prone regions because strong shifting winds can rapidly change fire behavior.

---

# Warm Fronts

## 6.17 What Is a Warm Front?

A **warm front** forms when advancing warm air replaces retreating cold air.

On weather maps, it is shown as a red line with semicircles pointing in the direction of movement.

```text
────◡────◡────◡────
```

Warm air is less dense, so it rises gradually over the colder air.

The shallow slope often creates a broad area of layered clouds and precipitation ahead of the surface boundary.

### Illustration 3: Warm-front cross section

```text
                 high clouds
              ☁       ☁
            /
          /   warm air rising
        /
------/---------------------- ground
 COLD AIR         WARM AIR →
```

---

## 6.18 Classic Warm-Front Cloud Sequence

A typical sequence may be:

1. Cirrus
2. Cirrostratus
3. Altostratus
4. Nimbostratus
5. Low stratus or fog

```text
CIRRUS
   ↓
CIRROSTRATUS
   ↓
ALTOSTRATUS
   ↓
NIMBOSTRATUS
   ↓
LOW CLOUDS AND FOG
```

The clouds generally thicken and lower as the front approaches.

---

## 6.19 Weather Ahead of a Warm Front

Ahead of the front, you may observe:

- High clouds increasing
- Halo around Sun or Moon
- Falling pressure
- East or southeast wind
- Thickening overcast
- Steady rain or snow
- Lowering cloud base
- Reduced visibility

In winter, warm fronts are major producers of:

- Snow
- Sleet
- Freezing rain
- Cold rain
- Icing

Because the warm air rides over cold air near the ground, dangerous freezing precipitation may occur long before surface temperatures rise.

---

## 6.20 Weather During Warm-Front Passage

As the surface front arrives:

- Temperature rises
- Dew point rises
- Wind shifts
- Pressure steadies
- Fog or drizzle may occur
- Precipitation may weaken
- Low clouds may persist

The exact wind shift depends on location and storm track.

---

## 6.21 Weather Behind a Warm Front

Behind the front lies the **warm sector**.

Conditions may include:

- Warmer temperatures
- Higher humidity
- Southerly winds
- Low clouds
- Haze
- Scattered showers
- Increasing instability

The warm sector often sits between a warm front and an approaching cold front.

This region can become favorable for severe thunderstorms when moisture and wind shear are strong.

---

# Stationary Fronts

## 6.22 What Is a Stationary Front?

A **stationary front** forms when neither air mass advances strongly enough to displace the other.

It is shown as alternating blue triangles and red semicircles on opposite sides of the line.

```text
──▲──◡──▲──◡──
```

The front may remain in the same general area for hours or days.

### Weather along a stationary front

Possible conditions include:

- Persistent clouds
- Light rain or drizzle
- Repeated thunderstorms
- Fog
- Large temperature differences across a short distance
- Flooding if storms repeatedly move along the boundary

A stationary front is not necessarily quiet. Sometimes it simply means the trouble has found a comfortable chair.

---

## 6.23 Frontogenesis

**Frontogenesis** is the strengthening or development of a front.

It occurs when temperature contrasts become sharper.

Processes that support frontogenesis include:

- Winds pushing different air masses together
- Differential heating
- Terrain effects
- Upper-level forcing
- Confluence in the wind field

Strengthening fronts often produce stronger lift and more active weather.

---

## 6.24 Frontolysis

**Frontolysis** is the weakening or dissipation of a front.

It occurs when temperature contrasts decrease.

Mixing, changing wind patterns, and surface modification can all weaken a boundary.

A front drawn on yesterday’s map may become vague or disappear entirely by today.

---

# Occluded Fronts

## 6.25 What Is an Occluded Front?

An **occluded front** forms when a faster-moving cold front catches a warm front.

On maps, it is shown as a purple line with alternating triangles and semicircles on the same side.

```text
──▲◡──▲◡──▲◡──
```

As the fronts merge, the warm air is lifted away from the ground.

Occlusion is often a sign that a midlatitude cyclone is mature.

### Illustration 4: Occlusion process

```text
STAGE 1
cold front trails low
warm front extends ahead

STAGE 2
cold front catches warm front

STAGE 3
warm air lifted aloft
occluded front forms
```

---

## 6.26 Cold-Type and Warm-Type Occlusions

### Cold occlusion

The air behind the cold front is colder than the air ahead of the warm front.

It wedges beneath both.

### Warm occlusion

The air behind the cold front is cool, but the air ahead is even colder.

The advancing air rises over the colder surface air.

Warm occlusions are more common in some coastal and northern regions.

In practice, occluded systems often produce widespread clouds, rain, snow, and shifting winds.

---

# Drylines and Other Boundaries

## 6.27 The Dryline

A **dryline** is a boundary separating moist air from dry air.

Unlike a cold front, the temperature difference may be small.

The dew-point difference can be dramatic.

Drylines are especially common in the central and southern Great Plains of the United States.

To the east:

- Warm, humid air
- Higher dew points
- Greater storm fuel

To the west:

- Hot, dry air
- Lower dew points
- Deep mixing

### Illustration 5: Dryline cross section

```text
DRY AIR           MOIST AIR
hot, low dew point    warm, high dew point
      →        ↑        ←
             storms
```

---

## 6.28 Why Drylines Trigger Storms

Dry air is often denser at the same pressure level because it may be deeply mixed and arranged beneath warmer air aloft.

Convergence along the boundary forces moist air upward.

If a cap weakens, thunderstorms can erupt rapidly.

Dryline storms may become severe because they often form in environments with:

- High instability
- Strong wind shear
- Abundant low-level moisture
- Steep lapse rates
- Strong upper-level winds

---

## 6.29 The Cap

A **cap** is a layer of warm air above cooler or more humid surface air.

It suppresses rising motion.

A weak cap may prevent morning storms, allowing heat and moisture to build.

If the cap breaks later, explosive thunderstorm development can occur.

A strong cap may prevent storms entirely.

The cap is meteorology’s version of keeping a lid on things until the lid decides it has had enough.

---

## 6.30 Outflow Boundaries

Thunderstorm downdrafts spread cool air across the ground.

The leading edge of that air is called an **outflow boundary** or gust front.

It may behave like a miniature cold front.

Signs include:

- Sudden wind shift
- Temperature drop
- Pressure jump
- Blowing dust
- Shelf cloud
- New storm development

Outflow boundaries can travel far from their parent storms and trigger new thunderstorms hours later.

---

## 6.31 Sea-Breeze Fronts

A sea breeze creates a boundary between cool marine air and warmer inland air.

This boundary is called a **sea-breeze front**.

It may be visible as:

- A line of cumulus clouds
- A radar fine line
- A wind shift
- A temperature drop
- A humidity increase

In humid climates, sea-breeze fronts often trigger afternoon thunderstorms.

Where two sea-breeze boundaries collide, storms may become especially concentrated.

---

## 6.32 Lake-Breeze Fronts

Large lakes can produce similar boundaries.

During warm seasons, cool lake air moves inland beneath warmer air.

This can:

- Lower coastal temperatures
- Produce fog
- Trigger thunderstorms inland
- Suppress storms near the immediate shoreline
- Shift local wind direction

The effect depends on lake size, wind direction, and background weather.

---

## 6.33 Mountain and Valley Boundaries

Terrain creates boundaries that do not always appear on standard weather maps.

Examples include:

- Cold-air drainage zones
- Upslope convergence
- Canyon winds
- Rain-shadow boundaries
- Elevated inversions
- Pass winds

Mountain weather can change sharply across short distances because the terrain repeatedly lifts, blocks, funnels, and redirects air.

---

# Midlatitude Cyclones

## 6.34 The Classic Midlatitude Storm

A **midlatitude cyclone** is a large low-pressure system common between about 30 and 60 degrees latitude.

It often includes:

- Warm front
- Cold front
- Warm sector
- Occluded front
- Broad cloud shield
- Strong upper-level support

These systems transport heat northward and cold air southward.

They are a major way the atmosphere reduces temperature contrasts between equator and pole.

### Illustration 6: Mature midlatitude cyclone

```text
                  WARM FRONT
             ◡ ◡ ◡ ◡ ◡
                    /
                   L
                 ↺
                  \
             ▲ ▲ ▲ ▲ ▲
              COLD FRONT
```

In the Northern Hemisphere, winds circulate counterclockwise around the low.

---

## 6.35 The Norwegian Cyclone Model

The classic life cycle includes several stages.

### Stage 1: Stationary boundary

Cold and warm air lie beside one another.

### Stage 2: Wave develops

A disturbance creates a kink in the boundary.

A low-pressure center forms.

### Stage 3: Open wave

Warm and cold fronts become distinct.

The warm sector expands.

### Stage 4: Mature cyclone

The low deepens.

Clouds, wind, and precipitation become widespread.

### Stage 5: Occlusion

The cold front catches the warm front.

Warm air is lifted from the surface.

### Stage 6: Dissipation

Temperature contrasts weaken.

The system loses energy.

---

## 6.36 Cyclone Weather Sequence

If a midlatitude cyclone passes north of you in the Northern Hemisphere, a common sequence is:

1. High clouds increase
2. Pressure falls
3. Warm-front rain develops
4. Temperature rises
5. Humid warm-sector weather arrives
6. Cold-front storms pass
7. Wind shifts west or northwest
8. Temperature and dew point fall
9. Pressure rises
10. Skies gradually clear

A storm passing south may produce a very different experience, including cold rain, snow, or persistent northeast wind.

Storm track matters enormously.

---

## 6.37 Bomb Cyclones

A rapidly strengthening midlatitude cyclone is sometimes called a **bomb cyclone**.

The technical process is called **bombogenesis**.

It refers to a rapid fall in central pressure over a specified period, adjusted for latitude.

These storms can produce:

- Very strong wind
- Heavy snow
- Coastal flooding
- Large waves
- Rapid weather changes

The name sounds like a disaster movie because apparently “rapid cyclogenesis” was not dramatic enough for television.

---

## 6.38 Nor’easters

A **nor’easter** is a powerful storm affecting the eastern coast of North America.

The name refers to the strong northeasterly winds commonly experienced along the coast ahead of the low.

Nor’easters may produce:

- Heavy rain
- Heavy snow
- Coastal flooding
- High surf
- Strong wind
- Beach erosion

They often strengthen near the warm Gulf Stream where strong ocean temperature contrasts supply energy and moisture.

---

## 6.39 Alberta Clippers

An **Alberta clipper** is a fast-moving winter low that travels southeast from western Canada into the northern or eastern United States.

Clippers often bring:

- Brief snow
- Strong wind
- Rapid temperature drops
- Dangerous wind chill
- Light, powdery accumulation

They usually contain less moisture than storms from the Gulf or Atlantic, but their speed and cold can still cause major disruption.

---

## 6.40 Panhandle Hooks

A **Panhandle hook** is a storm track in which a low forms or strengthens near the Texas or Oklahoma Panhandle and curves northeastward.

These systems can draw rich Gulf moisture northward and produce:

- Severe thunderstorms
- Heavy rain
- Blizzard conditions northwest of the track
- Ice near the transition zone
- Strong wind

They are classic examples of one storm producing spring, summer, autumn, and winter weather at the same time.

---

# Reading a Front on a Weather Map

## 6.41 Surface Clues

To locate a front, look for a zone where several variables change together.

A front may be marked by:

- Temperature contrast
- Dew-point contrast
- Wind shift
- Pressure trough
- Cloud band
- Precipitation line
- Visibility change

One clue alone may be misleading.

A real front is usually identified from the combined pattern.

---

## 6.42 Wind Shifts

Typical Northern Hemisphere wind shifts include:

### Cold front

Before: south or southwest  
After: west or northwest

### Warm front

Before: east or southeast  
After: south or southwest

These are common patterns, not universal rules.

Terrain, coastlines, storm track, and local circulations can alter them.

---

## 6.43 Pressure Behavior Near Fronts

Ahead of an approaching front, pressure often falls.

Near passage, it may reach a minimum.

Behind a cold front, pressure often rises sharply.

Warm fronts can produce a more gradual pressure change.

A **pressure trough** may mark a wind shift even when temperature contrast is weak.

---

## 6.44 Dew Point as a Frontal Clue

Dew point often reveals fronts more clearly than temperature.

For example:

- 72°F dew point ahead of a cold front
- 48°F dew point behind it

Temperature may differ by only a few degrees, especially at night or under clouds.

The moisture contrast can still be dramatic.

This is why meteorologists examine dew-point maps closely.

---

## 6.45 Radar Clues

Fronts and boundaries may appear on radar as:

- Lines of thunderstorms
- Bands of steady rain
- Fine lines caused by insects, dust, or moisture contrasts
- Arcs of outflow
- Repeating storms along a stationary front

A radar line does not always mean rain is reaching the ground.

Some boundaries are visible because insects and particles collect in converging air.

---

## 6.46 Satellite Clues

Satellite images reveal large-scale frontal structure.

Common patterns include:

- Comma-shaped cloud shields
- Narrow cold-frontal bands
- Broad warm-frontal cloud layers
- Dry slots wrapping into lows
- Convective clusters along boundaries
- Spiral cloud bands around mature cyclones

Water-vapor imagery can also show dry and moist air streams aloft.

---

# Fronts and Severe Weather

## 6.47 Why Fronts Trigger Thunderstorms

Fronts force air upward.

If the warm air is moist and unstable, the lift may produce deep convection.

Storm intensity depends on:

- Instability
- Moisture
- Wind shear
- Strength of lift
- Upper-level support
- Storm motion
- Frontal orientation

A front is the trigger, but not always the fuel.

---

## 6.48 Squall Lines

A **squall line** is a long, organized line of thunderstorms.

It often forms along or ahead of a cold front.

Hazards include:

- Damaging straight-line wind
- Frequent lightning
- Heavy rain
- Hail
- Brief tornadoes

The leading edge may move rapidly and produce a shelf cloud.

---

## 6.49 Prefrontal Troughs

Sometimes the strongest storms form ahead of the cold front along a **prefrontal trough**.

This is a zone of lower pressure and wind convergence.

Because it lies inside warm, moist air, it may be more favorable for severe storms than the cold front itself.

The labeled blue line on the map is not always where the main action is. Weather maps, like people, occasionally bury the lede.

---

## 6.50 Triple Points

A **triple point** is where three boundaries meet, commonly:

- Cold front
- Warm front
- Occluded front

Strong convergence and changing wind may make this area favorable for severe weather.

However, triple points do not automatically produce tornadoes.

The broader environment still matters.

---

## 6.51 Warm Fronts and Tornado Risk

Warm fronts can increase tornado risk near and just south of the boundary.

There, winds may be:

- Backed toward the east or southeast
- Strongly sheared with height
- Rich in low-level moisture

Storms interacting with a warm front may gain rotation.

Low clouds and rain can make these situations especially difficult to observe visually.

---

# Winter Fronts

## 6.52 Arctic Fronts

An **Arctic front** marks the leading edge of extremely cold air.

It may produce:

- Rapid temperature collapse
- Strong north wind
- Snow squalls
- Blowing snow
- Flash freezing
- Dangerous wind chill

Temperatures can fall dozens of degrees in only a few hours.

Wet roads may freeze rapidly after passage.

---

## 6.53 Freezing-Rain Zones

Freezing rain commonly develops north of a warm front.

Warm air flows above a shallow layer of freezing air near the surface.

```text
          WARM AIR
       rain forms and falls
-----------------------------
    SHALLOW COLD AIR
drops freeze on contact
----------------------------- ground
```

Small changes in frontal position can shift the icing zone significantly.

This makes winter forecasting especially delicate.

---

## 6.54 Heavy Snow Bands

Strong frontogenesis can produce narrow bands of intense snow.

Within a band:

- Snowfall rates may become extreme
- Visibility may fall sharply
- Roads may deteriorate quickly
- Thunder and lightning may occur

A location only a few miles away may receive much less snow.

Frontal snow bands are one reason winter totals can vary so dramatically across short distances.

---

# Field Observation  
## Detect a Front Passing

Choose a day when a front is forecast to pass.

Record every hour:

- Temperature
- Dew point
- Pressure
- Wind direction
- Wind speed
- Cloud type
- Precipitation

Look for the exact time when several variables change.

A cold-front passage might show:

```text
BEFORE
78°F
dew point 69°F
southwest wind
falling pressure

DURING
heavy shower
gusty shifting wind

AFTER
64°F
dew point 46°F
northwest wind
rising pressure
```

That sequence is a front written in numbers.

---

# Try It  
## Make an Air-Mass Map

Print or sketch a map of North America.

Mark likely source regions for:

- cA
- cP
- mP
- cT
- mT

Draw arrows showing how each air mass might move.

Then write one likely weather effect for your region.

This exercise helps turn letter codes into actual geography.

---

# Try It  
## Follow a Front for Three Days

Use daily weather maps.

Track one front over three days.

Record:

- Position
- Direction
- Speed
- Associated low
- Cloud pattern
- Precipitation
- Temperature change behind it

Notice whether the front:

- Strengthens
- Weakens
- Stalls
- Becomes occluded
- Leaves the map

Fronts are easier to understand as moving systems than as colored lines frozen in time.

---

# Weather Safety  
## Rapid Frontal Changes

Fronts can create dangerous conditions quickly.

Be especially alert for:

- Lightning ahead of a cold front
- Sudden damaging wind
- Dust reducing visibility
- Flash freezing
- Rapid temperature drops
- Freezing rain north of a warm front
- Heavy snow bands
- Severe-weather watches and warnings

Do not assume the rain must begin before the wind arrives.

Outflow and gust fronts can reach you well ahead of the precipitation.

---

# Chapter Summary

Air masses are large bodies of air with similar temperature and moisture properties.

They form over broad source regions and are classified as continental or maritime and Arctic, Polar, or Tropical.

Air masses change as they travel over different surfaces.

Fronts are sloping boundaries between contrasting air masses.

Cold fronts usually force warm air upward rapidly and often produce narrow bands of active weather.

Warm fronts lift warm air gradually and commonly produce widespread layered clouds and precipitation.

Stationary fronts can cause prolonged cloudy or stormy weather.

Occluded fronts form when a cold front catches a warm front.

Drylines separate moist and dry air and frequently trigger severe thunderstorms.

Outflow, sea-breeze, lake-breeze, and terrain boundaries can behave like smaller fronts.

Midlatitude cyclones organize fronts into large rotating storm systems.

Temperature, dew point, pressure, wind, radar, and satellite patterns all help locate fronts.

The meeting place between air masses is where the atmosphere does much of its most interesting work.

---

## Weather-Watcher’s Vocabulary

**Air mass:** A large body of air with relatively uniform temperature and moisture.  
**Source region:** The area over which an air mass develops.  
**Continental:** Formed over land; generally dry.  
**Maritime:** Formed over water; generally moist.  
**Arctic:** Extremely cold air from high-latitude ice or snow regions.  
**Polar:** Cold air from high latitudes.  
**Tropical:** Warm air from low latitudes.  
**Front:** A boundary between contrasting air masses.  
**Cold front:** The leading edge of advancing cold air.  
**Warm front:** The leading edge of advancing warm air.  
**Stationary front:** A front with little net movement.  
**Occluded front:** A front formed when a cold front overtakes a warm front.  
**Dryline:** A boundary between dry and moist air.  
**Frontogenesis:** The strengthening or formation of a front.  
**Frontolysis:** The weakening of a front.  
**Warm sector:** Warm air between a warm front and cold front.  
**Outflow boundary:** The leading edge of cool thunderstorm air.  
**Squall line:** A long organized line of thunderstorms.  
**Midlatitude cyclone:** A large rotating low-pressure system associated with fronts.  
**Bombogenesis:** Rapid strengthening of a midlatitude cyclone.  
**Triple point:** A location where three frontal boundaries meet.

**Next: Chapter 7 — Thunderstorms: From Fair-Weather Cloud to Violent Storm**

<!-- End of source chapter -->

---

# Chapter 7  
## Thunderstorms: From Fair-Weather Cloud to Violent Storm

A thunderstorm begins with rising air.

At first, it may look harmless: a small cumulus cloud with a flat base and bright white top. If the air is warm, humid, unstable, and able to keep rising, that cloud can grow into a tower several miles high.

Inside it, water freezes, winds accelerate, electrical charges separate, and rain begins to fall.

The result is a thunderstorm: one of the atmosphere’s most efficient ways to turn heat and moisture into noise, light, wind, ice, and trouble.

---

## 7.1 What Makes a Thunderstorm?

Three basic ingredients are required:

- Moisture
- Instability
- Lift

Meteorologists sometimes call these the thunderstorm ingredients.

### Moisture

Water vapor supplies the cloud material and releases latent heat when it condenses.

Common moisture sources include:

- Warm oceans
- The Gulf of Mexico
- Lakes
- Wet soil
- Vegetation
- Moist air transported by wind

### Instability

The atmosphere must allow warm air to continue rising.

A rising air parcel is unstable when it remains warmer and less dense than the surrounding air.

### Lift

Something must start the air moving upward.

Common triggers include:

- Cold fronts
- Warm fronts
- Drylines
- Sea-breeze boundaries
- Mountains
- Surface heating
- Thunderstorm outflow
- Upper-level disturbances

```text
MOISTURE + INSTABILITY + LIFT
              ↓
        THUNDERSTORM
```

Remove one ingredient and storm development may weaken or fail.

---

## 7.2 The Life Cycle of a Thunderstorm

A typical single-cell thunderstorm passes through three stages:

1. Cumulus stage
2. Mature stage
3. Dissipating stage

The entire cycle may last less than an hour, though organized storms can survive much longer.

---

## 7.3 The Cumulus Stage

The storm begins when warm, moist air rises.

As it rises:

- Pressure decreases
- Air expands
- Temperature falls
- Water vapor condenses
- Latent heat is released
- The updraft strengthens

During this stage, the cloud is dominated by rising air.

There is little or no precipitation reaching the ground.

The cloud may grow from small cumulus into towering cumulus.

### Illustration 1: Cumulus stage

```text
            growing tower
                ☁
                ↑
                ↑
          strong updraft
                ↑
        warm moist surface air
```

### Signs of rapid development

- Crisp cauliflower-shaped top
- Vertical growth from minute to minute
- Darkening base
- Pileus cloud above the tower
- Increasing size with little sideways spreading

A sharply defined cloud top is actively growing. A soft, frayed top is usually weakening or evaporating.

---

## 7.4 The Mature Stage

The mature stage begins when precipitation starts falling from the cloud.

Falling rain and ice drag air downward, creating a **downdraft**.

The storm now contains both:

- Updraft
- Downdraft

This is usually the most dangerous stage.

Possible hazards include:

- Lightning
- Heavy rain
- Hail
- Strong wind
- Flash flooding
- Tornadoes

### Illustration 2: Mature thunderstorm

```text
                ANVIL
       ───────────────────
             ice crystals
                 ↑
             UPDRAFT
                 ↑
        rain and hail core
                 ↓
             DOWNDRAFT
                 ↓
             gust front
────────────────────────── ground
```

The upper cloud may spread into an anvil when the updraft reaches the tropopause.

---

## 7.5 The Dissipating Stage

Eventually, the downdraft spreads through much of the cloud.

This cuts off the storm’s supply of warm, moist inflow.

The updraft weakens.

Rain may continue briefly, but the storm gradually collapses.

Signs include:

- Fading cloud edges
- Weakening rain
- Expanding cool outflow
- Anvil debris remaining after the lower cloud disappears

```text
        weakening cloud
             ☁
             ↓
       downdraft dominates
             ↓
        lightening rain
```

The leftover anvil may persist long after the main storm has ended.

---

## 7.6 Why Some Storms Last Longer

A simple thunderstorm often destroys itself.

Its downdraft falls through the same region as the updraft and cuts off the inflow.

Long-lived storms survive because wind shear separates the updraft from the downdraft.

This allows warm, moist air to continue entering one part of the storm while rain-cooled air exits another.

Wind shear is therefore one of the main ingredients for storm organization.

---

# Thunderstorm Types

## 7.7 Single-Cell Thunderstorms

A **single-cell storm** is a small, short-lived thunderstorm dominated by one main updraft and downdraft.

It is common on warm summer afternoons with weak wind shear.

Typical characteristics:

- Lifespan of roughly 30–60 minutes
- Brief heavy rain
- Lightning
- Gusty wind
- Small hail possible
- Localized flooding possible

Single-cell storms are sometimes called **pulse storms** when they intensify rapidly and collapse soon afterward.

Despite their short lives, they can produce sudden damaging wind.

---

## 7.8 Multicell Clusters

A **multicell cluster** contains several thunderstorm cells at different stages of development.

New cells form along the outflow from older cells.

At any moment, the cluster may contain:

- Growing towers
- Mature storms
- Dissipating cells

Multicell clusters can last for hours because new storms continually replace old ones.

Hazards include:

- Heavy rain
- Frequent lightning
- Hail
- Strong wind
- Local flooding

### Illustration 3: Multicell cluster

```text
NEW CELL      MATURE CELL      OLD CELL
   ↑              ⚡              ↓
 growing       heavy rain      weakening
```

---

## 7.9 Multicell Lines
