Thunderstorms may organize into a long line.

These are often called:

- Squall lines
- Convective lines
- Quasi-linear convective systems

They frequently form along or ahead of cold fronts.

The main hazard is often damaging straight-line wind, though hail and tornadoes are also possible.

A shelf cloud may mark the leading edge.

---

## 7.10 Supercells

A **supercell** is a highly organized thunderstorm with a deep, persistent rotating updraft called a **mesocyclone**.

Supercells are less common than ordinary thunderstorms but produce a large share of significant severe weather.

They can last for several hours and travel long distances.

Possible hazards include:

- Very large hail
- Destructive wind
- Tornadoes
- Frequent lightning
- Flash flooding

### Illustration 4: Supercell structure

```text
                 ANVIL
        ─────────────────────
              overshooting top
                    ↑
          rotating main updraft
                 ↺  ↑
                    ↑
        hail core        rain core
             ↓               ↓

      inflow → wall cloud
──────────────────────────── ground
             gust front →
```

---

## 7.11 The Mesocyclone

A mesocyclone is a rotating region within a thunderstorm updraft.

It may be several miles wide.

It does not mean a tornado is already present.

Most mesocyclones do not produce tornadoes.

However, persistent low-level rotation within a supercell greatly increases tornado potential.

Mesocyclones are detected using:

- Doppler radar
- Visual storm structure
- Wind observations
- Lightning and satellite data
- Trained spotter reports

---

## 7.12 How a Supercell Begins to Rotate

Strong vertical wind shear can create a horizontal tube of spinning air near the ground.

Imagine a rolling pin rotating horizontally.

A strong thunderstorm updraft can tilt part of that horizontal rotation into the vertical.

```text
WIND SHEAR CREATES HORIZONTAL SPIN

     ↻  ↻  ↻  ↻

UPDRAFT TILTS SPIN UPRIGHT

          ↺
          ↑
          ↑
```

The rising air then stretches the rotation, causing it to spin faster.

This is similar to an ice skater pulling in their arms.

The process can create a rotating updraft.

---

## 7.13 Classic Supercell Features

A classic supercell may contain:

- Rain-free base
- Wall cloud
- Rotating updraft
- Rear-flank downdraft
- Forward-flank downdraft
- Hail core
- Hook-shaped radar echo
- Overshooting top

These features do not always appear clearly from the ground.

Rain, terrain, darkness, and distance can hide important storm structure.

---

## 7.14 High-Precipitation Supercells

A **high-precipitation**, or HP, supercell contains heavy rain wrapped around much of the rotating region.

It can be especially dangerous because:

- Tornadoes may be rain-wrapped
- Visibility is poor
- Flash flooding may occur
- Large hail may be hidden
- Wind-driven rain can make escape difficult

HP supercells are common in humid environments.

---

## 7.15 Low-Precipitation Supercells

A **low-precipitation**, or LP, supercell has a sculpted updraft and relatively little visible rain.

It may produce:

- Large hail
- Strong rotation
- Lightning
- Tornadoes
- Sparse rainfall

LP storms often form in drier environments near the edge of rich moisture.

They can look elegant from a distance, which is a poor reason to underestimate them.

---

## 7.16 Bow Echoes

A line of thunderstorms may bend outward into a bow shape on radar.

This is called a **bow echo**.

The bowing segment often marks strong winds surging forward.

Bow echoes can produce widespread straight-line wind damage.

Important features may include:

- Strong central wind core
- Rear-inflow jet
- Rotating ends
- Embedded tornadoes

### Illustration 5: Bow echo

```text
RADAR VIEW

     (
      (
       (
      strong wind pushing line outward
```

---

## 7.17 Derechos

A **derecho** is a widespread, long-lived windstorm produced by an organized thunderstorm system.

It can produce damaging wind over hundreds of miles.

Derechos are associated with:

- Bow echoes
- Fast-moving convective systems
- Extensive straight-line wind damage
- Multiple severe wind reports

The word does not simply mean “a particularly nasty thunderstorm.”

It refers to a specific large-scale wind event.

---

## 7.18 Mesoscale Convective Systems

A **mesoscale convective system**, or MCS, is a large organized group of thunderstorms that acts as a single weather system.

Forms include:

- Squall lines
- Bow echoes
- Convective clusters
- Mesoscale convective complexes

An MCS may last through the night and travel across several states.

It can produce:

- Widespread heavy rain
- Flash flooding
- Severe wind
- Frequent lightning
- Embedded tornadoes

---

## 7.19 Training Thunderstorms

Thunderstorms sometimes repeatedly move over the same area.

This is called **training**, because the storms resemble train cars following the same track.

Training can occur along:

- Stationary fronts
- Outflow boundaries
- Slow-moving storm lines
- Moisture corridors

It is a major cause of flash flooding.

Several moderate storms over the same location may be more dangerous than one intense storm moving quickly through.

---

# Thunderstorm Anatomy

## 7.20 The Updraft

The updraft is the rising current that feeds the storm.

Strong updrafts can exceed highway speeds and may support:

- Large amounts of water
- Hailstones
- Ice crystals
- Supercooled droplets

The strongest updraft region often has little precipitation because drops and hail are being carried upward or held aloft.

This creates a **rain-free base** beneath some severe storms.

---

## 7.21 The Downdraft

A downdraft forms when air is pulled downward by:

- Falling precipitation
- Evaporative cooling
- Melting ice
- Drag from rain and hail

Cool downdraft air spreads outward when it reaches the ground.

This creates storm outflow.

Downdrafts can be gentle or violently destructive.

---

## 7.22 The Gust Front

The leading edge of cool thunderstorm outflow is called a **gust front**.

It behaves like a small cold front.

Signs of an approaching gust front include:

- Sudden wind increase
- Wind shift
- Temperature drop
- Dust or leaves lifting
- Shelf cloud
- Pressure rise
- Rapidly approaching rain

The gust front may arrive several minutes before the rain.

---

## 7.23 The Shelf Cloud

A shelf cloud forms above the gust front.

Warm air is forced upward over the spreading cool outflow.

Shelf clouds are:

- Broad
- Low
- Horizontal
- Attached to the storm’s leading edge

They usually indicate strong wind rather than tornado formation.

A turbulent, ragged underside may make the cloud look as though the sky has grown teeth.

---

## 7.24 The Wall Cloud

A wall cloud is a localized lowering beneath the storm’s main updraft.

It often forms near the interface between warm inflow and rain-cooled air.

A wall cloud becomes especially concerning when it shows:

- Persistent rotation
- Rapid upward motion
- Strong warm inflow
- Increasing organization
- A tightening area of rotation

Not every wall cloud produces a tornado.

A rotating wall cloud is still a good reason to stop admiring the view and get inside.

---

## 7.25 The Rear-Flank Downdraft

In a supercell, the **rear-flank downdraft**, or RFD, descends around the back side of the rotating updraft.

It can:

- Wrap dry air around the mesocyclone
- Create a clear slot
- Produce damaging wind
- Help concentrate low-level rotation
- Play a role in tornado formation

Tornado development depends on a delicate balance.

If the downdraft is too cold or too strong, it may undercut the updraft and prevent a tornado.

---

## 7.26 The Forward-Flank Downdraft

The **forward-flank downdraft** forms in the main rain and hail area ahead of the rotating updraft.

It may contain:

- Heavy rain
- Large hail
- Strong wind
- Cool outflow

A sharp boundary often separates this air from the warm inflow feeding the storm.

That boundary can intensify low-level rotation.

---

# Lightning and Thunder

## 7.27 How Thunderstorms Become Electrified

Inside a thunderstorm, ice crystals, graupel, and supercooled water collide in strong turbulent currents.

These collisions separate electrical charge.

A common charge arrangement is:

- Positive charge near the storm top
- Negative charge in the middle
- Smaller positive region near the base

The exact structure can be more complicated.

As charge separation grows, the electric field becomes strong enough to break down the insulating properties of air.

Lightning occurs.

---

## 7.28 Types of Lightning

Lightning may occur:

- Within a cloud
- Between clouds
- From cloud to ground
- From ground to cloud
- From cloud into clear air

### Intra-cloud lightning

Occurs within one cloud and is the most common type.

It may light the entire cloud from inside.

### Cloud-to-ground lightning

Connects cloud charge with the surface.

It is especially dangerous to people, structures, trees, and power systems.

### Cloud-to-cloud lightning

Travels between separate clouds.

### Cloud-to-air lightning

Extends from the cloud into surrounding air without obvious ground contact.

---

## 7.29 How a Cloud-to-Ground Flash Develops

A typical negative cloud-to-ground flash begins with a faint channel called a **stepped leader** moving downward.

As it approaches the ground, upward streamers rise from objects below.

When a connection forms, a powerful **return stroke** travels upward through the channel.

This is the bright flash people see.

The process may repeat several times along the same path, causing lightning to flicker.

### Illustration 6: Cloud-to-ground lightning

```text
cloud base
   ↓ stepped leader
   ↓
   ↓
   ↑ upward streamer
tree or structure
   ↑
RETURN STROKE
```

The visible return stroke moves upward, though the overall discharge connects cloud and ground.

---

## 7.30 Positive Lightning

Some lightning carries positive charge from the upper part of a storm to the ground.

Positive flashes are less common but can be:

- More powerful
- Longer-lasting
- Farther from the rain core
- More likely to ignite fires

A positive flash may strike many miles from the main thunderstorm.

This is one reason blue sky overhead does not guarantee safety.

---

## 7.31 Bolts from the Blue

A **bolt from the blue** leaves the side or top of a thunderstorm, travels through clear air, and strikes the ground far from the rain.

It may occur after the storm appears to be moving away.

Lightning danger continues as long as thunder can be heard.

The sky does not care that your picnic has technically resumed.

---

## 7.32 Why Thunder Happens

Lightning heats the surrounding air almost instantly to temperatures hotter than the surface of the Sun.

The air expands explosively, creating a shock wave.

That wave becomes the sound we call thunder.

Thunder may sound like:

- Sharp crack
- Loud boom
- Long rumble
- Repeated rolling

The sound depends on distance, terrain, cloud structure, and the shape of the lightning channel.

Different parts of a long channel may be different distances from the observer, so the sound arrives over several seconds.

---

## 7.33 Estimating Lightning Distance

Light travels so fast that the flash is seen nearly instantly.

Sound travels much more slowly.

Count the seconds between lightning and thunder.

A useful estimate is:

```text
Distance in miles ≈ seconds ÷ 5
```

or

```text
Distance in kilometers ≈ seconds ÷ 3
```

Example:

```text
15 seconds ÷ 5 ≈ 3 miles
```

This is only an estimate.

If you hear thunder at all, you are already close enough to be struck.

Do not use the count as permission to stay outside.

---

## 7.34 Heat Lightning

“Heat lightning” is not a special type of lightning.

It is simply distant lightning from a thunderstorm too far away for thunder to be heard.

The flash may appear near the horizon on a warm summer night.

The storm may be dozens of miles away.

---

## 7.35 Sprites, Jets, and Elves

Powerful thunderstorms can produce brief electrical flashes above the storm.

These are called **transient luminous events**.

Examples include:

### Sprites

Red flashes extending high above thunderstorms.

### Blue jets

Blue emissions rising from storm tops.

### Elves

Rapid expanding rings of light near the lower ionosphere.

These occur far above ordinary lightning and are difficult to observe from the ground.

They reveal that thunderstorm electricity reaches much farther upward than once believed.

---

# Hail

## 7.36 How Hail Forms

Hail develops inside strong thunderstorms.

A small ice particle is carried upward into a region containing supercooled water droplets.

The droplets freeze onto the particle.

The hailstone may move through the storm repeatedly, gaining new layers.

It grows until:

- The updraft weakens
- It moves out of the updraft
- It becomes too heavy to remain aloft

Then it falls.

---

## 7.37 Hailstone Layers

Cut hailstones sometimes show alternating clear and cloudy ice layers.

Clear ice can form when water freezes slowly and trapped air escapes.

Cloudy ice can form when water freezes quickly and traps bubbles.

The layers record changes in the hailstone’s journey through the storm.

They do not reliably indicate a precise number of trips through the updraft, because growth paths are complex.

---

## 7.38 Why Large Hail Requires Strong Updrafts

Large hailstones fall quickly.

To keep them suspended, the storm needs a powerful updraft.

This is why large hail is often associated with:

- Supercells
- Strong instability
- Steep lapse rates
- High cloud bases in some environments
- Strong wind shear

Large hail may fall outside the heaviest rain.

A storm can produce damaging hail even when rainfall seems light.

---

## 7.39 Hail Size Descriptions

Hail is often compared with familiar objects.

| Approximate diameter | Common description |
|---:|---|
| ¼ inch | Pea |
| ½ inch | Marble |
| ¾ inch | Penny |
| 1 inch | Quarter |
| 1½ inches | Ping-pong ball |
| 1¾ inches | Golf ball |
| 2½ inches | Tennis ball |
| 4 inches | Softball |

Official severe-weather thresholds vary by country.

In the United States, hail one inch in diameter or larger meets the severe thunderstorm criterion.

Avoid picking up large hail while the storm is ongoing. Another batch may be descending with less charm and more velocity.

---

# Heavy Rain and Flooding

## 7.40 Why Thunderstorms Produce Intense Rain

Thunderstorms can process enormous quantities of water vapor.

Heavy rainfall becomes more likely when:

- Air contains abundant moisture
- Storms move slowly
- Warm-cloud layers are deep
- Updrafts continually replace condensed water
- Storms train over the same area
- Terrain forces additional lift

A tropical air mass can produce exceptionally high rainfall rates.

---

## 7.41 Flash Floods

A **flash flood** develops rapidly, often within minutes or hours.

It may be caused by:

- Intense thunderstorm rain
- Training storms
- Dam or levee failure
- Debris blocking drainage
- Heavy rain on burned land
- Urban runoff

Flash floods are dangerous because water rises quickly and may carry:

- Vehicles
- Trees
- Rocks
- Mud
- Debris

Low-lying roads, underpasses, creeks, and drainage channels can become deadly with little warning.

---

## 7.42 Urban Flooding

Cities flood quickly because pavement and roofs prevent water from soaking into the ground.

Storm drains may be overwhelmed or blocked.

Water may collect in:

- Underpasses
- Parking lots
- Basement entrances
- Low intersections
- Highway dips

A road that looks shallow may be washed out beneath the surface.

Never drive through floodwater.

Water is excellent at hiding missing pavement. Very rude of it.

---

# Damaging Wind

## 7.43 Straight-Line Wind

Thunderstorms can produce destructive wind without tornadoes.

This is called **straight-line wind**.

It usually results from downdraft air spreading outward after reaching the ground.

Damage may include:

- Trees falling in roughly the same direction
- Roof damage
- Power outages
- Flying debris
- Structural collapse

Straight-line wind can be as strong as tornado wind.

The name describes the wind pattern, not the level of danger.

---

## 7.44 Downbursts

A **downburst** is a strong downdraft that spreads outward after striking the ground.

Two common sizes are:

### Microburst

Generally less than about 2.5 miles, or 4 kilometers, across.

### Macroburst

Larger than a microburst and capable of widespread damage.

Downbursts may be:

- Wet, with heavy rain
- Dry, with little rain at the ground

### Illustration 7: Downburst

```text
         storm cloud
             ↓
       powerful downdraft
             ↓
             ↓
          impacts ground
        ← ←     → →
      damaging outflow
```

---

## 7.45 Microbursts and Aircraft

Microbursts are especially dangerous during aircraft takeoff and landing.

An aircraft may first encounter a headwind and gain lift, then enter the downdraft, followed by a strong tailwind and sudden loss of lift.

Modern detection systems and pilot training have greatly improved safety, but microbursts remain a serious aviation hazard.

---

## 7.46 Evaporative Cooling and Wind

Rain falling into dry air may evaporate.

Evaporation cools the air.

The cooled air becomes denser and accelerates downward.

This process can produce strong wind even from storms with relatively little rain at the surface.

Dry environments are especially favorable for this type of downburst.

---

# Tornadoes

## 7.47 What Is a Tornado?

A tornado is a violently rotating column of air in contact with both the ground and a cloud base.

The visible funnel is made of condensed water droplets, dust, or debris.

A tornado may exist even when the condensation funnel does not visibly reach the ground.

Signs of ground contact include:

- Rotating dust
- Debris cloud
- Damage
- Power flashes
- Spray over water

---

## 7.48 How Tornadoes Form

There is no single simple tornado process.

In many supercell tornadoes:

1. Wind shear creates horizontal rotation.
2. The updraft tilts and stretches the rotation.
3. A mesocyclone forms.
4. Downdrafts interact with the updraft.
5. Rotation tightens near the ground.
6. A tornado develops.

Scientists continue studying exactly why some rotating storms produce tornadoes while similar storms do not.

Tornado formation depends on the temperature, moisture, and strength of storm outflow as well as the structure of the low-level wind.

---

## 7.49 Non-Supercell Tornadoes

Not all tornadoes come from supercells.

Tornadoes can also form along:

- Gust fronts
- Developing storm lines
- Sea-breeze boundaries
- Landspout circulations
- Tropical cyclone rainbands

These tornadoes are often weaker, but they can still cause serious damage.

---

## 7.50 Landspouts

A **landspout** is a tornado that develops from rotation already present near the ground beneath a growing thunderstorm.

The circulation is stretched upward by the developing updraft.

Landspouts usually do not form from a strong, established mesocyclone.

They are often:

- Narrow
- Short-lived
- Difficult to detect on radar
- Less intense than strong supercell tornadoes

Some can still be damaging.

---

## 7.51 Waterspouts

A **waterspout** is a rotating column of air over water.

There are two main types.

### Fair-weather waterspout

Forms beneath developing cumulus clouds in light-wind environments.

It often begins at the water surface and grows upward.

### Tornadic waterspout

A tornado associated with a severe thunderstorm that moves over water or forms there.

Tornadic waterspouts can be powerful and dangerous.

Boats should avoid all waterspouts.

The ocean has enough moving parts without adding a rotating column of air.

---

## 7.52 Tornado Shapes

Tornadoes may appear as:

- Rope
- Cone
- Stovepipe
- Wedge
- Multiple vortices
- Rain-wrapped circulation

Shape does not reliably determine strength.

A thin rope tornado can be strong.

A large wedge can be weak in places and violent in others.

Damage surveys and measured effects are used to estimate intensity.

---

## 7.53 Multiple-Vortex Tornadoes

Some tornadoes contain smaller rotating vortices circling within the main circulation.

These **suction vortices** can cause narrow streaks of extreme damage beside areas of lesser damage.

They may appear and disappear rapidly.

This helps explain why one building can be destroyed while another nearby remains standing.

---

## 7.54 The Enhanced Fujita Scale

In the United States, tornado intensity is rated using the **Enhanced Fujita scale**, or EF scale.

Ratings are based on damage indicators and estimated wind speed.

| Rating | General damage level |
|---:|---|
| EF0 | Light |
| EF1 | Moderate |
| EF2 | Considerable |
| EF3 | Severe |
| EF4 | Devastating |
| EF5 | Incredible |

The rating is assigned after the tornado, not while it is occurring.

A tornado warning does not tell you the future EF rating.

Any tornado deserves immediate shelter.

---

# Radar and Thunderstorms

## 7.55 What Weather Radar Measures

Weather radar sends pulses of energy into the atmosphere.

Some energy reflects from:

- Rain
- Snow
- Hail
- Insects
- Dust
- Debris

The radar measures the returned signal.

Modern Doppler radar can estimate:

- Precipitation location
- Precipitation intensity
- Motion toward or away from the radar
- Storm rotation
- Wind patterns
- Debris signatures

Radar does not see a storm exactly as the human eye does.

It samples reflected energy at different heights and distances.

---

## 7.56 Reflectivity

**Reflectivity** shows how strongly objects return radar energy.

Higher values may indicate:

- Heavy rain
- Large drops
- Hail
- Dense precipitation

But high reflectivity does not always mean heavy rain.

Hail reflects strongly and can exaggerate rainfall estimates.

A bright radar color is an invitation to investigate, not a complete diagnosis.

---

## 7.57 Velocity

Doppler radar measures motion toward or away from the radar.

Velocity data can reveal rotation.

When strong inbound and outbound winds appear close together, meteorologists may identify a velocity couplet.

```text
toward radar  |  away from radar
      →       |       ←
          tight rotation
```

Radar only measures the component of wind along the radar beam.

It does not directly measure all wind directions.

---

## 7.58 Radar Hook Echo

A **hook echo** is a hook-shaped reflectivity feature sometimes seen on a supercell.

It can form as precipitation wraps around the rotating updraft.

A hook echo may indicate tornado potential, but:

- Not every hook produces a tornado
- Tornadoes can occur without a classic hook
- Radar interpretation requires velocity data and context

The hook is evidence, not a little red arrow labeled “tornado here.”

---

## 7.59 Debris Signatures

Modern dual-polarization radar can sometimes distinguish irregular debris from ordinary rain or hail.

A **tornado debris signature** occurs when radar detects objects lofted by a tornado.

This is strong evidence that a tornado is causing damage.

However, the absence of a debris signature does not mean no tornado exists.

Weak tornadoes or distant storms may not loft enough detectable debris.

---

## 7.60 Radar Limitations

Radar has important limitations:

- The beam rises above the ground with distance
- Mountains may block the beam
- Heavy rain can weaken the signal beyond it
- Ground clutter can mimic precipitation
- Low-level rotation may be missed far from the radar
- Data arrives in scans rather than continuously

Warnings combine radar with:

- Surface observations
- Spotter reports
- Satellite data
- Lightning data
- Computer guidance
- Environmental analysis

---

# Forecasting Thunderstorms

## 7.61 CAPE

**Convective Available Potential Energy**, or CAPE, estimates the energy available to a rising air parcel.

Higher CAPE generally means stronger potential updrafts.

But CAPE alone does not guarantee storms.

Lift must overcome any inhibition, and storm organization depends heavily on wind shear.

A day with very high CAPE and no trigger may remain quiet.

A day with moderate CAPE and strong shear may produce dangerous storms.

---

## 7.62 CIN

**Convective inhibition**, or CIN, is the energy preventing surface air from rising freely.

It is often associated with a cap.

Some inhibition can be helpful for severe storms because it prevents weak early convection from using up moisture and instability.

Too much inhibition prevents storms from forming.

Forecasting often comes down to whether the cap will break, and when.

---

## 7.63 Lifted Index

The **lifted index** compares the temperature of a lifted surface parcel with the surrounding air aloft.

Negative values indicate the parcel is warmer than its environment and therefore unstable.

More negative values generally indicate greater instability.

Like CAPE, it is only one piece of the forecast.

---

## 7.64 Storm-Relative Helicity

**Storm-relative helicity** estimates how much streamwise rotation a storm may ingest from the low-level wind.

Higher values can support rotating updrafts when other ingredients are favorable.

It is especially useful in assessing supercell and tornado potential.

The term sounds like something from a science-fiction reactor room, but it is essentially a measure of organized spin available to a storm.

---

## 7.65 Shear Versus Instability

Different combinations produce different storm types.

| Instability | Wind shear | Likely tendency |
|---|---|---|
| Low | Low | Weak showers or no storms |
| High | Low | Pulse storms, downbursts |
| Moderate | Strong | Organized severe storms possible |
| High | Strong | Supercells and major severe weather possible |

These are broad tendencies.

Storm mode also depends on boundaries, moisture depth, capping, and upper-level forcing.

---

## 7.66 Significant Tornado Environments

Strong tornadoes are most likely when several ingredients overlap:

- Rich low-level moisture
- Strong instability
- Strong vertical wind shear
- Curved low-level wind profile
- Persistent storm-scale rotation
- Favorable boundary interactions
- Manageable storm outflow temperature

No single index guarantees a tornado.

The atmosphere is not a vending machine where enough points make one drop out.

---

# Lightning Safety

## 7.67 When to Seek Shelter

Seek shelter immediately when:

- Thunder is heard
- Lightning is seen nearby
- A storm warning is issued
- Dark growing clouds approach
- Wind suddenly increases

Safe locations include:

- Substantial enclosed buildings
- Hard-topped enclosed vehicles

Unsafe locations include:

- Trees
- Pavilions
- Porches
- Dugouts
- Open garages
- Tents
- Beaches
- Boats
- Hilltops

---

## 7.68 Lightning Indoors

Inside a building during a thunderstorm:

- Avoid plumbing
- Avoid corded electronics
- Stay away from windows
- Do not lean against concrete walls
- Avoid direct contact with electrical systems

Lightning can travel through wiring, pipes, and metal reinforcement.

Wireless devices are generally safe when not connected to chargers.

---

## 7.69 The Thirty-Minute Rule

Wait at least 30 minutes after the last thunder before resuming outdoor activity.

A storm can produce lightning after the rain ends or after the main cloud appears to move away.

The final strike does not announce itself as the final strike. Inconsiderate, but true.

---

# Tornado Safety

## 7.70 Best Shelter

The safest available place is:

- A purpose-built storm shelter
- A basement
- A small interior room on the lowest floor
- A hallway away from windows

Put as many walls as possible between you and the outside.

Protect your head and neck with:

- Helmet
- Mattress
- Heavy blanket
- Cushion
- Arms

Wear sturdy shoes if time allows.

---

## 7.71 Mobile Homes

Mobile homes are not safe during tornadoes, even when tied down.

Move to a designated shelter before the storm arrives.

Do not wait until you can see a tornado.

Visibility may be blocked by rain, darkness, trees, or buildings.

---

## 7.72 Vehicles

A vehicle is highly vulnerable in a tornado.

Best practice depends on circumstances, but generally:

- Drive to a sturdy shelter when there is time
- Do not shelter beneath an overpass
- Do not attempt to outrun a nearby tornado through traffic
- Keep seat belt fastened if escape is impossible
- Get below window level and protect your head

Overpasses can funnel wind and expose people to debris.

They are not tornado shelters.

---

## 7.73 Schools and Large Buildings

In schools, offices, and large buildings:

- Follow the emergency plan
- Move away from windows
- Avoid large-span roofs such as gyms
- Use interior hallways or designated shelters
- Protect the head and neck

Large open rooms may have roofs that are more vulnerable to collapse.

---

# Flash-Flood Safety

## 7.74 Turn Around, Do Not Drown

Never drive through flooded roads.

Water depth is difficult to judge.

The road may be washed away.

Moving water can push or float a vehicle.

People routinely underestimate water because it looks flatter and calmer than it is.

A barricade is not a suggestion and certainly not a puzzle.

---

## 7.75 Flooding on Foot

Avoid walking into moving water.

Even shallow water can knock a person down.

Floodwater may contain:

- Sewage
- Chemicals
- Sharp debris
- Snakes or insects
- Hidden holes
- Electrical hazards

Move to higher ground.

Do not enter drainage channels or flooded underpasses.

---

# Field Observation  
## Watch a Storm Develop Safely

From indoors or from a safe distant location, observe an approaching thunderstorm.

Record:

- Time first cumulus appears
- Rate of vertical growth
- Anvil formation
- Wind shift
- Temperature change
- First thunder
- Arrival of rain
- Strongest gust
- Duration of storm

Notice whether the gust front arrives before the rain.

Do not remain outdoors once thunder is audible.

The storm will still be scientifically interesting through glass.

---

# Try It  
## Map Lightning Distance

From a safe indoor location:

1. Observe a flash.
2. Count seconds to thunder.
3. Divide by five for approximate miles.
4. Repeat for several flashes.

If the time decreases, the active lightning is getting closer.

If it increases, the storm may be moving away.

Do not use this exercise to delay shelter.

You should already be sheltered before counting.

---

# Try It  
## Build a Storm Timeline

After a storm, use:

- Weather radar history
- Local observations
- Photos
- Pressure readings
- Rain gauge totals

Construct a timeline showing:

- Storm initiation
- First warning
- Peak intensity
- Passage
- Dissipation

This helps connect what you saw at the ground with what radar showed above.

---

# Field Guide: Warning Signs

Seek shelter promptly when you notice:

| Sign | Possible meaning |
|---|---|
| Rapidly growing tower | Strong updraft developing |
| Anvil forming | Thunderstorm has reached maturity |
| Shelf cloud | Strong gust front approaching |
| Rotating wall cloud | Tornado potential |
| Greenish dark sky | Large water or hail content may be present |
| Frequent lightning | Strong electrification and active storm |
| Sudden cool gust | Thunderstorm outflow arriving |
| Roaring sound | Strong wind or tornado possible |
| Debris or dust rotation | Tornado may be on the ground |

A green sky does not guarantee hail or tornadoes. It should be considered with radar, warnings, and other storm signs.

---

# Chapter Summary

Thunderstorms require moisture, instability, and lift.

A typical storm passes through cumulus, mature, and dissipating stages.

The mature stage contains both updrafts and downdrafts and produces the greatest hazards.

Wind shear separates storm inflow from outflow and allows storms to become organized and long-lived.

Thunderstorms may occur as single cells, multicell clusters, squall lines, mesoscale convective systems, or supercells.

Supercells contain persistent rotating updrafts and can produce large hail, damaging wind, and tornadoes.

Lightning forms through electrical charge separation involving ice and supercooled water.

Thunder is produced when lightning rapidly heats and expands the surrounding air.

Hail grows in strong updrafts through repeated collection of supercooled water.

Downbursts and straight-line winds can be as destructive as tornadoes.

Flash flooding often results from slow-moving, training, or moisture-rich storms.

Radar helps detect precipitation, wind, rotation, hail, and debris, but it has important limitations.

No single atmospheric index predicts severe weather by itself.

Thunder, lightning, rotating clouds, sudden wind, and official warnings should always be taken seriously.

---

## Weather-Watcher’s Vocabulary

**Thunderstorm:** A storm that produces lightning and thunder.  
**Updraft:** Rising air within a storm.  
**Downdraft:** Descending air within a storm.  
**Gust front:** The leading edge of thunderstorm outflow.  
**Pulse storm:** A short-lived storm that intensifies and collapses rapidly.  
**Multicell storm:** A storm complex containing cells at different stages.  
**Supercell:** A thunderstorm with a persistent rotating updraft.  
**Mesocyclone:** A deep rotating region within a thunderstorm.  
**Squall line:** A long organized line of thunderstorms.  
**Bow echo:** A bowed radar structure associated with strong wind.  
**Derecho:** A widespread, long-lived convective windstorm.  
**Training:** Repeated movement of storms over the same area.  
**Shelf cloud:** A broad cloud above a thunderstorm gust front.  
**Wall cloud:** A localized lowering beneath a storm updraft.  
**Downburst:** A strong downdraft that spreads outward at the ground.  
**Microburst:** A small, intense downburst.  
**CAPE:** Energy available to rising convective air.  
**CIN:** Energy that suppresses convection.  
**Helicity:** A measure related to organized rotation available to a storm.  
**Reflectivity:** Radar measurement of returned energy from precipitation and other objects.  
**Velocity:** Radar measurement of motion toward or away from the radar.  
**Tornado debris signature:** Radar evidence of debris lofted by a tornado.  
**Landspout:** A tornado developing from near-surface rotation beneath a growing storm.  
**Waterspout:** A rotating column of air over water.

**Next: Chapter 8 — Tornadoes in Detail: Rotation, Forecasting, and Survival**

<!-- End of source chapter -->

---

# Chapter 8  
## Tornadoes in Detail: Rotation, Forecasting, and Survival

Tornadoes are among the most concentrated forms of severe weather on Earth.

A hurricane may span hundreds of miles. A winter storm may affect several states. A tornado may be only a few hundred yards wide—yet within that narrow path, wind can tear apart buildings, hurl debris, uproot trees, and transform familiar places in seconds.

Tornadoes are frightening partly because they are small, fast, and difficult to predict precisely. Meteorologists can identify environments favorable for tornadoes and warn when rotation develops, but they still cannot reliably name the exact neighborhood where a tornado will form far in advance.

Forecasting narrows the danger from days, to hours, to minutes.

Survival depends on using those minutes well.

---

## 8.1 What Counts as a Tornado?

A **tornado** is a violently rotating column of air that extends from a thunderstorm to the ground.

Three features matter:

- The air is rotating.
- The circulation is connected to a cloud.
- The circulation reaches the surface.

A visible funnel is not required.

Some tornadoes are hidden by rain, darkness, trees, buildings, dust, or low cloud. In other cases, the visible condensation funnel stops above the ground while the rotating air extends all the way down.

Surface clues may include:

- Rotating dust
- Debris rising
- Power flashes
- Spray over water
- A moving damage pattern
- Trees or vegetation bending in converging directions

### Illustration 1: Funnel cloud versus tornado

```text
FUNNEL CLOUD

cloud base
    ↓
visible rotating funnel
    ↓
does not reach ground

TORNADO

cloud base
    ↓
rotating column
    ↓
surface contact
dust or debris present
```

---

## 8.2 The Tornado Is Larger Than the Funnel

The visible condensation funnel shows only part of the circulation.

Strong wind may extend beyond it.

A narrow-looking funnel may have a much wider damaging wind field near the surface. Conversely, a broad cloud lowering may contain only a small intense circulation.

This is why visual size is a poor measure of danger.

The visible part is the costume. The wind field is the actor.

---

## 8.3 Tornado Size

Tornado width varies greatly.

Some are:

- Only a few yards wide
- A few hundred yards wide
- More than a mile wide
- Occasionally wider still

The damage path may widen, narrow, curve, or become discontinuous.

A tornado can strengthen or weaken rapidly.

Its width does not directly determine its intensity.

A narrow tornado can be violent. A broad tornado can contain weaker and stronger zones.

---

## 8.4 Tornado Duration

Many tornadoes last only a few minutes.

Others persist for:

- Twenty minutes
- An hour
- Longer in rare cases

A long-lived tornado may travel dozens of miles.

The apparent path may include changes in:

- Direction
- Speed
- Width
- Strength
- Visibility

Some tornadoes briefly lift and redevelop nearby.

This can create separate damage segments from the same parent storm.

---

## 8.5 Tornado Motion

Tornadoes usually move with their parent thunderstorm, but the path can shift.

Common forward speeds range from slow walking pace to highway speed.

Some tornadoes:

- Move northeast
- Move east
- Curve north
- Turn unexpectedly
- Nearly stall
- Accelerate rapidly

The idea that tornadoes always travel southwest to northeast is false.

Most follow the broader storm motion, but local changes are possible.

Never rely on a single “usual” direction.

---

# Rotation in the Atmosphere

## 8.6 Where Tornado Rotation Begins

Tornadoes do not begin because the entire thunderstorm suddenly spins like a top.

Rotation usually develops through several stages.

Strong wind shear may create horizontal rotation near the ground.

Imagine a tube of air rolling sideways.

```text
FASTER WIND ALOFT  → → → → →

SLOWER WIND BELOW  → →

Result:
horizontal rolling motion
↻  ↻  ↻  ↻
```

A thunderstorm updraft can tilt this rotating tube upward.

```text
horizontal rotation
      ↻
      ↻
      ↻

strong updraft
      ↑
      ↑

rotation becomes vertical
      ↺
```

The rising air then stretches the rotating column.

Stretching causes it to spin faster, much like a figure skater pulling in their arms.

---

## 8.7 Directional and Speed Shear

Wind shear comes in two main forms.

### Speed shear

Wind speed increases with height.

Example:

```text
surface:      10 mph
3,000 feet:   30 mph
10,000 feet:  60 mph
```

### Directional shear

Wind direction changes with height.

Example:

```text
surface wind: southeast
middle wind:  south
upper wind:   southwest
```

When both occur together, the wind profile may curve strongly with height.

This can help storms develop organized rotation.

---

## 8.8 Storm-Relative Inflow

A storm does not merely experience the environmental wind. It also moves through that wind.

Meteorologists therefore examine **storm-relative wind**—the airflow as experienced from the storm’s point of view.

A storm moving northeast may ingest air from the southeast even if the surface wind measured at a weather station appears more southerly.

The direction and strength of this inflow help determine how much rotation the storm can acquire.

---

## 8.9 The Mesocyclone

A **mesocyclone** is a broad rotating region within a thunderstorm updraft.

It may be several miles wide.

A tornado is much smaller.

```text
SUPERCELL

      broad mesocyclone
       ↺         ↺
          ↺
           ↓
      smaller tornado
           ↺
```

A mesocyclone is not a tornado, and many mesocyclones never produce one.

The key forecasting problem is determining when broad storm rotation will tighten and reach the ground.

That remains one of severe meteorology’s hardest questions.

---

## 8.10 Low-Level Rotation

Tornado potential increases when rotation becomes:

- Stronger
- Lower in the storm
- More persistent
- More compact
- Closely tied to the main updraft

Meteorologists pay special attention to rotation near the storm base.

A rotating circulation several miles above the ground may never produce a tornado.

A tightening circulation near the lowest radar scan is more concerning.

---

## 8.11 Stretching

A rotating column spins faster when it becomes narrower and taller.

This is called **vortex stretching**.

```text
WIDE, SLOW ROTATION

   ↺      ↺
      ↺

STRETCHED BY UPDRAFT

      ↑
      ↑
      ↺
      ↺

NARROWER, FASTER ROTATION
```

Strong rising motion can intensify rotation dramatically.

But rotation also depends on the temperature and momentum of nearby downdrafts.

---

# The Role of Downdrafts

## 8.12 Rear-Flank Downdraft

The **rear-flank downdraft**, or RFD, wraps around the back side of a rotating supercell.

It may appear visually as:

- A clear slot
- A bright gap
- A wrapping curtain of rain
- A descending cloud-free region

The RFD can help concentrate rotation near the surface.

But if it becomes too cold or forceful, it may undercut the updraft and weaken tornado development.

This balance is delicate.

---

## 8.13 Forward-Flank Downdraft

The **forward-flank downdraft** contains much of the storm’s main rain and hail.

A boundary separates its cool air from the warm inflow.

That boundary can provide additional horizontal rotation.

The tornado may form near the intersection of:

- Warm inflow
- Rear-flank downdraft
- Forward-flank boundary
- Rotating updraft

### Illustration 2: Simplified supercell near the ground

```text
                 heavy rain and hail
                       ↓↓↓
             FORWARD-FLANK DOWNDRAFT

warm inflow →   WALL CLOUD
                    ↺
                 TORNADO
                    ↺

          REAR-FLANK DOWNDRAFT
                 wraps around
```

---

## 8.14 Temperature of the Downdraft

A downdraft does not need to be extremely cold to help form a tornado.

In many tornadic storms, the air descending near the tornado is only moderately cooler than the inflow.

Very cold outflow often spreads quickly and pushes the updraft away from the surface circulation.

The most favorable balance often involves downdraft air that is cool enough to descend but not so cold that it destroys the inflow.

Meteorology loves Goldilocks conditions: not too hot, not too cold, and irritatingly difficult to measure.

---

# Tornado Development

## 8.15 A Simplified Tornadogenesis Sequence

A common supercell tornado sequence may look like this:

1. Strong vertical wind shear creates horizontal rotation.
2. A thunderstorm updraft tilts the rotation vertically.
3. The updraft stretches and strengthens the rotation.
4. A mesocyclone forms.
5. Downdrafts wrap around the low-level circulation.
6. Rotation tightens near the ground.
7. A tornado develops.
8. Cool outflow eventually may cut off the inflow.
9. The tornado weakens.

This is a simplified model.

Real storms may take different paths, and researchers continue refining the details.

---

## 8.16 The Wall Cloud

A **wall cloud** is a localized lowering beneath the rain-free base of a storm.

It may form as rain-cooled air is drawn into the updraft.

Some of that air rises, cools, and condenses at a lower altitude than the surrounding cloud base.

A wall cloud is most concerning when it is:

- Persistent
- Rotating
- Rapidly rising
- Becoming more compact
- Connected to strong inflow

A nonrotating wall cloud may produce no tornado.

A rotating one requires immediate caution.

---

## 8.17 Inflow Tails

A narrow cloud band may extend from the wall cloud toward the storm’s precipitation area.

This is sometimes called an **inflow tail** or **tail cloud**.

It indicates moist air being drawn toward the updraft.

An inflow tail does not point toward the tornado like an arrow. It shows the direction from which air is feeding the storm.

---

## 8.18 Clear Slots

A bright or cloud-free area may appear behind a wall cloud.

This can mark descending rear-flank air wrapping around the mesocyclone.

The wall cloud may take on a horseshoe or circular shape.

A clear slot combined with tightening rotation is a significant warning sign.

Do not interpret clearer sky beside a rotating storm as improving weather. That is exactly the sort of confidence trick the atmosphere enjoys.

---

## 8.19 Funnel Formation

As pressure decreases inside tightening rotation, air cools.

Water vapor may condense, producing a visible funnel.

The funnel may:

- Descend from the cloud
- Form partway down
- Appear suddenly
- Remain ragged
- Become smooth and narrow
- Never fully reach the ground visually

The circulation may contact the surface before the condensation funnel does.

---

## 8.20 Debris Cloud

When rotation reaches the ground, dust and debris may rise beneath the funnel.

This **debris cloud** may be the first clear evidence of a tornado.

In humid conditions, the visible funnel may connect quickly to it.

In dry conditions, there may be little visible condensation.

At night, debris may be invisible except during lightning flashes—which is not a viewing strategy worth pursuing.

---

# Types of Tornadoes

## 8.21 Supercell Tornadoes

Supercell tornadoes form from storms with persistent rotating updrafts.

They include most strong and violent tornadoes.

Common features include:

- Mesocyclone
- Rotating wall cloud
- Strong inflow
- Rear-flank downdraft
- Hook echo
- Tornado debris signature in some cases

They may be long-lived and difficult to see because of rain.

---

## 8.22 Landspouts

A **landspout** forms when a growing thunderstorm stretches pre-existing rotation near the ground.

Unlike classic supercell tornadoes, landspouts usually do not develop from a deep mesocyclone.

They often form:

- Along boundaries
- Beneath rapidly growing cumulus
- In weak-shear environments
- Before heavy precipitation develops

They are often weaker but can still damage buildings and vehicles.

---

## 8.23 QLCS Tornadoes

Tornadoes can form within squall lines and other **quasi-linear convective systems**, or QLCSs.

These tornadoes often develop quickly along small rotating kinks in the line.

They may be:

- Short-lived
- Fast-moving
- Wrapped in heavy rain
- Difficult to detect early
- Embedded among damaging straight-line winds

QLCS tornadoes are especially dangerous at night because the entire storm line may already be producing strong wind.

---

## 8.24 Tropical-Cyclone Tornadoes

Tropical storms and hurricanes can produce tornadoes in their rainbands.

These tornadoes are often:

- Brief
- Fast-moving
- Difficult to see
- Embedded in heavy rain
- More common on the storm’s right-front side relative to motion in the Northern Hemisphere

They may occur far from the hurricane’s center.

A weakening tropical cyclone can still produce tornadoes well inland.

---

## 8.25 Waterspouts

Waterspouts occur over water.

### Fair-weather waterspouts

Usually form beneath developing cumulus clouds in relatively light-wind conditions.

Their development often begins near the surface.

A visible sequence may include:

1. Dark spot on the water
2. Spiral pattern
3. Spray ring
4. Visible funnel
5. Mature waterspout

### Tornadic waterspouts

These are ordinary tornadoes associated with severe storms that form over water or move from land onto water.

They can be intense.

All waterspouts are dangerous to boats and people nearby.

---

## 8.26 Gustnadoes

A **gustnado** is a brief rotating circulation along a gust front.

It is not connected to the cloud-base rotation of a tornado.

Gustnadoes may lift:

- Dust
- Leaves
- Lightweight debris

They can produce localized damage but usually are weaker and shorter-lived than tornadoes.

They are closer to rotating outflow than to a full thunderstorm vortex.

---

## 8.27 Dust Devils

A **dust devil** is a small rotating column of air formed by intense surface heating.

It is not a tornado because it is not connected to a thunderstorm.

Dust devils usually form on:

- Hot sunny days
- Dry bare ground
- Light-wind afternoons

Most are harmless, though stronger ones can toss lightweight objects and damage temporary structures.

---

# Tornado Appearance

## 8.28 Rope Tornadoes

A rope tornado is narrow and often curved.

Rope shapes are common during the weakening stage, but a rope tornado can still be dangerous.

The circulation may remain intense even as it narrows.

---

## 8.29 Cone Tornadoes

Cone tornadoes have a wider base at the cloud and narrow toward the ground.

They are among the classic tornado shapes.

A cone can strengthen, widen, or transition into a rope.

---

## 8.30 Stovepipe Tornadoes

A stovepipe tornado has nearly parallel sides.

It may appear broad, solid, and vertical.

This form often indicates a well-established circulation, though shape alone does not determine strength.

---

## 8.31 Wedge Tornadoes

A wedge tornado appears wider than it is tall from the observer’s perspective.

It may fill a large part of the horizon.

Wedge tornadoes are often strong, but not every wedge is violent.

Rain and low cloud can also make a tornado look wider than its actual circulation.

---

## 8.32 Multiple-Vortex Tornadoes

Some tornadoes contain smaller vortices orbiting within the main circulation.

These subvortices can produce narrow streaks of extreme damage.

A house may be destroyed while another nearby is less affected.

This does not mean the tornado “jumped.”

The internal wind structure varied sharply over short distances.

### Illustration 3: Multiple-vortex tornado

```text
MAIN CIRCULATION
        ↺

small subvortices
   ↺      ↺
      ↺
```

---

## 8.33 Rain-Wrapped Tornadoes

Heavy precipitation may completely hide a tornado.

This is common in:

- High-precipitation supercells
- Squall lines
- Tropical rainbands
- Nighttime storms

A rain-wrapped tornado may be invisible until it is extremely close.

This is why warning information matters more than visual confirmation.

Do not wait to see it.

The tornado is not applying for a visibility permit.

---

# Tornado Intensity

## 8.34 Why Wind Is Hard to Measure

Direct wind measurements inside tornadoes are rare.

Most instruments are not placed exactly in the path, and those that are may fail.

Radar can estimate wind above the ground, but not always the strongest near-surface speed.

As a result, tornado intensity is usually estimated from damage.

---

## 8.35 The Enhanced Fujita Scale

The **Enhanced Fujita scale**, or EF scale, rates tornado damage from EF0 to EF5.

| Rating | General damage |
|---:|---|
| EF0 | Light |
| EF1 | Moderate |
| EF2 | Considerable |
| EF3 | Severe |
| EF4 | Devastating |
| EF5 | Incredible |

The rating is based on:

- Type of structure or object
- Quality of construction
- Degree of damage
- Estimated wind needed to cause it

A poorly built structure may fail at a lower wind speed than a well-built one.

Survey teams therefore examine construction details carefully.

---

## 8.36 Damage Indicators

Surveyors use damage indicators such as:

- One- and two-family homes
- Schools
- Commercial buildings
- Metal buildings
- Towers
- Utility poles
- Hardwood trees
- Softwood trees

Each indicator has several degrees of damage.

For example, roof loss does not mean the same thing on every structure.

The way the roof was attached matters enormously.

---

## 8.37 EF Ratings Are Not Real-Time Forecasts

A tornado receives an EF rating only after damage is surveyed.

During a warning, no one knows the final rating.

A seemingly small tornado may intensify.

A radar-indicated tornado may be hidden.

Every tornado warning should be treated seriously regardless of expected strength.

---

## 8.38 Path Width and Rating

Tornado width is not part of the EF rating.

A wide tornado may have lower-end damage.

A narrow tornado may produce extreme damage.

Intensity, width, and duration are separate characteristics.

People often blend them together because the most memorable tornadoes are frequently large and violent. The atmosphere does not owe us that visual consistency.

---

# Forecasting Tornadoes

## 8.39 Days in Advance

Several days before an event, forecasters examine broad patterns.

They look for:

- Strong upper-level troughs
- Rich moisture returning north
- Surface low development
- Strong wind shear
- Warm fronts
- Drylines
- Large instability
- Powerful jet-stream winds

Forecast confidence is usually regional at this stage.

Meteorologists may identify a broad area where severe storms are possible, not a specific town.

---

## 8.40 The Day Before

By the day before an event, forecasters can often better estimate:

- Storm timing
- Frontal position
- Moisture quality
- Instability
- Storm mode
- Tornado potential
- Main hazard region

Uncertainty may remain about clouds, morning storms, the cap, and exact boundary placement.

A shift of fifty miles can greatly change who is at highest risk.

---

## 8.41 The Morning of the Event

Forecasters examine real observations, including:

- Surface temperature
- Dew point
- Wind
- Pressure
- Balloon soundings
- Satellite imagery
- Radar
- Cloud cover
- Boundary position

The atmosphere may differ from computer-model predictions.

Morning storms might reduce instability—or leave an outflow boundary that increases tornado risk later.

The real atmosphere always gets the final edit.

---

## 8.42 Watches

A **tornado watch** means conditions are favorable for tornadoes in and near the watch area.

A watch usually covers a broad region and may last several hours.

During a watch:

- Review your shelter location
- Keep alerts enabled
- Charge devices
- Bring pets inside
- Avoid long outdoor trips
- Be ready to act quickly

A watch means preparation.

It does not mean a tornado is currently approaching your home.

---

## 8.43 Warnings

A **tornado warning** means a tornado has been detected, indicated by radar, or reported by observers.

A warning usually covers a much smaller area than a watch.

During a warning:

- Go to shelter immediately
- Do not wait for visual confirmation
- Protect your head and neck
- Bring children and pets with you
- Continue monitoring updates from shelter

A warning means action.

The distinction is simple:

```text
WATCH = conditions are favorable

WARNING = danger is occurring or imminent
```

---

## 8.44 Radar-Indicated Tornadoes

A tornado warning may be issued when radar detects strong low-level rotation even before a tornado is confirmed.

This provides valuable lead time.

Some radar-indicated circulations never produce a tornado.

That does not mean the warning was unnecessary.

Waiting for confirmed damage would turn warning into obituary.

---

## 8.45 Confirmed Tornadoes

A tornado may be confirmed by:

- Trained spotters
- Law enforcement
- Emergency managers
- Video
- Debris reports
- Radar debris signature

A confirmed tornado warning deserves immediate action, especially when the circulation is moving toward your location.

---

## 8.46 Particularly Dangerous Situations

Some warnings or watches may use especially strong wording when forecasters believe a dangerous tornado outbreak or major tornado is possible.

The exact terminology varies by weather service and country.

The important point is not the label itself.

Read the warning details:

- Location
- Direction
- Speed
- Expected arrival
- Hazard
- Recommended action

Do not spend shelter time debating whether the wording sounds dramatic enough.

---

# Radar Signs

## 8.47 Velocity Couplets

Doppler radar can measure motion toward and away from the radar.

A **velocity couplet** occurs when strong inbound and outbound velocities appear close together.

```text
toward radar     away from radar
     →→→   |   ←←←

close spacing suggests rotation
```

The tighter and stronger the couplet, the more concerning the rotation may be.

Its height above the ground also matters.

---

## 8.48 Gate-to-Gate Shear

When strongly opposing radar velocities appear in adjacent sampling areas, meteorologists may describe this as strong **gate-to-gate shear**.

This can signal a compact circulation.

However, radar resolution decreases with distance.

A circulation far from the radar may be difficult to sample accurately.

---

## 8.49 Hook Echoes

A hook echo may form when precipitation wraps around the mesocyclone.

It often appears on the storm’s rear flank.

A classic hook can support tornado concern, but it is neither required nor sufficient.

Some tornadic storms never display a clean hook.

Some hook-shaped echoes produce no tornado.

Radar interpretation is always about patterns, not a single pretty shape.

---

## 8.50 Debris Signatures

Dual-polarization radar can sometimes identify debris lofted by a tornado.

A **tornado debris signature** strongly supports that a tornado is on the ground.

It is most convincing when:

- Located with strong rotation
- Moving consistently with the storm
- Extending vertically through multiple radar scans
- Occurring where debris is available

A debris signature may appear only after damage has begun.

It is confirmation, not a substitute for earlier warning.

---

## 8.51 Radar Beam Height

Radar beams rise above the ground with distance.

Far from the radar, the lowest scan may sample thousands of feet above the surface.

This means:

- Low-level rotation may be missed
- Tornadoes may form beneath the beam
- Warning confidence may be lower
- Spotter reports become especially valuable

Radar coverage is excellent but not magical.

No meteorological machine gets to be magical. The maintenance budget would be unbearable.

---

# Visual Warning Signs

## 8.52 Rotating Wall Cloud

A rotating wall cloud is one of the most important visual signs.

Look for:

- Broad organized rotation
- Rising motion
- Persistent shape
- Strong inflow
- Rapid tightening

Rotation may be easier to see by watching cloud features move around a common center.

Do not stand outside studying it when a warning is active.

Observation is useful until it starts competing with survival.

---

## 8.53 Funnel Cloud

A funnel cloud is a rotating condensation funnel extending from the cloud.

It may indicate tornado development.

But remember:

- It may not be visible through rain
- It may not reach the ground visually
- A tornado can exist before the funnel fully forms

The funnel is one clue, not the definition.

---

## 8.54 Ground Rotation

Dust, debris, leaves, or spray rotating beneath a storm can indicate a tornado even when no funnel is visible.

A rapidly organizing debris cloud is an emergency.

Do not assume it is “just wind.”

---

## 8.55 Power Flashes

Damaged electrical equipment may produce bright blue, green, or white flashes.

Power flashes can reveal a tornado’s location at night.

But transformers and lines can also flash during straight-line wind.

A series of moving flashes beneath strong radar rotation is especially concerning.

---

## 8.56 Roaring Sounds

People often compare tornado sound to:

- Freight train
- Jet engine
- Waterfall
- Deep rumble

Sound depends on:

- Distance
- Wind
- Terrain
- Debris
- Building structure
- Tornado size

Do not wait to hear a roar.

By the time it is obvious, the tornado may be dangerously close.

---

## 8.57 Green Skies

Some severe storms produce a green or turquoise tint.

This may result from sunlight interacting with deep storm clouds and large amounts of water or ice.

A green sky can accompany hail or severe storms, but it does not guarantee a tornado.

Treat it as a clue that the storm may be intense, not as a tornado detector.

---

# Tornado Safety at Home

## 8.58 Best Shelter Locations

The safest options are:

1. Purpose-built storm shelter
2. Basement
3. Small interior room on the lowest floor
4. Interior hallway
5. Closet or bathroom away from windows

Choose a location with:

- No windows
- Many walls between you and outdoors
- Minimal roof span
- Strong structural framing

Bathrooms can be useful because pipes and wall framing may add support, but the bathtub itself is not a magic shield.

---

## 8.59 Protect the Head and Neck

Most tornado injuries are caused by:

- Flying debris
- Falling objects
- Collapsing materials
- Broken glass

Protect your head and neck with:

- Bicycle helmet
- Sports helmet
- Mattress
- Heavy blanket
- Pillows
- Arms and hands

Keep helmets in or near the shelter area.

The helmet you can reach in ten seconds is better than the perfect one stored in the attic, which is a terrible place to visit during a tornado warning.

---

## 8.60 Shoes and Clothing

Wear sturdy shoes during severe weather.

After a tornado, floors and yards may contain:

- Nails
- Glass
- Splintered wood
- Metal
- Electrical wires

Long pants and a jacket can reduce cuts.

Keep shoes, flashlights, and basic supplies near the shelter.

---

## 8.61 Children

Teach children:

- Where to shelter
- What the alert sounds mean
- How to protect their head
- That practice does not mean danger is occurring
- To move quickly without searching for toys

Practice the route calmly.

A familiar routine reduces confusion during a real warning.

---

## 8.62 Pets

Bring pets inside before storms arrive.

Keep:

- Leashes
- Carriers
- Treats
- Identification
- Towels

near the shelter.

Do not delay shelter while searching outdoors for a frightened animal during a warning.

Preparation solves this before panic gets a vote.

---

## 8.63 Upper Floors

If you live in an apartment on an upper floor, identify a lower shelter location in advance.

Options may include:

- Ground-floor interior hallway
- Designated community shelter
- Interior stairwell
- Nearby sturdy building

Do not use an elevator during severe weather.

Arrange access before the warning begins.

---

## 8.64 Basements

In a basement, shelter:

- Beneath sturdy furniture
- Away from windows
- Away from heavy objects above
- Near an interior wall
- Under the strongest portion of the structure when known

Avoid areas beneath:

- Refrigerators
- Pianos
- Heavy cabinets
- Large water heaters

If the house collapses, those objects may fall through the floor.

---

# Mobile Homes and Vehicles

## 8.65 Mobile Homes

Mobile and manufactured homes are highly vulnerable to tornado wind.

Anchoring helps with some wind hazards but does not make them safe in a strong tornado.

Leave for a sturdier shelter before the storm arrives.

During a watch, consider relocating early if storms are expected.

Do not wait for a warning if reaching shelter takes time.

---

## 8.66 Vehicles

Vehicles offer poor protection from tornadoes.

If there is enough time, drive to a sturdy building outside the tornado’s path.

If a tornado is nearby:

- Do not race it through traffic
- Do not park under an overpass
- Keep your seat belt fastened
- Lower yourself below window level
- Protect your head

Leaving the vehicle for a low spot may expose you to debris, flooding, lightning, and traffic.

There is no universally safe roadside option.

The best solution is earlier shelter.

---

## 8.67 Why Overpasses Are Dangerous

Overpasses are not tornado shelters.

They may:

- Funnel and accelerate wind
- Expose people to flying debris
- Offer little protection
- Create traffic jams
- Block evacuation routes

Video of people surviving beneath one created a persistent myth.

Survival does not prove safety.

People survive bad decisions all the time. It is a very unreliable certification system.

---

# Schools, Offices, and Public Buildings

## 8.68 Large Rooms

Avoid large open spaces such as:

- Gymnasiums
- Auditoriums
- Cafeterias
- Warehouses
- Big-box stores

Wide roofs may collapse when supporting walls fail.

Move to:

- Interior hallways
- Small rooms
- Reinforced shelter areas
- Lower floors

Stay away from glass.

---

## 8.69 Workplaces

Workplaces should have:

- Defined shelter areas
- Alert systems
- Accountability procedures
- Drills
- Backup lighting
- Plans for visitors
- Plans for employees with disabilities

A warning is not the time for a committee meeting about where everyone ought to go.

That should already be settled.

---

## 8.70 Hospitals and Care Facilities

Facilities with patients who cannot move quickly require detailed planning.

Plans may include:

- Moving beds away from windows
- Closing doors
- Protecting patients with blankets
- Relocating those at greatest risk first
- Using interior corridors
- Maintaining essential equipment

Tornado safety in medical settings depends heavily on staff preparation and building design.

---

# Nighttime Tornadoes

## 8.71 Why Night Tornadoes Are Especially Dangerous

Nighttime tornadoes are harder to detect because:

- People are asleep
- Funnels are difficult to see
- Rain may hide the circulation
- Outdoor sirens may not wake people indoors
- Storms may move quickly

Every home in tornado-prone areas should have multiple ways to receive warnings.

---

## 8.72 Warning Methods

Useful warning methods include:

- Phone emergency alerts
- Weather radio
- Trusted weather app
- Local television or radio
- Community alert systems

Do not rely only on outdoor warning sirens.

They are mainly designed for people outdoors.

Keep the phone loud enough to wake you and permit emergency alerts through do-not-disturb settings.

---

## 8.73 Before Going to Sleep

On a severe-weather night:

- Charge your phone
- Turn alert volume on
- Place shoes nearby
- Prepare helmets
- Bring pets inside
- Clear the route to shelter
- Know the expected storm window

This takes a few minutes and greatly reduces nighttime confusion.

---

# Tornado Myths

## 8.74 “Open the Windows”

Do not open windows.

It wastes time and exposes you to glass.

Buildings are damaged by extreme wind and debris, not because trapped indoor pressure needs to escape.

Go to shelter immediately.

The tornado will handle the window-opening without your assistance.

---

## 8.75 “Highways Protect You”

Roads do not repel tornadoes.

Tornadoes cross highways, rivers, hills, and city boundaries.

A path may appear to follow terrain temporarily, but there is no protective road effect.

---

## 8.76 “Tornadoes Avoid Cities”

Tornadoes can strike cities.

Urban areas occupy less land than rural areas, so direct hits are statistically less common.

Buildings do not create a protective shield.

Cities may actually make observation more difficult because structures block the view.

---

## 8.77 “Rivers Stop Tornadoes”

Tornadoes can cross rivers and lakes.

Water may change surface friction slightly, but it does not stop the storm-scale circulation.

A tornado may weaken, strengthen, or remain unchanged while crossing water.

---

## 8.78 “Mountains Prevent Tornadoes”

Mountains influence storms but do not make tornadoes impossible.

Tornadoes can occur in valleys, hills, and mountainous regions.

Terrain may hide the tornado and reduce warning time.

---

## 8.79 “The Southwest Corner Is Always Safest”

The old advice to shelter in the southwest corner of a basement is unreliable.

Tornadoes move in many directions, and debris can enter from any side.

Choose an interior location away from windows and heavy objects above.

---

## 8.80 “A Small Tornado Is Safe to Watch”

A small or distant-looking tornado may:

- Move quickly
- Grow wider
- Change direction
- Be closer than it appears
- Hide stronger wind beyond the funnel

Perspective is deceptive.

Storms also tend to move faster than spectators make decisions, especially spectators holding phones.

---

# After a Tornado

## 8.81 Immediate Hazards

After the storm, watch for:

- Downed power lines
- Gas leaks
- Broken glass
- Unstable walls
- Exposed nails
- Floodwater
- Fires
- Damaged trees
- Additional storms

Do not enter heavily damaged buildings unless authorities say it is safe.

---

## 8.82 Injuries

Provide basic first aid when safe.

Call emergency services for serious injuries.

Do not move an injured person unless:

- Fire is present
- Structural collapse is imminent
- Another immediate hazard exists

Use clean cloth to control bleeding.

Wear gloves if available.

---

## 8.83 Gas and Electricity

If you smell gas:

- Leave immediately
- Avoid flames
- Do not switch electrical devices on or off
- Notify the utility or emergency services from a safe location

Treat all downed wires as energized.

Stay far away from wires touching:

- Vehicles
- Trees
- Fences
- Water
- Buildings

---

## 8.84 Documentation

When safe, photograph damage for insurance and emergency records.

Record:

- Time
- Location
- Structural damage
- Vehicle damage
- Fallen trees
- Utility damage

Do not enter unstable areas merely for better photographs.

A dramatic insurance picture is not worth becoming additional claim documentation.

---

## 8.85 Helping Others

Check neighbors when conditions are safe, especially:

- Older adults
- People with disabilities
- Families with small children
- Anyone living alone

Avoid blocking roads needed by emergency crews.

Do not enter restricted damage zones.

Well-intentioned traffic can delay rescue operations.

---

# Field Observation  
## Study a Tornado Warning Afterward

After a tornado event, review:

- Watch issuance time
- Warning issuance time
- Radar images
- Storm motion
- Spotter reports
- Damage path
- Warning updates

Create a timeline.

Ask:

- How much lead time was available?
- What radar sign appeared first?
- Was the tornado visible?
- Did the storm change direction?
- Were warnings updated as it moved?

This is one of the safest and most useful ways to study tornadoes: afterward, indoors, with coffee and no airborne lumber.

---

# Try It  
## Build a Home Tornado Plan

Write down:

- Primary shelter
- Backup shelter
- Route from each bedroom
- How alerts will be received
- Who gets the children
- Who gets the pets
- Where helmets and shoes are stored
- Where flashlights are stored
- Where medications are kept

Then practice moving to shelter.

Time the drill.

The goal is not panic or speed for its own sake. It is removing hesitation.

---

# Try It  
## Make a Shelter Kit

A compact shelter kit may include:

- Flashlight
- Batteries
- Weather radio
- Phone charger
- Sturdy shoes
- Helmets
- First-aid supplies
- Whistle
- Water
- Essential medications
- Pet leash
- Identification copies

Keep it where you shelter.

A kit in the garage is not especially useful when the garage is the first place you should avoid.

---

# Field Guide: Tornado Clues

| Clue | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Rotating wall cloud | Organized low-level rotation |
| Rapid upward motion | Strong updraft |
| Tightening rotation | Possible tornadogenesis |
| Clear slot wrapping in | Rear-flank downdraft influence |
| Debris beneath funnel | Ground contact |
| Moving power flashes | Possible damage path |
| Strong inflow wind | Air feeding storm |
| Rain-wrapped circulation | Tornado may be hidden |
| Radar velocity couplet | Rotation detected |
| Radar debris signature | Tornado likely on ground |

No single visual clue is required.

A warning should be followed even when the sky outside looks unimpressive.

---

# Chapter Summary

A tornado is a rotating column of air connecting a thunderstorm with the ground.

The visible funnel may show only part of the damaging circulation.

Strong vertical wind shear can create horizontal rotation that a thunderstorm updraft tilts and stretches vertically.

A mesocyclone is a broad rotating updraft; a tornado is a smaller circulation near the ground.

Downdrafts help shape low-level rotation, but very cold outflow can disrupt tornado formation.

Tornadoes may form from supercells, squall lines, tropical rainbands, and non-supercell boundary circulations.

Tornado size, duration, width, and intensity are separate characteristics.

The Enhanced Fujita scale estimates intensity from damage after the storm.

Forecasting progresses from broad regional outlooks to watches and then warnings.

A watch means prepare. A warning means shelter immediately.

The safest locations are purpose-built shelters, basements, or small interior rooms on the lowest floor.

Protecting the head and neck is essential.

Mobile homes, vehicles, overpasses, large open rooms, and windows provide poor protection.

Nighttime tornadoes are especially dangerous, so every household needs multiple reliable warning methods.

The most important tornado decision is usually not identifying the exact cloud feature.

It is moving to shelter before the storm removes the choice.

---

## Weather-Watcher’s Vocabulary

**Tornado:** A rotating column of air in contact with a thunderstorm and the ground.  
**Funnel cloud:** A rotating condensation funnel not visibly reaching the surface.  
**Mesocyclone:** A broad rotating updraft within a thunderstorm.  
**Tornadogenesis:** The process by which a tornado forms.  
**Vortex stretching:** Intensification of rotation as a vortex is stretched vertically.  
**Rear-flank downdraft:** Descending air wrapping around the back of a supercell.  
**Forward-flank downdraft:** The rain- and hail-filled downdraft ahead of the main updraft.  
**Wall cloud:** A localized lowering beneath a thunderstorm updraft.  
**Inflow:** Warm, moist air entering a storm.  
**Velocity couplet:** Adjacent radar winds moving toward and away from the radar.  
**Hook echo:** A hook-shaped precipitation pattern associated with some supercells.  
**Tornado debris signature:** Radar evidence of lofted debris.  
**Landspout:** A tornado produced by stretching near-surface rotation beneath a growing storm.  
**QLCS tornado:** A tornado embedded within a thunderstorm line.  
**Waterspout:** A rotating column of air over water.  
**Gustnado:** A brief rotating circulation along a gust front.  
**Enhanced Fujita scale:** A damage-based tornado intensity scale.  
**Tornado watch:** Conditions are favorable for tornadoes.  
**Tornado warning:** A tornado is occurring or may occur imminently.  
**Rain-wrapped tornado:** A tornado hidden by heavy precipitation.  
**Multiple-vortex tornado:** A tornado containing smaller rotating subvortices.

**Next: Chapter 9 — Tropical Cyclones: Hurricanes, Typhoons, Storm Surge, and Inland Flooding**

<!-- End of source chapter -->

---

# Chapter 9  
## Tropical Cyclones: Hurricanes, Typhoons, Storm Surge, and Inland Flooding

A tropical cyclone is a vast rotating heat engine built from warm ocean water, moist air, thunderstorms, and Earth’s rotation.

Unlike a midlatitude cyclone, it does not draw most of its energy from the collision of warm and cold air masses. A mature tropical cyclone is powered mainly by heat and moisture released from the ocean and transferred upward through deep thunderstorms.

The same type of storm is called a **hurricane**, **typhoon**, or **tropical cyclone** depending on where it forms. The physics does not care which ocean basin supplied the stationery. [World Meteorological Organization](https://wmo.int/topics/tropical-cyclone?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

---

## 9.1 One Storm, Several Regional Names

The general scientific term is **tropical cyclone**.

Regional names include:

| Region | Common name for a mature storm |
|---|---|
| North Atlantic | Hurricane |
| Eastern and central North Pacific | Hurricane |
| Western North Pacific | Typhoon |
| Indian Ocean and South Pacific | Tropical cyclone |
| Parts of the Indian Ocean | Additional regional classifications may be used |

A hurricane and a typhoon are therefore not different kinds of storms. They are tropical cyclones occurring in different ocean basins. [World Meteorological Organization](https://wmo.int/topics/tropical-cyclone?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

---

## 9.2 Tropical Versus Midlatitude Cyclones

Both tropical and midlatitude cyclones are rotating low-pressure systems, but their structures differ.

### Tropical cyclone

- Develops mainly over warm ocean water
- Has a warm core
- Usually lacks warm and cold fronts
- Contains thunderstorms surrounding its center
- Draws energy from warm water and condensation
- Often has its strongest winds near the surface

### Midlatitude cyclone

- Develops along strong temperature contrasts
- Usually contains fronts
- May have a cold core aloft
- Draws energy from horizontal temperature differences
- Often covers a broader area
- Can form over either land or water

A tropical cyclone can later transform into a midlatitude cyclone through **extratropical transition**.

---

## 9.3 The Ingredients for Development

Tropical cyclone formation generally requires several ingredients working together:

- Warm ocean water
- A deep layer of ocean heat
- Moist air through much of the lower and middle atmosphere
- Atmospheric instability
- A pre-existing disturbance
- Relatively weak vertical wind shear
- Sufficient distance from the equator
- Outflow high in the atmosphere

Ocean temperatures near or above roughly **80°F, or 27°C**, are commonly favorable, but warm surface water alone is not enough. The warmth must often extend below the immediate surface so the storm does not quickly churn colder water upward. [NOAA](https://www.noaa.gov/jetstream/tropical/tropical-cyclone-introduction?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

```text
WARM OCEAN
     +
MOIST, UNSTABLE AIR
     +
PRE-EXISTING DISTURBANCE
     +
LOW WIND SHEAR
     +
EARTH’S ROTATION
     ↓
POSSIBLE TROPICAL CYCLONE
```

Even when all these ingredients are present, development is not guaranteed. Tropical cyclogenesis is less a recipe than a difficult audition in which most candidates do not get the part. [National Ocean Service](https://oceanservice.noaa.gov/facts/how-hurricanes-form.html?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

---

## 9.4 Why Warm Water Matters

Warm ocean water supplies moisture through evaporation.

As moist air rises in thunderstorms:

1. Pressure decreases.
2. The air expands and cools.
3. Water vapor condenses.
4. Latent heat is released.
5. The rising air warms relative to its surroundings.
6. The updraft becomes more buoyant.

This released heat helps lower pressure through the depth of the storm and supports continued rising motion.

More surface air then flows inward toward the lower pressure.

That air acquires additional heat and moisture from the ocean, rises, and continues the cycle.

### Illustration 1: The tropical heat engine

```text
                    OUTFLOW ALOFT
              ← ← ←           → → →
                         ↑
                condensation releases
                     latent heat
                         ↑
                   THUNDERSTORMS
                         ↑
surface air spirals inward toward low pressure
            → → →       L       ← ← ←
~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~
       WARM OCEAN: HEAT + WATER VAPOR
```

---

## 9.5 Why Tropical Cyclones Rarely Form at the Equator

The Coriolis effect is weak near the equator.

Without enough Coriolis influence, a developing disturbance has difficulty organizing a broad rotating circulation.

Most tropical cyclones therefore begin at least several degrees of latitude away from the equator.

They do not form exactly where the ocean is hottest merely because the water has excellent enthusiasm.

---

## 9.6 The Need for a Disturbance

Tropical cyclones rarely begin from completely undisturbed air.

A pre-existing disturbance supplies:

- Low pressure
- Converging wind
- Thunderstorms
- Some initial rotation

Possible starting points include:

- Tropical waves
- Monsoon trough disturbances
- Decaying frontal boundaries
- Low-pressure areas
- Clusters of thunderstorms
