- Disturbances associated with the Madden–Julian Oscillation

In the Atlantic, many long-lived hurricanes begin as tropical waves moving westward from Africa.

---

## 9.7 Tropical Waves

A **tropical wave** is an elongated area of relatively low pressure moving through the tropics.

It may produce:

- Wind shifts
- Clusters of thunderstorms
- Heavy rain
- Gusty squalls
- Increased moisture

Most tropical waves do not become tropical cyclones.

Development becomes more likely when thunderstorms persist near a growing circulation and environmental wind shear remains weak.

---

## 9.8 Why Wind Shear Can Destroy Development

A tropical cyclone works best when its circulation is vertically aligned.

Strong vertical wind shear can:

- Tilt the vortex
- Push thunderstorms away from the center
- Introduce dry air
- Disrupt the warm core
- Expose the low-level circulation

```text
LOW WIND SHEAR

     upper center
          │
          │
     lower center
well aligned

STRONG WIND SHEAR

upper storms pushed away →
             /
            /
     low-level center
tilted and disrupted
```

A mature storm may survive some shear, but strong persistent shear commonly prevents strengthening.

---

# Stages of Development

## 9.9 Tropical Disturbance

A **tropical disturbance** is an organized area of thunderstorms that may show some signs of circulation but does not yet meet the criteria for a tropical depression.

Forecasters monitor:

- Thunderstorm persistence
- Surface pressure
- Wind circulation
- Organization
- Environmental conditions
- Computer-model guidance

The National Hurricane Center’s tropical weather outlook identifies areas where formation may occur during the coming days. [National Hurricane Center](https://www.nhc.noaa.gov/gtwo.php?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

---

## 9.10 Potential Tropical Cyclone

The National Hurricane Center may designate a disturbance as a **potential tropical cyclone** when it has not yet become a tropical cyclone but is expected to bring tropical-storm or hurricane conditions to land within the normal watch or warning period.

This allows watches and warnings to begin before the circulation has fully developed.

The designation does not mean the system is imaginary. It means the hazard may arrive faster than the taxonomy.

---

## 9.11 Tropical Depression

A **tropical depression** has an organized closed circulation and maximum sustained winds of **33 knots or less**, equivalent to 38 mph or less under the U.S. one-minute wind convention.

It receives a number rather than a regular storm name. [National Hurricane Center](https://www.nhc.noaa.gov/data/tcr/index.php?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

A depression can still produce:

- Heavy rain
- Flash flooding
- Mudslides
- Gusty wind
- Dangerous surf

The label describes wind speed, not total danger.

---

## 9.12 Tropical Storm

A system becomes a **tropical storm** when its maximum sustained winds reach **34–63 knots**, or approximately **39–73 mph**.

It is then assigned a name from the basin’s official list. [National Hurricane Center](https://www.nhc.noaa.gov/data/tcr/index.php?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

Naming makes storms easier to track, discuss, and distinguish when more than one system is active. [World Meteorological Organization](https://wmo.int/resources/wmo-fact-sheets/tropical-cyclone-naming?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

---

## 9.13 Hurricane or Typhoon

In the Atlantic and eastern or central North Pacific, the system becomes a **hurricane** when maximum sustained winds reach at least **64 knots**, or 74 mph.

In the western North Pacific, a storm at this strength is called a **typhoon**. [World Meteorological Organization](https://community.wmo.int/site/knowledge-hub/programmes-and-initiatives/aviation/aviation-hazards-tropical-cyclones?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

A hurricane may continue strengthening through several categories, but category describes wind only.

---

## 9.14 Major Hurricane

A **major hurricane** is a Category 3, 4, or 5 hurricane on the Saffir–Simpson Hurricane Wind Scale.

It has sustained winds of at least 111 mph.

The term does not mean that lower-category storms are harmless.

A Category 1 hurricane can produce catastrophic flooding or storm surge under the wrong circumstances.

---

# Storm Structure

## 9.15 The Main Parts

A mature tropical cyclone may contain:

- Eye
- Eyewall
- Spiral rainbands
- Central dense overcast
- Low-level inflow
- Upper-level outflow

The eye, eyewall, and rainbands are its most recognizable structural features. [NOAA](https://www.noaa.gov/jetstream/tropical/tropical-cyclone-introduction/tropical-cyclone-structure?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

### Illustration 2: Tropical cyclone from above

```text
        spiral rainband
             \

      \                 /
       \   EYEWALL     /
        \   _____     /
         \ / EYE \   /
          |       |
         / \_____/
        /
  spiral rainband

Northern Hemisphere rotation: counterclockwise
```

---

## 9.16 The Eye

The **eye** is the relatively calm central region of a strong tropical cyclone.

Inside the eye:

- Air generally sinks
- Clouds may thin or break
- Winds may become lighter
- Pressure is lowest near the center
- Skies may briefly appear blue

Eyes vary greatly in size.

Some are only a few miles wide. Others span many tens of miles.

The eye is not proof that the storm has ended.

The opposite eyewall will arrive after the center passes, often with wind from the opposite direction.

---

## 9.17 The Eyewall

The **eyewall** is the ring of intense thunderstorms surrounding the eye.

It usually contains:

- Strongest sustained wind
- Most violent gusts
- Heaviest rain
- Strongest updrafts
- Greatest turbulence
- Frequent lightning in some storms

Air spiraling inward cannot reach the exact center at the surface, so it rises rapidly in the eyewall.

The eyewall is generally the most dangerous part of the wind circulation.

---

## 9.18 Spiral Rainbands

Curved bands of showers and thunderstorms rotate around the center.

Rainbands may produce:

- Torrential rain
- Strong wind gusts
- Tornadoes
- Brief calmer gaps
- Repeated flooding

Outer bands can reach land long before the center.

They may also affect locations hundreds of miles from the eye.

A distant center is not a promise of a quiet afternoon.

---

## 9.19 The Central Dense Overcast

On satellite imagery, the **central dense overcast**, or CDO, is the broad shield of deep thunderstorms near the storm’s center.

In strengthening storms, it may become:

- More circular
- Colder-topped
- More symmetrical
- Better aligned with the low-level center

An eye may emerge within it as the cyclone intensifies.

---

## 9.20 Inflow and Outflow

Near the surface, air spirals inward.

As it approaches the center, it rises through thunderstorms.

Near the top of the storm, air spreads outward.

Healthy upper-level outflow removes rising air from the center and allows additional air to rise from below.

```text
             UPPER OUTFLOW
       ← ← ←             → → →
                   ↑
                   ↑
                EYEWALL
                   ↑
surface inflow →   L   ← surface inflow
```

Restricted outflow can limit intensification.

---

## 9.21 Warm Core

A tropical cyclone has a **warm core**.

The central atmosphere is warmer than the surrounding atmosphere through a deep layer.

This warmth develops from latent heat released by persistent thunderstorms and from sinking air within the eye.

The warm air column expands, producing low pressure at the surface and high pressure aloft.

---

## 9.22 Pressure and Wind

In general, lower central pressure is associated with stronger wind, but the relationship is not exact.

Wind also depends on:

- Pressure outside the storm
- Size of the circulation
- Radius of maximum wind
- Forward speed
- Storm structure
- Latitude

A compact hurricane may have fierce wind near the eye but a small wind field.

A large storm may have somewhat weaker maximum wind but spread damaging conditions over a much larger area.

---

# The Saffir–Simpson Hurricane Wind Scale

## 9.23 What the Scale Measures

The Saffir–Simpson Hurricane Wind Scale classifies hurricanes from Category 1 through Category 5 according to maximum sustained wind speed.

It does **not** directly classify:

- Storm surge
- Rainfall
- Flooding
- Tornado potential
- Storm size
- Forward speed

This limitation matters enormously. Storm surge depends on more than category, including storm size, coastline shape, ocean depth, forward motion, and wind direction. [National Hurricane Center](https://www.nhc.noaa.gov/qa/qa_200906_jamie_rhome.shtml?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

---

## 9.24 Category 1

**Sustained wind: 74–95 mph**

Possible effects:

- Roof and siding damage
- Broken large branches
- Uprooted shallow-rooted trees
- Power outages
- Dangerous coastal water
- Damage to poorly secured structures

Category 1 is the lowest hurricane category, not a starter hurricane with training wheels.

---

## 9.25 Category 2

**Sustained wind: 96–110 mph**

Possible effects:

- Major roof and siding damage
- Many fallen trees
- Roads blocked by debris
- Widespread power loss
- Greater structural damage
- Serious coastal impacts

---

## 9.26 Category 3

**Sustained wind: 111–129 mph**

Category 3 begins the major-hurricane range.

Possible effects:

- Major structural damage
- Roof decking lost
- Numerous trees uprooted
- Electricity and water unavailable for extended periods
- Severe coastal damage

---

## 9.27 Category 4

**Sustained wind: 130–156 mph**

Possible effects:

- Severe damage to well-built homes
- Loss of much of the roof structure
- Exterior walls damaged
- Extensive tree and utility damage
- Areas isolated for days or weeks
- Long-lasting power and water outages

---

## 9.28 Category 5

**Sustained wind: 157 mph or higher**

Possible effects:

- Large percentage of framed homes destroyed
- Roof failure
- Wall collapse
- Extensive isolation of communities
- Catastrophic infrastructure damage
- Areas uninhabitable for extended periods

There is no Category 6 on the operational scale.

Wind beyond the Category 5 threshold remains Category 5.

---

## 9.29 Category Can Change Quickly

A hurricane may strengthen or weaken before landfall.

Its category at one advisory does not guarantee its landfall intensity.

Even after wind weakens:

- Storm surge may already be moving inland
- Rainfall may increase
- Rivers may continue rising
- Tornadoes may develop
- Large waves may persist

A storm does not surrender its other hazards when the category number drops.

---

# Rapid Intensification

## 9.30 What Is Rapid Intensification?

**Rapid intensification** is a substantial increase in a tropical cyclone’s maximum sustained wind over a relatively short period.

It can occur when a storm crosses:

- Very warm water
- Deep ocean heat
- Moist air
- Weak wind shear
- Favorable upper-level outflow

Internal storm organization must also cooperate.

Rapid intensification near land is especially dangerous because evacuation decisions may have been based on a weaker earlier forecast.

---

## 9.31 Ocean Heat Content

Sea-surface temperature measures only the ocean’s skin.

A hurricane’s wind churns the water and can bring colder water upward.

If warm water extends to considerable depth, this mixing produces less cooling.

Regions with high **ocean heat content** can therefore support stronger or more prolonged intensification.

Warm eddies and deep currents may matter even when a surface-temperature map looks fairly uniform.

---

## 9.32 Eyewall Replacement Cycles

Strong hurricanes sometimes develop a second ring of thunderstorms outside the original eyewall.

The outer eyewall strengthens and cuts off moisture to the inner one.

During this **eyewall replacement cycle**:

- Maximum wind may temporarily decrease
- The eye may become less distinct
- The wind field may expand
- The new eyewall may contract
- Reintensification may follow

A slightly weaker maximum wind does not necessarily mean the overall threat has shrunk.

The storm may merely be remodeling—and, as renovations go, this one is appalling.

---

# Tropical Cyclone Motion

## 9.33 Steering Currents

Tropical cyclones are carried by broader atmospheric winds called **steering currents**.

The storm’s track depends on:

- Subtropical high-pressure systems
- Upper-level troughs
- Ridges
- Nearby cyclones
- Storm depth
- Latitude

A strong subtropical ridge often pushes storms westward.

A weakness in the ridge may allow a storm to turn north.

An approaching trough may then pull it northeastward.

---

## 9.34 Recurvature

**Recurvature** occurs when a westward-moving tropical cyclone turns poleward and then eastward.

A common Northern Hemisphere track is:

```text
westward → northwestward → northward → northeastward
```

Not every cyclone recurves.

Some continue west into land.

Others turn early and remain over the ocean.

---

## 9.35 Wobbles

A tropical cyclone does not move in a perfectly smooth line.

The center may wobble because of:

- Eyewall changes
- Asymmetric thunderstorms
- Interaction with land
- Internal vortex motion
- Measurement uncertainty

Short-term wobbles can shift the eye locally but usually do not represent a permanent change in the larger forecast track.

Trying to forecast landfall from one radar wobble is how otherwise sensible people begin arguing with geometry.

---

## 9.36 The Track Forecast Cone

The forecast cone represents the probable track of the **center** of a tropical cyclone.

It does not show the storm’s size.

Hazards can occur far outside it.

The operational cone is designed so that roughly two-thirds of historical official track errors over the relevant sample fall within its boundary. [National Hurricane Center](https://www.nhc.noaa.gov/aboutcone.shtml?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

```text
         future uncertainty widens

current center ●───<────<────<

The cone concerns the likely center track,
not the full area of wind, rain, surge, or tornadoes.
```

During 2026, the National Hurricane Center is also producing an experimental cone using a revised methodology, while the operational cone remains available. [National Hurricane Center](https://www.nhc.noaa.gov/pdf/2026NHCNewProductsAndServices.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

---

## 9.37 The Center Line Is Not a Promise

The line through the cone is only one forecast track.

The center can move anywhere within or even outside the cone.

Focusing on the center line creates several mistakes:

- People outside it may feel falsely safe.
- People directly under it may think impacts are certain.
- Storm size is ignored.
- Forecast uncertainty is misunderstood.

The correct question is not merely, “Where is the line?”

It is, “Which hazards could reach my location, and when?”

---

# Storm Surge

## 9.38 What Is Storm Surge?

**Storm surge** is an abnormal rise of seawater generated by a storm above the level expected from the normal astronomical tide.

Strong onshore wind pushes water toward the coast.

Low atmospheric pressure contributes somewhat, but wind is usually the dominant cause.

Surge can inundate:

- Beaches
- Islands
- Estuaries
- Marshes
- Rivers
- Bays
- Coastal neighborhoods

It is one of the deadliest tropical-cyclone hazards.

---

## 9.39 Storm Tide

**Storm tide** is the actual water level produced by combining:

- Storm surge
- Astronomical tide

```text
STORM TIDE =
NORMAL TIDE + STORM SURGE
```

A storm arriving near high tide can produce a higher total water level than the same surge arriving at low tide.

Waves then ride on top of the storm tide, increasing damage.

---

## 9.40 What Controls Surge Height?

Storm surge depends on:

- Storm size
- Wind speed
- Wind direction
- Forward speed
- Angle of approach
- Shape of the coastline
- Depth and slope of the sea floor
- Bays and estuaries
- Astronomical tide
- Local rivers and rainfall

This is why hurricane category alone cannot predict surge. [National Hurricane Center](https://www.nhc.noaa.gov/qa/qa_200906_jamie_rhome.shtml?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

A large lower-category storm can produce worse surge than a compact higher-category storm.

---

## 9.41 Shallow Coastal Water

Surge can become especially severe where the continental shelf is broad and shallow.

Water pushed toward shore cannot easily move downward, so it piles up.

Bays and narrowing estuaries may funnel water higher.

A steep offshore shelf may limit surge somewhat, though waves can still be destructive.

---

## 9.42 The Most Dangerous Side

In the Northern Hemisphere, the highest surge frequently occurs near and to the right of where the center crosses the coast because the storm’s rotational wind and forward motion may combine to produce strong onshore flow.

But coastline geometry can shift the location of worst flooding.

Never use a simple “right side” rule instead of an official local surge forecast.

Water does not read the textbook cross section before entering the neighborhood.

---

## 9.43 Surge Moves Through Waterways

Storm surge can travel inland through:

- Rivers
- Creeks
- Canals
- Bays
- Sounds
- Drainage systems

A home need not sit directly on the open beach to flood.

Surge can enter from behind, beside, or through connected waterways.

Some coastal residents discover too late that “not oceanfront” and “not surge-prone” are entirely different statements.

---

## 9.44 Waves on Top of Surge

Large waves ride above the raised storm-tide level.

These waves add:

- Repeated impact
- Erosion
- Structural battering
- Floating debris
- Dune destruction

A building may withstand standing water but fail under repeated breaking waves.

Wave action is one reason beachfront structures suffer such extreme damage.

---

## 9.45 Surge Can Arrive Before Landfall

Water may begin rising many hours before the eye reaches land.

Roads can flood before the strongest wind arrives.

Evacuation routes may close earlier than expected.

Waiting until landfall to leave a surge zone is not late planning. It is absence of planning wearing shoes.

---

# Rainfall and Inland Flooding

## 9.46 Rain Is Not Determined by Category

Tropical-cyclone rainfall depends largely on:

- Storm speed
- Moisture
- Size
- Terrain
- Rainband structure
- Interaction with fronts
- Repeated movement over one area

A weak or former tropical storm can produce catastrophic inland flooding.

Wind classification may decrease rapidly after landfall while the moisture continues inland.

---

## 9.47 Slow-Moving Storms

A slow storm can produce extreme rainfall totals because the same region remains beneath rainbands for hours or days.

Flood risk rises when:

- Ground is already saturated
- Rivers are high
- Terrain funnels runoff
- Storms train over one area
- Drainage is poor

The most dangerous part of the storm may occur well after the center crosses the coast.

---

## 9.48 Terrain and Landslides

Mountains force moist tropical air upward, increasing rainfall.

Steep terrain can produce:

- Flash flooding
- Landslides
- Mudflows
- Rockfalls
- Washed-out roads
- Isolated communities

Tropical cyclones may cause devastating mountain disasters even after their winds weaken dramatically.

---

## 9.49 River Flooding

Rainwater continues flowing into rivers long after rain stops.

River crests may occur:

- Hours later
- Days later
- Far downstream
- After skies have cleared

A sunny day after a tropical cyclone does not mean the flood threat is over.

The water is still working through the system, with none of the urgency of the people waiting on it.

---

## 9.50 Urban Flooding

Urban areas flood quickly because roofs, roads, and parking lots prevent infiltration.

Problems include:

- Overwhelmed storm drains
- Underpass flooding
- Sewage backups
- Basement flooding
- Disabled vehicles
- Hidden road damage

Never drive through floodwater.

Depth, current, contamination, and pavement condition are difficult to judge.

---

# Wind Hazards

## 9.51 Sustained Wind and Gusts

Tropical-cyclone wind is reported using sustained speed and gusts.

In U.S. hurricane advisories, maximum sustained wind is based on a one-minute average.

Gusts can be considerably stronger.

Wind varies because of:

- Rainbands
- Eyewall vortices
- Terrain
- Buildings
- Trees
- Elevation
- Storm motion

A weather station reading is not necessarily the wind experienced at every nearby location.

---

## 9.52 Wind Increases with Height

Wind usually strengthens above the ground because surface friction decreases.

Upper floors of high-rise buildings may experience substantially stronger wind than street level.

Wind can also accelerate:

- Around corners
- Between buildings
- Over ridges
- Across bridges
- Through gaps

Sheltering near windows on an upper floor is especially dangerous.

---

## 9.53 Flying Debris

Many injuries occur from objects accelerated by wind.

Possible debris includes:

- Roofing
- Signs
- Branches
- Patio furniture
- Fencing
- Glass
- Construction materials

Outdoor objects should be brought inside or securely anchored before tropical-storm-force wind begins.

Once strong wind starts, retrieval becomes a poor little action movie with no worthwhile ending.

---

## 9.54 Trees and Power Lines

Saturated soil makes trees easier to uproot.

Wind may knock trees into:

- Homes
- Vehicles
- Roads
- Utility lines

Power outages can last much longer than the storm.

Downed wires must always be treated as energized.

Generators must be operated outdoors and far from windows, doors, and vents because carbon monoxide can accumulate indoors.

---

# Tornadoes in Tropical Cyclones

## 9.55 Why They Form

Tropical cyclones contain strong low-level wind shear, especially in rainbands.

Small thunderstorms within the bands can acquire rotation and produce tornadoes.

These tornadoes are often:

- Brief
- Fast-moving
- Rain-wrapped
- Difficult to see
- Relatively small

They may occur far from the storm center.

---

## 9.56 Favored Region

In the Northern Hemisphere, tornadoes are often most frequent in the right-front portion of the storm relative to its motion.

There, the cyclone’s circulation and forward motion may combine to strengthen low-level wind shear.

However, tornadoes can occur in other quadrants.

Always follow local warnings rather than using the storm center as a homemade tornado map.

---

# Coastal Waves and Rip Currents

## 9.57 Dangerous Surf Far Away

A tropical cyclone can create large swells that travel hundreds of miles beyond the storm.

Beaches may experience:

- Powerful breaking waves
- Shore erosion
- Rip currents
- Dangerous inlets
- Overwash

These hazards may occur under sunny skies while the cyclone remains far offshore.

NHC notes that tropical cyclones can generate dangerous waves and deadly rip currents at large distances from the storm. [National Hurricane Center](https://www.nhc.noaa.gov/prepare/hazards.php?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

---

## 9.58 Rip Currents

A rip current is a concentrated flow moving away from shore through the surf zone.

If caught:

- Remain calm
- Do not fight directly against the current
- Swim parallel to the beach when able
- Float or tread water if necessary
- Signal for help

The current generally pulls away from shore, not beneath the water.

The danger comes from exhaustion and panic.

---

# Weakening and Transition

## 9.59 Landfall

**Landfall** occurs when the center of a tropical cyclone crosses the coastline.

The strongest impacts may begin before landfall and continue afterward.

A storm can produce surge without the center making landfall nearby.

A center passing just offshore can still produce severe coastal effects.

---

## 9.60 Why Land Weakens a Storm

Over land, the cyclone loses access to warm ocean moisture.

Land also increases friction and disrupts circulation.

Mountains can tear apart the low-level center.

However, weakening may take time, especially when:

- The storm moves quickly
- Terrain is flat
- Soil is wet
- The circulation is large
- The center remains near warm water

Hurricane-force wind can sometimes penetrate well inland.

---

## 9.61 The Brown-Ocean Effect

In rare circumstances, a tropical cyclone may weaken more slowly over very wet, warm land or marshy regions.

Saturated ground can provide heat and moisture resembling some oceanic energy exchange.

This is sometimes called the **brown-ocean effect**.

It is unusual and does not mean land generally fuels hurricanes.

---

## 9.62 Extratropical Transition

As a tropical cyclone moves into higher latitudes, it may encounter:

- Colder water
- Strong wind shear
- Fronts
- Jet-stream disturbances
- Large temperature contrasts

It can transform into an **extratropical cyclone**.

During transition, the storm may:

- Lose its symmetric warm core
- Develop fronts
- Expand its wind field
- Accelerate
- Produce heavy rain and strong wind over a broad region

Losing tropical status does not mean losing danger.

It means the storm has changed machinery.

---

## 9.63 Remnant Lows

After deep thunderstorms disappear, the remaining circulation may be classified as a **remnant low**.

A remnant low may still produce:

- Gusty wind
- Heavy rain
- Rough surf
- Flooding

It may also redevelop if it returns over favorable warm water.

---

# Observation and Forecasting

## 9.64 Satellites

Satellites are essential because tropical cyclones spend much of their lives over data-sparse oceans.

Satellite imagery reveals:

- Cloud organization
- Eye formation
- Thunderstorm intensity
- Rainband structure
- Outflow
- Wind shear
- Dry-air intrusion
- Sea-surface conditions

Infrared imagery estimates cloud-top temperature.

Very cold cloud tops often indicate deep thunderstorms.

---

## 9.65 Scatterometers

A satellite scatterometer estimates ocean-surface wind by measuring microwave energy reflected from wind-roughened water.

It can help identify:

- Closed circulations
- Wind speed
- Wind direction
- Storm size
- Areas of tropical-storm-force wind

Heavy rain can complicate the measurement.

---

## 9.66 Hurricane Hunter Aircraft

Specially equipped aircraft fly into tropical cyclones to collect observations.

They measure:

- Pressure
- Temperature
- Humidity
- Wind
- Flight-level structure
- Storm-center location

They release **dropsondes**, instrument packages that descend through the storm and transmit atmospheric data.

These observations improve analyses and computer forecasts.

---

## 9.67 Stepped-Frequency Microwave Radiometer

Some reconnaissance aircraft carry instruments that estimate surface wind by observing microwave emissions from the ocean.

The measurements help map strong wind beneath the aircraft.

Heavy rain and sea conditions can affect interpretation, so the data is combined with other observations.

---

## 9.68 Radar Near Land

When a tropical cyclone approaches radar range, meteorologists can observe:

- Eye and eyewall
- Rainbands
- Motion
- Tornado-producing cells
- Wind velocity
- Eyewall replacement
- Rainfall distribution

Radar does not measure the entire hurricane at once, and the beam samples higher altitude farther from the site.

Aircraft and satellites remain important.

---

## 9.69 Buoys and Coastal Stations

Buoys, ships, weather stations, and tide gauges measure:

- Wind
- Pressure
- Waves
- Water level
- Temperature

Some instruments fail during extreme conditions.

A missing observation during landfall does not mean the wind conveniently stopped at the equipment rack.

---

## 9.70 Computer Models

Forecast models simulate the atmosphere and ocean.

Forecasters compare:

- Global models
- Regional hurricane models
- Ensemble systems
- Statistical guidance
- Ocean-coupled models

Different models may disagree because of uncertainties in:

- Initial storm structure
- Atmospheric steering
- Ocean conditions
- Wind shear
- Internal eyewall processes

The official forecast blends model guidance with human analysis.

---

## 9.71 Track Versus Intensity Forecasting

Track forecasts have improved substantially over decades.

Intensity remains more difficult because it depends on small-scale internal changes and ocean-atmosphere interactions.

A track may be forecast fairly well while rapid intensification remains uncertain.

That is why preparation must account for a range of plausible strength, not merely the current category.

---

# Watches, Warnings, and Hazard Products

## 9.72 Tropical Storm Watch

A **tropical storm watch** means tropical-storm conditions are possible within the specified area, generally within the established watch period.

Use the time to:

- Finish preparations
- Review evacuation plans
- Secure property
- Fuel vehicles
- Charge devices
- Monitor forecasts closely

---

## 9.73 Tropical Storm Warning

A **tropical storm warning** means tropical-storm conditions are expected within the warning area, generally within the established warning period.

Outdoor preparations should be completed before conditions deteriorate.

---

## 9.74 Hurricane Watch

A **hurricane watch** means hurricane conditions are possible in the specified area.

A watch is the signal to prepare for action, especially if evacuation may be necessary.

Do not wait for a warning before beginning a complicated evacuation.

---

## 9.75 Hurricane Warning

A **hurricane warning** means hurricane conditions are expected within the warned area.

Preparations should be completed quickly.

Follow evacuation instructions from local officials.

The warning area is defined using established coastal breakpoints and segments. [National Hurricane Center](https://www.nhc.noaa.gov/breakpoints/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

---

## 9.76 Storm Surge Watch and Warning

A **storm surge watch** means life-threatening inundation from rising water is possible.

A **storm surge warning** means life-threatening inundation is expected or occurring.

These products focus on water danger rather than wind category.

For people in coastal evacuation zones, they may be the most important alerts of the entire storm.

---

## 9.77 Extreme Wind Warning

An **extreme wind warning** may be issued when exceptionally dangerous eyewall wind is imminent or occurring.

Anyone in the warned area should immediately move into the strongest available shelter and stay away from windows.

This is not the time to watch the eye arrive from a porch.

The porch has not been briefed.

---

# Names and Seasonal Lists

## 9.78 Why Storms Receive Names

Names make it easier to:

- Communicate warnings
- Track multiple storms
- Remember individual events
- Reduce confusion
- Coordinate public information

Regional committees overseen through the World Meteorological Organization maintain naming lists. [World Meteorological Organization](https://community.wmo.int/site/knowledge-hub/programmes-and-initiatives/tropical-cyclone-programme-tcp/tropical-cyclone-naming?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

---

## 9.79 Retired Names

A storm name may be retired when a cyclone is exceptionally deadly or destructive.

The name is then replaced on future lists.

Retirement avoids reusing a name strongly associated with a historic disaster. [World Meteorological Organization](https://community.wmo.int/site/knowledge-hub/programmes-and-initiatives/tropical-cyclone-programme-tcp/tropical-cyclone-naming?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

A retired name is not an honor.

It is closer to a permanent entry in the atmosphere’s disciplinary file.

---

# Hurricane Preparation

## 9.80 Know Your Hazards

Your risk depends on location.

Ask:

- Am I in a storm-surge evacuation zone?
- Is my home vulnerable to river flooding?
- Are large trees near the house?
- Is the structure built for high wind?
- Could access roads flood?
- Do I depend on electrical medical equipment?
- How long could I manage without utilities?

Preparation should be hazard-specific.

Buying twelve flashlights does not solve storm surge.

---

## 9.81 Know Your Evacuation Zone

An evacuation zone is usually based mainly on storm-surge risk, not simply distance from the beach.

Know:

- Your zone
- Evacuation routes
- Shelter locations
- Transportation plan
- Destination
- Pet arrangements

Leave when local officials instruct you.

Do not choose a route that crosses low bridges or surge-prone roads unless it is part of the official plan.

---

## 9.82 Evacuate From Water, Shelter From Wind

A useful general principle is:

**Evacuate from storm surge and flooding. Shelter from wind in a sturdy structure.**

This does not replace local orders.

A poorly built or mobile home may also require evacuation from wind.

The right decision depends on building strength, flood exposure, and official guidance.

---

## 9.83 Supplies

A household supply kit may include:

- Water
- Shelf-stable food
- Medications
- Flashlights
- Batteries
- Weather radio
- Phone power banks
- First-aid supplies
- Hygiene items
- Cash
- Important documents
- Pet food and supplies
- Infant or elder-care items

Plan for outages and delayed access after the storm.

---

## 9.84 Water

Store enough safe drinking water for household members and pets.

Water systems may fail or become contaminated.

Containers should be:

- Clean
- Food-safe
- Sealed
- Stored away from chemicals

Follow local public-health guidance about quantity and storage time.

---

## 9.85 Medications and Medical Equipment

Before the storm:

- Refill prescriptions when possible
- Charge battery-powered equipment
- Arrange backup electricity
- Keep medication lists
- Protect refrigerated medicine
- Register for local assistance programs when available

Anyone dependent on electricity should consider evacuating early if reliable backup power is uncertain.

---

## 9.86 Documents

Protect copies of:

- Identification
- Insurance policies
- Medical information
- Property records
- Emergency contacts
- Pet vaccination records

Store them in waterproof containers and secure digital backups.

Photograph the home and major possessions before the storm.

Insurance companies are strangely unmoved by the argument, “Well, you should have seen the television.”

---

## 9.87 Vehicles

Before conditions deteriorate:

- Fill the fuel tank or charge the vehicle
- Move it away from flood-prone areas
- Avoid parking beneath trees
- Carry emergency supplies
- Know alternate routes

Electric vehicles should not be charged or stored where storm surge or deep flooding is expected.

Flood-damaged vehicles can contain electrical and fire hazards.

---

## 9.88 Securing the Home

Possible preparations include:

- Installing shutters
- Closing approved storm panels
- Reinforcing garage doors
- Bringing outdoor furniture inside
- Trimming hazardous branches in advance
- Clearing gutters and drains
- Securing propane tanks
- Moving valuables above likely flood level

Do not tape windows as a substitute for shutters or impact protection.

Tape may hold some broken pieces together but does not prevent the glass from breaking.

---

## 9.89 Generator Safety

Operate portable generators:

- Outdoors
- Far from doors
- Far from windows
- Far from vents
- Under dry, properly ventilated protection
- According to manufacturer instructions

Never use one in:

- House
- Garage
- Basement
- Shed
- Enclosed porch

Carbon monoxide is odorless and can kill quickly.

Do not connect a generator directly to household wiring without an approved transfer system.

---

# During the Storm

## 9.90 Shelter Location

During severe wind:

- Stay indoors
- Use an interior room
- Remain away from windows
- Close interior doors
- Keep shoes nearby
- Use helmets if structural failure is possible
- Monitor trusted alerts

The strongest room is often:

- Small
- Interior
- Windowless
- On a lower floor

But surge and flooding override the usual lower-floor advice.

Never shelter below expected water level.

---

## 9.91 During the Eye

If the eye passes overhead, conditions may become temporarily calm.

Do not go outside.

The opposite eyewall may arrive suddenly with wind from the reverse direction.

Debris loosened during the first half may become airborne during the second.

The eye is an intermission, not the end credits.

---

## 9.92 Floodwater

Do not walk, swim, or drive through floodwater.

It may contain:

- Strong current
- Sewage
- Chemicals
- Sharp debris
- Animals
- Electrical hazards
- Hidden holes
- Washed-out roads

Move to higher ground before escape routes close.

---

## 9.93 High-Rise Buildings

In a high-rise:

- Stay below the top floors when possible
- Move away from windows
- Use an interior hallway or room
- Expect stronger wind at elevation
- Avoid elevators during outages
- Prepare for water and power loss

Building management plans should be reviewed before storm conditions begin.

---

# After the Storm

## 9.94 Wait for Official Clearance

Do not return to an evacuated area until officials say it is safe.

Delays may result from:

- Flooded roads
- Downed power lines
- Gas leaks
- Fires
- Damaged bridges
- Unstable buildings
- Search-and-rescue operations

Returning early can place you and rescuers in additional danger.

---

## 9.95 Carbon Monoxide

After tropical cyclones, carbon-monoxide poisoning is a major preventable hazard.

Possible sources include:

- Generators
- Charcoal grills
- Camp stoves
- Vehicles idling near buildings
- Fuel-burning equipment

Use these only in properly ventilated outdoor locations.

A partially open garage door is not sufficient ventilation.

---

## 9.96 Electrical Hazards

Assume every downed wire is energized.

Stay away from:

- Wires
- Wet electrical equipment
- Flooded outlets
- Trees touching lines
- Vehicles touching lines

Have damaged systems inspected by qualified professionals.

Do not energize a flooded building until it has been declared safe.

---

## 9.97 Flooded Buildings

Before entering:

- Check for structural movement
- Smell for gas
- Watch for wildlife
- Wear protective footwear
- Avoid standing water near electricity
- Use flashlights rather than flames

Floodwater can weaken foundations and contaminate walls, insulation, furniture, and ventilation systems.

---

## 9.98 Heat After the Storm

Power outages often coincide with extreme heat and humidity.

Risks include:

- Heat exhaustion
- Heat stroke
- Dehydration
- Spoiled food
- Medication-storage problems

Check on neighbors, especially:

- Older adults
- Young children
- People with medical conditions
- Anyone living alone

The hurricane may have left, but the weather has not necessarily become friendly.

---

# Field Observation  
## Read a Tropical Cyclone Advisory

Choose an active or archived tropical cyclone advisory.

Identify:

- Center location
- Maximum sustained wind
- Central pressure
- Movement
- Watches and warnings
- Wind-field size
- Forecast track
- Key hazards

Then answer:

1. Is the storm’s center expected near land?
2. How far do tropical-storm-force winds extend?
3. Is surge expected?
4. Which locations face heavy rain?
5. Could hazards occur outside the cone?

This exercise teaches you to read the storm as a collection of hazards rather than one category number.

---

# Try It  
## Compare Two Storms of the Same Category

Choose two historical hurricanes with the same landfall category.

Compare:

- Size
- Forward speed
- Storm surge
- Rainfall
- Track
- Central pressure
- Damage pattern

You will likely find that equal category does not mean equal disaster.

The category number is useful, but it is not a biography.

---

# Try It  
## Make a Household Hurricane Decision Chart

Write three headings:

```text
STAY
EVACUATE
DECIDE EARLY
```

Under them, list your household’s conditions.

For example:

### Evacuate

- Mandatory order issued
- Storm-surge zone threatened
- Mobile home
- Medical power needs cannot be supported
- Only access road likely to flood

### Stay in sturdy shelter

- Outside flood and surge zones
- Strong building
- No evacuation order
- Supplies available
- Interior shelter area identified

### Decide early

- Pets require special arrangements
- Long drive to safety
- Elderly family member
- Bridges on route
- Limited fuel or transportation

Do this before a storm exists.

Decision-making works better when the wind is not already rearranging the patio.

---

# Weather Safety  
## The Five Hurricane Hazards

Never focus on wind alone.

The principal hazards are:

1. **Storm surge**
2. **Inland flooding**
3. **Destructive wind**
4. **Tornadoes**
5. **Dangerous surf and rip currents**

A sixth should be added after landfall:

**Carbon monoxide and electrical danger during recovery.**

---

# Chapter Summary

Tropical cyclones are rotating warm-core storms fueled primarily by warm ocean water, moisture, and latent heat.

The terms hurricane, typhoon, and tropical cyclone describe the same general storm type in different regions.

Development requires warm water, moisture, instability, a pre-existing disturbance, weak vertical wind shear, and enough Coriolis effect to support rotation.

Tropical cyclones progress from disturbances to depressions, tropical storms, and hurricanes or typhoons.

The eye is relatively calm, while the eyewall normally contains the strongest wind and heaviest rain.

Spiral rainbands can produce flooding, tornadoes, and damaging wind far from the center.

The Saffir–Simpson scale classifies wind only. It does not measure surge, rainfall, size, tornadoes, or overall destructive potential.

Storm surge is abnormal coastal water rise generated mainly by storm wind and modified by coastline shape, ocean depth, tide, storm size, speed, and approach angle.

Tropical cyclones can cause catastrophic inland flooding after their wind classification weakens.

The forecast cone represents uncertainty in the center’s track, not the full hazard area.

Watches mean prepare. Warnings mean complete action and follow official instructions.

Evacuate from storm surge and serious flood danger. Shelter from wind in a sturdy structure outside expected inundation.

The storm’s category is only one number.

The water, wind, rain, waves, tornadoes, and aftermath each deserve their own attention.

---

## Weather-Watcher’s Vocabulary

**Tropical cyclone:** A rotating warm-core low-pressure system developing over tropical or subtropical waters.  
**Hurricane:** A tropical cyclone of at least 74 mph in the Atlantic or eastern and central North Pacific.  
**Typhoon:** The regional name for a hurricane-strength tropical cyclone in the western North Pacific.  
**Tropical disturbance:** An organized area of tropical weather without a fully developed cyclone.  
**Tropical depression:** A tropical cyclone with maximum sustained wind of 38 mph or less under the U.S. classification.  
**Tropical storm:** A named tropical cyclone with sustained wind of 39–73 mph.  
**Major hurricane:** A Category 3, 4, or 5 hurricane.  
**Eye:** The relatively calm center of a strong tropical cyclone.  
**Eyewall:** The ring of intense thunderstorms surrounding the eye.  
**Rainband:** A curved band of showers and thunderstorms orbiting the center.  
**Central dense overcast:** A thick shield of deep clouds near the cyclone’s center.  
**Warm core:** A storm structure whose central atmospheric column is warmer than its surroundings.  
**Rapid intensification:** A large increase in tropical-cyclone wind over a short period.  
**Eyewall replacement cycle:** The process in which an outer eyewall replaces an inner one.  
**Landfall:** The crossing of a coastline by the cyclone’s center.  
**Storm surge:** Abnormal storm-driven rise of seawater above normal tide level.  
**Storm tide:** The combined height of astronomical tide and storm surge.  
**Recurvature:** A turn from westward motion toward the pole and then eastward.  
**Forecast cone:** The probable region for the future center track based on historical forecast error.  
**Extratropical transition:** Transformation of a tropical cyclone into a frontal midlatitude cyclone.  
**Potential tropical cyclone:** A disturbance expected to threaten land before becoming a formal tropical cyclone.  
**Hurricane watch:** Hurricane conditions are possible.  
**Hurricane warning:** Hurricane conditions are expected.  
**Storm surge warning:** Life-threatening coastal inundation is expected or occurring.

**Next: Chapter 10 — Winter Weather: Snow, Sleet, Freezing Rain, Blizzards, and Dangerous Cold**

<!-- End of source chapter -->

---

# Chapter 10  
## Winter Weather: Snow, Sleet, Freezing Rain, Blizzards, and Dangerous Cold

Winter weather is not simply rain that became ambitious.

A winter storm is a three-dimensional puzzle involving moisture, rising air, and temperature layers that may be only a few hundred feet thick. The temperature at your doorstep tells only the final part of the story. A snowflake may fall through several miles of atmosphere, melt into a raindrop, partly refreeze, or remain liquid below freezing before it reaches the ground.

That journey determines whether you receive soft snow, bouncing sleet, invisible black ice, or a glaze heavy enough to bring down trees and power lines.

---

## 10.1 The Three Ingredients

Most winter storms require:

- Cold air
- Moisture
- Lift

### Cold air

At least part of the atmosphere must be cold enough to form and preserve ice crystals.

The surface does not always need to be below freezing for snow to reach the ground.

### Moisture

Moisture may arrive from:

- Oceans
- Lakes
- The Gulf of Mexico
- Unfrozen soil
- Atmospheric rivers
- Evaporation beneath the cloud

### Lift

Air must rise and cool.

Common lifting mechanisms include:

- Fronts
- Low-pressure systems
- Mountains
- Upper-level disturbances
- Convergence
- Lake-effect circulations

```text
COLD AIR + MOISTURE + LIFT
              ↓
        WINTER WEATHER
```

The same basic storm ingredients that make rain also make snow. Winter merely turns the temperature profile into a fussy little gatekeeper.

---

## 10.2 Most Precipitation Begins as Ice

In many middle-latitude clouds, precipitation begins as snow or ice crystals even when rain eventually reaches the ground.

High in the cloud:

1. Ice crystals grow.
2. They join into snowflakes.
3. They begin falling.
4. Their fate depends on the temperature layers below.

A snowflake may:

- Remain frozen and reach the ground as snow
- Melt and become rain
- Melt and refreeze into sleet
- Melt and remain supercooled until it freezes on contact

The vertical temperature profile often determines precipitation type more than the temperature measured at ground level. [National Weather Service](https://www.weather.gov/source/zhu/ZHU_Training_Page/winter_stuff/winter_wx/winter_wx.html?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

---

## 10.3 Reading a Winter Temperature Profile

Meteorologists examine the temperature from the cloud to the ground using:

- Weather balloons
- Aircraft observations
- Radar
- Surface stations
- Computer models
- Satellite data

A graph of temperature with altitude is often called a **sounding**.

The freezing line—32°F or 0°C—is especially important.

### All layers below freezing

Snow can remain frozen.

### Warm layer above freezing aloft

Snowflakes may melt.

### Deep cold layer below the warm layer

Melted drops may refreeze into sleet.

### Shallow cold layer near the surface

Drops may remain liquid and freeze only after touching something, producing freezing rain.

### Illustration 1: Four precipitation profiles

```text
SNOW

cloud
  ❄
  ❄      cold
  ❄      cold
  ❄      cold
ground   cold


SLEET

cloud
  ❄      cold
  ↓
  💧     warm layer: snow melts
  ↓
  ●      deep cold layer: drop refreezes
ground   ice pellets


FREEZING RAIN

cloud
  ❄      cold
  ↓
  💧     deep warm layer: complete melting
  ↓
  💧     shallow cold layer
ground   freezes on contact


RAIN

cloud
  ❄
  ↓
  💧     warm air
  💧     warm air
ground   rain
```

A change of only a few degrees—or a modest shift in the depth of one atmospheric layer—can turn a snow forecast into an ice storm.

---

# Snow

## 10.4 How Snow Crystals Begin

Snow crystals usually form when water vapor deposits directly onto microscopic ice-forming particles.

These **ice nuclei** may include:

- Mineral dust
- Biological particles
- Clay
- Certain aerosols

The crystal grows as additional water vapor attaches to it.

Because ice has a hexagonal molecular structure, snow crystals develop sixfold symmetry.

Every branch experiences broadly similar atmospheric conditions as it grows, which is why the arms resemble one another.

---

## 10.5 Why Snowflakes Have Different Shapes

Snow-crystal form depends largely on:

- Temperature
- Humidity
- Time spent in each cloud layer
- Collisions
- Turbulence

Common forms include:

- Plates
- Stellar plates
- Columns
- Needles
- Dendrites
- Capped columns
- Bullet rosettes
- Irregular crystals

### Illustration 2: Snow-crystal families

```text
PLATE          COLUMN         NEEDLE

  ✳             ▯              ─


DENDRITE       CAPPED COLUMN   ROSETTE

  ❄             ◉─◉            ✣
```

The elaborate branching snowflakes seen in photographs generally grow in a favorable combination of temperature and high moisture.

Cloud conditions can change several times during a crystal’s descent, producing complicated or irregular shapes.

---

## 10.6 A Snowflake Is Usually a Collection

The word **snowflake** often refers to several individual snow crystals joined together.

As crystals fall, they collide and stick.

This process is called **aggregation**.

Aggregation is favored when temperatures are near freezing because the crystals may be slightly wet or sticky.

Large, feathery flakes are therefore common during relatively mild snowfalls.

Very cold snow often consists of smaller, drier crystals that do not join as readily.

---

## 10.7 Riming

Snow crystals sometimes collide with supercooled cloud droplets.

The droplets freeze onto the crystal.

This process is called **riming**.

Light riming softens the crystal’s delicate outline.

Heavy riming can transform it into a rounded white pellet called **graupel**.

```text
snow crystal + supercooled droplets
             ↓
           riming
             ↓
           graupel
```

---

## 10.8 Graupel

**Graupel** consists of soft, opaque pellets formed when supercooled droplets freeze onto snow crystals.

It is sometimes called:

- Snow pellets
- Soft hail
- Tapioca snow

Graupel usually:

- Bounces lightly
- Crushes easily
- Appears white and opaque
- Falls during convective winter showers

It is not sleet.

Sleet forms from melted and refrozen precipitation. Graupel grows through riming inside a cloud.

Graupel has the texture of a snowflake wearing body armor, but not especially good body armor.

---

## 10.9 Snowfall Near or Above Freezing

Snow can fall when the surface temperature is above 32°F.

This may happen when:

- The layer of above-freezing air is shallow
- Snowfall is heavy
- The air is dry
- Melting and evaporation cool the air
- Ground temperatures remain cold
- Large flakes fall quickly

Snow may melt on roads while accumulating on grass, vehicles, roofs, or elevated surfaces.

The official air temperature is usually measured several feet above the ground. Individual surfaces may be colder or warmer.

---

## 10.10 Wet-Bulb Cooling

When precipitation falls into dry air, some evaporates or sublimates.

These processes absorb heat and cool the air.

The temperature may fall toward the **wet-bulb temperature**.

This means a location slightly above freezing can cool to freezing after precipitation begins.

The effect can:

- Change rain to snow
- Allow accumulation sooner than expected
- Strengthen surface cold air
- Create unexpected icing

Winter storms have a rude habit of manufacturing some of their own cold.

---

## 10.11 Snow-to-Liquid Ratio

Snowfall depth is often compared with the amount of liquid water it contains.

A common shorthand is:

```text
10 inches of snow
≈
1 inch of liquid water
```

This **10-to-1 ratio** is only a rough average.

Actual ratios vary widely.

### Wet snow

May have ratios such as:

- 5:1
- 8:1
- 10:1

### Dry, fluffy snow

May have ratios such as:

- 15:1
- 20:1
- Higher in very favorable cold conditions

The ratio depends on:

- Crystal shape
- Cloud temperature
- Wind
- Riming
- Compaction
- Melting near the surface

One inch of rainfall does not automatically become ten inches of snow. The atmosphere has not agreed to that conversion rate.

---

## 10.12 Wet Snow

Wet snow falls near freezing and contains relatively high liquid-water content.

It tends to:

- Stick to trees and power lines
- Pack easily
- Become heavy
- Compact rapidly
- Produce slush
- Strain roofs and structures

Wet snow is excellent for snowballs and significantly less charming when attached to every pine branch above the electrical grid.

---

## 10.13 Dry Snow

Dry snow usually falls at lower temperatures.

It tends to be:

- Light
- Powdery
- Easily blown
- Less adhesive
- More prone to drifting

Dry snow can greatly reduce visibility in strong wind even after snowfall has ended.

It also compacts less initially, allowing deeper accumulation from the same liquid-water amount.

---

## 10.14 Snow Compaction

Fresh snow settles over time.

Compaction occurs through:

- Gravity
- Wind
- Partial melting
- Crystal breakdown
- Additional snow weight

A storm may deposit ten inches, yet the measured depth may later decrease without much melting.

Snowfall is the amount that fell during a period.

Snow depth is the amount remaining on the ground at a given time.

These are not always equal.

---

## 10.15 Measuring Snowfall

A proper snowfall measurement is generally made on a flat surface away from:

- Buildings
- Trees
- Drifts
- Bare wind-scoured areas
- Heated surfaces

Observers take several measurements and average them.

Snow may be cleared from a measuring board at prescribed intervals depending on the observation standard.

Measuring only the deepest drift will impress your friends and disappoint every meteorologist involved.

---

## 10.16 Snow Depth and Snowfall

Suppose:

- Six inches falls in the morning.
- It compacts to four inches.
- Four more inches falls in the afternoon.

Total snowfall may be ten inches.

Final snow depth may be eight inches or less.

This is why reports must distinguish between:

- New snowfall
- Storm-total snowfall
- Current snow depth
- Snow water equivalent

---

## 10.17 Snow Water Equivalent

**Snow water equivalent**, or SWE, is the depth of water that would result if the snowpack melted.

SWE matters for:

- Water supply
- Flood forecasting
- Reservoir management
- Agriculture
- Avalanche forecasting
- Roof loading

Two snowpacks of equal depth can contain very different amounts of water.

---

# Sleet

## 10.18 What Is Sleet?

In American weather terminology, **sleet** consists of small ice pellets.

It typically forms when:

1. Snow falls into a layer above freezing.
2. The snow partly or completely melts.
3. The drops enter a sufficiently deep cold layer near the ground.
4. They refreeze before landing.

Sleet usually bounces when it strikes a hard surface. [National Weather Service](https://www.weather.gov/rnk/measure_icing?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

```text
SNOW
  ↓
WARM LAYER
snow melts
  ↓
DEEP COLD LAYER
drops refreeze
  ↓
SLEET
```

---

## 10.19 Why Sleet Accumulates

Sleet pellets can accumulate like snow.

A heavy sleet event may cover roads with a dense, granular layer.

Unlike freezing rain, sleet does not normally coat every object in a smooth glaze.

But it can make:

- Walking difficult
- Roads slippery
- Snow removal harder
- Travel noisy and unpleasant

A deep sleet layer can be surprisingly heavy and may take considerable time to melt.

---

## 10.20 Sleet Versus Hail

Sleet and hail are both ice, but they form differently.

### Sleet

- Associated with winter temperature layers
- Usually small
- Forms from melted precipitation refreezing
- Often falls from layered clouds

### Hail

- Forms in thunderstorm updrafts
- Can become very large
- Grows by collecting supercooled water
- Commonly falls during warmer seasons

Calling sleet “tiny hail” is understandable, but meteorologically it is the wrong tiny frozen nuisance.

---

## 10.21 International Terminology

Outside the United States, the word **sleet** may be used differently, sometimes describing a rain-and-snow mixture.

For international forecasts, pay attention to the official local terminology rather than assuming the American definition.

A forecast translation is not the ideal place for a surprise vocabulary lesson.

---

# Freezing Rain

## 10.22 What Is Freezing Rain?

**Freezing rain** is liquid precipitation that freezes when it strikes a surface at or below freezing.

It usually forms when:

1. Snow develops aloft.
2. The snow completely melts in a warm layer.
3. Drops pass through a shallow subfreezing layer near the surface.
4. They remain liquid until contact.
5. They freeze onto roads, trees, wires, and structures.

The surface cold layer is too shallow for the drops to refreeze into sleet before reaching the ground. [National Weather Service](https://www.weather.gov/rnk/measure_icing?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

---

## 10.23 Supercooled Raindrops

Water can remain liquid below its normal freezing point when it lacks a suitable surface on which to freeze.

This is called **supercooling**.

When a supercooled drop strikes:

- A road
- Branch
- Wire
- Vehicle
- Aircraft
- Railing

it can freeze almost immediately.

The result is a smooth layer of glaze ice.

---

## 10.24 Glaze Ice

**Glaze** is the clear or translucent ice coating produced by freezing rain or freezing drizzle.

It can be difficult to see.

A road may appear merely wet while actually coated in ice.

This is one source of **black ice**.

The term black ice does not mean the ice itself is black. It is transparent enough that the dark road remains visible beneath it.

The road looks normal, which is rather underhanded.

---

## 10.25 Why Bridges Freeze First

Bridges and overpasses lose heat from:

- The top
- The bottom
- Both sides

Ordinary roads receive some heat from the ground below.

Elevated road surfaces can therefore cool more quickly and freeze sooner.

Other early-icing locations include:

- Shaded roads
- North-facing slopes
- Ramps
- Untreated side streets
- Areas near water
- Elevated walkways

The warning signs saying “Bridge Ices Before Road” are not decorative regional folklore.

---

## 10.26 Freezing Drizzle

**Freezing drizzle** consists of tiny supercooled droplets that freeze on contact.

Unlike much freezing rain, it may develop in shallow clouds without a classic ice-crystal process.

Freezing drizzle can produce:

- Thin glaze
- Dangerous roads
- Aircraft icing
- Slick sidewalks
- Poor visibility

Because it may look insignificant, people often underestimate it.

A barely visible coating can be enough to make pavement nearly frictionless.

---

## 10.27 Ice Accretion

**Ice accretion** is the amount of ice that builds on an exposed surface.

It is not always equal to the liquid precipitation amount.

Accretion depends on:

- Surface temperature
- Air temperature
- Wind
- Precipitation rate
- Drop size
- Object shape
- Sunlight
- Runoff before freezing

Strong wind may increase ice buildup on the windward side of objects while also causing some drops to miss or run off.

---

## 10.28 Why Small Ice Amounts Matter

Even a thin glaze can make roads and sidewalks treacherous.

Greater accumulations can:

- Bend branches
- Break trees
- Bring down utility lines
- Damage communication systems
- Collapse vulnerable structures
- Block roads

The National Weather Service describes an ice storm as a damaging freezing-rain event, with significant ice accretion often around one-quarter inch or more, though warning criteria can vary locally. [National Weather Service](https://www.weather.gov/apx/winterdefinitions?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

---

## 10.29 Ice Load on Trees and Wires

Ice adds weight to every exposed surface.

A cylindrical wire or branch becomes coated on all sides.

Wind then increases the mechanical stress.

Damage is worse when:

- Trees still have leaves
- Ice is thick
- Wind is strong
- Soil is saturated
- Trees are already weakened
- Ice persists for many hours

A tree may survive the initial icing and fail later when wind rises.

---

## 10.30 Power Outages

Ice storms can cause widespread outages because:

- Ice loads wires
- Branches fall onto lines
- Utility poles break
- Roads block repair crews
- Cold slows restoration
- Damage covers a large area

Prepare for outages before precipitation begins.

Once roads glaze, retrieving supplies becomes a deeply unnecessary sequel.

---

# Mixed Precipitation

## 10.31 Wintry Mix

A **wintry mix** may contain some combination of:

- Snow
- Sleet
- Freezing rain
- Rain

The type may change repeatedly as temperature layers shift.

One neighborhood may receive snow while another ten miles away receives freezing rain.

Elevation can also matter.

A hilltop may sit above a shallow cold layer and receive rain while a nearby valley remains below freezing and ices.

---

## 10.32 The Rain–Snow Line

The rain–snow boundary is rarely a perfectly sharp line.

Near it, precipitation may alternate between:

- Rain
- Wet snow
- Melting flakes
- Sleet
- Mixed rain and snow

Heavy precipitation can cool the atmosphere and shift the snow line.

Warm ground may also delay accumulation even while snow falls heavily.

---

## 10.33 Warm Nose

Meteorologists sometimes call the above-freezing layer aloft a **warm nose** because it protrudes into the otherwise cold temperature profile.

Its depth and warmth determine whether falling snow:

- Does not melt
- Partly melts
- Completely melts

Below it, the depth of the cold layer determines whether the result is sleet or freezing rain.

### Illustration 3: Warm nose

```text
ALTITUDE
   ↑

cold air       |
               |\
freezing line  | \____
               |      \  ← warm nose
               |       \
cold surface   |        \____
               +---------------- temperature
```

---

## 10.34 Cold-Air Damming

**Cold-air damming** occurs when shallow, dense cold air becomes trapped against a mountain range.

In the eastern United States, cold air can become wedged along the eastern side of the Appalachian Mountains.

Warm, moist air may move above it.

This setup can produce:

- Freezing rain
- Sleet
- Low clouds
- Fog
- Persistent cold temperatures

Computer models may erode shallow cold air too quickly, making these forecasts particularly difficult.

Cold air, once wedged into a valley or against a mountain, can be magnificently stubborn.

---

## 10.35 Shallow Arctic Air

Arctic fronts can produce a thin but very cold surface layer.

Warm air may flow over it while surface temperatures remain below freezing.

This can create long corridors of:

- Freezing rain
- Sleet
- Icy roads
- Rapid temperature drops

The cold layer may be only a fraction of the total atmospheric depth, yet it determines what happens at ground level.

---

# Major Winter Storm Systems

## 10.36 Midlatitude Cyclones

Many major winter storms are midlatitude cyclones.

They bring together:

- Cold air to the north and west
- Warm moist air to the south and east
- Strong upper-level winds
- Fronts
- Rising motion
- Widespread precipitation

A single cyclone may produce:

- Rain in the warm sector
- Severe thunderstorms farther south
- Freezing rain near the warm front
- Heavy snow northwest of the low
- Blizzard conditions behind it

One storm can distribute several seasons with impressive administrative efficiency.

---

## 10.37 The Typical Snow Zone

In a classic Northern Hemisphere cyclone, heavy snow often falls north and northwest of the surface low.

There:

- Air remains cold
- Moisture wraps around the low
- Strong lift develops
- Winds may intensify
- Snow bands may form

The exact location depends on the storm track and atmospheric temperature profile.

A shift of 50 miles can dramatically change snowfall totals.

---

## 10.38 The Deformation Zone

The **deformation zone** is a region where air currents stretch and reshape the precipitation field.

It often develops northwest of a mature low.

Persistent lift can produce a broad band of heavy snow.

Satellite and radar imagery may show a curved or comma-shaped precipitation shield.

The deformation band can remain over one area for hours even while other parts of the storm move rapidly.

---

## 10.39 Frontogenesis and Snow Bands

Strong temperature contrasts can intensify into a narrow zone through **frontogenesis**.

Air responds by rising on one side and sinking on the other.

This may produce narrow, intense snow bands with:

- Very high snowfall rates
- Sudden whiteout conditions
- Sharp edges
- Thunder
- Large differences over short distances

A town beneath the band may receive a foot of snow while another nearby gets only a few inches.

Winter storms occasionally forecast with a ruler and then verify with a knife.

---

## 10.40 Nor’easters

A **nor’easter** is a strong cyclone affecting the East Coast of North America.

It commonly brings northeasterly coastal wind ahead of the low.

Possible hazards include:

- Heavy snow
- Rain
- Coastal flooding
- Large waves
- Beach erosion
- Strong wind
- Mixed precipitation

A small shift east or west can determine whether a major city receives heavy snow, cold rain, or a messy transition between both.

---

## 10.41 Alberta Clippers

An **Alberta clipper** is a fast-moving system that travels southeastward from western Canada.

Clippers often bring:

- Light to moderate powdery snow
- Strong northwest wind
- Rapid temperature falls
- Blowing snow
- Dangerous wind chill

They usually carry less moisture than storms drawing directly from the Gulf or Atlantic.

Their speed can make a modest snowfall unusually disruptive.

---

## 10.42 Colorado and Panhandle Lows

Storms developing near the Rockies or southern High Plains may move northeast across the central United States.

They can draw warm Gulf moisture over cold air and produce:

- Heavy snow
- Blizzards
- Ice
- Severe thunderstorms
- Strong wind

The northwest side may face blizzard conditions while the southeast side faces tornadoes.

That is not exaggeration. It is simply the same cyclone being overqualified.

---

## 10.43 Miller-Type Coastal Storms

Forecasters sometimes classify East Coast winter cyclones using broad development patterns.

One type travels from the Gulf or Southeast toward the coast.

Another involves an inland low weakening while a new low develops near the coast.

These patterns can affect:

- Snowfall placement
- Coastal wind
- Precipitation changes
- Timing
- Dry slots

The classifications are useful frameworks, not rigid rules.

---

# Lake-Effect Snow

## 10.44 How Lake-Effect Snow Forms

Lake-effect snow develops when cold air moves over relatively warm, unfrozen water.

The air gains:

- Heat
- Moisture
- Instability

As it reaches the downwind shore, it rises and forms snow showers or narrow snow bands.

```text
COLD AIR → → → → → →

      warm lake
~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~
heat and moisture rise
       ↑ ↑ ↑ ↑

                  intense snow
                     ❄❄❄
                downwind shore
```

---

## 10.45 The Temperature Difference

Lake-effect becomes more likely when the air several thousand feet above the surface is much colder than the lake.

The larger temperature contrast increases instability.

Other important factors include:

- Wind direction
- Wind speed
- Moisture depth
- Lake temperature
- Ice cover
- Terrain
- Length of travel over water

---

## 10.46 Fetch

**Fetch** is the distance wind travels over open water.

A longer fetch allows the air to collect more heat and moisture.

A subtle wind-direction change can shift a narrow snow band from one community to another.

This produces dramatic differences in snowfall over only a few miles.

---

## 10.47 Single and Multiple Bands

Strong directional organization may produce one dominant lake-effect band.

Less organized flow may produce several parallel bands.

A single band can remain nearly stationary and generate enormous local totals.

Outside it, skies may be bright and snowfall light.

This is one of weather’s more theatrical uses of selective attention.

---

## 10.48 Lake-Effect Thundersnow

Strong lake-effect convection can produce:

- Lightning
- Thunder
- Graupel
- Very heavy snow
- Rapidly changing visibility

This is **thundersnow**.

Thunder may be muffled because snow absorbs and scatters sound.

Lightning during snow is uncommon but not mysterious; it develops through vigorous convective charge separation much like lightning in warm-season storms.

---

## 10.49 Lake Enhancement

A larger winter storm may draw extra moisture and instability from a lake.

This is called **lake enhancement**.

The resulting snow is partly synoptic storm precipitation and partly intensified by the lake.

Totals near the shore or downwind highlands may exceed surrounding areas.

---

# Mountain Snow

## 10.50 Orographic Lift

When moist air flows toward mountains, it is forced upward.

The air cools, condenses, and may produce heavy snow on the windward slopes.

Mountain snowfall depends on:

- Wind direction
- Moisture
- Terrain height
- Atmospheric stability
- Snow level
- Storm duration

A storm may produce little snow in a valley but several feet at higher elevations.

---

## 10.51 Snow Level

The **snow level** is the approximate elevation where precipitation changes from mainly rain below to mainly snow above.

It is not identical to the freezing level.

Snow can survive some distance below the altitude where temperature first rises above freezing.

Heavy precipitation and dry air may lower the snow level through melting and evaporative cooling.

---

## 10.52 Upslope Snow

Cold moist air flowing uphill can produce widespread clouds and snow.

Upslope snow may occur even when the main low-pressure center is far away.

It can affect:

- Mountain foothills
- High plains
- Elevated plateaus

The snow may persist as long as the low-level flow remains directed toward the terrain.

---

## 10.53 Snow Squalls

A **snow squall** is a brief, intense burst of snow accompanied by gusty wind.

It may produce:

- Sudden whiteout
- Rapid road icing
- Sharp temperature drop
- Near-zero visibility
- Multi-vehicle crashes

Accumulation may be modest, but the abrupt change makes squalls extremely dangerous.

A clear road can become nearly impassable within minutes.

---

## 10.54 Ground Blizzard

A **ground blizzard** occurs when strong wind lifts snow that has already fallen.

New snow may be minimal or absent.

The blowing snow can reduce visibility to near zero.

Cold, dry powder is particularly easy to loft.

This is why a storm can end while blizzard conditions continue—or even begin afterward.

---

# Blizzards

## 10.55 What Is a Blizzard?

A blizzard is defined by wind and visibility, not by snowfall total.

Under the standard U.S. definition, blizzard conditions involve:

- Sustained wind or frequent gusts of at least 35 mph
- Considerable falling or blowing snow
- Visibility frequently reduced to one-quarter mile or less
- Conditions lasting at least three hours [National Weather Service](https://www.weather.gov/source/zhu/ZHU_Training_Page/winter_stuff/winter_wx/winter_glossary.html?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

Heavy snowfall and extreme cold often accompany blizzards, but neither is required. [NOAA National Severe Storms Laboratory](https://www.nssl.noaa.gov/education/svrwx101/winter/types/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

---

## 10.56 Whiteout

A **whiteout** is a condition in which contrast and visual references nearly disappear.

The ground and sky blend together.

People may be unable to judge:

- Distance
- Direction
- Horizon
- Slope
- Road edge

Whiteouts may occur during:

- Heavy falling snow
- Blowing snow
- Fog over snow
- Flat diffuse lighting

A person can become disoriented only a short distance from shelter.

---

## 10.57 Drifting Snow

Wind redistributes snow into uneven piles called **drifts**.

Drifts form around:

- Buildings
- Fences
- Trees
- Road cuts
- Vehicles
- Terrain changes

One area may be swept nearly bare while another accumulates several feet.

Drifting can repeatedly close a road after it has been plowed.

---

## 10.58 Why Driving Becomes Impossible

Blizzards combine several hazards:

- Low visibility
- Snow-covered roads
- Drifting
- Strong crosswinds
- Rapidly changing road edges
- Extreme cold
- Vehicle breakdown risk

Even experienced drivers can lose the road.

Emergency crews may be unable to reach stranded motorists.

The correct blizzard driving technique is often not driving.

---

# Cold Waves

## 10.59 What Is a Cold Wave?

A **cold wave** is a rapid and significant drop in temperature that creates hazardous conditions.

The definition varies by region because people, buildings, agriculture, and infrastructure are adapted to different climates.

A temperature that is routine in Minnesota may be highly disruptive in South Carolina.

Impacts matter as much as the number.

---

## 10.60 Arctic High Pressure

Cold outbreaks often occur when strong Arctic or polar high pressure moves south.

The dense cold air spreads near the surface.

Clear skies and light winds near the center of the high can allow intense nighttime cooling.

Along the high’s edges, pressure gradients may produce biting wind and dangerous wind chill.

---

## 10.61 Radiational Cooling

On clear, calm nights, the ground loses heat rapidly through infrared radiation.

Snow cover strengthens the cooling because it:

- Reflects daytime sunlight
- Insulates the ground’s stored heat
- Emits infrared energy efficiently
- Supports cold surface inversions

Low valleys may become much colder than nearby slopes as dense air drains downhill.

---

## 10.62 Wind Chill

Wind chill estimates how cold conditions feel to exposed human skin because wind increases heat loss.

It combines:

- Air temperature
- Wind speed

Wind does not reduce an object below the actual air temperature, but it can make the object cool more quickly.

The wind-chill index is intended to describe risk to people and animals, particularly frostbite and hypothermia. [National Weather Service](https://www.weather.gov/bou/windchill?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

---

## 10.63 Wind-Chill Example

Suppose:

- Air temperature: 0°F
- Wind: 15 mph

The wind chill is approximately −19°F.

At that combination, exposed skin can freeze in roughly 30 minutes. [National Weather Service](https://www.weather.gov/safety/cold-wind-chill-chart?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

Risk increases as:

- Temperature decreases
- Wind speed rises
- Exposure lengthens
- Skin becomes wet
- Clothing is inadequate

Wind-chill charts provide estimates, not personal immunity timers.

Do not schedule 29 minutes outside because the chart says frostbite at 30.

---

# Frostbite

## 10.64 What Is Frostbite?

**Frostbite** occurs when skin and underlying tissue freeze.

Commonly affected areas include:

- Fingers
- Toes
- Nose
- Ears
- Cheeks
- Chin

Early signs may include:

- Numbness
- Tingling
- Pale or waxy skin
- Firmness
- Loss of coordination

Severe frostbite can cause permanent tissue damage.

---

## 10.65 What to Do

Move the person into warmth.

Seek medical help.

Remove wet or constricting clothing and jewelry when practical.

Do not:

- Rub the area
- Massage it
- Apply direct fire
- Use very hot water
- Walk on frostbitten feet unless necessary for safety

Rewarming should be controlled.

Do not thaw tissue if there is a serious risk it will freeze again, because refreezing can cause greater damage.

---

# Hypothermia

## 10.66 What Is Hypothermia?

**Hypothermia** occurs when the body loses heat faster than it can produce it and core temperature falls dangerously low.

It can occur:

- In extreme cold
- In cool rain
- In strong wind
- After immersion in cold water
- Indoors without heat
- At temperatures well above freezing

The National Weather Service identifies warning signs including uncontrolled shivering, confusion, memory loss, slurred speech, drowsiness, and exhaustion. [National Weather Service](https://www.weather.gov/safety/cold-faqs?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

---

## 10.67 Warning Signs

Possible signs include:

- Intense shivering
- Confusion
- Poor judgment
- Clumsiness
- Slurred speech
- Drowsiness
- Slow breathing
- Weak pulse
- Shivering that later stops

A person with hypothermia may not recognize the danger.

Confusion is part of the illness, which makes self-assessment unreliable.

---

## 10.68 What to Do

Call emergency services.

Move the person to shelter.

Handle them gently.

Remove wet clothing and replace it with dry layers.

Warm the body’s core using:

- Blankets
- Dry clothing
- Shared body warmth
- Warm packs placed around the chest, neck, and groin

Do not apply intense direct heat to arms and legs.

Do not give alcohol.

If the person is fully alert and able to swallow, warm nonalcoholic drinks may help while professional care is arranged.

---

# Dressing for Cold

## 10.69 Layering

A useful cold-weather clothing system includes:

### Base layer

Moves moisture away from skin.

### Insulating layer

Traps warm air.

### Outer layer

Blocks wind and precipitation.

Several adjustable layers usually work better than one extremely heavy garment.

---

## 10.70 Avoid Cotton When Wet

Cotton absorbs moisture and loses much of its insulating ability when wet.

For prolonged outdoor cold, better materials include:

- Wool
- Synthetic fleece
- Moisture-wicking fabrics
- Insulated outerwear

Cotton can be comfortable in dry, mild conditions. It becomes a questionable colleague once sweat, snow, or rain joins the project.

---

## 10.71 Cover Extremities

Protect:

- Head
- Ears
- Face
- Hands
- Feet

Mittens often keep hands warmer than gloves because fingers share heat.

Footwear should be:

- Insulated
- Dry
- Not overly tight
- Large enough for warm socks

Tight clothing can reduce circulation and increase cold injury risk.

---

## 10.72 Staying Dry

Moisture accelerates heat loss.

Prevent sweating by:

- Removing layers before strenuous activity
- Opening ventilation
- Slowing the pace
- Changing damp clothing
- Keeping snow out of boots and cuffs

Being slightly cool at the beginning of activity may be safer than becoming soaked in sweat.

---

# Winter Travel

## 10.73 Before Driving

Check:

- Forecast
- Road conditions
- Warnings
- Fuel or battery charge
- Tire condition
- Windshield fluid
- Vehicle lights
- Emergency supplies

Tell someone:

- Your route
- Departure time
- Expected arrival

Postpone travel when officials advise it.

No appointment becomes more important after your vehicle enters a ditch.

---

## 10.74 Vehicle Emergency Kit

A winter vehicle kit may include:

- Blankets
- Warm clothing
- Water
- Food
- Flashlight
- Phone charger
- First-aid supplies
- Ice scraper
- Small shovel
- Traction material
- Jumper cables or battery pack
- Reflective markers
- Necessary medication

Store items where they remain accessible if the trunk is blocked or frozen shut.

---

## 10.75 Driving on Snow

On snow-covered roads:

- Slow down gradually
- Increase following distance
- Avoid sudden steering
- Brake gently
- Accelerate smoothly
- Expect bridges to be worse
- Avoid cruise control
- Clear the entire vehicle of snow

Snow flying from a roof can blind another driver or become dangerous debris.

---

## 10.76 Driving on Ice

Ice may provide almost no useful traction.

Four-wheel drive can help a vehicle move forward.

It does not create superior braking or cornering.

If conditions are icy:

- Reduce speed drastically
- Make no sudden inputs
- Leave extreme stopping distance
- Avoid hills when possible
