- Do not assume treated roads are safe

The best vehicle for freezing rain is often the one parked at home.

---

## 10.77 If the Vehicle Skids

Remain calm.

Look where you want to go.

Ease off the accelerator.

Avoid slamming the brakes.

Steer gently in the direction needed to regain alignment.

Electronic stability systems help, but they cannot overcome the laws of friction, which are famously resistant to negotiation.

---

## 10.78 If Stranded

Usually remain with the vehicle unless safe shelter is very close and clearly reachable.

The vehicle provides:

- Shelter
- Visibility to rescuers
- Protection from wind
- Stored supplies

Call for help and share your location.

Run the engine only intermittently if safe.

Keep the exhaust pipe clear of snow to reduce carbon-monoxide risk.

Crack a window slightly when the engine runs.

Do not let snow bury the vehicle completely.

---

# Home Winter Safety

## 10.79 Heating

Have heating systems inspected and maintained.

Use only equipment intended for indoor heating.

Never heat the home using:

- Charcoal grill
- Camp stove
- Outdoor heater
- Gas oven
- Running vehicle
- Generator indoors

These can produce deadly carbon monoxide.

---

## 10.80 Carbon Monoxide

Carbon monoxide is:

- Colorless
- Odorless
- Potentially fatal

Install working carbon-monoxide detectors near sleeping areas and on appropriate levels of the home.

Symptoms of exposure may include:

- Headache
- Dizziness
- Nausea
- Weakness
- Confusion
- Loss of consciousness

Leave the building and seek emergency help if poisoning is suspected.

---

## 10.81 Frozen Pipes

Pipes are vulnerable in:

- Exterior walls
- Crawl spaces
- Garages
- Cabinets
- Unheated rooms

Before severe cold:

- Insulate exposed pipes
- Disconnect outdoor hoses
- Close crawl-space openings when appropriate
- Allow warm air to reach plumbing
- Follow local guidance about dripping faucets
- Know the main shutoff location

If a pipe freezes, do not use an open flame to thaw it.

A frozen pipe becoming a house fire is a crossover episode nobody requested.

---

## 10.82 Roof Snow

Heavy wet snow can place enormous weight on roofs.

Warning signs of structural stress may include:

- Unusual creaking
- Sagging
- Cracked walls
- Sticking doors
- Roof deformation

Do not climb onto an icy roof.

Snow removal from large or vulnerable structures should be performed using appropriate equipment and professional guidance.

---

## 10.83 Ice Dams

An **ice dam** forms when:

1. Heat escaping through the roof melts snow.
2. Water flows toward colder eaves.
3. It refreezes.
4. Ice blocks additional meltwater.
5. Water backs beneath roofing materials.

Ice dams can cause leaks and interior damage.

Prevention includes:

- Attic insulation
- Air sealing
- Proper ventilation
- Safe roof maintenance

---

## 10.84 Shoveling Snow

Snow shoveling can be strenuous.

Cold constricts blood vessels while exertion increases cardiovascular demand.

To reduce risk:

- Warm up first
- Push rather than lift when possible
- Take small loads
- Work slowly
- Take frequent breaks
- Stay hydrated
- Stop for chest pain, severe shortness of breath, or dizziness

Winter-storm deaths often result indirectly from crashes, exposure, and overexertion rather than from snowflakes themselves. [NOAA National Severe Storms Laboratory](https://www.nssl.noaa.gov/education/svrwx101/winter/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

---

# Winter Forecast Products

## 10.85 Watches, Warnings, and Advisories

In general:

### Watch

Hazardous winter weather is possible.

Prepare and monitor updates.

### Warning

Serious winter weather is expected, imminent, or occurring.

Take protective action.

### Advisory

Hazardous conditions are expected but are generally below warning-level criteria.

Use caution and adjust plans.

Exact snowfall and ice thresholds vary by region because local climate and impacts differ. [National Weather Service](https://www.weather.gov/bgm/winterterms?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

---

## 10.86 Winter Storm Warning

A winter storm warning may be issued when significant snow, sleet, freezing rain, or a dangerous combination is expected to meet local warning criteria.

The details matter more than the title.

Read:

- Expected amount
- Timing
- Precipitation type
- Wind
- Travel impacts
- Location-specific wording

A six-inch snowfall may be routine in one region and paralyzing in another.

---

## 10.87 Ice Storm Warning

An ice storm warning indicates damaging freezing-rain accumulation is expected.

Possible impacts include:

- Nearly impossible travel
- Tree damage
- Utility failure
- Long power outages

Preparation should emphasize heat, power, communication, and avoiding travel—not merely acquiring a more impressive ice scraper.

---

## 10.88 Blizzard Warning

A blizzard warning means strong wind and falling or blowing snow are expected to create prolonged, dangerously low visibility.

Snowfall amount may be secondary.

Travel can become life-threatening because rescuers may be unable to reach stranded people.

---

## 10.89 Snow Squall Warning

A snow squall warning may be issued for a short-lived but intense snow burst creating sudden dangerous travel.

Unlike a large winter storm warning, it may cover a narrow area and brief time.

When one is issued:

- Avoid or delay travel
- Reduce speed gradually if already driving
- Turn on headlights
- Increase following distance
- Do not stop in a travel lane

---

## 10.90 Cold Weather Alerts

The National Weather Service simplified several U.S. cold-hazard products beginning with the 2024–25 cold season, replacing older “wind chill” product names with broader **extreme cold** and **cold weather** terminology. [National Weather Service](https://www.weather.gov/news/243009-cold-hazard-simplification?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

Because alert systems and local criteria can change, follow the wording and instructions in your current local forecast.

The essential distinction remains:

```text
WATCH = prepare

WARNING = act

ADVISORY = use heightened caution
```

---

# Forecasting Snow Amounts

## 10.91 Why Snow Forecasts Change

Snow totals depend on:

- Storm track
- Temperature profile
- Snow ratio
- Band placement
- Precipitation intensity
- Surface temperature
- Dry-air intrusion
- Sleet mixing
- Compaction
- Storm speed

A minor change in one variable can shift totals substantially.

Forecast changes are not necessarily evidence that forecasters had no idea. They are often evidence that the atmosphere continued supplying new information, the shameless flirt.

---

## 10.92 Forecast Ranges

A snowfall forecast expressed as a range—such as four to eight inches—communicates uncertainty.

Do not interpret the upper number as a promise or the lower number as failure.

Useful questions include:

- What is the most likely amount?
- What could cause a lower total?
- What could cause a higher total?
- Is sleet expected?
- Could a narrow heavy band develop?
- When will roads become hazardous?

---

## 10.93 Probability Maps

Modern forecasts may show the probability of receiving at least a certain amount.

For example:

- Probability of at least one inch
- Probability of at least six inches
- Probability of at least twelve inches

These maps distinguish likelihood from a single deterministic total.

A 30 percent chance of twelve inches means twelve inches is a meaningful possibility, not that 30 percent of your driveway will receive it.

---

## 10.94 Bust Scenarios

Common reasons a snow forecast underperforms include:

- Storm tracks farther away
- Warm layer arrives
- Dry air reduces precipitation
- Sleet replaces snow
- Snow ratio is lower
- Heavy band sets up elsewhere

Reasons it may overperform include:

- Stronger lift
- Colder temperatures
- Higher snow ratio
- Band remains stationary
- Precipitation begins earlier
- Warm layer fails to arrive

Good forecasts explain these alternatives rather than hiding behind one exquisitely confident number.

---

# Field Observation  
## Identify Winter Precipitation

From a safe sheltered location, observe what is falling.

### Snow

- Soft flakes
- May stick
- Does not bounce sharply

### Sleet

- Hard pellets
- Bounces
- Taps against windows

### Freezing rain

- Looks like ordinary rain
- Creates glaze
- Coats objects

### Graupel

- Soft white pellets
- Crushes easily
- Often falls during showers

Record how the type changes with time and temperature.

Do not conduct the “Is the sidewalk icy?” test by stepping confidently onto it. Use your eyes, a railing, and a trace of dignity.

---

# Try It  
## Build a Temperature-Profile Model

You will need:

- Four transparent cups
- Ice water
- Warm water
- Small paper snowflakes or beads
- Labels

Arrange the cups vertically in a diagram representing layers of atmosphere.

Create four profiles:

1. Cold all the way down
2. Cold–warm–deep cold
3. Cold–warm–shallow cold
4. Cold–warm all the way to ground

Trace the path of a snowflake through each.

Label the result:

- Snow
- Sleet
- Freezing rain
- Rain

This model makes the invisible layers easier to remember.

---

# Try It  
## Measure Snow Water Equivalent

Collect a representative sample of fresh snow in a straight-sided container.

Measure its depth.

Allow it to melt indoors.

Measure the liquid-water depth.

Then calculate:

```text
snow depth ÷ liquid depth
=
snow-to-liquid ratio
```

Example:

```text
8 inches snow ÷ 0.5 inch water
=
16:1 ratio
```

Avoid collecting from drifts, roofs, or plowed piles.

Those are not samples. They are snow propaganda.

---

# Field Observation  
## Watch a Cold Front Arrive

During a forecast Arctic-front passage, record:

- Temperature
- Dew point
- Pressure
- Wind direction
- Wind speed
- Precipitation
- Road condition

Take observations every 30 minutes if safe.

Look for:

- Abrupt wind shift
- Rapid temperature fall
- Pressure rise
- Rain changing to snow
- Wet pavement freezing

The transition can happen much faster than the storm itself.

---

# Weather Safety  
## Before a Winter Storm

Prepare before roads become dangerous.

- Charge phones and power banks
- Refill essential medication
- Check heating fuel
- Bring pets inside
- Protect pipes
- Stock basic food and water
- Prepare flashlights
- Test smoke and carbon-monoxide detectors
- Move vehicles away from vulnerable trees
- Avoid unnecessary travel

A forecast is most useful while stores still contain bread and society has not begun its traditional ceremonial run on milk.

---

# Weather Safety  
## During an Ice Storm

- Stay off roads
- Avoid walking beneath trees and wires
- Expect delayed branch failure
- Keep generators outdoors
- Use flashlights instead of candles when possible
- Conserve phone battery
- Report outages safely
- Never approach downed lines

Listen for cracking branches.

Ice damage may continue after precipitation stops.

---

# Weather Safety  
## During a Blizzard

- Remain indoors
- Avoid travel
- Keep emergency heat sources safe
- Bring animals to shelter
- Stay informed
- Limit outdoor exposure
- Mark exterior doors if drifting could block them
- Keep vents clear when safe to do so

If you must go outside, use a tether or stay within sight of the building during whiteout conditions.

People have become lost only yards from shelter.

---

# Chapter Summary

Winter precipitation often begins as ice or snow high in the cloud.

The complete temperature profile from cloud to ground determines whether precipitation reaches the surface as snow, sleet, freezing rain, or rain.

Snow remains frozen when the atmospheric column is sufficiently cold.

Sleet forms when snow melts and then refreezes within a deep subfreezing layer.

Freezing rain forms when snow melts and falls through a shallow cold layer, remaining liquid until it freezes on contact.

Graupel forms when supercooled droplets freeze onto snow crystals and is different from sleet and hail.

Snowfall depth depends on crystal type, temperature, moisture, compaction, and snow-to-liquid ratio.

Freezing rain can produce severe travel danger and damaging ice loads even when accumulation appears modest.

Blizzards are defined by strong wind, blowing snow, severely reduced visibility, and duration—not by a required snowfall total.

Lake-effect snow develops when cold air gains heat and moisture over relatively warm water.

Mountains enhance snow by forcing moist air upward.

Snow squalls can create sudden whiteouts and road icing even with limited total accumulation.

Wind chill describes increased human heat loss in cold moving air.

Frostbite freezes tissue; hypothermia dangerously lowers the body’s core temperature.

Most winter-storm deaths and injuries occur through indirect hazards such as vehicle crashes, exposure, carbon monoxide, and overexertion.

The most dangerous winter surface may not be the one buried beneath a foot of snow.

It may be the one that merely looks wet.

---

## Weather-Watcher’s Vocabulary

**Snow crystal:** An individual crystal of ice formed in the atmosphere.  
**Snowflake:** One or more snow crystals joined together.  
**Aggregation:** The joining of snow crystals into flakes.  
**Riming:** Freezing of supercooled droplets onto an ice crystal.  
**Graupel:** A soft ice pellet formed through heavy riming.  
**Snow-to-liquid ratio:** Snowfall depth compared with its melted-water depth.  
**Snow water equivalent:** The amount of liquid water contained in a snowpack.  
**Sleet:** Ice pellets formed when melted or partly melted precipitation refreezes before reaching the ground.  
**Freezing rain:** Supercooled liquid rain that freezes on contact.  
**Freezing drizzle:** Tiny supercooled droplets that freeze on exposed surfaces.  
**Glaze:** A smooth ice coating created by freezing precipitation.  
**Black ice:** Thin transparent ice that allows the road beneath to remain visible.  
**Warm nose:** A layer of above-freezing air within an otherwise cold atmosphere.  
**Cold-air damming:** Shallow cold air trapped against terrain.  
**Frontogenesis:** Strengthening of a temperature boundary that may produce concentrated lift and snow.  
**Lake-effect snow:** Snow generated when cold air crosses relatively warm open water.  
**Fetch:** The distance air travels across open water.  
**Snow squall:** A brief, intense snow shower with gusty wind and sharply reduced visibility.  
**Ground blizzard:** Blizzard conditions produced mainly by previously fallen snow.  
**Blizzard:** Strong wind and blowing snow reducing visibility to one-quarter mile or less for at least three hours under the standard U.S. definition.  
**Whiteout:** Loss of visible contrast and horizon in snow or blowing snow.  
**Wind chill:** An estimate of human heat loss caused by cold air and wind.  
**Frostbite:** Freezing injury to skin and underlying tissue.  
**Hypothermia:** A dangerous reduction in core body temperature.  
**Snow level:** Approximate elevation separating mainly rain from mainly snow.  
**Ice accretion:** Ice accumulated on an exposed surface.

**Next: Chapter 11 — Floods, Droughts, Heat Waves, and Wildfire Weather**

<!-- End of source chapter -->

---

# Chapter 11  
## Floods, Droughts, Heat Waves, and Wildfire Weather

Not every weather disaster arrives with a rotating cloud or a dramatic radar hook.

Some hazards build quietly. A river rises overnight. Soil dries for months. A city remains hot after sunset. A grass fire finds a gust of wind and crosses a road before anyone fully understands what is happening.

Flood, drought, extreme heat, and wildfire weather may appear unrelated, but they are connected by the movement—or absence—of water and energy.

Too much water, too little water, too much heat, and too much dry wind can each reshape a landscape.

Sometimes the same place experiences all four in a single year, because the atmosphere is not especially committed to thematic consistency.

---

# Floods

## 11.1 What Is a Flood?

A **flood** occurs when water covers land that is normally dry.

Flooding may result from:

- Heavy rain
- Prolonged rain
- Tropical cyclones
- Rapid snowmelt
- Ice jams
- Storm surge
- Dam or levee failure
- Blocked drainage
- Repeated thunderstorms
- Water flowing from higher terrain

Floods vary greatly in size and speed.

A large river flood may develop over days or weeks.

A flash flood may form within minutes.

Both can be deadly.

---

## 11.2 The Main Types of Flooding

Common flood types include:

- Flash flooding
- River flooding
- Urban flooding
- Coastal flooding
- Areal flooding
- Burn-scar flooding
- Ice-jam flooding
- Dam- or levee-failure flooding

These categories can overlap.

A tropical cyclone may produce coastal surge, urban drainage failure, flash flooding in hills, and river flooding farther inland—all from the same storm.

---

## 11.3 Flash Floods

A **flash flood** develops rapidly, usually within minutes or a few hours of the triggering event.

It may be caused by:

- Intense thunderstorms
- Training rainbands
- Dam failure
- Steep terrain
- Narrow canyons
- Burned or frozen ground
- Urban pavement
- Debris blocking a channel

Flash floods can rise with astonishing speed.

A dry creek bed can become a violent torrent before rain begins at the observer’s location because the storm may be miles upstream.

When a flash-flood warning is issued, move to higher ground immediately and never enter floodwater on foot or in a vehicle. [National Weather Service](https://www.weather.gov/safety/flood?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

### Illustration 1: Flash flooding upstream

```text
THUNDERSTORM OVER HILLS
        ☁☁☁
       heavy rain
          ↓↓↓
      steep runoff
          ↓
    narrow creek rises
          ↓
FLASH FLOOD reaches a dry area downstream
```

---

## 11.4 Why Flash Floods Move So Quickly

Several factors accelerate runoff.

### Intense rainfall

Rain falls faster than the soil or drainage system can absorb it.

### Steep terrain

Gravity carries water downhill rapidly.

### Impermeable surfaces

Pavement, roofs, rock, frozen soil, and compacted ground prevent infiltration.

### Narrow channels

Water becomes concentrated within canyons, creeks, drainage ditches, and culverts.

### Saturated soil

Ground already filled with water cannot absorb much more.

### Burn scars

Fire-damaged soil may repel water and lack vegetation to slow runoff.

When several factors combine, a short storm can produce an extreme flood.

---

## 11.5 River Flooding

River flooding generally develops more slowly than flash flooding.

It may follow:

- Days of rain
- Widespread snowmelt
- Upstream flooding
- Tropical-cyclone rainfall
- Frozen or saturated soil
- Repeated storms within a watershed

A river may continue rising after local rain ends because water from the entire drainage basin is still moving toward it.

The highest level, called the **crest**, may occur hours or days later.

Clear skies are not a cancellation notice for upstream water.

---

## 11.6 Watersheds

A **watershed** is an area of land that drains toward the same stream, river, lake, or outlet.

High ground forms the boundaries between watersheds.

Water falling on opposite sides of a ridge may travel into entirely different river systems.

### Illustration 2: A watershed

```text
          RIDGE
        /       \
 rain ↓           ↓ rain
     creek       creek
        \         /
         \       /
          RIVER
            ↓
         larger basin
```

Understanding the watershed explains why rainfall far away can flood a local river.

---

## 11.7 Urban Flooding

Cities are especially vulnerable to rapid flooding because water cannot soak readily into:

- Roads
- Parking lots
- Sidewalks
- Roofs
- Compacted construction soil

Storm drains have limited capacity.

They may also become blocked by:

- Leaves
- Trash
- Sediment
- Debris

Water then collects in:

- Underpasses
- Low intersections
- Parking garages
- Basements
- Subway entrances
- Highway dips

A storm that would cause minor ponding in a field may become dangerous in a paved basin.

---

## 11.8 Pluvial Flooding

**Pluvial flooding** occurs when heavy rain overwhelms local drainage before streams or rivers leave their banks.

It may happen far from any named river.

Common locations include:

- Low streets
- Poorly drained neighborhoods
- Flat urban areas
- Depressions
- Areas with undersized drainage systems

This is one reason a home outside a mapped river floodplain may still flood.

Water is not particularly respectful of paperwork.

---

## 11.9 Coastal Flooding

Coastal flooding may result from:

- Storm surge
- High astronomical tide
- Strong onshore wind
- Large waves
- Persistent low pressure
- Rising rivers meeting elevated seawater

A coastal storm may push water into bays, marshes, rivers, and drainage canals.

Flooding can occur well inland through connected waterways.

Waves riding on top of elevated water add impact and erosion.

---

## 11.10 Tidal Flooding

Some low-lying coastal communities experience flooding during unusually high tides even without a major storm.

This is sometimes called:

- Tidal flooding
- High-tide flooding
- Nuisance flooding
- Sunny-day flooding

Minor flooding can close roads, enter storm drains, and affect property.

A modest weather system arriving during a high astronomical tide can make the event substantially worse.

---

## 11.11 Burn-Scar Flooding

Wildfire removes vegetation and changes soil.

After a severe fire:

- Roots no longer hold soil
- Leaves and stems no longer slow rain
- Ash and debris clog channels
- Soil may become water-repellent
- Runoff accelerates

Even moderate rainfall can then trigger:

- Flash floods
- Mudflows
- Debris flows
- Rockfall

The danger may persist for years while vegetation recovers.

### Illustration 3: Before and after wildfire

```text
BEFORE FIRE

trees and roots slow water
rain → soil infiltration

AFTER FIRE

bare slope
rain → rapid runoff
      → mud, rocks, ash
      → debris flow
```

---

## 11.12 Debris Flows

A **debris flow** is a fast-moving mixture of:

- Water
- Mud
- Rock
- Trees
- Ash
- Other debris

It may move with the consistency of wet concrete.

Debris flows can destroy structures and fill channels rapidly.

They are especially dangerous below:

- Burn scars
- Steep slopes
- Unstable mountainsides
- Recently disturbed ground

A debris flow is not merely dirty floodwater. It is a moving mass with enormous weight and force.

---

## 11.13 Ice-Jam Flooding

River ice can break apart and become lodged at:

- Bends
- Bridges
- Islands
- Narrow sections
- Shallow areas

Water backs up behind the blockage.

If the jam releases suddenly, flooding may occur downstream as well.

Ice-jam floods can rise rapidly and are difficult to predict precisely.

---

## 11.14 Dam and Levee Failure

A dam stores water.

A levee is an embankment intended to keep water within a channel or away from developed land.

Failure may result from:

- Extreme inflow
- Erosion
- Structural damage
- Overtopping
- Poor maintenance
- Earthquake
- Internal seepage

Water released by a failure can move rapidly and reach areas not usually associated with ordinary river flooding.

Evacuation instructions must be followed immediately.

---

# How Rain Becomes Runoff

## 11.15 Infiltration

**Infiltration** is the movement of water into the soil.

Its rate depends on:

- Soil type
- Vegetation
- Moisture
- Compaction
- Ground slope
- Rainfall intensity
- Frozen conditions
- Urban development

Sandy soil often absorbs water faster than clay-rich soil.

Dry ground does not always absorb water well. Hard, crusted, or water-repellent soil may initially shed intense rainfall.

---

## 11.16 Saturation

Soil becomes saturated when its available pore spaces are filled with water.

Additional rain then becomes surface runoff or moves slowly through the ground.

Saturated slopes are more vulnerable to:

- Landslides
- Tree failure
- Erosion
- Foundation problems

Flood risk depends not only on how much rain is falling now, but also on what happened during previous days and weeks.

---

## 11.17 Rainfall Rate Versus Total

Both rainfall intensity and duration matter.

### High rate, short duration

May cause:

- Flash flooding
- Urban flooding
- Small-stream flooding

### Lower rate, long duration

May cause:

- River flooding
- Saturated soil
- Landslides
- Reservoir overflow

### Repeated storms

May cause both.

A two-inch rainfall in twenty minutes behaves very differently from two inches spread across two days.

Same water. Very different entrance.

---

## 11.18 Training

**Training** occurs when storms repeatedly travel over the same location.

Each storm adds water to ground that is already becoming saturated.

Training is common along:

- Stationary fronts
- Slow-moving boundaries
- Tropical rainbands
- Persistent low-level jets
- Mountain slopes

Radar may show a line of cells moving along the same path like railway cars.

The local rainfall total may become extreme even if each individual storm is only moderately strong.

---

## 11.19 Atmospheric Rivers

An **atmospheric river** is a long, narrow corridor carrying large amounts of water vapor through the atmosphere.

When it reaches land and is forced upward, it can produce heavy rain or mountain snow.

Atmospheric rivers may be beneficial by supplying water and snowpack.

Strong or slow-moving events can produce:

- Flooding
- Landslides
- Debris flows
- Roof loading
- Rapid snowmelt

The term describes concentrated moisture transport, not a literal floating river with banks.

---

## 11.20 Rain-on-Snow Events

Warm rain falling on an existing snowpack can increase flood risk.

Water may come from:

- Rainfall
- Melting snow
- Condensation and heat transfer
- Frozen ground limiting infiltration

If rivers are already high or ice-covered, the danger increases further.

Mountain regions can experience major flooding when a warm moist storm raises the snow level rapidly.

---

# Flood Forecasting

## 11.21 Rain Gauges

Rain gauges measure precipitation reaching a point.

They may be:

- Manual
- Tipping-bucket
- Weighing
- Automated

A gauge provides valuable ground truth but represents only one location.

Thunderstorm rainfall can vary sharply over short distances.

A gauge receiving one inch does not mean the entire county received one inch.

---

## 11.22 Radar Rainfall Estimates

Weather radar estimates rainfall by measuring returned energy from precipitation.

Radar provides broad coverage but can be affected by:

- Hail
- Beam height
- Terrain blockage
- Changes in drop size
- Signal loss
- Bright-band effects
- Distance from radar

Forecasters combine radar with rain gauges and models.

One instrument catches the rain. The other interrogates it from afar. Neither is flawless.

---

## 11.23 Stream Gauges

Stream gauges measure river or creek level and sometimes flow rate.

They help forecasters track:

- Rising water
- Flood stage
- Crests
- Upstream movement
- Long-term trends

Gauge data may be transmitted automatically.

Some locations have little or no gauge coverage, especially on small creeks vulnerable to flash flooding.

---

## 11.24 Flood Stage

**Flood stage** is a locally defined river level at which water begins causing meaningful impacts.

Categories may include:

- Action stage
- Minor flood stage
- Moderate flood stage
- Major flood stage

The same numerical river height can mean very different things at different gauge sites.

Always read the impact description associated with the local gauge.

---

## 11.25 Flood Inundation Maps

An inundation map estimates which areas may be covered at different river levels.

It may help show:

- Roads affected
- Neighborhoods threatened
- Expected depth
- Evacuation routes
- Critical facilities at risk

Maps are models, not perfect predictions.

Actual flooding may differ because of debris, levee conditions, drainage problems, erosion, or changing river channels.

---

# Flood Watches and Warnings

## 11.26 Flood Watch

A flood watch means conditions are favorable for flooding.

It covers a broad area and may be issued before heavy rain begins.

During a watch:

- Know whether you are in a low area
- Monitor forecasts
- Charge devices
- Avoid camping in channels or washes
- Review evacuation routes
- Be ready to move

A watch means prepare.

---

## 11.27 Flash Flood Warning

A flash flood warning means rapid flooding is occurring, imminent, or strongly indicated.

During a warning:

- Move to higher ground
- Leave low areas
- Avoid creeks and drainage channels
- Never drive into floodwater
- Follow evacuation orders

Flood watches indicate that flooding is possible; warnings indicate that flooding is imminent or occurring in a more specific area. [National Weather Service](https://www.weather.gov/pub/FSWPW2floodwarningsmonday?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

---

## 11.28 Flash Flood Emergency

A **flash flood emergency** is used for an exceptionally dangerous situation involving a severe threat to life and potentially catastrophic damage.

It may involve:

- Numerous rescues
- Major inundation
- Dam failure
- Rapidly rising destructive water
- Populated areas in immediate danger

Treat the wording literally.

This is not merely a warning with better typography.

---

## 11.29 River Flood Warning

A river flood warning is issued when flooding is expected or occurring along a river or larger stream.

It may remain in effect for days.

Pay attention to:

- Expected crest
- Time of crest
- Impact locations
- Road closures
- Upstream rainfall
- Later forecast changes

---

# Flood Safety

## 11.30 Never Drive Through Floodwater

Floodwater may hide:

- Washed-out pavement
- Missing bridges
- Deep channels
- Debris
- Strong current
- Electrical hazards

A vehicle can lose traction, float, or be pushed sideways.

Even trucks and sport-utility vehicles are vulnerable.

The National Weather Service’s guidance is unambiguous: it is never safe to drive or walk into floodwater. [National Weather Service](https://www.weather.gov/safety/flood-turn-around-dont-drown?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

### Illustration 4: Hidden road damage

```text
what the driver sees:

~~~~~~~~ shallow-looking water ~~~~~~~~

what may be underneath:

road ______      ______ road
            \____/
        washed-out section
```

---

## 11.31 Floodwater on Foot

Moving water can knock a person down.

Standing water may conceal:

- Holes
- Sharp metal
- Sewage
- Chemicals
- Snakes
- Broken glass
- Uncovered drains

Children should never play in floodwater.

Even apparently calm water may be contaminated or electrically dangerous.

---

## 11.32 If a Vehicle Becomes Trapped

Avoid flooded roads in the first place.

If water is rising around a stationary vehicle and a safe route to higher ground remains available, leave before the vehicle is swept away.

If water is moving rapidly, conditions become extremely dangerous and situation-dependent.

Call emergency services and follow local rescue guidance.

There is no clever driving maneuver that converts a submerged road back into infrastructure.

---

## 11.33 At Home

Before flooding:

- Move valuables upward
- Know how to shut off utilities
- Keep documents waterproof
- Clear safe drainage areas
- Prepare medications
- Move vehicles early
- Know evacuation routes

During flooding:

- Move to a higher level
- Avoid flooded basements
- Do not touch electrical equipment while wet
- Leave if instructed
- Do not enter an attic without an escape tool if water is rising

A roof may be safer than a sealed attic where rising water can trap occupants.

---

# Drought

## 11.34 What Is Drought?

A **drought** is an extended period of abnormally dry conditions that produces water shortages or other meaningful impacts.

It is not simply “hot weather” or “a week without rain.”

Drought generally involves a precipitation deficiency lasting a season or longer, though some rapidly developing droughts emerge over shorter periods when heat and wind sharply increase water loss. [Drought.gov](https://www.drought.gov/what-is-drought/drought-basics?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

Drought may develop gradually enough that people fail to notice it until:

- Lawns brown
- Streams shrink
- Wells decline
- Crops suffer
- Fire risk rises
- Water restrictions begin

---

## 11.35 Types of Drought

Drought is often divided into several overlapping types.

### Meteorological drought

Less precipitation than normal for a region and season.

### Agricultural drought

Insufficient soil moisture for crops, pasture, and vegetation.

### Hydrological drought

Reduced streamflow, reservoir levels, groundwater, and lake levels.

### Socioeconomic drought

Water shortage affects communities, industries, energy, food supply, or economic activity.

These types do not begin at the same time. Meteorological drought may appear first, while reservoirs and groundwater respond more slowly. [National Weather Service](https://www.weather.gov/safety/drought-types?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

---

## 11.36 Drought Is Regional

Drought depends on what is normal for the location.

Ten inches of yearly rain might be ordinary in a desert.

The same total would be an extraordinary drought in a humid climate.

Forecasters compare conditions with:

- Local climatology
- Seasonal needs
- Water demand
- Soil type
- Crop cycles
- Reservoir capacity

Drought is an anomaly and an impact, not one universal rainfall number.

---

## 11.37 How Drought Develops

Drought may begin with a persistent weather pattern that redirects storms.

Contributing factors include:

- High-pressure ridges
- Weak storm tracks
- Reduced tropical moisture
- Low snowpack
- High temperature
- Strong dry wind
- Early snowmelt
- Increased evaporation
- Repeated failed rainy seasons

Once soil dries, more solar energy heats the ground rather than evaporating water.

This can intensify heat and dryness.

---

## 11.38 The Soil-Moisture Feedback

Wet soil uses substantial energy for evaporation.

Dry soil cannot.

When soil dries:

1. Less energy goes into evaporation.
2. More energy heats the surface.
3. Air temperature rises.
4. Humidity may decrease.
5. Vegetation loses more water.
6. Drought may worsen.

```text
DRY SOIL
   ↓
LESS EVAPORATION
   ↓
MORE SURFACE HEATING
   ↓
HOTTER, DRIER AIR
   ↓
FASTER WATER LOSS
```

This feedback can strengthen heat waves.

---

## 11.39 Flash Drought

A **flash drought** develops unusually quickly.

It may emerge over weeks rather than seasons.

Common ingredients include:

- Below-normal rainfall
- High temperature
- Strong sunshine
- Wind
- Low humidity
- Rapid soil-moisture loss
- High crop-water demand

Flash drought can catch farmers and water managers by surprise because conditions deteriorate much faster than traditional drought.

---

## 11.40 Snow Drought

A **snow drought** occurs when mountain snowpack is unusually low.

It may result from:

- Too little precipitation
- Warm storms producing rain instead of snow
- Early melting
- Sublimation
- Dust darkening the snow surface

Snow drought reduces water available during spring and summer melt.

Regions dependent on mountain runoff may experience shortages even if winter rainfall near cities seemed adequate.

---

## 11.41 Groundwater Drought

Groundwater responds slowly.

A rainy week may improve surface conditions without restoring underground aquifers.

Groundwater drought may persist after:

- Lawns turn green
- Streams rise temporarily
- Drought maps improve

Deep aquifers may require seasons or years to recover.

One dramatic storm cannot always repay a long hydrological debt.

---

## 11.42 The U.S. Drought Monitor

The U.S. Drought Monitor combines many indicators and expert analysis.

Its categories are:

| Category | Meaning |
|---|---|
| D0 | Abnormally dry |
| D1 | Moderate drought |
| D2 | Severe drought |
| D3 | Extreme drought |
| D4 | Exceptional drought |

D0 is not officially drought; it marks unusual dryness or incomplete recovery. D1 is the least intense drought category and D4 the most intense. [U.S. Drought Monitor](https://droughtmonitor.unl.edu/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

The map summarizes broad conditions. It does not replace local water-supply or agricultural information.

---

## 11.43 Drought Impacts

Drought may affect:

- Crops
- Livestock
- Forests
- Drinking water
- Hydroelectric generation
- River transport
- Wildlife
- Water quality
- Building foundations
- Recreation
- Fire behavior

Low streamflow can concentrate pollutants and raise water temperature.

Dry clay soil may shrink around foundations.

Trees weakened by drought become more vulnerable to insects, disease, wind, and later fire.

---

## 11.44 Ending a Drought

Drought recovery depends on:

- Duration
- Severity
- Season
- Soil depth
- Reservoir storage
- Groundwater
- Snowpack
- Rainfall distribution

Light rain may improve vegetation without restoring reservoirs.

Heavy rain may run off too rapidly to recharge groundwater.

The best recovery often comes from repeated moderate precipitation over a long period.

The drought did not arrive in an afternoon. It may decline to leave with similar reluctance.

---

## 11.45 Drought Safety and Conservation

During drought:

- Follow water restrictions
- Repair leaks
- Water plants efficiently
- Avoid unnecessary outdoor water use
- Choose drought-tolerant landscaping
- Reduce fire ignition risk
- Protect wells and pumps
- Monitor vulnerable livestock and vegetation

Conservation decisions vary by region.

Local authorities understand the water system better than generic national advice.

---

# Heat Waves

## 11.46 What Is a Heat Wave?

A **heat wave** is a period of unusually hot weather lasting several days or longer.

Its definition depends on local climate.

A temperature of 95°F may be ordinary in one region and record-breaking in another.

Heat risk depends on:

- Daytime maximum temperature
- Nighttime minimum temperature
- Humidity
- Duration
- Wind
- Sun exposure
- Urban conditions
- Health and acclimatization

A single hot afternoon can be dangerous.

Several hot days and warm nights are far more stressful.

---

## 11.47 Why Warm Nights Matter

The body needs time to cool and recover.

When nighttime temperatures remain high:

- Indoor spaces stay warm
- Sleep is disrupted
- The body retains heat
- Vulnerable people receive little relief
- Risk accumulates from day to day

Buildings without air conditioning may become hotter each afternoon.

A heat wave is not reset at midnight just because the forecast changes calendar dates.

---

## 11.48 The Urban Heat Island

Cities often remain warmer than surrounding rural areas, especially at night.

Contributing factors include:

- Dark roofs and pavement
- Limited vegetation
- Heat stored in masonry
- Waste heat from vehicles and buildings
- Reduced airflow
- Street canyons
- Less evaporative cooling

Neighborhoods within the same city may have very different heat exposure depending on tree cover, building density, and surface materials.

---

## 11.49 Heat Index

The **heat index** combines air temperature and relative humidity to estimate how hot conditions feel to the human body in shaded, light-wind conditions.

High humidity reduces the evaporation of sweat.

When sweat cannot evaporate efficiently, the body has difficulty releasing heat.

The heat index may underestimate stress in:

- Direct sunlight
- Heavy clothing
- Physical work
- Poor ventilation
- Hot indoor spaces

The National Weather Service uses the heat index as one tool for describing heat stress, but actual risk also depends on exposure, health, and local conditions. [National Weather Service](https://www.weather.gov/arx/heat_index?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

---

## 11.50 Wet-Bulb Temperature

The **wet-bulb temperature** reflects how cool air can become through evaporation.

It depends on temperature and humidity.

As humidity increases, evaporation becomes less effective and wet-bulb temperature rises.

High wet-bulb conditions are dangerous because the human body depends heavily on evaporation of sweat.

However, real-world heat illness can occur well below theoretical physiological limits, especially during prolonged exposure, exertion, sun, age, medication effects, or lack of acclimatization.

---

## 11.51 Wet-Bulb Globe Temperature

The **wet-bulb globe temperature**, or WBGT, estimates heat stress using:

- Humidity
- Air temperature
- Wind
- Sun angle
- Solar radiation

It is often used for:

- Athletics
- Military activity
- Outdoor work
- Schools
- Event planning

WBGT differs from the ordinary wet-bulb temperature and from the heat index.

It is particularly useful for people exercising or working in direct sun.

---

## 11.52 HeatRisk

NWS HeatRisk is a color- and number-based tool that considers how unusual and potentially dangerous forecast heat may be, including overnight conditions and duration.

It supplements—not replaces—official watches, advisories, and warnings. [NWS Weather Prediction Center](https://www.wpc.ncep.noaa.gov/heatrisk/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

A heat forecast should therefore be read using several pieces of information:

- Temperature
- Heat index
- Overnight low
- HeatRisk
- Official alerts
- Local public-health advice

---

# Heat Illness

## 11.53 Heat Cramps

Heat cramps are painful muscle spasms associated with exertion, sweating, and salt loss.

They commonly affect:

- Legs
- Abdomen
- Arms

Move to a cooler place, stop strenuous activity, and drink appropriate fluids.

Persistent symptoms or underlying health concerns require medical guidance.

Heat cramps can be an early warning that the body is struggling.

---

## 11.54 Heat Exhaustion

Possible signs include:

- Heavy sweating
- Weakness
- Headache
- Dizziness
- Nausea
- Cool or clammy skin
- Fast pulse
- Muscle cramps
- Faintness

Move the person into air conditioning or shade.

Loosen clothing.

Apply cool wet cloths.

Offer water if the person is alert and able to drink.

Seek medical help if symptoms worsen or do not improve promptly.

---

## 11.55 Heat Stroke

**Heat stroke is a medical emergency.**

Possible signs include:

- Confusion
- Loss of consciousness
- Seizure
- Very high body temperature
- Hot skin
- Rapid pulse
- Abnormal behavior

Sweating may be present or absent.

Call emergency services immediately.

Begin rapid cooling with:

- Cool water
- Wet cloths
- Ice packs around the neck, armpits, and groin
- Air movement
- Immersion when appropriate and safely supervised

Do not delay emergency care.

The brain is overheating. This is not a “drink something and see how it goes” situation.

---

## 11.56 People at Greater Risk

Heat can affect anyone, but risk is higher for:

- Older adults
- Infants and young children
- Pregnant people
- Outdoor workers
- Athletes
- People without air conditioning
- People with chronic illness
- People taking certain medications
- People experiencing homelessness
- Anyone isolated or unable to seek help

Some medications affect sweating, circulation, fluid balance, or temperature regulation.

Medical questions should be discussed with a qualified clinician rather than answered by abruptly stopping prescribed treatment.

---

## 11.57 Acclimatization

The body gradually adapts to heat over repeated exposure.

Acclimatization can improve:

- Sweating efficiency
- Circulation
- Salt conservation
- Work tolerance

It takes time and can be partly lost after an absence from hot conditions.

Workers and athletes returning after vacation, illness, or cool weather should increase exposure gradually.

Being “used to summer” in April is usually a charming fiction.

---

## 11.58 Hydration

Drink regularly during heat exposure.

The amount needed varies with:

- Body size
- Activity
- Temperature
- Humidity
- Health
- Clothing

During prolonged heavy sweating, electrolytes may also need replacement.

Excessive water intake can be dangerous as well, particularly if it dilutes blood sodium.

A practical strategy is regular drinking combined with breaks, shade, and attention to symptoms rather than trying to defeat heat through heroic gallon consumption.

---

## 11.59 Fans

Fans increase air movement and can help sweat evaporate.

They are useful when indoor conditions are moderately hot.

In extreme indoor heat, a fan may become insufficient and can sometimes add hot airflow without adequately cooling the body.

Seek air conditioning or a cooling center during dangerous heat.

A fan is a tool, not a force field.

---

## 11.60 Cars

A parked vehicle can heat rapidly even when:

- Windows are cracked
- Outside temperature seems mild
- The car is in partial shade
- The stop is expected to be brief

Never leave children, vulnerable adults, or pets inside a parked vehicle.

Check the entire vehicle before locking it.

Develop a routine that prevents a sleeping child from being overlooked.

---

# Heat Watches and Warnings

## 11.61 Heat Advisory

A heat advisory means dangerous heat conditions are expected, generally below the threshold for the highest-level warning.

Exact criteria vary by region because climate and vulnerability differ.

Take precautions:

- Reduce strenuous activity
- Use air conditioning
- Drink fluids
- Check on others
- Avoid the hottest period
- Never leave anyone in a vehicle

---

## 11.62 Extreme Heat Watch

An extreme heat watch means extremely dangerous heat conditions are possible.

Use the time to prepare:

- Confirm access to cooling
- Check air-conditioning equipment
- Adjust work or travel
- Contact vulnerable relatives
- Review medication storage
- Locate cooling centers

---

## 11.63 Extreme Heat Warning

An extreme heat warning means extremely dangerous heat is expected or occurring.

NWS guidance emphasizes staying in air-conditioned spaces when possible, limiting outdoor activity, drinking water, taking frequent shaded breaks, and checking family and neighbors. [National Weather Service](https://www.weather.gov/safety/heat-ww?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

The exact numerical criteria vary among forecast offices.

Read the local alert rather than memorizing one national threshold.

---

# Wildfire Weather

## 11.64 Weather Does Not Start Every Fire

Wildfires require an ignition source.

Ignitions may come from:

- Lightning
- Power lines
- Machinery
- Vehicles
- Campfires
- Debris burning
- Fireworks
- Arson
- Other human activity

Weather does not ignite every fire, but it controls how easily fuels burn and how rapidly flames spread.

The key elements are:

- Dry fuel
- Low humidity
- Wind
- Heat
- Atmospheric instability

---

## 11.65 The Fire Triangle

Fire requires:

- Heat
- Fuel
- Oxygen

```text
             HEAT
              ▲
             / \
            /   \
       FUEL ----- OXYGEN
```

Wildland fire behavior also depends on:

- Weather
- Topography
- Fuel type
- Fuel moisture

Removing any side of the basic fire triangle can stop combustion.

In landscapes, unfortunately, oxygen is usually rather available.

---

## 11.66 Fuel

Wildland fuel includes:

- Grass
- Leaves
- Needles
- Shrubs
- Branches
- Fallen trees
- Peat
- Living vegetation
- Structures within developed areas

Fuel characteristics matter:

- Size
- Moisture
- Arrangement
- Continuity
- Density
- Height

Fine fuels such as dry grass respond quickly to changes in humidity.

Large logs respond more slowly.

---

## 11.67 Fuel Moisture

**Fuel moisture** describes the amount of water contained in vegetation and dead material.

Low fuel moisture makes ignition and spread easier.

Dead grasses and small twigs can dry rapidly during:

- Low humidity
- Sun
- Wind
- Heat

Live vegetation generally changes more slowly, though prolonged drought can severely reduce its moisture.

---

## 11.68 Relative Humidity and Fire

Low relative humidity encourages fuel to lose moisture.

Overnight humidity recovery is important.

If humidity remains low through the night:

- Fuels stay dry
- Fire activity may continue
- Crews receive less relief
- The next day begins from a more dangerous condition

Hot dry wind can lower fuel moisture further and increase oxygen flow to flames.

---

## 11.69 Wind

Wind affects fire by:

- Tilting flames toward unburned fuel
- Carrying heat ahead
- Transporting embers
- Supplying oxygen
- Increasing spread rate
- Producing rapid direction changes

A fire’s behavior can change within seconds when a front, thunderstorm outflow, or terrain-driven wind arrives.

Wind is often the most immediate weather threat to firefighters.

---

## 11.70 Spotting

**Spotting** occurs when wind carries burning embers ahead of the main fire.

Embers may ignite new fires across:

- Roads
- Rivers
- Firebreaks
- Cleared areas
- Neighborhoods

Spot fires can trap people who assumed the main flame front was the only threat.

A road is a transportation feature, not a magical anti-ember moat.

---

## 11.71 Temperature

High temperature contributes to:

- Lower fuel moisture
- Greater heat stress
- Stronger surface instability
- Faster drying
- More active burning

Temperature alone does not determine fire danger.

A hot humid calm day may be less critical than a cooler dry windy day.

---

## 11.72 Atmospheric Instability

Unstable air encourages vigorous upward motion.

Over a fire, this can:

- Strengthen convection
- Draw air inward
- Increase flame height
- Carry embers higher
- Produce erratic surface wind
- Build large smoke columns

Extremely intense fires may begin creating their own cloud systems.

---

## 11.73 Terrain

Fire usually spreads faster uphill.

Flames and rising heat preheat vegetation above the fire.

Terrain also funnels and redirects wind through:

- Canyons
- Gaps
- Ridges
- Saddles
- Slopes

A fire on a slope can accelerate rapidly.

Canyons may behave like chimneys.

The landscape does not merely sit beneath the weather; it grabs the weather and points it at things.

---

## 11.74 Upslope and Downslope Winds

During the day, heated slopes often produce upslope wind.

At night, cooled dense air may drain downslope.

These predictable daily patterns can be disrupted by:

- Fronts
- Thunderstorms
- Strong regional wind
- Fire-generated circulation

Fire crews monitor both the expected slope wind and the larger weather pattern.

---

## 11.75 Downslope Wind Events

Strong descending wind can warm and dry as it compresses.

Regional examples include:

- Santa Ana winds
- Diablo winds
- Chinook winds
- Foehn winds

These events may produce:

- Very low humidity
- Strong gusts
- Rapid fire spread
- Falling branches and power lines
- Difficult aircraft operations

A fire that was manageable under light wind may become uncontrollable quickly.

---

# Fire-Generated Weather

## 11.76 Convection Columns

An intense fire heats air, which rises in a column.

The column contains:

- Smoke
- Ash
- Water vapor
- Hot gases
- Burning material

Strong columns may reach several miles high.

The surrounding air flows inward near the ground to replace the rising air.

This can strengthen and complicate local wind.

---

## 11.77 Pyrocumulus

A **pyrocumulus**, often abbreviated pyroCu, is a cumulus cloud generated or enhanced by intense surface heating from fire or volcanic activity.

As hot air rises, it cools and water vapor condenses.

The resulting cloud may look like a rapidly growing thunderstorm tower rising from smoke.

### Illustration 5: Pyrocumulus

```text
           white cloud tower
                ☁☁
                 ↑
             condensation
                 ↑
          smoke convection
                 ↑
              WILDFIRE
```

---

## 11.78 Pyrocumulonimbus

A **pyrocumulonimbus**, or pyroCb, is a fire-generated thunderstorm.

It may produce:

- Lightning
- Strong updrafts
- Powerful downdrafts
- Gust fronts
- Hail in some cases
- Extreme ember transport

Lightning from the cloud can ignite additional fires.

Downdrafts can cause sudden changes in fire direction.

A wildfire creating a thunderstorm is the atmosphere and landscape entering an extremely unhealthy collaboration.

---

## 11.79 Fire Whirls

A **fire whirl** is a rotating column of hot air, gases, flame, or debris associated with intense heating and wind shear.

Most are small.

Large fire whirls can be highly destructive.

They differ from tornadoes in their origin, though exceptionally intense fire-generated vortices may sometimes reach tornadic strength.

Visual appearance alone may not reveal the full circulation.

---

# Dry Thunderstorms

## 11.80 Lightning With Little Rain

A **dry thunderstorm** produces lightning while little precipitation reaches the ground.

Rain may evaporate in dry air beneath the cloud.

Hazards include:

- New fire ignition
- Strong downdrafts
- Dry microbursts
- Gusty shifting wind
- Little rainfall to extinguish fires

Dry thunderstorms are especially dangerous after prolonged hot, dry weather.

---

## 11.81 Holdover Fires

Lightning may ignite smoldering fire within:

- Roots
- Logs
- Peat
- Thick vegetation

The fire may remain hidden for hours or days before wind and drying cause it to flare.

These are sometimes called **holdover fires**.

Rain occurring with the lightning does not always eliminate the risk.

---

# Fire-Weather Alerts

## 11.82 Fire Weather Watch

A fire weather watch means critical fire-weather conditions may develop.

It alerts fire and land-management agencies to prepare.

The exact criteria vary by region based on:

- Fuel conditions
- Humidity
- Wind
- Season
- Local vegetation

A watch does not mean a wildfire is definitely burning.

---

## 11.83 Red Flag Warning

A **Red Flag Warning** means critical fire-weather conditions are occurring or expected shortly.

It generally involves a dangerous combination of dry fuels and weather favorable for ignition and rapid spread.

NWS criteria are locally defined rather than identical nationwide. [National Weather Service](https://www.weather.gov/lot/firewx_definition?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

During a Red Flag Warning:

- Avoid outdoor burning
- Do not use equipment that produces sparks near dry vegetation
- Secure trailer chains
- Fully extinguish fires
- Follow local restrictions
- Be prepared for evacuation if a wildfire is nearby

---

## 11.84 Fire Warning

A **fire warning** may be issued when a major uncontrolled fire threatens populated areas or important roads.

It concerns an active fire threat rather than merely favorable weather conditions. [National Weather Service](https://www.weather.gov/oun/fireweather?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

Follow evacuation instructions immediately.

Do not wait to see flames.

Smoke, embers, congestion, and road closures may reach you first.

---

# Smoke and Air Quality

## 11.85 Wildfire Smoke

Smoke contains a mixture of:

- Gases
- Water vapor
- Organic compounds
- Ash
- Fine particles

Fine particulate matter, especially **PM2.5**, can travel deep into the lungs and enter the bloodstream.

Smoke may affect areas hundreds or thousands of miles from the fire.

A clear view of the Sun through orange haze may be visually interesting and physiologically rather less appealing.

---

## 11.86 Smoke Near the Ground

A smoke plume seen by satellite does not always mean poor air quality at ground level.

Smoke may remain high aloft.

Conversely, ground-level smoke can become concentrated beneath:

- Temperature inversions
- Calm wind
- Mountain-valley circulation
- Nighttime stable layers

AirNow’s Fire and Smoke Map combines information about fine-particle pollution, fires, and smoke, while noting that visible satellite plumes do not necessarily describe ground-level conditions. [AirNow Fire Map](https://fire.airnow.gov/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

---

## 11.87 Sensitive Groups

Smoke risk is greater for:

- Children
- Older adults
- Pregnant people
- People with asthma
- People with heart or lung disease
- Outdoor workers
- Anyone exercising outdoors

During unhealthy smoke:

- Stay indoors with clean filtered air
- Close windows and doors
- Reduce outdoor exertion
- Follow medical action plans
- Use appropriately fitted respirators when recommended
- Monitor local air-quality guidance

Cloth face coverings are not designed to filter fine smoke particles effectively.

---

## 11.88 Indoor Air

To reduce indoor smoke:

- Use a properly sized air cleaner
- Run suitable HVAC filtration
- Create a cleaner-air room
- Avoid indoor smoke sources
- Limit opening doors
- Set vehicle ventilation to recirculate when driving through smoke

Do not create other hazards through unsafe improvised electrical setups or ozone-producing devices.

---

# Wildfire Evacuation

## 11.89 Prepare Before Fire Approaches

Know:

- More than one route
- Local alert systems
- Evacuation zones
- Where pets will go
- Which documents matter
- What medications are needed
- How to shut windows and vents
- Where family members will meet

Keep the vehicle fueled or charged.

Wildfire evacuation often happens faster than hurricane evacuation.

There may be no leisurely packing montage.

---

## 11.90 Ready, Set, Go

Some agencies use a three-stage framework:

### Ready

Prepare property, supplies, alerts, and plans.

### Set

Conditions are dangerous. Be ready to leave immediately.

### Go

Evacuate now.

Terminology varies by location.

Follow the exact instructions of local authorities.

---

## 11.91 Do Not Delay

Leave early when:

- Ordered to evacuate
- Smoke limits visibility
- Embers begin falling
- Only one road is available
- Mobility or medical needs require extra time
- Animals must be transported
- Wind is increasing

Late evacuation creates traffic, exposes people to flame and smoke, and blocks emergency vehicles.

The final ten minutes are rarely improved by adding one more suitcase.

---

## 11.92 If Trapped

Avoid becoming trapped through early evacuation.

If escape becomes impossible:

- Call emergency services
- Move away from heavy vegetation
- Seek a substantial structure or large cleared area
- Close windows and doors
- Turn off systems drawing outside air
- Stay low beneath smoke
- Follow official emergency instructions

Vehicles and open ground provide limited protection and conditions vary greatly.

There is no universal last-second wildfire trick.

Early departure is the real survival technique.

---

# Connections Between Hazards

## 11.93 Drought and Heat

Drought reduces evaporative cooling.

Heat increases evaporation and plant stress.

Together they can reinforce one another.

```text
DROUGHT
   ↓
DRIER SOIL
   ↓
LESS EVAPORATIVE COOLING
   ↓
MORE HEAT
   ↓
FASTER DRYING
```

---

## 11.94 Heat and Wildfire

Extreme heat:

- Dries fine fuels
- Stresses vegetation
- Raises ignition probability
- Increases firefighter heat stress
- May deepen unstable mixing

But wind and fuel conditions remain crucial.

The hottest day is not always the most dangerous fire day.

---

## 11.95 Wildfire and Flood

After a wildfire, reduced vegetation and altered soil increase runoff.

A region can move from fire emergency to flood emergency with the first intense rain.

The danger may be greatest when the fire itself is no longer making headlines.

---

## 11.96 Drought and Sudden Flooding

Drought does not prevent flash floods.

Hard dry soil may absorb intense rain poorly.

Sparse vegetation provides less resistance to runoff.

After a long dry period, a sudden storm can produce:

- Urban flooding
- Erosion
- Debris flow
- Rapid creek rise

“Dry lately” is not the same as “safe from water.”

---

## 11.97 Climate Versus Weather

An individual flood, heat wave, drought, or wildfire cannot be explained by one factor alone.

Weather determines the immediate event.

Climate influences the background conditions and changing probabilities.

Land use, water management, vegetation, infrastructure, and human exposure also affect the outcome.

A hazard becomes a disaster where physical conditions meet vulnerability.

The weather supplies the event. We often help decide how painful it becomes.

---

# Field Observation  
## Map Water After Rain

After a safe moderate rainfall, walk around your property or neighborhood.

Observe:

- Where water collects
- Which drains flow
- Where soil absorbs water
- Which slopes produce runoff
- Whether gutters discharge safely
- Which roads dip

Sketch a simple map.

Do not enter drainage channels or inspect during active flooding.

The goal is to identify the water’s preferred routes before it begins improvising through the house.

---

# Try It  
## Measure Infiltration

You will need:

- A large metal can with both ends removed
- A ruler
- Water
- A timer

Press the can several centimeters into soil.

Pour in a measured depth of water.

Time how long it takes to soak in.

Repeat on:

- Lawn
- Bare soil
- Compacted ground
- Mulched garden soil

Compare the rates.

This simple experiment shows why identical rainfall can produce different runoff in neighboring places.

---

# Field Observation  
## Track Drought

Once each week, record:

- Rainfall
- Soil condition
- Stream level
- Lawn or crop stress
- Tree condition
- Local drought category
- Water restrictions
- Fire danger

Take a photograph from the same location.

Drought develops gradually, so repeated observation is more revealing than memory.

Human memory is excellent at stories and rather slippery around last month’s grass color.

---

# Try It  
## Compare Surface Temperatures

On a hot sunny afternoon, compare:

- Asphalt
- Grass
- Shade
- Bare soil
- Concrete
- Tree-covered ground

Use an infrared thermometer when available, or compare nearby shaded air temperatures carefully.

Record the results.

Notice how vegetation and shade alter the local heat environment.

Do not touch metal or pavement to test whether it is “actually that hot.” The thermometer is already handling that assignment.

---

# Field Observation  
## Estimate Fire-Weather Change

On a dry day, note:

- Temperature
- Relative humidity
- Wind
- Cloud cover
- Vegetation dryness

Repeat in late afternoon and after sunset.

Look for:

- Falling humidity
- Increasing gusts
- Wind shift
- Poor nighttime humidity recovery
- Smoke appearing on the horizon

Do not intentionally ignite anything.

That should not require saying, yet history has been unhelpful on this point.

---

# Weather Safety  
## A Four-Hazard Checklist

### Flood

- Move to higher ground
- Avoid floodwater
- Know upstream risk
- Follow evacuation orders

### Drought

- Conserve water
- Protect wells and vegetation
- Follow restrictions
- Reduce fire ignition

### Heat

- Use air conditioning
- Drink regularly
- Reduce exertion
- Check vulnerable people
- Treat confusion as an emergency

### Wildfire

- Monitor alerts
- Avoid sparks and flame
- Prepare multiple routes
- Evacuate early
- Protect yourself from smoke

---

# Chapter Summary

Flooding occurs when water covers normally dry land and may develop slowly or within minutes.

Flash floods are especially dangerous because they rise rapidly and can occur far downstream from the heaviest rain.

Urban surfaces, steep terrain, saturated soil, burn scars, and narrow channels accelerate runoff.

River flooding may continue after rain ends because water is still moving through the watershed.

Never drive or walk into floodwater.

Drought is a prolonged abnormal shortage of precipitation or water, not merely hot or temporarily dry weather.

Meteorological, agricultural, hydrological, and socioeconomic drought describe different parts of the same developing shortage.

Heat, wind, and low humidity can create flash drought and intensify longer-term drought.

Heat-wave danger depends on duration, humidity, warm nights, sun, health, and access to cooling.

Heat stroke causes neurological symptoms and is a medical emergency requiring immediate cooling and emergency assistance.

Wildfire behavior depends on fuel, weather, and terrain.

Low humidity dries fuels, wind accelerates fire and carries embers, and unstable air supports vigorous fire convection.

Pyrocumulus and pyrocumulonimbus clouds can form above intense fires and may produce lightning and dangerous wind shifts.

A Red Flag Warning means critical fire-weather conditions are occurring or expected, not necessarily that a wildfire is already burning.

Wildfire smoke can harm people far from the flames.

Drought can worsen heat and wildfire risk.

Fire can increase later flood and debris-flow danger.

A long dry spell can still end in a flash flood.

Water shortage and water excess are not opposites that politely wait their turn.

They are hazards that can follow each other through the same landscape.

---

## Weather-Watcher’s Vocabulary

**Flood:** Water covering land that is normally dry.  
**Flash flood:** Rapid flooding developing within minutes or hours.  
**Watershed:** Land area draining toward a common water body.  
**Infiltration:** Movement of water into soil.  
**Runoff:** Water flowing across the surface.  
**Pluvial flooding:** Flooding caused by rainfall overwhelming local drainage.  
**Flood stage:** River level at which defined flooding impacts begin.  
**Crest:** Highest level reached by a river during a flood event.  
**Debris flow:** Fast-moving mixture of water, soil, rock, and debris.  
**Atmospheric river:** A narrow corridor carrying concentrated atmospheric moisture.  
**Training:** Repeated storms moving over the same location.  
**Drought:** Extended abnormal dryness producing water shortage or impacts.  
**Meteorological drought:** Precipitation deficit relative to normal.  
**Agricultural drought:** Insufficient soil moisture for crops or vegetation.  
**Hydrological drought:** Reduced rivers, reservoirs, lakes, or groundwater.  
**Flash drought:** Drought that develops unusually quickly.  
**Snow drought:** Abnormally low mountain snowpack or snow-water storage.  
**Heat wave:** A prolonged period of unusually hot weather.  
**Heat index:** Apparent temperature based on heat and humidity.  
**Wet-bulb temperature:** Temperature reflecting potential evaporative cooling.  
**Wet-bulb globe temperature:** Heat-stress measure incorporating temperature, humidity, wind, and solar radiation.  
**Heat exhaustion:** Heat illness involving weakness, heavy sweating, dizziness, or nausea.  
**Heat stroke:** Life-threatening overheating with altered mental status or other neurological symptoms.  
**Fuel moisture:** Water content of vegetation and burnable material.  
**Spotting:** New fires ignited by wind-carried embers.  
**Pyrocumulus:** Cumulus cloud generated or enhanced by intense fire heating.  
**Pyrocumulonimbus:** Fire-generated thunderstorm.  
**Fire whirl:** Rotating column associated with strong fire heating and wind shear.  
**Dry thunderstorm:** Thunderstorm producing lightning with little rain reaching the ground.  
**Fire Weather Watch:** Alert that critical fire-weather conditions may develop.  
**Red Flag Warning:** Alert that critical fire-weather conditions are occurring or expected shortly.

**Next: Chapter 12 — Weather Maps, Radar, Satellites, and Forecast Models: How Modern Forecasting Works**

<!-- End of source chapter -->

---

# Chapter 12  
## Weather Maps, Radar, Satellites, and Forecast Models: How Modern Forecasting Works

A modern forecast begins with an impossible-sounding task:

Measure an atmosphere that covers the planet, changes every second, extends miles above the surface, and contains countless interacting motions too small and too numerous to observe directly.

Meteorologists manage this by combining several imperfect views:

- Surface stations measure conditions where people live.
- Weather balloons sample the atmosphere vertically.
- Radar watches precipitation and wind near storms.
- Satellites observe broad regions from space.
- Aircraft, ships, and buoys fill important gaps.
- Computer models calculate how the atmosphere may evolve.
- Human forecasters decide what all that evidence actually means.

No instrument sees everything. No model knows the future. Forecasting works because many partial views are combined, compared, corrected, and continuously updated.

The forecast on your phone is the end of a very long argument between physics, observations, computers, and one stubborn atmosphere.

---

# Observing the Atmosphere

## 12.1 The Forecasting Cycle

Weather prediction follows a repeating cycle:

```text
OBSERVE
   ↓
ANALYZE THE CURRENT ATMOSPHERE
   ↓
RUN FORECAST MODELS
   ↓
COMPARE POSSIBLE OUTCOMES
   ↓
CREATE FORECASTS AND WARNINGS
   ↓
OBSERVE WHAT ACTUALLY HAPPENS
   ↓
UPDATE
```

Each new observation improves the estimate of current conditions.

That estimate becomes the starting point for the next forecast.

A poor starting picture can produce a poor prediction even if the model equations are excellent. This is sometimes described as “garbage in, garbage out,” which is not elegant terminology but has survived because it keeps being annoyingly true.

---

## 12.2 Surface Weather Stations

Surface stations measure conditions near the ground.

Common observations include:

- Temperature
- Dew point
- Atmospheric pressure
- Wind direction
- Wind speed
- Visibility
- Cloud cover
- Precipitation
- Present weather

Stations may be operated by:

- National weather agencies
- Airports
- Universities
- Agricultural networks
- Road departments
- Private companies
- Volunteer observers
- Home weather enthusiasts

A station represents one point.

Conditions can differ greatly only a few miles away because of:

- Elevation
- Trees
- Buildings
- Water
- Pavement
- Storm boundaries
- Rainfall variation

One thermometer at the airport is not secretly measuring your backyard through sheer confidence.

---

## 12.3 Automated Airport Observations

Many airports use automated systems to report weather regularly.

These observations are especially important because aviation requires reliable information about:

- Visibility
- Cloud ceilings
- Wind
- Pressure
- Precipitation
- Thunderstorms
- Freezing conditions

Airport observations often appear in coded reports called **METARs**.

A simplified example might look like:

```text
KCAE 191356Z 22008KT 10SM FEW030 29/22 A2994
```

This can be decoded as:

```text
KCAE       station identifier
191356Z    observation on the 19th at 13:56 UTC
22008KT    wind from 220° at 8 knots
10SM       visibility 10 statute miles
FEW030     few clouds at 3,000 feet
29/22      temperature 29°C, dew point 22°C
A2994      pressure 29.94 inches of mercury
```

The code is compact because pilots prefer weather reports to arrive before the aircraft does.

---

## 12.4 Personal Weather Stations

Home weather stations can provide useful neighborhood information.

They may measure:

- Temperature
- Humidity
- Pressure
- Wind
- Rainfall
- Solar radiation

Their accuracy depends heavily on installation.

Common problems include:

- Thermometers in direct sunlight
- Sensors mounted against walls
- Rain gauges beneath trees
- Anemometers blocked by roofs
- Heated pavement nearby
- Poor maintenance
- Insects occupying the rain gauge as though it were desirable real estate

Personal stations are valuable, but unusual readings should be checked against nearby official observations.

---

## 12.5 Ships and Buoys

Much of Earth is ocean, where ordinary weather stations are sparse.

Ships and buoys measure conditions such as:

- Wind
- Pressure
- Air temperature
- Water temperature
- Waves
- Humidity
- Ocean currents

Buoys are especially important for:

- Tropical cyclone monitoring
- Marine forecasting
- Coastal storms
- Ocean-model initialization
- Tsunami detection in specialized networks

A buoy may provide the first direct evidence that a developing ocean storm is strengthening.

It may also stop reporting at the most dramatic moment, because apparently even machinery understands narrative tension.

---

## 12.6 Aircraft Observations

Commercial and research aircraft collect atmospheric information while flying.

Measurements may include:

- Temperature
- Wind
- Pressure
- Turbulence
- Humidity

Aircraft observations help fill gaps between weather-balloon launches.

Special reconnaissance aircraft fly into tropical cyclones and release instrument packages called **dropsondes**.

These measure the atmosphere from the aircraft down toward the ocean.

Aircraft data are especially valuable because much weather develops in the vast spaces between ground stations.

---

# Weather Balloons and Soundings

## 12.7 Radiosondes

A **radiosonde** is a small instrument package carried upward by a weather balloon.

It commonly measures:

- Pressure
- Temperature
- Humidity
- Position

Wind is calculated by tracking the balloon’s movement.

The balloon expands as atmospheric pressure decreases and eventually bursts.

The instrument then descends by parachute.

Regular balloon launches remain essential because they provide a direct vertical profile of the atmosphere—something a surface station cannot do.

---

## 12.8 Why Vertical Profiles Matter

Two days can have the same surface temperature and humidity but completely different weather potential.

The atmosphere above may contain:

- A strong cap
- Very cold air aloft
- A warm layer producing freezing rain
- Strong wind shear
- Dry air
- Deep moisture
- An inversion
- A jet stream

A balloon sounding reveals these layers.

Surface conditions are the first page. The sounding gives you the rest of the plot.

---

## 12.9 The Skew-T Diagram

Meteorologists commonly display balloon data on a graph called a **Skew-T log-P diagram**.

It may look intimidating because it contains:

- Temperature
- Dew point
- Pressure
- Altitude
- Wind speed
- Wind direction
- Stability information
- Moisture layers
- Freezing levels

A simplified reading begins with two lines:

- Temperature
- Dew point

```text
temperature line       dew-point line
       /                       /
      /                       /
     /       dry layer       /
    /                       /
   / lines close together  /
      moist or cloudy layer
```

When the lines are close, the air is moist.

When they are far apart, the air is dry.

The chart can also help forecasters estimate:

- Thunderstorm instability
- Cloud levels
- Hail potential
- Tornado-supporting wind shear
- Precipitation type
- Fog
- Downburst risk

It is not actually trying to frighten beginners. That is simply a side benefit of the graphic design.

---

## 12.10 Wind Barbs

Wind on maps and soundings is often shown with a **wind barb**.

The shaft points toward the direction from which the wind comes.

Feathers indicate speed.

```text
short feather      5 knots
long feather      10 knots
triangle pennant  50 knots
```

For example:

```text
one long + one short feather = 15 knots
two long feathers            = 20 knots
```

A circle with no shaft represents calm wind.

Wind barbs allow a large amount of information to fit into a small space.

---

# Surface Weather Maps

## 12.11 What a Surface Map Shows

A surface analysis map may include:

- High- and low-pressure centers
- Isobars
- Fronts
- Temperature
- Dew point
- Wind
- Cloud cover
- Precipitation
- Visibility
- Pressure tendency

It is a snapshot of the lower atmosphere at a particular time.

```text
        H                 L
      1028              1002
       ↘                ↺
        \          warm front
         \         ◡ ◡ ◡ ◡
          cold front
        ▲ ▲ ▲ ▲
```

The map allows you to see relationships that are difficult to notice from a list of observations.

---

## 12.12 Station Models

A **station model** compresses a local weather observation into symbols around a central circle.

A simplified arrangement is:

```text
temperature          pressure
       72     ◕          146
          ↘
dew point             visibility
       64                 10
```

The center circle shows cloud cover.

The wind barb shows direction and speed.

Other symbols may show:

- Rain
- Snow
- Fog
- Thunderstorms
- Pressure tendency
- Current weather

Station models look cryptic until their layout becomes familiar. Then they become a compact little weather confession.

---

## 12.13 Decoding Pressure

Surface station models often shorten sea-level pressure to three digits.

For example:

```text
146
```

To decode it, place a decimal before the final digit and add either 9 or 10 at the beginning, choosing the value closest to ordinary sea-level pressure.

```text
146 → 1014.6 mb
```

Another example:

```text
872 → 987.2 mb
```

This saves space on crowded maps.

It also provides beginners with a completely unnecessary trust exercise.

---

## 12.14 Isobars

**Isobars** connect locations with equal atmospheric pressure.

They reveal:

- Pressure-system shape
- Pressure gradients
- Wind potential
- Troughs
- Ridges
- Storm development

Closely spaced isobars usually indicate stronger wind.

Widely spaced isobars usually indicate lighter wind.

```text
STRONG GRADIENT

1016  1012  1008  1004

WEAK GRADIENT

1016       1012       1008
```

The wind does not necessarily follow isobars exactly near the ground because friction turns it partly toward lower pressure.

---

## 12.15 Pressure Troughs

A **pressure trough** is an elongated region of relatively low pressure.

It may be marked by:

- Wind shift
- Convergence
- Clouds
- Showers
- Thunderstorms

Not every trough contains a strong temperature contrast.

A cold front is a type of boundary often associated with a trough, but a trough can exist without a clear front.

---

## 12.16 Pressure Ridges

A **ridge** is an elongated area of relatively high pressure.

Surface ridges often bring:

- Sinking air
- Lighter wind
- Drying conditions
- Clearer skies

But a ridge can also trap:

- Fog
- Cold air
- Heat
- Smoke
- Pollution

“High pressure means sunny” is useful beginner shorthand, not a blood oath from the atmosphere.

---

## 12.17 Front Symbols

Common surface-map symbols include:

```text
COLD FRONT
blue triangles pointing forward
────▲────▲────▲────

WARM FRONT
red semicircles pointing forward
────◡────◡────◡────

STATIONARY FRONT
alternating symbols on opposite sides
──▲──◡──▲──◡──

OCCLUDED FRONT
alternating symbols on the same side
──▲◡──▲◡──▲◡──
```

The symbols point in the direction the front is moving.

---

## 12.18 Temperature and Dew-Point Maps

A temperature map shows where air is warm or cold.

A dew-point map often reveals air masses and boundaries more clearly.

For example:

```text
west of boundary: dew point 42°F
east of boundary: dew point 71°F
```

That sharp moisture contrast may identify:

- Cold front
- Dryline
- Sea-breeze boundary
- Outflow boundary

Dew point often exposes what temperature politely tried to conceal.

---

## 12.19 Three-Hour Pressure Change

Pressure-change maps show where pressure has risen or fallen over several hours.

Rapidly falling pressure may indicate:

- Strengthening low pressure
- Approaching storm
- Increasing wind

Rapidly rising pressure may indicate:

- Strong cold-air arrival
- Departing low
- Building high pressure

The pattern matters more than one isolated reading.

---

# Upper-Air Maps

## 12.20 Why Meteorologists Use Pressure Levels

Upper-air charts are commonly drawn on pressure surfaces rather than fixed altitudes.

Important levels include:

- 850 millibars
- 700 millibars
- 500 millibars
- 300 or 250 millibars

The actual altitude of each pressure surface varies.

Warm air columns are thicker than cold air columns, so a particular pressure level sits higher in warm regions and lower in cold regions.

---

## 12.21 The 850-Millibar Chart

The 850-millibar level is commonly around 5,000 feet above sea level, though its height varies.

It is useful for examining:

- Low-level temperature
- Moisture
- Warm-air advection
- Cold-air advection
- Low-level jets
- Rain–snow potential

It is high enough to reduce some surface effects but low enough to represent important near-ground air masses.
