In high terrain, the 850-millibar surface may be underground, which limits its usefulness there. Even weather charts occasionally run directly into a mountain.

---

## 12.22 The 700-Millibar Chart

The 700-millibar level is roughly around 10,000 feet.

Forecasters use it to study:

- Midlevel moisture
- Rising and sinking motion
- Dry layers
- Temperature
- Mountain effects

A dry 700-millibar layer may limit widespread rain or help strengthen downdrafts when precipitation evaporates into it.

---

## 12.23 The 500-Millibar Chart

The 500-millibar level is often near 18,000 feet.

It is one of the most widely used upper-air charts.

It shows:

- Troughs
- Ridges
- Closed lows
- Shortwaves
- General storm movement
- Broad temperature patterns

```text
RIDGE                    TROUGH

      /\                   \  /
     /  \                   \/
```

Weather systems commonly move with the broader midlevel flow, though surface features do not always follow it perfectly.

---

## 12.24 Height Contours

A 500-millibar map usually shows **geopotential height** contours rather than sea-level pressure.

Lower heights are generally associated with:

- Colder air columns
- Upper-level troughs
- Cyclonic flow

Higher heights are generally associated with:

- Warmer air columns
- Upper-level ridges
- Anticyclonic flow

Closely spaced height lines indicate stronger upper-level wind.

---

## 12.25 The 300- and 250-Millibar Charts

These levels are often used to examine the jet stream.

They reveal:

- Jet streaks
- Upper-level divergence
- Strong wind cores
- Troughs and ridges
- Potential storm support

A **jet streak** is a region of especially strong wind within the jet stream.

Certain parts of a jet streak can favor rising air and surface-storm development.

The exact pattern depends on curvature and the surrounding wind field, but the practical lesson is:

A storm below may strengthen when the upper atmosphere efficiently removes air from above it.

---

# Weather Radar

## 12.26 What Radar Does

Radar stands for **radio detection and ranging**.

Weather radar sends pulses of microwave energy into the atmosphere.

Some energy is scattered back by objects such as:

- Raindrops
- Snowflakes
- Hail
- Insects
- Birds
- Dust
- Debris

The radar measures:

- Return strength
- Travel time
- Motion toward or away from the radar
- Characteristics of the targets

The U.S. NEXRAD network consists of roughly 160 high-resolution S-band Doppler radars jointly operated by the National Weather Service, Federal Aviation Administration, and U.S. Air Force. [NCEI](https://www.ncei.noaa.gov/products/radar/next-generation-weather-radar?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

---

## 12.27 Radar Range

Radar energy travels outward in a narrow beam.

The farther it travels, the higher it samples above the ground because:

- Earth curves away
- The beam is usually angled slightly upward
- Atmospheric refraction bends it only partially toward Earth

```text
radar  → beam → → → → higher above ground

      curved Earth surface
_____/________________________
```

Far from the radar, the lowest scan may pass above:

- Shallow storms
- Low-level rotation
- Freezing layers
- Ground-level rain

This is one of radar’s most important limitations.

---

## 12.28 Volume Scans

A weather radar does not stare only at one elevation.

It scans through several angles, producing a three-dimensional sampling volume.

A complete set of elevation scans is called a **volume scan**.

Different scanning strategies may be used depending on whether conditions are:

- Quiet
- Rainy
- Convective
- Severely stormy

Rapid scanning improves storm monitoring but involves tradeoffs in coverage and data quality.

---

## 12.29 Reflectivity

**Reflectivity** measures the strength of returned radar energy.

It is commonly displayed in units of **dBZ**.

Higher reflectivity usually indicates larger or more numerous targets.

Possible interpretations include:

| Reflectivity tendency | Possible target |
|---|---|
| Very low | Light snow, drizzle, insects |
| Moderate | Rain or snow |
| High | Heavy rain |
| Very high | Hail, intense rain, melting snow |

Color scales vary among radar applications.

A red pixel in one app may not represent exactly the same dBZ value as red in another.

Never infer storm severity from color drama alone. Some apps dress an ordinary shower as though it has just entered professional wrestling.

---

## 12.30 Base Reflectivity

**Base reflectivity** shows return strength from one radar elevation angle.

It is useful for examining:

- Storm structure
- Precipitation intensity
- Fine boundaries
- Hail cores
- Hook echoes
- Snow bands

The lowest elevation scan is often most useful near the radar because it samples conditions closest to the ground.

---

## 12.31 Composite Reflectivity

**Composite reflectivity** displays the strongest reflectivity detected anywhere in the vertical column above each location.

It can reveal intense precipitation aloft that the lowest scan may miss.

But it may exaggerate what is reaching the ground.

For example:

- Hail exists high in the storm
- Heavy snow is melting or evaporating below
- Strong echoes are far above the surface

Composite reflectivity answers:

“What is the strongest target anywhere above this point?”

It does not always answer:

“What is falling on the point?”

---

## 12.32 Doppler Velocity

Doppler radar detects motion toward or away from the radar.

It does not directly measure every wind direction.

```text
green or cool colors:
motion toward radar

red or warm colors:
motion away from radar
```

The exact display convention varies, but the toward–away principle remains.

Velocity is useful for finding:

- Rotation
- Strong wind
- Fronts
- Outflow boundaries
- Wind shifts
- Downbursts

---

## 12.33 Base Velocity

**Base velocity** shows radial motion at one radar elevation.

A storm rotating near the radar may show adjacent regions moving:

- Toward the radar
- Away from the radar

```text
toward radar | away from radar
    →→→      |      ←←←
       rotation
```

The spacing, strength, altitude, persistence, and storm context all matter.

Two opposing colors touching on a phone screen is not automatically a tornado. Occasionally it is just the color scale flirting with panic.

---

## 12.34 Storm-Relative Velocity

A moving storm creates broad background motion in velocity data.

**Storm-relative velocity** subtracts an estimate of storm movement, making internal circulations easier to see.

This can highlight:

- Mesocyclones
- Tornado circulations
- Embedded rotations
- Rear-inflow jets

Its accuracy depends on choosing a reasonable storm motion.

A bad motion estimate can distort the display.

---

## 12.35 Range Folding and Aliasing

Radar velocity has limits.

When wind motion exceeds the radar’s unambiguous measurement range, values may wrap into the opposite color.

This is called **aliasing** or **range folding** in certain contexts.

Forecasters recognize these patterns using:

- Surrounding velocity
- Multiple scans
- Radar-processing algorithms
- Storm structure

A bizarre patch of color may represent extreme wind—or a measurement that has folded over on itself like a cheap lawn chair.

---

# Dual-Polarization Radar

## 12.36 Horizontal and Vertical Pulses

Traditional radar primarily examined returned energy in one orientation.

Dual-polarization radar sends and receives energy in both horizontal and vertical orientations.

This provides information about the:

- Shape
- Size
- Orientation
- Uniformity

of targets.

Dual polarization improves the ability to distinguish rain, hail, snow, biological targets, clutter, and tornado debris, and it can improve precipitation estimation. [National Weather Service](https://www.weather.gov/bmx/radar_dualpol?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

---

## 12.37 Differential Reflectivity

**Differential reflectivity**, or ZDR, compares horizontal and vertical returned energy.

It helps infer target shape.

### Positive ZDR

Often suggests horizontally flattened objects, such as larger raindrops.

### Near-zero ZDR

May suggest nearly spherical targets, including:

- Small droplets
- Tumbling hail
- Some ice particles

ZDR can help identify:

- Drop size
- Hail
- Melting zones
- Rainfall characteristics

---

## 12.38 Correlation Coefficient

**Correlation coefficient**, commonly abbreviated CC or ρhv, measures how similarly the horizontal and vertical radar signals behave.

High values usually indicate a uniform type of precipitation.

Lower values may indicate a mixture of shapes and sizes, such as:

- Melting snow
- Hail mixed with rain
- Birds or insects
- Tornado debris

A low correlation coefficient collocated with strong rotation and reflectivity can help identify lofted debris.

---

## 12.39 Specific Differential Phase

**Specific differential phase**, or KDP, measures how the phase difference between horizontal and vertical signals changes along the radar beam.

It can help estimate heavy rainfall, especially when:

- Rain is intense
- Hail is mixed with rain
- The signal is partially weakened

KDP is less directly influenced by radar calibration than ordinary reflectivity.

This makes it valuable for flash-flood forecasting.

---

## 12.40 Hydrometeor Classification

Radar algorithms combine dual-polarization variables to estimate what kind of target is present.

Possible categories include:

- Light rain
- Heavy rain
- Snow
- Wet snow
- Ice crystals
- Graupel
- Hail
- Biological targets
- Ground clutter

These classifications describe what exists at the radar beam’s height.

Precipitation may change before reaching the ground—for example, snow may melt into rain below the beam. [National Weather Service](https://www.weather.gov/jan/dualpolupgrade-applications?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

---

## 12.41 The Melting Layer

As snowflakes fall into above-freezing air, they begin to melt.

Partly melted flakes strongly reflect radar energy, creating a band of enhanced reflectivity called the **bright band**.

On radar, this can be mistaken for heavier precipitation.

Dual-polarization data help identify the melting layer.

The bright band is not a ring of unexpectedly aggressive rain. It is snow undergoing a career transition.

---

## 12.42 Tornado Debris Signature

A tornado debris signature may appear when radar detects irregular objects lofted into the air.

A convincing signature commonly combines:

- Strong low-level rotation
- Reflectivity
- Low correlation coefficient
- Consistent storm movement

It strongly suggests a tornado is causing damage.

But its absence does not prove no tornado exists.

A tornado may be:

- Too weak to loft substantial debris
- Too far from the radar
- In an area with little available debris
- Below the sampled beam

---

# Radar Artifacts and False Echoes

## 12.43 Ground Clutter

Radar energy may reflect from:

- Buildings
- Hills
- Towers
- Trees

This creates **ground clutter**.

Clutter usually remains stationary and is often strongest near the radar.

Filtering removes much of it, but not always perfectly.

---

## 12.44 Anomalous Propagation

Unusual temperature and moisture layers can bend the radar beam more strongly toward the ground.

This is called **anomalous propagation**, or AP.

It may create false precipitation echoes from distant ground targets.

AP often appears:

- At night
- Beneath strong inversions
- Near coasts
- During stable weather

The radar may insist it is raining where the sky has made no such arrangement.

---

## 12.45 Biological Targets

Radar can detect:

- Birds
- Bats
- Insects

Large migration events may produce broad radar echoes, especially near sunrise or sunset.

Insects also collect along convergence boundaries, allowing radar to reveal:

- Fronts
- Sea breezes
- Outflow boundaries

Sometimes the radar is mapping weather by observing where bugs have been pushed, which is both ingenious and mildly insulting to the bugs.

---

## 12.46 Virga

Radar may detect precipitation that evaporates before reaching the ground.

This is especially common:

- In dry climates
- Beneath high cloud bases
- During early storm development
- With light snow

Always compare radar with:

- Surface observations
- Humidity
- Cloud-base height
- Reports from the ground

Radar detects objects aloft, not dampness on your shoes.

---

## 12.47 Beam Blockage

Mountains and other terrain can partially or completely block a radar beam.

This produces:

- Missing data
- Reduced coverage
- Artificially weak echoes
- Blind sectors

Mountain regions often require several radars, satellite data, observations, and numerical guidance to compensate.

---

# Satellite Meteorology

## 12.48 Why Satellites Matter

Satellites observe enormous areas where ground instruments are sparse.

They are essential for tracking:

- Clouds
- Tropical cyclones
- Wildfire smoke
- Fog
- Snow cover
- Dust
- Volcanic ash
- Moisture
- Lightning
- Ocean and land temperatures

The GOES-R series provides advanced imagery, atmospheric measurements, and continuous lightning mapping over much of the Western Hemisphere. [Goes-R](https://www.goes-r.gov/mission/mission.html?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

---

## 12.49 Geostationary Satellites

A geostationary satellite orbits above the equator at roughly 22,300 miles, or 35,800 kilometers.

It moves at the same rotational rate as Earth and therefore appears fixed above one longitude.

This allows continuous observation of the same broad region. [Goes-R](https://www.goes-r.gov/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

Geostationary satellites are especially useful for:

- Rapidly changing storms
- Hurricanes
- Cloud motion
- Severe-weather development
- Wildfires
- Short-term forecasting

---

## 12.50 Polar-Orbiting Satellites

Polar-orbiting satellites travel much closer to Earth and pass near the poles.

As Earth rotates beneath them, they eventually observe most of the planet.

Advantages include:

- Finer detail
- Better views of high latitudes
- Atmospheric temperature and moisture measurements
- Ocean and land monitoring

Their disadvantage is that they do not continuously watch the same place.

Geostationary satellites stare. Polar orbiters patrol.

---

## 12.51 Visible Imagery

Visible satellite imagery measures reflected sunlight.

It resembles a black-and-white photograph of Earth.

It is useful for identifying:

- Cloud texture
- Snow cover
- Smoke
- Dust
- Storm boundaries
- Fog
- Cumulus growth

Visible imagery works only when sunlight is available. [National Weather Service](https://www.weather.gov/mrx/sattype?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

### Brighter areas may include

- Thick clouds
- Snow
- Ice
- Dense smoke

### Darker areas may include

- Ocean
- Forest
- Thin cloud
- Cloud shadows

Snow and cloud can look similar, but snow remains fixed while clouds move.

---

## 12.52 Infrared Imagery

Infrared satellite imagery measures emitted thermal energy.

It works day and night.

Colder objects generally appear differently from warmer ones according to the selected color scale.

High cloud tops are cold.

Low clouds and the ground are usually warmer.

Infrared imagery is useful for estimating:

- Cloud-top temperature
- Thunderstorm depth
- Hurricane convection
- Nighttime cloud cover
- Broad weather-system structure

Very cold cloud tops often indicate deep convection, but the coldest cloud is not automatically producing the most severe surface weather.

---

## 12.53 Enhanced Infrared

Enhanced infrared imagery assigns colors to ranges of cloud-top temperature.

This makes very cold thunderstorm tops easier to recognize.

It may highlight:

- Overshooting tops
- Rapidly cooling clouds
- Tropical convection
- Mesoscale convective systems

The color palette is artificial.

Purple does not mean the cloud has become spiritually dangerous. It represents a temperature interval.

---

## 12.54 Water-Vapor Imagery

Water-vapor imagery senses radiation associated mainly with moisture in the middle and upper atmosphere.

It reveals:

- Moist plumes
- Dry slots
- Upper-level circulation
- Troughs
- Jet-stream disturbances
- Tropical moisture transport

Dark areas often indicate drier air at the sampled level.

Bright areas often indicate higher moisture or high cloud.

Water-vapor imagery does not directly show all water vapor through the entire atmosphere.

A dark image does not prove the surface air is dry.

---

## 12.55 True-Color and Natural-Color Imagery

Modern satellite channels can be combined to create images resembling natural color.

These can show:

- Clouds
- Smoke
- Dust
- Snow
- Ocean color
- Vegetation
- Burn scars

Other combinations are deliberately false-color so particular features stand out.

A false-color image is not fake.

It is translated into colors the human eye can interpret more easily.

---

## 12.56 Fog Products

At night, visible imagery is unavailable.

Satellite channels can be compared to distinguish low cloud and fog from the warmer ground.

Forecasters combine these products with:

- Surface visibility
- Temperature
- Dew point
- Terrain
- Sunrise trends

Satellite fog detection may struggle beneath higher clouds because the low layer is hidden.

The fog is still there. It has simply acquired a roof.

---

## 12.57 Fire Detection

Satellites can detect thermal signals from active fires.

They can also track:

- Smoke plumes
- Burn scars
- Fire growth
- Pyrocumulus
- Wind-driven smoke movement

Small or obscured fires may be missed.

Cloud, terrain, sensor resolution, and satellite timing affect detection.

---

## 12.58 Dust and Volcanic Ash

Special satellite products help identify airborne dust and volcanic ash.

These hazards matter for:

- Aviation
- Air quality
- Visibility
- Transportation
- Solar energy
- Health

Volcanic ash can damage aircraft engines, so satellite monitoring is critically important even far from the eruption.

---

# Lightning Detection

## 12.59 Ground-Based Lightning Networks

Ground networks detect radio signals emitted by lightning.

They can estimate:

- Flash location
- Time
- Polarity
- Peak current
- Flash type

These data help forecasters monitor:

- Storm intensity
- Fire ignitions
- Lightning trends
- Hazard location

Detection is very good but not perfect.

---

## 12.60 Geostationary Lightning Mapper

The **Geostationary Lightning Mapper**, or GLM, observes total lightning from space, including much lightning within clouds.

It provides continuous lightning information across a broad region. [Goes-R](https://www.goes-r.gov/mission/mission.html?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

Rapidly increasing lightning activity can indicate a strengthening updraft.

Lightning data are especially useful where radar coverage is limited, such as:

- Oceans
- Remote regions
- Radar gaps

---

## 12.61 Lightning Jumps

A sudden increase in total lightning is sometimes called a **lightning jump**.

It may occur as a storm’s updraft rapidly intensifies.

This can precede:

- Hail growth
- Stronger wind
- Severe storm development

A lightning jump is one clue, not an automatic severe-weather alarm.

Forecasters combine it with radar, satellite, and the storm environment.

---

# Numerical Weather Prediction

## 12.62 What a Weather Model Is

A numerical weather prediction model is a computer program that represents the atmosphere mathematically.

It divides the atmosphere into a three-dimensional grid.

At each grid location, it estimates variables such as:

- Temperature
- Pressure
- Wind
- Humidity
- Clouds
- Precipitation

The model then calculates how those variables change through time using physical equations.

Numerical weather prediction begins with observations assimilated into the model to estimate the current atmospheric state. [NCEI](https://www.ncei.noaa.gov/products/weather-climate-models/numerical-weather-prediction?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

---

## 12.63 The Model Grid

Imagine placing a three-dimensional lattice over Earth.

```text
horizontal grid:

+---+---+---+---+
|   |   |   |   |
+---+---+---+---+
|   |   |   |   |
+---+---+---+---+

with many vertical layers above each square
```

The distance between grid points is called **grid spacing** or approximate resolution.

A smaller grid spacing can represent smaller features, but it requires much greater computing power.

---

## 12.64 What Models Cannot Resolve

A model cannot explicitly represent features much smaller than its grid.

These may include:

- Individual clouds
- Small thunderstorms
- Street-level urban effects
- Narrow valleys
- Small lakes
- Tornadoes
- Individual wind gusts

Unresolved processes must be approximated using **parameterizations**.

This is one reason a model might forecast widespread afternoon thunderstorm potential without accurately placing each storm.

The model understands the party conditions. It has not seen the seating chart.

---

## 12.65 Parameterization

Parameterization estimates the combined effect of small-scale processes.

Models may parameterize:

- Cloud formation
- Convection
- Turbulence
- Surface exchange
- Radiation
- Soil moisture
- Snow
- Vegetation

Different parameterization choices can lead to different forecasts.

This helps explain why two models starting from similar observations may produce different rainfall or temperature predictions.

---

## 12.66 Initial Conditions

Before a forecast begins, the model needs the best possible estimate of the current atmosphere.

This estimate is called the **analysis**.

It combines:

- Surface observations
- Weather balloons
- Aircraft data
- Satellite measurements
- Radar
- Ships
- Buoys
- Previous model forecasts

The process of combining these is called **data assimilation**. NOAA defines it as combining observations and model information to create the best estimate of the Earth system’s present state. [EPIC](https://epic.noaa.gov/10-year-strategy-for-data-assimilation/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

---

## 12.67 Why the Starting State Is Imperfect

Observations are incomplete because:

- Stations are unevenly distributed
- Instruments have error
- Oceans have fewer measurements
- Some variables are indirect estimates
- The atmosphere changes between observation times
- Small-scale features go unsampled

Data assimilation fills gaps using physics and previous forecasts.

The resulting analysis is excellent but not exact.

Weather prediction begins from a carefully educated approximation.

---

## 12.68 Chaos and Forecast Error

The atmosphere is a chaotic system.

Tiny differences in the starting state can grow over time.

This is often popularly called the **butterfly effect**.

It does not mean a butterfly directly creates a hurricane.

It means that sufficiently small uncertainties can eventually lead to large differences in a nonlinear system.

Forecast error generally increases with time because:

- Initial uncertainty grows
- Model approximations accumulate
- Small-scale events influence larger patterns

This places a practical limit on precise long-range weather prediction.

---

# Major Model Types

## 12.69 Global Models

Global models cover the entire planet.

They are useful for:

- Large-scale weather patterns
- Tropical cyclones
- Jet streams
- Week-ahead forecasting
- Boundary conditions for regional models

Because they cover Earth, they generally use coarser grids than small regional models.

No weather system develops in isolation, so even a forecast for South Carolina benefits from calculations over the Pacific, Arctic, and Atlantic.

The atmosphere has dreadful respect for jurisdictional boundaries.

---

## 12.70 Regional Models

Regional models cover a smaller area at finer resolution.

They may better represent:

- Terrain
- Coastlines
- Thunderstorms
- Local wind
- Snow bands
- Sea breezes

They require information from a global model along their outer boundaries.

Errors in the larger model can therefore enter the regional forecast.

---

## 12.71 Convection-Allowing Models

A **convection-allowing model**, or CAM, uses a fine enough grid to represent many thunderstorm-scale motions more directly rather than relying entirely on traditional convective parameterization.

These models can help forecast:

- Storm mode
- Squall lines
- Supercell potential
- Heavy-rain placement
- Convective timing

They still cannot predict individual storms perfectly.

A simulated storm at 4:00 p.m. ten miles west of town should be interpreted as evidence of a favorable pattern—not a reservation under the storm’s name.

---

## 12.72 Deterministic Models

A deterministic forecast is one model run using one set of starting conditions.

It produces one scenario.

For example:

```text
Model A:
cold front passes at 6 p.m.
storms form near the city
```

This is easy to display but can hide uncertainty.

One run may look extremely precise while being precisely wrong.

---

## 12.73 Ensemble Forecasts

An **ensemble** consists of many model runs.

Each member begins with slightly different starting conditions, model physics, or both.

```text
member 1: storm tracks north
member 2: storm tracks over city
member 3: storm tracks south
member 4: weaker storm
member 5: faster storm
```

The spread among members estimates forecast uncertainty.

Ensembles help answer:

- How confident is the track?
- What is the chance of rain?
- Could temperatures fall below freezing?
- What is the range of snowfall?
- Is an extreme outcome plausible?

---

## 12.74 Ensemble Mean

The **ensemble mean** averages all members.

It can provide a useful broad pattern.

But averaging can blur important features.

Suppose ten members predict a narrow heavy-rain band in slightly different places.

The average may show a broad area of light rain that no member actually forecast.

The average can become a fictional compromise—meteorology by committee.

---

## 12.75 Ensemble Spread

**Spread** describes how much ensemble members differ.

### Small spread

Members generally agree.

Confidence may be higher.

### Large spread

Members disagree substantially.

Confidence is lower.

However, small spread does not guarantee accuracy.

All members may share the same bias or starting error.

Agreement is reassuring. It is not divine revelation.

---

## 12.76 Probability Forecasts

Ensembles can produce probabilities.

Examples include:

- 70% chance of measurable rain
- 30% chance of six inches of snow
- 10% chance of hurricane-force wind
- 60% chance of freezing temperatures

Probability communicates uncertainty more honestly than a single yes-or-no forecast.

It does not mean the predicted event will occur across that percentage of the area unless the product specifically says so.

---

## 12.77 Percentiles

Ensemble output may be presented using percentiles.

For example:

- 10th percentile snowfall
- Median snowfall
- 90th percentile snowfall

A simplified interpretation:

```text
10th percentile: reasonable low outcome
50th percentile: middle outcome
90th percentile: reasonable high outcome
```

This helps users plan for a range rather than obsess over one exact number.

---

# Common Forecast Models

## 12.78 Model Names

Forecasters may refer to models by acronyms or nicknames.

Examples include:

- American global models
- European global models
- Canadian models
- High-resolution regional models
- Hurricane-specific models
- Ensemble systems

Operational model configurations change over time, so the important skill is not memorizing a permanent league table.

It is understanding what each type of model is designed to do.

A model’s reputation does not make every run correct. Even the glamorous European one occasionally arrives wearing the wrong storm track.

---

## 12.79 Model Biases

Models can develop recurring tendencies, called biases.

Examples may include:

- Too warm near snow cover
- Too fast with shallow cold air
- Too much light precipitation
- Too little fog
- Storms initiating too early
- Tropical cyclones too strong or weak

Biases can change after model upgrades.

Forecasters monitor current performance rather than relying forever on weather folklore from three software versions ago.

---

## 12.80 Model Consistency

Forecasters compare a model with its previous runs.

### Consistent forecast

The model repeatedly shows a similar solution.

### Inconsistent forecast

The predicted storm changes track, speed, or strength from run to run.

Consistency can raise confidence, but not always.

A model can be consistently wrong because of a persistent bias.

Still, a storm track leaping 200 miles between runs is a useful hint not to engrave the snowfall total yet.

---

## 12.81 Model Agreement

When several independent models show similar outcomes, confidence may increase.

But models are not fully independent.

They may share:

- Observations
- Physical assumptions
- Boundary data
- Similar numerical methods

Agreement matters, but it should be interpreted alongside:

- Ensembles
- Observed trends
- Known biases
- Forecast lead time

---

# Artificial Intelligence and Weather Forecasting

## 12.82 Data-Driven Models

Modern weather centers are increasingly experimenting with and integrating data-driven or AI-based forecast systems.

These systems learn patterns from large historical atmospheric datasets.

Some can produce global predictions much faster than traditional numerical models.

NOAA has described data assimilation as relevant to both traditional numerical prediction and emerging AI forecast systems, and current research indicates that some data-driven systems can rival traditional models for certain forecasting tasks. [EPIC](https://epic.noaa.gov/10-year-strategy-for-data-assimilation/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

---

## 12.83 AI Does Not Eliminate Physics

AI weather systems still depend on:

- High-quality observations
- Historical reanalyses
- Traditional model data
- Careful validation
- Physical consistency checks

Potential advantages include:

- Speed
- Lower computational cost
- Pattern recognition
- Large ensemble generation

Challenges include:

- Rare extremes
- Changing climate conditions
- Physical consistency
- Explainability
- Predicting variables absent from training
- Dependence on training-data quality

The likely future is not “AI replaces meteorology.”

It is meteorologists using physics-based models, AI systems, observations, and ensembles together—because apparently forecasting needed even more opinions in the room.

---

# Forecast Time Scales

## 12.84 Nowcasting

**Nowcasting** focuses on the present through the next few hours.

It relies heavily on:

- Radar
- Satellite
- Lightning
- Surface observations
- Rapidly updating models
- Storm reports

Nowcasting is essential for:

- Thunderstorms
- Tornadoes
- Fog
- Snow squalls
- Flash floods
- Wind shifts

A twelve-hour-old model forecast becomes less important when radar shows the storm currently making other arrangements.

---

## 12.85 Short-Range Forecasting

Short-range forecasts generally cover the next few days.

They benefit from:

- Dense observations
- Frequent model runs
- High-resolution guidance
- Better knowledge of current systems

Forecast skill is usually highest in this period, though small thunderstorms and narrow snow bands remain difficult.

---

## 12.86 Medium-Range Forecasting

Medium-range forecasts extend several days to roughly a week or more.

They focus increasingly on broad patterns:

- Troughs and ridges
- Storm tracks
- Temperature trends
- Rain opportunities
- Tropical development

Exact timing and local amounts become less certain.

At this range, “a storm system may affect the region” is often more trustworthy than “rain begins at 2:17 p.m. beside your hydrangea.”

---

## 12.87 Extended Outlooks

Outlooks beyond roughly a week describe changing probabilities rather than detailed local weather.

They may indicate increased chances of:

- Above-normal temperature
- Below-normal temperature
- Wetter conditions
- Drier conditions

They should not be read as daily forecasts.

A two-week warm outlook does not mean every afternoon will be warm.

---

## 12.88 Seasonal Outlooks

Seasonal outlooks estimate probabilities for average temperature and precipitation over weeks or months.

They do not predict:

- Individual storms
- Specific snow days
- Exact hurricane landfalls
- Daily temperature sequences

A wetter-than-normal winter could consist of:

- Many light events
- A few major storms
- Mostly rain
- Mostly snow
- One extremely wet month

Climate outlooks tilt the odds. They do not write the calendar.

---

# Human Forecasting

## 12.89 What Forecasters Add

A human forecaster does more than choose a model.

Forecasters:

- Analyze current observations
- Identify model errors
- Compare ensembles
- Account for local terrain
- Evaluate storm evolution
- Interpret uncertainty
- Coordinate warnings
- Communicate risk
- Adjust forecasts as conditions change

Local knowledge matters.

A model may struggle with:

- Cold air trapped against mountains
- Sea-breeze thunderstorms
- Valley fog
- Urban heat
- Lake-effect bands
- Coastal wind shifts

Experienced forecasters know where the local atmosphere keeps its favorite tricks.

---

## 12.90 Forecast Collaboration

Forecasting involves collaboration among:

- Local weather offices
- National prediction centers
- Aviation forecasters
- River forecast centers
- Hurricane specialists
- Severe-weather specialists
- Emergency managers
- Broadcast meteorologists
- Researchers

A hurricane forecast, for example, may involve specialists analyzing:

- Track
- Intensity
- Storm surge
- Rainfall
- Tornadoes
- Waves
- Local evacuation impacts

The forecast seen by the public condenses an enormous coordinated process.

---

## 12.91 Forecast Discussion

Many weather services publish a technical forecast discussion explaining:

- Current conditions
- Model differences
- Forecaster reasoning
- Key uncertainties
- Expected hazards

These discussions can be among the most useful products for serious weather watchers.

They reveal not only what is forecast, but why.

A phrase such as “confidence is low due to model spread” tells you more than an app icon showing a determined little snowflake.

---

# Understanding Forecast Probability

## 12.92 Probability of Precipitation

The **probability of precipitation**, or PoP, expresses the chance that measurable precipitation will occur at a particular point during the forecast period.

A 40% chance of rain does not mean:

- It will rain for 40% of the day
- Rain will cover exactly 40% of the county
- Rain will fall at 40% intensity

It means the forecast probability of measurable precipitation at the specified location is 40%.

---

## 12.93 Area Forecasts

When a broad forecast zone is discussed, precipitation may be described using coverage terms such as:

- Isolated
- Scattered
- Numerous
- Widespread

These refer generally to spatial coverage rather than the chance at one exact point.

Forecast displays sometimes mix probability and coverage language, which is why reading the actual percentage is usually safer than trying to decode whether “scattered” sounds emotionally threatening.

---

## 12.94 Temperature Forecasts

Forecast high and low temperatures describe expected values for a location or representative area.

Actual readings may differ because of:

- Elevation
- Urban heat
- Cloud cover
- Wind
- Soil moisture
- Nearby water
- Local shading

A forecast of 90°F does not mean every thermometer will stop obediently at 90.

It is an estimate of the representative air temperature under standard measurement conditions.

---

## 12.95 “Feels Like” Temperature

Forecast applications may show:

- Heat index
- Wind chill
- Apparent temperature

These are calculated estimates of human thermal stress.

They are not measurements of actual air temperature.

Different services may use slightly different methods.

Always note which index is being displayed.

---

# Why Forecasts Change

## 12.96 New Observations Arrive

A forecast may change because real observations show that:

- A front is faster
- Moisture is greater
- Cold air is deeper
- Clouds are thicker
- A storm is stronger
- Wind shear differs from expectations

Updating a forecast is not evidence that forecasting failed.

Refusing to update after the atmosphere changes would be the actual failure.

---

## 12.97 Small Track Changes Matter

A shift of only 25–50 miles can transform:

- Heavy snow into sleet
- Storm surge into offshore wind
- Tornado risk into stable rain
- Heavy rain into dry weather
- Hurricane eyewall impact into a near miss

The regional forecast may have been broadly correct while the local outcome changes dramatically.

Weather systems are large. Hazard boundaries can be narrow.

---

## 12.98 Timing Errors

A model may forecast the correct event but mistime it.

For example:

- Front arrives four hours early
- Storm develops after sunset rather than afternoon
- Rain ends before freezing temperatures arrive
- Clouds clear later than expected

Timing affects temperature, instability, travel, and hazard severity.

A forecast can be geographically excellent and temporally mischievous.

---

## 12.99 Model Upgrades

Forecast models and observing systems are regularly upgraded.

Changes may include:

- Finer grids
- Better physics
- New observations
- Improved data assimilation
- More ensemble members
- Faster update cycles

Performance must be evaluated across many weather situations.

An upgrade may improve some forecasts while temporarily exposing new biases in others.

This is software deployment, except the production environment is the planet.

---

# Reading Radar Wisely

## 12.100 Do Not Chase the Exact Edge

Radar precipitation edges may differ from what reaches the ground because of:

- Beam height
- Evaporation
- Wind carrying precipitation
- Processing delay
- App refresh delay
- Radar location

A shower shown two miles away may already have reached you—or may evaporate entirely.

Radar is near-real-time, not clairvoyant glass.

---

## 12.101 Check the Loop

A single radar image shows location.

A loop shows:

- Direction
- Speed
- Growth
- Weakening
- Rotation
- Training
- New development

Trend is often more useful than color.

Ask:

- Is the storm strengthening?
- Is the line accelerating?
- Are new cells forming upstream?
- Is the circulation tightening?
- Is rain repeatedly crossing the same area?

The atmosphere is a movie. One screenshot can frame it rather unfairly.

---

## 12.102 Know the Radar Location

Velocity is measured relative to the radar.

To interpret toward and away motion, you need to know where the radar sits.

A storm east of the radar behaves differently in velocity display from the same wind pattern west of it.

Radar apps that hide the radar site make amateur velocity interpretation much harder than it needs to be.

---

## 12.103 Use Warnings, Not DIY Diagnosis

Radar is useful for awareness.

It should not replace official warnings.

Professionals use:

- Multiple radar products
- Several elevation angles
- Environmental data
- Satellite
- Lightning
- Spotter reports
- Nearby radars
- Training and experience

A phone image cannot always show:

- Low-level beam height
- Aliasing
- Radar artifacts
- Storm history
- Warning coordination

Use radar to understand the warning—not to overrule it from the sofa with one thumb and a dangerous amount of confidence.

---

# Reading Model Maps Wisely

## 12.104 One Run Is Not a Forecast

A single model image is one possible scenario.

Before trusting it, ask:

- Do other models agree?
- Do ensembles support it?
- Has it been consistent?
- How far into the future is it?
- Does the model handle this weather type well?
- What do forecasters say?

A dramatic ten-day snowfall map is generally closer to atmospheric fan fiction than actionable guidance.

---

## 12.105 Avoid Exact Totals Too Early

Long-range model maps may display exact amounts such as:

```text
7.3 inches snow
2.84 inches rain
104°F temperature
```

The decimal does not indicate certainty.

It is merely the exact output of an uncertain calculation.

Precision and accuracy are different things.

A model can be wrong to two decimal places with tremendous dignity.

---

## 12.106 Look for Signals

At longer ranges, focus on broad signals:

- Stormy pattern
- Cold-air outbreak
- Heat ridge
- Tropical development
- Heavy-rain potential
- Increased severe-weather setup

As the event approaches, the forecast can narrow toward:

- Timing
- Track
- Amounts
- Local impacts

Start broad. Add detail as confidence earns it.

---

# A Practical Forecasting Routine

## 12.107 Step 1: Check Current Conditions

Look at:

- Temperature
- Dew point
- Pressure
- Wind
- Cloud cover
- Radar
- Satellite

Ask what is happening now.

A forecast built without checking the present is like giving directions without knowing where you started.

---

## 12.108 Step 2: Find the Large-Scale Pattern

Identify:

- Nearest high and low
- Fronts
- Upper-level trough or ridge
- Jet stream
- Moisture source
- Storm track

This explains why weather is changing.

---

## 12.109 Step 3: Examine Local Ingredients

For thunderstorms:

- Moisture
- Instability
- Lift
- Wind shear

For winter weather:

- Temperature profile
- Moisture
- Lift
- Surface temperature

For fog:

- Temperature–dew-point spread
- Wind
- Cloud cover
- Ground moisture

For heat:

- Ridge
- Soil dryness
- Humidity
- Nighttime lows

Forecast by ingredients rather than by memorizing icons.

---

## 12.110 Step 4: Compare Guidance

Compare:

- Official forecast
- Forecast discussion
- Ensemble probabilities
- Model trends
- Radar and satellite evolution

Do not count model names as votes without considering their similarity and purpose.

Five nearly related models agreeing is not necessarily five independent witnesses.

---

## 12.111 Step 5: Identify Uncertainty

Ask:

- What could make the forecast wetter?
- What could make it drier?
- Could the front arrive earlier?
- Is shallow cold air being underestimated?
- Could storms remain isolated?
- Could clouds limit heating?
- Is there a narrow high-impact band?

The best forecast is not the one pretending uncertainty vanished.

It is the one telling you which uncertainty matters.

---

## 12.112 Step 6: Monitor Trends

As the event approaches, watch whether observations match expectations.

Examples:

- Dew points rising as forecast
- Cold air arriving faster
- Thunderstorms developing farther west
- Snow band becoming more organized
- Hurricane turning earlier
- Fog spreading after sunset

Forecast confidence should change when reality begins voting.

---

# Field Observation  
## Decode a Surface Map

Find a current surface weather map.

Identify:

1. The nearest high-pressure center
2. The nearest low-pressure center
3. Any fronts
4. The tightest isobar spacing
5. The broad wind direction
6. The warmest and coldest air
7. The highest dew points
8. Areas likely receiving precipitation

Then compare your interpretation with radar and satellite imagery.

A surface map gives you the structure.

Radar and satellite show how the structure is expressing itself.

---

# Try It  
## Read a Radar Loop

Watch a radar loop covering at least one hour.

Record:

- Direction of movement
- Speed
- Growth or weakening
- New cell development
- Areas receiving repeated rain
- Boundaries moving without precipitation

Pause at the beginning and predict where rain will be in thirty minutes.

Then watch the rest of the loop.

You will discover that extrapolation works fairly well until storms begin growing, weakening, splitting, or otherwise remembering that they are not colored stickers sliding across a map.

---

# Try It  
## Compare Visible and Infrared Satellite Images

During daylight, compare visible and infrared satellite images.

Find:

- Low clouds
- High clouds
- Snow
- Thunderstorms
- Clear ground

Notice:

- Visible imagery shows texture better.
- Infrared imagery shows temperature differences.
- Thin high cloud may look bright and cold in infrared.
- Low fog may resemble the ground in infrared but stand out in visible imagery.

Repeat after sunset.

Visible imagery disappears, while infrared continues working.

---

# Try It  
## Compare a Model With Reality

Choose a forecast-model image valid 24 hours ahead.

Save or note its prediction for:

- Rain location
- Temperature
- Wind
- Front position

The next day, compare it with observations.

Ask:

- Was the pattern correct?
- Was timing wrong?
- Was the storm misplaced?
- Was precipitation too broad?
- Did local terrain matter?
- Did later model runs improve?

This teaches the difference between using models and worshipping screenshots.

---

# Field Exercise  
## Build Your Own Forecast

For tomorrow, write a five-sentence forecast:

1. Current pattern
2. Expected overnight change
3. Tomorrow’s temperature
4. Rain or storm chance
5. Main uncertainty

Example:

> High pressure is moving offshore while warm, humid air returns from the south. Clouds should increase overnight, keeping temperatures mild. Tomorrow will be warmer, with highs near 82°F. Scattered thunderstorms may develop late in the afternoon as a weak front approaches. The biggest uncertainty is whether morning cloud cover will limit instability.

Compare it with what happens.

Do this repeatedly and your forecasts will improve—not because you develop psychic powers, but because your mistakes become specific enough to learn from.

---

# Weather Safety  
## Do Not Let the Map Replace the Warning

During dangerous weather:

- Follow official alerts
- Know your location
- Use multiple information sources
- Keep alerts enabled
- Do not rely on social-media screenshots
- Check timestamps
- Distinguish watches from warnings
- Move to shelter before investigating every radar detail

A beautiful radar image is still an image of something that may be attempting to remove your roof.

Admire it later.

---

# Chapter Summary

Modern forecasting combines observations, radar, satellites, numerical models, ensembles, and human judgment.

Surface stations measure local conditions, while weather balloons reveal temperature, moisture, and wind through the depth of the atmosphere.

Surface maps use station models, pressure systems, isobars, and fronts to show the structure of current weather.

Upper-air maps reveal troughs, ridges, jet streams, temperature patterns, and disturbances that guide surface systems.

Weather radar detects reflected energy from precipitation and other atmospheric targets.

Reflectivity estimates target strength, while Doppler velocity measures motion toward or away from the radar.

Dual-polarization radar provides information about target shape and uniformity, helping distinguish rain, snow, hail, biological echoes, clutter, and tornado debris.

Radar beams rise above the ground with distance, so low-level weather may be missed far from the radar.

Geostationary satellites continuously watch broad regions, while polar-orbiting satellites provide closer global observations.

Visible imagery shows reflected sunlight, infrared imagery estimates thermal characteristics, and water-vapor imagery reveals moisture and circulation mainly in the middle and upper atmosphere.

Numerical weather models calculate atmospheric change on three-dimensional grids.

Models begin from an imperfect analysis created through data assimilation.

Smaller-scale processes must sometimes be approximated, and forecast errors grow with time because the atmosphere is chaotic.

Global models describe broad patterns, regional models add detail, and convection-allowing models better represent many thunderstorm structures.

Ensemble forecasting uses multiple model runs to estimate probability and uncertainty.

Artificial-intelligence forecasting is becoming an important additional tool, but it still depends on observations, historical data, validation, and meteorological understanding.

Forecasts change when new observations arrive or uncertainty resolves.

A model run is guidance, not prophecy.

A radar color is evidence, not a diagnosis.

The best weather watcher begins with the present atmosphere, examines the larger pattern, compares several sources, and remains willing to change their mind.

That final part is also useful outside meteorology, but we will try not to become preachy.

---

## Weather-Watcher’s Vocabulary

**Surface station:** A location that measures weather near the ground.  
**METAR:** A coded aviation surface-weather observation.  
**Radiosonde:** An instrument package carried upward by a weather balloon.  
**Sounding:** A vertical profile of atmospheric conditions.  
**Skew-T:** A chart displaying temperature, dew point, pressure, wind, and atmospheric stability.  
**Wind barb:** A symbol showing wind direction and speed.  
**Station model:** A compact symbolic display of a surface weather observation.  
**Isobar:** A line connecting equal atmospheric pressure.  
**Pressure trough:** An elongated area of relatively low pressure.  
**Geopotential height:** The altitude of a pressure surface adjusted for gravity.  
**Radar:** A system that sends energy pulses and measures returned signals from atmospheric targets.  
**Reflectivity:** Radar measurement of returned energy strength.  
**dBZ:** The common logarithmic unit used for radar reflectivity.  
**Base velocity:** Radar measurement of motion toward or away from the radar at one elevation.  
**Dual polarization:** Radar transmission and reception in horizontal and vertical orientations.  
**Differential reflectivity:** A radar variable describing target shape differences.  
**Correlation coefficient:** A measure of how similarly horizontal and vertical radar signals behave.  
**Specific differential phase:** A radar variable useful for estimating rainfall and target characteristics.  
**Bright band:** Enhanced radar return from melting snow.  
**Ground clutter:** Radar echoes from stationary objects near the ground.  
**Anomalous propagation:** Unusual radar-beam bending that produces false or distorted echoes.  
**Geostationary satellite:** A satellite that remains over approximately the same point on Earth.  
**Polar-orbiting satellite:** A low-orbiting satellite that observes different parts of Earth on successive passes.  
**Visible imagery:** Satellite imagery based on reflected sunlight.  
**Infrared imagery:** Satellite imagery based on emitted thermal radiation.  
**Water-vapor imagery:** Satellite imagery emphasizing middle- and upper-atmospheric moisture patterns.  
**Data assimilation:** The process of combining observations and model information to estimate the current atmosphere.  
**Numerical weather prediction:** Computer simulation of future atmospheric conditions using mathematical equations.  
**Grid spacing:** The distance between calculation points in a weather model.  
**Parameterization:** Approximation of processes too small or complex for a model to resolve directly.  
**Deterministic forecast:** One model run producing one scenario.  
**Ensemble:** A collection of model runs used to estimate uncertainty.  
**Ensemble spread:** The amount of disagreement among ensemble members.  
**Nowcasting:** Forecasting weather over the present and next few hours.  
**Probability of precipitation:** The forecast chance of measurable precipitation at a location during a specified period.

**Next: Chapter 13 — Making Your Own Forecast: A Practical Daily Method**

---

# Chapter 13  
## Making Your Own Forecast: A Practical Daily Method

Forecasting does not begin with guessing tomorrow’s temperature.

It begins with understanding what the atmosphere is doing now.

A useful forecast is built in layers:

- Current conditions
- Large-scale pattern
- Local ingredients
- Timing
- Uncertainty
- Expected impacts

You do not need a meteorology degree to make a sensible short-range forecast. You need a repeatable method, a few reliable maps, and enough humility to notice when the atmosphere is making you look silly.

It will. That is part of the arrangement.

---

# The Forecasting Mindset

## 13.1 Start With a Question

A forecast should answer a specific question.

Examples:

- Will it rain this afternoon?
- Could thunderstorms become severe?
- Will roads freeze tonight?
- Will fog reduce visibility by morning?
- How hot will the house become if the power fails?
- Is outdoor work safe tomorrow?
- Will the cold front arrive before sunset?

The more specific the question, the more useful the forecast.

“Will the weather be bad?” is too vague.

Bad for what?

A breezy 45°F day may be unpleasant for a picnic, excellent for running, dangerous for a small boat, and utterly irrelevant to a conference call.

---

## 13.2 Forecast the Hazard, Not Just the Weather

Instead of asking only:

> Will it rain?

Ask:

- How hard?
- For how long?
- At what time?
- Will lightning occur?
- Could roads flood?
- Will wind accompany it?
- Is there a safe window before or after?

Instead of asking:

> Will it snow?

Ask:

- When will pavement temperatures fall?
- Will sleet mix in?
- Could snow rates overwhelm road treatment?
- Will wind reduce visibility?
- Will temperatures rise above freezing afterward?

A forecast becomes valuable when it connects atmospheric conditions with decisions.

---

## 13.3 Use More Than One Source

A practical daily forecast may use:

- Official forecast
- Surface observations
- Radar
- Satellite
- Weather maps
- Forecast discussion
- Ensemble guidance
- Local knowledge

Do not rely entirely on:

- One phone app
- One radar frame
- One social-media post
- One model run
- One unusually confident person standing beside a dramatic map

Confidence is not an atmospheric variable.

---

# Step 1: Observe the Weather Now

## 13.4 Look Outside First

Before opening a model, examine the sky.

Notice:

- Cloud type
- Cloud movement
- Visibility
- Wind
- Sunlight
- Precipitation
- Ground condition
- Changes during the past hour

The current sky may reveal:

- A front arriving earlier than forecast
- Fog lifting
- Cumulus growing rapidly
- High clouds thickening
- Dry air limiting clouds
- Storm outflow approaching

The atmosphere is already running. Check the live performance before reading tomorrow’s rehearsal notes.

---

## 13.5 Record the Basic Variables

A simple observation includes:

| Variable | What it tells you |
|---|---|
| Temperature | Current warmth or cold |
| Dew point | Actual atmospheric moisture |
| Relative humidity | Closeness to saturation |
| Pressure | Position and trend of weather systems |
| Wind direction | Source and movement of air |
| Wind speed | Pressure gradient and mixing |
| Cloud cover | Heating, moisture, and lift |
| Visibility | Fog, haze, smoke, or precipitation |

The trend matters as much as the current value.

A pressure of 29.90 inches may mean little by itself.

A pressure falling rapidly from 30.15 to 29.90 tells a story.

---

## 13.6 Compare Temperature and Dew Point

The difference between temperature and dew point is called the **dew-point depression**.

```text
temperature − dew point
=
dew-point depression
```

A small difference means the air is near saturation.

A large difference means the air is relatively dry.

Example:

```text
temperature: 68°F
dew point:   66°F
difference:   2°F
```

Fog, low clouds, or dew may be possible if cooling continues.

Another example:

```text
temperature: 92°F
dew point:   54°F
difference:  38°F
```

The air is much drier.

Thunderstorms may struggle unless deeper moisture arrives.

---

## 13.7 Watch Pressure Trend

Pressure tendency can reveal approaching changes.

### Falling pressure

May indicate:

- Approaching low pressure
- Strengthening storm
- Front nearing
- Increasing wind

### Rising pressure

May indicate:

- Departing low
- Building high
- Cold front already passed
- Improving weather

### Rapid change

Often matters more than the exact number.

A sharp pressure rise behind a front may accompany:

- Gusty wind
- Temperature drop
- Clearing
- Dry-air arrival

---

## 13.8 Watch Wind Direction

Wind shows where the air is coming from.

Ask:

- Is it coming from land or water?
- From a warm or cold region?
- From a humid or dry region?
- Is it shifting?
- Is it strengthening?

A wind shift may mark:

- Front
- Sea breeze
- Outflow boundary
- Dryline
- Pressure trough

A sudden cool gust from a new direction during a hot afternoon often means thunderstorm outflow is approaching, even if the rain is still distant.

---

# Step 2: Identify the Large-Scale Pattern

## 13.9 Find the Nearest High and Low

On a surface map, locate:

- High-pressure centers
- Low-pressure centers
- Fronts
- Troughs
- Closely spaced isobars

Then ask:

- Is the low approaching or departing?
- Are you in the warm sector?
- Is high pressure building?
- Is the pressure gradient tightening?

These features explain the broad wind and weather trend.

### Illustration 1: Basic surface-pattern reading

```text
               WARM FRONT
          ◡ ◡ ◡ ◡ ◡
                /
               L
             ↺
              \
         ▲ ▲ ▲ ▲ ▲
          COLD FRONT

H lies farther west behind the front
```

A location east of the cold front may expect warm humid air and storms.

A location west of it may expect cooler drier air.

---

## 13.10 Find the Upper-Level Trough or Ridge

A 500-millibar chart can reveal the large-scale pattern.

### Trough approaching

Often suggests:

- Increasing lift
- Cooler air aloft
- Storm development
- Unsettled weather

### Ridge building

Often suggests:

- Sinking air
- Warming
- Drier conditions
- Heat if persistent

The exact surface weather depends on moisture and local conditions.

A ridge over moist ground may produce heat and afternoon storms.

A trough crossing dry air may produce little rain.

The upper-air feature creates the opportunity. The lower atmosphere decides whether to use it.

---

## 13.11 Examine the Jet Stream

Look for:

- Strong wind cores
- Jet streaks
- Curved flow
- Upper divergence
- Shortwaves

A surface low may strengthen when upper-level flow supports rising air.

A storm may weaken when it loses that support.

You do not need to calculate every jet-streak circulation to use this idea.

A practical question is:

> Is the upper atmosphere helping air rise over my region?

If yes, clouds and precipitation become more likely—provided moisture exists.

---

## 13.12 Check the Moisture Source

Moisture may arrive from:

- Gulf
- Atlantic
- Pacific
- Nearby lake
- Wet soil
- Tropical system
- Low-level jet

Look for:

- Rising dew points
- Southerly wind
- Moisture plumes
- Low clouds
- Increasing precipitable water
- Warm humid air ahead of a front

A strong weather system without moisture may produce wind and clouds but little precipitation.

A weak disturbance in very moist air may produce heavy rain.

The lift starts the process. The moisture sets the budget.

---

# Step 3: Forecast by Ingredients

## 13.13 The Ingredient Method

Different weather hazards require different combinations of ingredients.

Instead of memorizing hundreds of situations, ask whether the necessary ingredients are present.

This approach is especially useful for:

- Thunderstorms
- Winter weather
- Fog
- Heat
- Wind
- Flooding

---

# Forecasting Thunderstorms

## 13.14 Thunderstorm Ingredients

Thunderstorms require:

```text
MOISTURE + INSTABILITY + LIFT
```

Severe thunderstorms also benefit from:

```text
WIND SHEAR
```

Ask four questions:

1. Is low-level moisture present?
2. Is the atmosphere unstable?
3. Is there a trigger?
4. Is wind shear strong enough to organize storms?

---

## 13.15 Moisture

Useful signs include:

- High dew point
- Moist southerly wind
- Low clouds
- Moisture returning after a front
- High precipitable water

A surface dew point in the upper 60s or 70s Fahrenheit often supports vigorous warm-season storms, though local climate matters.

Dew point alone does not guarantee storms.

It provides fuel.

---

## 13.16 Instability

Instability increases when:

- Surface air warms
- Low-level moisture increases
- Air aloft becomes colder
- A cap weakens
- Clouds clear and sunshine returns

Useful indicators include:

- CAPE
- Steep lapse rates
- Rapid cumulus growth
- Very warm humid surface air
- Cold air aloft

Morning cloud cover may reduce heating.

But clouds can also clear unexpectedly, allowing instability to rise rapidly.

A cloudy morning does not always cancel severe weather. Sometimes it merely delays the appointment.

---

## 13.17 Lift

Potential triggers include:

- Cold front
- Dryline
- Warm front
- Sea breeze
- Outflow boundary
- Mountain
- Upper-level shortwave
- Surface heating

A day may contain abundant instability and no storms because the cap never breaks.

Another day may have modest instability but strong lift, producing widespread storms.

---

## 13.18 Wind Shear

Wind shear helps determine storm organization.

### Weak shear

More likely:

- Pulse storms
- Short-lived cells
- Local downbursts

### Moderate or strong shear

More likely:

- Multicells
- Squall lines
- Supercells
- Persistent rotation

Storm mode matters because it changes the main hazard.

A supercell may favor large hail and tornadoes.

A solid line may favor widespread damaging wind.

---

## 13.19 Estimate Storm Timing

Thunderstorm timing often depends on:

- Maximum heating
- Front arrival
- Cap erosion
- Shortwave passage
- Boundary collision
- Overnight low-level jet

Common patterns include:

### Afternoon pulse storms

Peak heating from midafternoon into evening.

### Cold-front storms

Develop along or ahead of the advancing boundary.

### Nocturnal storms

Strengthen after dark as the low-level jet increases moisture and convergence.

### Morning storms

May be remnants of an overnight complex.

Do not assume thunderstorms require the hottest hour of the day.

The atmosphere works nights, weekends, and most holidays.

---

## 13.20 Watch Cumulus Behavior

Cumulus clouds can reveal whether convection is succeeding.

### Healthy growing cumulus

- Crisp edges
- Darkening bases
- Increasing vertical growth
- Repeated towers in the same area

### Struggling cumulus

- Flat shallow tops
- Fraying edges
- Rapid evaporation
- Little vertical growth

A field of rapidly growing cumulus along a boundary is a sign to check radar and alerts.

---

# Forecasting Fog

## 13.21 Fog Ingredients

Fog becomes more likely when:

- Air is moist
- Temperature approaches dew point
- Wind is light
- Skies are clear or partly clear
- Ground is wet
- Cold air settles into low terrain

Different fog types require different setups.

---

## 13.22 Radiation Fog

Radiation fog is favored by:

- Clear night
- Light wind
- Moist ground
- Small temperature–dew-point spread
- Long nighttime cooling
- Valleys or low areas

Too much wind mixes the air and prevents a shallow saturated layer.

No wind at all may limit mixing of moisture upward.

A light breeze often works best.

The atmosphere appreciates moderation in fog production, if nowhere else.

---

## 13.23 Advection Fog

Advection fog is favored when:

- Warm moist air crosses colder water or land
- Wind is present
- The cold surface extends over a broad area

Unlike radiation fog, it may persist through daytime.

Coastal areas are especially vulnerable.

---

## 13.24 Fog Burn-Off

Fog usually dissipates when:

- Sun warms the ground
- Wind increases
- Drier air arrives
- Clouds mix the layer
- Temperature rises above the dew point

Dense fog may persist longer beneath:

- Overcast
- Inversion
- Light wind
- Cold ground

Forecasting the formation time is often easier than forecasting the exact moment visibility improves.

Fog is reluctant to leave once it has made itself comfortable.

---

# Forecasting Winter Precipitation

## 13.25 Start With the Full Temperature Profile

Do not forecast precipitation type from surface temperature alone.

Ask:

1. Does snow form aloft?
2. Is there a warm layer?
3. How warm and deep is it?
4. Is there a cold layer below?
5. How deep is the cold layer?
6. What is the road temperature?

```text
cold all the way down     → snow
warm layer + deep cold    → sleet
warm layer + shallow cold → freezing rain
warm to ground            → rain
```

---

## 13.26 Surface Temperature

Surface temperature affects:

- Road icing
- Accumulation
- Melting
- Ice accretion
- Transition timing

But a surface temperature of 34°F does not rule out snow.

Heavy snow can cool the air and accumulate on elevated or grassy surfaces.

A surface temperature of 30°F does not guarantee snow.

A warm layer aloft may produce freezing rain.

---

## 13.27 Wet-Bulb Temperature

During precipitation onset, evaporation may cool the air toward the wet-bulb temperature.

If the air is dry, temperature can fall several degrees.

This can turn:

- Rain into snow
- Rain into freezing rain
- Wet roads into icy roads

A forecast should consider not only the current temperature but how much cooling is possible once precipitation begins.

---

## 13.28 Ground and Pavement Temperature

Accumulation depends on:

- Recent air temperature
- Sun angle
- Road treatment
- Traffic
- Surface type
- Snow rate
- Time of day

A road warmed by sunlight may resist accumulation at first.

A shaded bridge may freeze quickly.

Snowfall rate can eventually overwhelm a warm surface.

The flakes are not impressed by yesterday’s high once enough of them arrive together.

---

## 13.29 Snow Bands

To assess heavy snow-band potential, look for:

- Strong frontogenesis
- Deep moisture
- Instability aloft
- Slow movement
- Deformation zone
- Terrain enhancement

A narrow band can produce drastically higher totals than surrounding areas.

Forecast ranges should account for this.

---

# Forecasting Heat

## 13.30 Heat Ingredients

Extreme heat becomes more likely with:

- Strong upper ridge
- Clear skies
- Dry soil
- Weak wind
- Downslope flow
- High humidity
- Warm nights
- Urban heat storage

Dry heat and humid heat stress the body differently, but both can be dangerous.

---

## 13.31 Forecast the Overnight Low

The nighttime minimum may matter as much as the afternoon maximum.

Warm nights prevent recovery.

Check:

- Dew point
- Cloud cover
- Wind
- Urban location
- Soil moisture
- Air conditioning availability

High dew points limit nighttime cooling.

Clouds can also trap heat.

A forecast of 98°F with a low of 79°F may be more dangerous than a hotter day followed by a cool night.

---

## 13.32 Soil Moisture

Dry soil favors higher daytime temperature because less energy is used for evaporation.

Wet soil may limit daytime heat but increase humidity.

This can create two different kinds of discomfort:

- Hotter and drier
- Slightly cooler but more humid

The thermometer and the human body may disagree about which one is worse.

The human body usually wins that argument by sweating on the evidence.

---

# Forecasting Strong Wind

## 13.33 Wind Ingredients

Strong surface wind becomes more likely when:

- Pressure gradient is tight
- Strong wind exists aloft
- Daytime mixing is deep
- Cold air arrives behind a front
- Terrain accelerates flow
- Thunderstorms produce downdrafts

Check:

- Isobar spacing
- 850-millibar wind
- Temperature lapse rate
- Cloud cover
- Front timing
- Local terrain

---

## 13.34 Mixing Down Strong Wind

Strong wind above the ground may reach the surface when the lower atmosphere becomes turbulent.

Daytime sunshine often deepens mixing.

Cold air moving over warmer ground can also create turbulence.

A forecast may therefore call for stronger gusts in the afternoon than in the morning, even if the large-scale pressure gradient changes little.

---

## 13.35 Momentum Transfer in Showers

Rain showers can transport stronger wind from aloft downward.

A day with modest sustained wind may produce sharp gusts beneath showers.

This is common behind cold fronts.

Dark low clouds and sudden bursts of rain may mark stronger mixing.

---

# Forecasting Heavy Rain and Flooding

## 13.36 Flood Ingredients

Heavy rain becomes more dangerous when:

- Atmospheric moisture is high
- Storms move slowly
- Rain repeats over one area
- Soil is saturated
- Terrain is steep
- Drainage is poor
- Rivers are already high

Ask:

- How much rain is forecast?
- Over what period?
- Has it rained recently?
- Will storms train?
- Is the area urban or mountainous?
- Are burn scars present?

---

## 13.37 Precipitable Water

**Precipitable water** estimates the depth of liquid water that would result if all vapor in a vertical atmospheric column condensed.

High values indicate a moist atmosphere.

They do not guarantee heavy rain, but they increase the potential when lift and storm organization are favorable.

A very moist atmosphere with slow storm motion deserves attention.

---

## 13.38 Storm Motion

Slow storms produce higher local totals.

Training occurs when new cells form upstream and move over the same location.

On radar, look for:

- Repeated cells following one path
- Boundary parallel to storm motion
- New development on the upstream end
- Rain totals increasing quickly

The storm’s forward movement may be modest even while individual cells move rapidly.

---

# Step 4: Use Radar and Satellite

## 13.39 Radar Answers “Where and How Fast?”

Use radar to determine:

- Precipitation location
- Motion
- Growth
- Weakening
- Storm structure
- Training
- Boundaries

Always watch a loop rather than one frame.

A loop reveals whether the forecast is verifying.

---

## 13.40 Satellite Answers “What Is Developing?”

Visible satellite can reveal:

- Clearing
- Cumulus growth
- Cloud boundaries
- Smoke
- Fog
- Snow cover

Infrared imagery can reveal:

- Cooling cloud tops
- Deepening storms
- High clouds
- Nighttime development

Water-vapor imagery can reveal:

- Dry air
- Moist plumes
- Upper disturbances
- Troughs

Radar may show precipitation only after a cloud has developed.

Satellite often shows the cloud’s early attempts.

---

## 13.41 Look Upstream

Weather usually arrives from somewhere.

Examine conditions upstream along the expected wind or storm track.

Ask:

- Is the front stronger there?
- Are dew points rising?
- Are storms forming?
- Is cloud cover clearing?
- Is cold air deeper than expected?
- Is pressure rising rapidly?

Upstream observations often provide the most practical short-term forecast.

Tomorrow’s weather may currently be inconveniencing someone two states away.

---

# Step 5: Compare Forecast Guidance

## 13.42 Start With the Official Forecast

The official forecast should be your baseline because it combines:

- Observations
- Models
- Local knowledge
- Human analysis
- Coordination

Then use other information to understand:

- Why
- When
- How certain
- What could change

Do not treat the official forecast as merely one random opinion beside twelve screenshots from strangers.

---

## 13.43 Read the Forecast Discussion

A forecast discussion often explains:

- Model disagreements
- Timing uncertainty
- Severe-weather ingredients
- Winter precipitation concerns
- Confidence level
- Local terrain effects

Useful phrases include:

### “High confidence”

Models and observations strongly support the forecast.

### “Low confidence”

Important uncertainty remains.

### “Conditional threat”

A hazard becomes possible only if a key ingredient develops.

### “Bust potential”

The event could underperform substantially.

### “High-end scenario”

A less likely but more severe outcome.

These phrases are not hedging. They are the forecast’s honesty showing.

---

## 13.44 Compare Ensembles

Use ensembles to examine:

- Range of outcomes
- Confidence
- Extreme possibilities
- Timing spread
- Track spread

For snow, compare:

- Median
- Low-end outcome
- High-end outcome
- Probability of threshold amounts

For rain, compare:

- Probability of measurable rain
- Probability of flooding totals
- Storm-position spread

For temperature, compare:

- Freeze probability
- Record-heat probability
- Overnight-low range

---

## 13.45 Do Not Model-Shop

Model-shopping means selecting whichever model gives the result you prefer.

Examples:

- Snow lover chooses the snowiest model
- Vacationer chooses the driest model
- Storm enthusiast chooses the most violent simulation
- Event planner chooses the one run with a sunny gap

This is not forecasting.

It is atmospheric online shopping with no return policy.

Use the full range and ask why one outcome is more likely.

---

# Step 6: Write the Forecast

## 13.46 A Useful Forecast Structure

A clear personal forecast can use five parts:

1. Pattern
2. Timing
3. Conditions
4. Hazards
5. Uncertainty

Example:

> A cold front will approach from the west during the afternoon. Warm, humid air ahead of it should allow scattered thunderstorms to develop after about 3 p.m. Any storm could produce heavy rain and lightning, with a smaller risk of damaging wind. Storms should move east during the evening, followed by cooler and drier air overnight. The main uncertainty is how much morning cloud cover limits afternoon instability.

This is more useful than:

> High 86. Fifty percent chance of storms.

The second forecast is not wrong. It is merely underdressed.

---

## 13.47 Include Decision Windows

A decision-oriented forecast might say:

> Outdoor work should be safest before 1 p.m. Thunderstorm risk increases from midafternoon through early evening. Conditions should improve after 8 p.m., though wet ground may remain.

This gives the user:

- Safe period
- Risk period
- Improvement time
- Remaining impact

A weather forecast should help someone choose what to do.

Otherwise it is just atmosphere-themed trivia.

---

## 13.48 Separate Most Likely From Worst Reasonable

Example:

### Most likely

Scattered storms with brief heavy rain.

### Reasonable high-end outcome

A few storms produce damaging wind and localized flooding.

### Less likely outcome

Morning clouds prevent most storm development.

This makes uncertainty useful rather than vague.

---

## 13.49 Use Ranges

Ranges are often more honest than exact values.

Examples:

- High 84–87°F
- Rainfall one-half to one inch
- Snow three to six inches
- Front arriving between 4 and 7 p.m.

The range should reflect real uncertainty, not laziness.

An absurdly broad range such as “zero to twelve inches of snow” is technically inclusive and practically useless.

---

# Step 7: Verify and Learn

## 13.50 Compare Forecast With Reality

After the event, ask:

- Did the front arrive on time?
- Was temperature too high or low?
- Did storms form where expected?
- Was moisture deeper?
- Did the cap hold?
- Did fog lift late?
- Did the snow band shift?
- Did wind gust more than expected?

Do not merely decide whether the forecast was “right.”

Identify why it succeeded or failed.

That is how skill improves.

---

## 13.51 Keep a Forecast Journal

A simple forecast journal may contain:

| Date | Forecast | Main reasoning | Confidence | Outcome | Lesson |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|

Example:

| Date | Forecast | Main reasoning | Outcome | Lesson |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| June 12 | Afternoon storms likely | High dew point, front approaching | Only one shower | Cap stronger than expected |
| Jan. 4 | Snow changing to sleet | Warm layer moving north | Verified | Sounding trend was useful |

Patterns will emerge.

You may discover that you:

- Overestimate rain coverage
- Underestimate fog
- Trust models too much in shallow cold air
- Miss terrain effects
- Forecast thunderstorms too early

Knowing your own bias is almost as valuable as knowing a model’s bias.

Almost.

---

## 13.52 Calibration

A well-calibrated forecaster uses probabilities honestly.

If you issue ten forecasts with a 70% chance of rain, rain should occur about seven times over the long run.

Calibration is not judged from one event.

A 20% event can happen.

A 90% event can fail.

Probability describes uncertainty, not a guarantee.

---

# A Daily Ten-Minute Forecast Routine

## 13.53 Minute 1: Look Outside

Observe:

- Sky
- Wind
- Visibility
- Ground

---

## 13.54 Minute 2: Check Current Numbers

Record:

- Temperature
- Dew point
- Pressure trend
- Wind

---

## 13.55 Minutes 3–4: Review Surface Map

Find:

- High
- Low
- Fronts
- Moisture boundary

---

## 13.56 Minutes 5–6: Review Radar and Satellite

Ask:

- What is moving toward me?
- What is developing?
- What is weakening?

---

## 13.57 Minutes 7–8: Check Official Forecast and Discussion

Identify:

- Main expected weather
- Timing
- Hazard
- Confidence
- Forecaster concern

---

## 13.58 Minute 9: Check Ensemble or Model Range

Look for:

- Agreement
- Spread
- High-end possibility
- Timing differences

---

## 13.59 Minute 10: Write Three Sentences

Use:

1. What is happening
2. What will happen
3. What could change

Example:

> High pressure is moving offshore, and humid air is returning from the south. Clouds will increase tonight, with showers becoming possible near sunrise. The main uncertainty is whether the rain reaches the area before the morning commute.

Ten minutes is enough to create a thoughtful forecast.

It is also short enough that the family may not yet have filed a missing-person report.

---

# A Longer Forecasting Checklist

## 13.60 Current Conditions

- Temperature
- Dew point
- Pressure trend
- Wind
- Clouds
- Visibility
- Recent rainfall

## 13.61 Surface Pattern

- Highs and lows
- Fronts
- Troughs
- Dryline
- Outflow boundaries
- Isobar spacing

## 13.62 Upper-Air Pattern

- Ridge or trough
- Shortwave
- Jet stream
- Temperature aloft
- Wind shear

## 13.63 Moisture

- Surface dew point
- Moisture depth
- Precipitable water
- Moisture source
- Dry-air intrusion

## 13.64 Stability

- CAPE
- Cap
- Lapse rates
- Cloud cover
- Surface heating

## 13.65 Timing

- Front arrival
- Peak heating
- Shortwave passage
- Sunrise or sunset
- Storm motion

## 13.66 Hazards

- Lightning
- Wind
- Hail
- Tornado
- Flood
- Ice
- Heat
- Fog

## 13.67 Uncertainty

- Model spread
- Boundary placement
- Cloud cover
- Moisture return
- Temperature profile
- Storm mode

---

# Common Forecasting Mistakes

## 13.68 Starting With the Model

A model is a forecast of the atmosphere.

It is not the atmosphere.

Begin with observations.

If the model says clear skies but a thick cloud shield already exists, the model has lost the opening argument.

---

## 13.69 Ignoring Dew Point

Temperature tells you how warm the air is.

Dew point tells you how much moisture it contains.

Ignoring dew point can lead to poor forecasts of:

- Fog
- Heat stress
- Thunderstorms
- Overnight temperature
- Cloud base

Humidity is not decorative metadata.

---

## 13.70 Treating Rain Chance as Duration

A 60% chance of rain does not mean six hours of a ten-hour day will be rainy.

It does not describe duration.

Check:

- Expected coverage
- Timing
- Movement
- Rainfall amount

A brief afternoon storm and a six-hour soaking can share the same probability.

That is why the icon alone is such a shameless little minimalist.

---

## 13.71 Assuming Tomorrow Repeats Today

Persistence is useful when the weather pattern is stable.

It fails near:

- Fronts
- Tropical systems
- Strong upper disturbances
- Developing storms
- Air-mass changes

A hot clear afternoon today does not guarantee the same tomorrow if a cold front is six hours away.

---

## 13.72 Overreacting to One Model Run

A dramatic model run may disappear on the next cycle.

Before reacting, ask:

- Is it supported by ensembles?
- Is the pattern plausible?
- Is it consistent?
- Do observations support it?
- Is the lead time too long?

The most exciting model is often the least emotionally responsible one.

---

## 13.73 Ignoring Local Terrain

Mountains, lakes, cities, valleys, and coasts modify forecasts.

Examples:

- Valley fog
- Sea-breeze storms
- Downslope warming
- Mountain snow
- Urban nighttime heat
- Cold-air pooling
