Local effects become more important as the forecast area becomes smaller.

---

## 13.74 Forecasting Exact Thunderstorm Locations Too Early

Thunderstorms depend on small-scale boundaries and rapid interactions.

At longer lead times, forecast:

- General area
- Time window
- Coverage
- Hazard type

As radar trends develop, narrow the location.

An exact storm track six hours before initiation is often confidence dressed as cartography.

---

## 13.75 Ignoring What Could Prevent the Event

Every forecast should include failure modes.

For thunderstorms:

- Cap holds
- Clouds limit heating
- Moisture fails to return

For snow:

- Warm layer arrives
- Storm tracks farther away
- Dry air reduces precipitation

For fog:

- Wind stays too strong
- Clouds prevent cooling
- Drier air arrives

Forecasting is not only asking, “Why will this happen?”

It is also asking, “What could stop it?”

---

# Field Exercise  
## Forecast Tomorrow Morning

Before bed, predict:

- Overnight low
- Fog or no fog
- Cloud cover
- Wind
- Sunrise conditions

Use:

- Temperature–dew-point spread
- Cloud cover
- Wind
- Ground moisture
- Pressure pattern

Check the result at sunrise.

Morning forecasts are useful because overnight cooling exposes many atmospheric relationships clearly.

It also gives you a chance to be wrong before breakfast, which keeps the ego pleasantly manageable.

---

# Try It  
## Forecast a Cold-Front Passage

Track an approaching cold front.

Predict:

- Arrival time
- Wind shift
- Temperature change
- Rain duration
- Pressure trend
- Clearing time

Then record what happens.

A front provides a complete forecasting lesson in one moving boundary.

---

# Try It  
## Make a Thunderstorm Decision Forecast

For an outdoor activity, write:

```text
SAFE WINDOW:
RISK WINDOW:
MAIN HAZARD:
CONFIDENCE:
BACKUP PLAN:
```

Example:

```text
SAFE WINDOW:
8 a.m.–1 p.m.

RISK WINDOW:
2–7 p.m.

MAIN HAZARD:
Lightning and brief heavy rain

CONFIDENCE:
Moderate

BACKUP PLAN:
Move activity indoors if thunder is heard
```

This turns meteorology into an actual decision instead of a decorative weather obsession.

---

# Try It  
## Score Your Forecast

Use a five-point scale.

| Score | Result |
|---:|---|
| 5 | Timing, type, and impact nearly exact |
| 4 | Correct overall with minor timing or amount error |
| 3 | Main event correct, details poor |
| 2 | Partial success |
| 1 | Mostly wrong |
| 0 | Atmosphere filed a formal rebuttal |

Then write one sentence explaining the largest error.

The explanation matters more than the score.

---

# Weather Safety  
## Know When Amateur Forecasting Ends

Personal forecasting is useful for understanding and planning.

It should never replace official warnings for:

- Tornadoes
- Flash floods
- Hurricanes
- Severe thunderstorms
- Blizzards
- Extreme heat
- Wildfire evacuation
- Storm surge

When an official warning is issued:

- Follow it
- Act promptly
- Do not wait for your own forecast to agree
- Do not overrule trained forecasters from one radar image
- Do not remain outside to verify the warning visually

The purpose of learning weather is not to become the person who says, “Technically, I wasn’t convinced,” while debris passes through the room.

---

# Chapter Summary

A useful forecast begins with a specific question and focuses on decisions and hazards.

Current observations should be checked before models.

Temperature, dew point, pressure trend, wind, clouds, and visibility reveal the atmosphere’s present state.

Surface maps show highs, lows, fronts, and pressure gradients.

Upper-air maps reveal troughs, ridges, jet streams, and disturbances controlling larger weather patterns.

Forecasting by ingredients is more reliable than memorizing isolated weather signs.

Thunderstorms require moisture, instability, and lift; organized severe storms also depend on wind shear.

Fog depends on moisture, cooling, wind, and surface conditions.

Winter precipitation requires analysis of the entire temperature profile, not merely the surface reading.

Heat forecasts should consider humidity, soil moisture, urban effects, duration, and nighttime temperature.

Flood forecasts depend on rainfall rate, duration, soil condition, storm movement, terrain, and drainage.

Radar shows where precipitation is and how it is changing.

Satellite imagery reveals cloud development, clearing, moisture, and upper-level structure.

Official forecasts and forecast discussions should form the baseline.

Ensembles help describe ranges and uncertainty.

One model run is a scenario, not a forecast.

A good forecast explains the most likely outcome, the main hazard, the timing, and what could change.

Verification and forecast journals turn mistakes into skill.

The goal is not to predict every cloud perfectly.

It is to understand enough of the atmosphere to make better decisions before the weather makes them for you.

---

## Weather-Watcher’s Vocabulary

**Forecast question:** The specific decision or weather outcome being predicted.  
**Dew-point depression:** The difference between temperature and dew point.  
**Pressure tendency:** The change in atmospheric pressure over time.  
**Ingredient forecasting:** Predicting weather by identifying the physical ingredients required for an event.  
**Conditional threat:** A hazard that depends on a particular ingredient or process developing.  
**Forecast confidence:** The degree of certainty in a predicted outcome.  
**Decision window:** A period when conditions are expected to be safer or more hazardous.  
**Forecast range:** A span of plausible values rather than one exact prediction.  
**High-end scenario:** A less likely but more severe plausible outcome.  
**Forecast verification:** Comparison of a forecast with what actually happened.  
**Calibration:** The long-term accuracy of stated forecast probabilities.  
**Persistence forecast:** A forecast assuming current conditions will continue.  
**Model-shopping:** Selecting only the model output that matches a preferred outcome.  
**Upstream observation:** Weather information from the region where an approaching system is coming from.  
**Failure mode:** A condition that could prevent a forecast event from occurring.  
**Decision forecast:** A forecast written to support a specific practical choice.

**Next: Chapter 14 — Climate Patterns: El Niño, La Niña, the Jet Stream, and Why Seasons Differ**

<!-- End of source chapter -->

---

# Chapter 14  
## Climate Patterns: El Niño, La Niña, the Jet Stream, and Why Seasons Differ

Weather describes what the atmosphere is doing now or over the next several days.

Climate describes the patterns that emerge when weather is observed over much longer periods.

A rainy Tuesday is weather.

A region’s typical wet season is climate.

A single cold winter does not disprove global warming, just as one unusually warm afternoon does not prove it. Climate is found in averages, ranges, frequencies, records, and long-term trends—not in one dramatic encounter with a coat rack.

Between daily weather and century-scale climate lies another important category: **climate variability**.

Large patterns in the oceans and atmosphere can shift rainfall, temperature, storms, drought, and jet-stream behavior for months or even years. El Niño and La Niña are the best-known examples, but they are only part of a much larger system.

---

# Weather, Climate, and Variability

## 14.1 What Is Climate?

Climate is the long-term statistical description of weather in a place or region.

It includes:

- Average temperature
- Average precipitation
- Seasonal cycles
- Typical wind
- Humidity
- Snowfall
- Frequency of extremes
- Length of growing season
- Probability of certain events

Climate is not merely an average.

Two places can have the same annual average temperature but very different climates.

For example:

- One may have mild temperatures year-round.
- Another may have hot summers and severe winters.

The average alone hides the range.

---

## 14.2 Climate Normals

Meteorologists often compare current weather with a **climate normal**.

A climate normal is an average calculated over a standard multi-decade period.

Normals may include:

- Daily high and low temperature
- Monthly rainfall
- Seasonal snowfall
- First and last freeze dates
- Heating and cooling requirements

Normals are reference points, not promises.

A normal high of 80°F does not mean the afternoon temperature is supposed to be exactly 80°F.

It means that 80°F is near the average of many years, most of which were behaving individually.

---

## 14.3 Average Versus Typical

The average can sometimes describe a condition that rarely occurs.

Suppose five winter days have highs of:

```text id="bgjr60"
30°F, 35°F, 55°F, 70°F, 75°F
```

The average is:

```text id="rk48ds"
53°F
```

Yet only one day was close to 53°F.

A useful climate description also considers:

- Median
- Range
- Variability
- Percentiles
- Frequency of extremes

Climate statistics should tell you not only where the middle lies but how far the weather likes to wander from it.

---

## 14.4 Climate Variability

**Climate variability** refers to natural changes in climate conditions over months, years, or decades.

These variations may arise from:

- Ocean circulation
- Atmospheric circulation
- Volcanic eruptions
- Solar cycles
- Land-surface changes
- Sea ice
- Interactions among climate patterns

Climate variability can temporarily reinforce or oppose longer-term climate trends.

A warming climate does not eliminate cold years.

It shifts the background conditions within which individual years occur.

---

# Why Seasons Occur

## 14.5 Earth’s Tilt

Seasons are caused mainly by Earth’s axial tilt, not by its changing distance from the Sun.

Earth’s axis is tilted about 23.5 degrees relative to its orbit.

As Earth travels around the Sun, each hemisphere alternately tilts toward and away from the Sun.

### Illustration 1: Earth’s tilt and seasons

```text id="cnc9f3"
NORTHERN SUMMER

Northern Hemisphere tilted toward Sun
more direct sunlight
longer days


NORTHERN WINTER

Northern Hemisphere tilted away from Sun
less direct sunlight
shorter days
```

When the Northern Hemisphere has summer, the Southern Hemisphere has winter.

Six months later, the arrangement reverses.

---

## 14.6 Sun Angle

A high Sun angle concentrates energy over a smaller surface area.

A low Sun angle spreads the same energy over a larger area.

```text id="9mhcou"
HIGH SUN ANGLE

      ↓↓↓
   concentrated
  ───────────── ground


LOW SUN ANGLE

   ↘   ↘   ↘
 energy spread over
 ───────────────── ground
```

Low-angle sunlight also travels through more atmosphere, increasing scattering and absorption.

This contributes to weaker winter heating.

---

## 14.7 Day Length

Longer days allow more time for solar heating.

Shorter days provide less energy and more time for nighttime cooling.

Near the equator, day length changes relatively little.

At high latitudes, seasonal changes are extreme.

Polar regions experience:

- Very long summer days
- Very short winter days
- Midnight Sun
- Polar night

Day length and Sun angle work together to create the seasonal cycle.

---

## 14.8 Earth–Sun Distance

Earth’s orbit is slightly elliptical.

Earth is actually closest to the Sun during Northern Hemisphere winter and farthest during Northern Hemisphere summer.

This proves distance is not the main cause of seasons.

The effect of axial tilt is much stronger.

The Northern Hemisphere receives winter while marginally closer to the Sun, which is the astronomical equivalent of standing near a heater while facing the wrong direction.

---

## 14.9 Seasonal Lag

The warmest and coldest periods usually occur after the summer and winter solstices.

This delay is called **seasonal lag**.

After the summer solstice:

- Daylight gradually decreases
- But incoming solar energy may still exceed heat loss
- Land and water continue warming

After the winter solstice:

- Daylight gradually increases
- But heat loss may still exceed incoming energy
- Temperatures continue falling

The ocean has a longer seasonal lag than land because water heats and cools slowly.

---

# Land, Water, and Regional Climate

## 14.10 Continental and Maritime Climates

Land changes temperature more quickly than water.

### Continental climate

Usually features:

- Hotter summers
- Colder winters
- Larger daily temperature ranges
- Larger seasonal temperature ranges

### Maritime climate

Usually features:

- Cooler summers
- Milder winters
- Smaller temperature ranges
- More humidity
- Frequent clouds or fog in some regions

A coastal location may have a much milder climate than an inland location at the same latitude.

---

## 14.11 Why Water Changes Temperature Slowly

Water has a high heat capacity.

It also distributes heat through:

- Mixing
- Currents
- Evaporation
- Depth

Sunlight can penetrate below the water surface, while most land heating occurs in a thin upper layer.

This allows oceans and large lakes to store enormous amounts of energy.

They release it gradually, moderating nearby temperatures.

---

## 14.12 Ocean Currents

Ocean currents move heat around the planet.

Warm currents can make nearby coastal climates milder.

Cold currents can produce:

- Cool coastal air
- Fog
- Stable conditions
- Dry coastal climates

Currents are driven by:

- Wind
- Earth’s rotation
- Temperature differences
- Salinity differences
- Continental boundaries
- Ocean-basin shape

The ocean is not a stationary blue background. It is a moving climate system with waves on top.

---

## 14.13 Mountains and Climate

Mountains influence climate by:

- Forcing air upward
- Producing rain shadows
- Blocking cold air
- Redirecting wind
- Creating elevation zones
- Trapping air in valleys

Temperature generally decreases with altitude.

A mountain region may contain several climates within a short horizontal distance.

A dry valley, snowy ridge, and wet forest may all exist within the same county.

---

## 14.14 Latitude

Latitude strongly influences climate because it affects:

- Sun angle
- Day length
- Seasonal variation
- Coriolis effect
- Major circulation zones

Low latitudes receive more direct solar energy on average.

High latitudes receive less direct energy and experience larger seasonal changes in daylight.

But latitude does not determine climate alone.

Elevation, oceans, wind, terrain, and currents can produce major exceptions.

---

# Global Atmospheric Circulation

## 14.15 Uneven Heating

The equator receives more solar energy than the poles.

If Earth did not move heat, the tropics would become much hotter and polar regions much colder.

The atmosphere and oceans transport heat poleward.

This creates global circulation.

A simple nonrotating planet might develop one large circulation cell in each hemisphere.

Earth’s rotation divides the flow into several major zones.

---

## 14.16 The Three-Cell Model

A simplified model divides each hemisphere into:

- Hadley cell
- Ferrel cell
- Polar cell

### Illustration 2: Global circulation cells

```text id="eh9xwn"
90°N     POLAR CELL
          ↓      ↑

60°N  subpolar low

        FERREL CELL
          ↑      ↓

30°N  subtropical high

        HADLEY CELL
          ↓      ↑

0°       equatorial low
```

The Southern Hemisphere has a broadly similar arrangement.

Real circulation is less tidy because of continents, mountains, seasons, and changing weather systems.

Still, the model explains many major climate belts.

---

## 14.17 Hadley Cells

Strong solar heating near the equator causes warm, moist air to rise.

High in the atmosphere, the air moves poleward.

It eventually sinks near 20–35 degrees latitude.

Near the surface, it flows back toward the equator.

This creates the **Hadley circulation**.

Its consequences include:

- Tropical rain belts near rising air
- Subtropical deserts near sinking air
- Trade winds
- Subtropical jet streams

---

## 14.18 The Intertropical Convergence Zone

The **Intertropical Convergence Zone**, or ITCZ, is a belt where tropical trade winds meet.

Air rises, producing:

- Deep thunderstorms
- Heavy rainfall
- Cloud bands
- Light and variable surface wind

The ITCZ shifts north and south with the seasons, generally following the region of strongest heating.

Its movement helps create tropical wet and dry seasons.

### Illustration 3: ITCZ migration

```text id="u19ihp"
NORTHERN SUMMER

ITCZ shifts north
tropical rain belt follows


NORTHERN WINTER

ITCZ shifts south
rain belt moves toward Southern Hemisphere
```

---

## 14.19 Trade Winds

Near the surface, air flowing toward the equator is turned by the Coriolis effect.

This creates:

- Northeast trade winds in the Northern Hemisphere
- Southeast trade winds in the Southern Hemisphere

Trade winds influence:

- Tropical rainfall
- Ocean currents
- Tropical waves
- Hurricane tracks
- El Niño and La Niña

Their name comes from their historical importance to sailing routes and commerce.

---

## 14.20 Subtropical Highs

Air descending in the Hadley circulation creates broad subtropical high-pressure belts.

These regions tend to have:

- Sinking air
- Dry conditions
- Clear skies
- Weak average rainfall

Many of the world’s major deserts lie near 30 degrees latitude.

Examples include parts of:

- Sahara
- Arabian Peninsula
- Australia
- Southern Africa
- Southwestern North America

Not every subtropical location is desert because oceans, currents, mountains, and seasonal wind modify the basic pattern.

---

## 14.21 The Ferrel Cell

The Ferrel cell occupies much of the middle latitudes.

Its average surface winds are the **westerlies**, flowing generally from west to east.

This region contains:

- Fronts
- Midlatitude cyclones
- Jet streams
- Strong seasonal weather changes

Unlike the Hadley cell, the Ferrel cell is driven largely by the activity of weather systems rather than acting as one simple direct overturning circulation.

It is less a giant wheel and more the statistical aftermath of many storms arguing.

---

## 14.22 Polar Cells

Cold dense air sinks near the poles and moves equatorward near the surface.

It rises again near the boundary with warmer midlatitude air.

This creates the polar cell.

Surface winds near the poles are often easterly.

The boundary between polar and midlatitude air is associated with:

- Polar front
- Strong temperature contrasts
- Midlatitude cyclones
- Polar jet stream

---

# Jet Streams and Seasonal Weather

## 14.23 Why the Jet Stream Shifts

The jet stream is linked to horizontal temperature contrasts.

In winter:

- Polar regions become much colder
- Equator-to-pole temperature contrast strengthens
- Jet streams often become stronger
- They tend to shift toward lower latitudes

In summer:

- Temperature contrast weakens
- Jet streams often become weaker
- They shift poleward

This seasonal movement affects storm tracks.

---

## 14.24 A Zonal Jet Stream

A **zonal** pattern has relatively straight west-to-east flow.

```text id="yh1snv"
→ → → → → → → → →
```

This often produces:

- Fast-moving weather systems
- Smaller north–south temperature swings
- Progressive changes
- Shorter-lived extremes

Storms generally keep moving.

The atmosphere behaves almost professionally.

---

## 14.25 A Meridional Jet Stream

A **meridional** pattern has large north–south waves.

```text id="yhmj8l"
      /\            /\
→ →  /  \    → →  /  \
    /    \        /    \
```

This can transport:

- Warm air far north
- Cold air far south
- Moisture into unusual regions
- Storms along slow tracks

Amplified patterns can support:

- Heat waves
- Cold outbreaks
- Flooding
- Drought
- Persistent weather

---

## 14.26 Blocking

A blocking pattern occurs when upper-level circulation becomes slow or nearly stationary.

Blocks can prevent ordinary weather systems from progressing.

A region beneath a block may experience prolonged:

- Heat
- Dryness
- Cold
- Rain
- Smoke
- Storminess

An omega block resembles the Greek letter Ω:

```text id="k2101q"
LOW       HIGH       LOW
  \        /\        /
   \      /  \      /
    \____/    \____/
```

Blocks can last for days or weeks.

The atmosphere occasionally misplaces the fast-forward button.

---

# The Ocean–Atmosphere System

## 14.27 Coupling

The ocean and atmosphere constantly exchange:

- Heat
- Water vapor
- Momentum
- Gases

Wind drives ocean currents.

Ocean temperatures influence:

- Air pressure
- Rainfall
- Thunderstorms
- Storm tracks
- Wind patterns

The atmosphere changes the ocean, and the ocean changes the atmosphere.

This two-way interaction is called **coupling**.

El Niño and La Niña are major examples of a coupled climate pattern.

---

# ENSO

## 14.28 What Is ENSO?

**ENSO** stands for the **El Niño–Southern Oscillation**.

It is a recurring climate pattern involving changes in:

- Tropical Pacific Ocean temperature
- Atmospheric pressure
- Trade winds
- Tropical rainfall
- Global circulation

ENSO has three broad phases:

- El Niño
- La Niña
- Neutral

The complete event is not merely a patch of warm or cold water.

The atmosphere must respond to the ocean.

---

## 14.29 Normal Tropical Pacific Conditions

Under typical neutral conditions:

- Trade winds blow from east to west.
- Warm surface water piles up in the western Pacific.
- Colder deep water rises near South America.
- Thunderstorms favor the warm western Pacific.
- Surface pressure is relatively higher in the east and lower in the west.

### Illustration 4: Neutral tropical Pacific

```text id="plqjfd"
SOUTH AMERICA                          INDONESIA

cooler water      trade winds → → →     warm water
upwelling ↑                              deep thunderstorms
```

The warm western Pacific may have sea level several inches higher than parts of the eastern tropical Pacific because wind continually pushes water westward.

---

## 14.30 Upwelling

**Upwelling** occurs when deeper water rises toward the surface.

Along the eastern tropical Pacific, trade winds help move surface water westward.

Cold nutrient-rich water rises to replace it.

Upwelling supports productive marine ecosystems and fisheries.

When El Niño weakens upwelling, ocean temperatures and marine conditions change substantially.

---

# El Niño

## 14.31 What Is El Niño?

El Niño occurs when the central and eastern tropical Pacific becomes unusually warm and the atmosphere responds.

Typical changes include:

- Weaker trade winds
- Reduced eastern-Pacific upwelling
- Warmer central or eastern Pacific water
- Thunderstorms shifting eastward
- Changing tropical pressure patterns
- Shifts in global jet streams

El Niño events vary in strength, location, duration, and impact.

No two are identical.

El Niño is not one giant weather machine with a single “rain here” button.

---

## 14.32 The Southern Oscillation

The atmospheric part of ENSO is called the **Southern Oscillation**.

It involves changes in pressure between the western and eastern tropical Pacific.

During El Niño:

- Pressure tends to rise in the western Pacific.
- Pressure tends to fall farther east.
- Trade winds weaken.

During La Niña, the pattern generally reverses.

Ocean temperature and atmospheric pressure are therefore linked parts of one system.

---

## 14.33 El Niño Development

A simplified sequence may be:

1. Trade winds weaken.
2. Warm western-Pacific water spreads eastward.
3. Upwelling weakens.
4. Eastern and central Pacific water warms.
5. Thunderstorms shift eastward.
6. Tropical heating alters global circulation.
7. Jet streams and storm tracks respond.

### Illustration 5: El Niño

```text id="to56l2"
SOUTH AMERICA                         INDONESIA

warmer water spreads east
← ← weakened trade winds

more thunderstorms shift toward
central and eastern Pacific
```

The initial weakening of trade winds may involve short-lived events such as strong westerly wind bursts.

---

## 14.34 El Niño and the Jet Stream

El Niño often strengthens and shifts the subtropical jet stream.

This can alter winter storm tracks across North America.

Broad tendencies may include:

- Wetter conditions across parts of the southern United States
- Milder conditions across parts of the northern tier
- Changed Pacific storm paths
- Reduced Atlantic hurricane activity during some El Niño years because of increased wind shear

These are statistical tendencies, not local guarantees.

The exact result depends on:

- Event strength
- Event location
- Other climate patterns
- Random weather variability
- Seasonal timing

---

## 14.35 El Niño Is Not the Same Everywhere

Some El Niño events concentrate the greatest warming in the eastern Pacific.

Others concentrate it farther west or near the central Pacific.

The location of the warmest water affects where tropical thunderstorms form.

That changes the atmospheric wave pattern.

Two events with similar average ocean temperatures can therefore produce different regional weather.

Naming both “El Niño” is useful, but it can hide meaningful differences.

---

## 14.36 Possible Global Effects

El Niño can influence:

- Rainfall
- Drought
- Tropical cyclone activity
- Temperature
- Fisheries
- Agriculture
- Wildfire risk
- Flooding

Possible tendencies include:

- Drier conditions in some western Pacific regions
- Wetter weather in parts of western South America
- Shifts in monsoon behavior
- Increased global average temperature during strong events

Impacts differ by season and region.

El Niño does not travel around personally causing each storm. It changes the large-scale odds.

---

# La Niña

## 14.37 What Is La Niña?

La Niña occurs when the central and eastern tropical Pacific becomes unusually cool and the atmosphere responds.

Typical conditions include:

- Stronger trade winds
- Enhanced eastern-Pacific upwelling
- Cooler central and eastern Pacific water
- Warm water concentrated farther west
- Thunderstorms shifting westward
- Changes in global jet streams

### Illustration 6: La Niña

```text id="ha5us6"
SOUTH AMERICA                         INDONESIA

stronger trade winds → → → → →

enhanced cold upwelling ↑
warm water pushed west
stronger western-Pacific thunderstorms
```

---

## 14.38 La Niña and North American Weather

Broad winter tendencies may include:

- Wetter conditions in parts of the Pacific Northwest
- Drier conditions across parts of the southern United States
- Colder outbreaks in portions of the northern tier
- A more northern storm track
- Increased Atlantic hurricane activity in some years because tropical Atlantic wind shear may be lower

Again, these are tendencies.

A La Niña winter can still produce southern snowstorms, heavy California rain, or unusual warmth in northern areas.

Climate patterns load the dice. They do not control every roll.

---

## 14.39 Multi-Year La Niña

La Niña sometimes persists through more than one winter.

This happens more often than consecutive El Niño events.

A multi-year event may produce repeated broad climate tendencies, but local outcomes can still differ substantially from one year to the next.

Other parts of the climate system continue changing.

---

# Neutral ENSO

## 14.40 Neutral Does Not Mean Normal Weather

ENSO-neutral conditions mean that neither El Niño nor La Niña is strongly established.

Neutral does not mean:

- Average rainfall everywhere
- Quiet hurricane season
- No drought
- No major winter storms
- Normal temperature every month

It means ENSO is not exerting a strong warm- or cool-phase influence.

Other patterns may dominate.

Neutral is the absence of one strong signal, not the atmosphere taking a personal day.

---

# The Walker Circulation

## 14.41 East–West Tropical Circulation

The **Walker circulation** is an east–west atmospheric circulation across the tropical Pacific.

Under neutral or La Niña-like conditions:

- Air rises over warm western-Pacific water.
- Air flows eastward aloft.
- Air sinks over the cooler eastern Pacific.
- Trade winds flow westward near the surface.

```text id="og1fci"
WESTERN PACIFIC                  EASTERN PACIFIC

warm water                       cool water
air rises ↑                      air sinks ↓
         → → flow aloft → →
         ← ← trade winds ← ←
```

El Niño weakens or shifts this circulation.

La Niña generally strengthens it.

---

# How ENSO Is Measured

## 14.42 Sea-Surface Temperature Regions

Scientists monitor several regions of the equatorial Pacific.

One widely discussed area is called **Niño 3.4**, located in the central tropical Pacific.

Average temperature departures there help describe ENSO conditions.

But sea temperature alone is insufficient.

Atmospheric wind, pressure, cloud, and rainfall must also show a consistent response.

---

## 14.43 Anomalies

An **anomaly** is the difference between an observed value and a long-term average.

```text id="axlz6g"
observed temperature
−
average temperature
=
temperature anomaly
```

Example:

```text id="22jyxu"
observed sea temperature: 28.2°C
average:                  27.4°C

anomaly:                  +0.8°C
```

Positive means warmer than average.

Negative means cooler than average.

Anomalies allow comparison among seasons and locations with different normal temperatures.

---

## 14.44 ENSO Advisories

Climate centers evaluate:

- Sea-surface temperatures
- Subsurface ocean heat
- Trade winds
- Pressure
- Rainfall
- Model forecasts

They then assess whether El Niño, La Niña, or neutral conditions are present or likely.

Because ENSO evolves gradually, outlooks are probabilistic.

Spring forecasts often carry greater uncertainty because ENSO prediction skill commonly decreases during that season.

The climate system has its own version of “ask again later.”

---

# Other Climate Patterns

## 14.45 The North Atlantic Oscillation

The **North Atlantic Oscillation**, or NAO, describes variations in pressure between the subtropical Atlantic and the far North Atlantic.

Its phase influences:

- North Atlantic storm tracks
- European winter weather
- Eastern North American temperature
- Greenland blocking
- Wind across the Atlantic

### Positive NAO tendency

Often associated with:

- Stronger west-to-east Atlantic flow
- Milder, wetter northern European winters
- Reduced blocking near Greenland

### Negative NAO tendency

Often associated with:

- More Greenland blocking
- Greater chance of cold air reaching eastern North America or Europe
- Slower storm patterns

The effects depend on the placement and interaction of other systems.

---

## 14.46 The Arctic Oscillation

The **Arctic Oscillation**, or AO, describes pressure and wind patterns around the Arctic.

### Positive AO

Strong polar circulation tends to keep the coldest air farther north.

### Negative AO

Weaker or disrupted circulation may allow cold air to move farther south.

A negative AO does not guarantee a cold outbreak at one location.

It increases the possibility that Arctic air can escape its usual neighborhood.

Where it goes depends on the larger wave pattern.

---

## 14.47 The Pacific–North American Pattern

The **Pacific–North American pattern**, or PNA, is a large atmospheric wave pattern over the North Pacific and North America.

A positive phase commonly features:

- Ridge over western North America
- Trough over eastern North America
- Warmer West
- Cooler East

A negative phase often favors the opposite broad arrangement.

The PNA can strongly influence North American winter temperature and storm tracks.

---

## 14.48 The Pacific Decadal Oscillation

The **Pacific Decadal Oscillation**, or PDO, describes a long-lived pattern of North Pacific sea-surface temperature variability.

It can persist for years or decades, though it is not perfectly periodic.

The PDO may interact with ENSO and influence:

- Marine ecosystems
- Western North American climate
- Ocean circulation
- Decadal temperature patterns

Scientists continue studying how much of the PDO is generated within the North Pacific and how much reflects tropical forcing.

Not every climate pattern arrives with a clean origin story.

---

## 14.49 The Madden–Julian Oscillation

The **Madden–Julian Oscillation**, or MJO, is a large region of enhanced and suppressed tropical rainfall that generally moves eastward around the tropics over several weeks.

It can influence:

- Tropical cyclone formation
- Monsoons
- Jet-stream patterns
- Cold outbreaks
- Rainfall
- ENSO evolution

The MJO is shorter-lived than ENSO and can change week-to-week weather probabilities.

It is especially useful in subseasonal forecasting—the difficult period between ordinary weather forecasts and seasonal outlooks.

---

## 14.50 The Quasi-Biennial Oscillation

The **Quasi-Biennial Oscillation**, or QBO, is a regular reversal of winds high in the tropical stratosphere.

The winds alternate between:

- Easterly
- Westerly

The cycle lasts roughly two to three years.

The QBO can influence:

- Tropical convection
- Stratospheric circulation
- Polar vortex behavior
- Some seasonal weather probabilities

Its surface impacts are subtle and depend on interaction with other patterns.

---

## 14.51 Monsoons

A **monsoon** is a seasonal shift in prevailing wind that produces a major change in rainfall.

It is not simply a heavy rainstorm.

Monsoon circulations develop because land and ocean heat differently.

During the warm season:

- Land becomes hotter than nearby ocean.
- Low pressure develops over land.
- Moist air flows inland.
- Rising air produces widespread rainfall.

During the cool season, the circulation may reverse or weaken.

---

## 14.52 The South Asian Monsoon

The South Asian monsoon is among the world’s most important seasonal climate systems.

It supports water and agriculture for enormous populations.

Its strength and timing depend on:

- Land–ocean temperature contrast
- Himalayan and Tibetan Plateau heating
- Indian Ocean conditions
- ENSO
- MJO
- Snow cover
- Atmospheric circulation

Too little rain can produce drought.

Too much can produce catastrophic flooding and landslides.

---

## 14.53 The North American Monsoon

The North American monsoon brings increased summer moisture into northwestern Mexico and the southwestern United States.

It can produce:

- Thunderstorms
- Flash floods
- Dust storms
- Lightning
- Local cooling
- Highly uneven rainfall

The season is not continuously rainy.

Long hot periods may be interrupted by bursts of intense storms.

---

# Teleconnections

## 14.54 Distant Connections

A **teleconnection** is a recurring relationship between climate patterns in distant regions.

For example, tropical Pacific heating during El Niño can alter atmospheric waves across the Pacific and North America.

The process is not instantaneous direct control.

Tropical thunderstorms release heat, which changes upper-level circulation and launches large atmospheric wave responses.

These can influence distant jet streams and storm tracks.

The tropical Pacific does not send North America a weather instruction memo. It changes the flow in which North American weather develops.

---

## 14.55 Statistical Tendency Versus Deterministic Forecast

Suppose El Niño winters are wetter than average in a region 65% of the time.

That does not mean:

- Every El Niño winter is wet
- Every storm is caused by El Niño
- Rain occurs on 65% of days

It means wet winters occur more frequently than they otherwise might.

Climate outlooks describe shifted odds.

Daily weather remains variable.

---

## 14.56 Competing Patterns

Several climate patterns may operate simultaneously.

For example:

- El Niño favors one storm track.
- A negative NAO favors blocking.
- The MJO temporarily enhances tropical rainfall.
- Local soil moisture affects temperature.
- Random weather disturbances still occur.

The final seasonal outcome emerges from all these influences.

This is why simple statements such as “El Niño means snow” are usually too crude.

The atmosphere has an ensemble cast and resents being reduced to one celebrity.

---

# Seasonal Outlooks

## 14.57 What Seasonal Forecasts Predict

Seasonal outlooks commonly estimate probabilities that average temperature or precipitation will fall into categories such as:

- Above normal
- Near normal
- Below normal

They do not predict exact daily weather.

A wetter-than-normal season may include long dry periods and a few exceptionally wet storms.

A warmer-than-normal winter may still contain a severe cold wave.

---

## 14.58 Equal Chances

An **equal chances** area does not mean normal conditions are expected.

It means forecasters do not have a strong enough signal to favor above-, near-, or below-normal conditions.

Any of the categories remains plausible.

Equal chances is uncertainty, not bland weather.

---

## 14.59 Probability Categories

Suppose an outlook shows:

```text id="lijbqg"
50% above normal
33% near normal
17% below normal
```

This does not mean temperatures will be 50% hotter.

It means the probability of the seasonal average falling in the above-normal category is 50%.

The categories are based on the historical distribution.

Climate probabilities require careful reading because percentages attract creative misunderstandings.

---

## 14.60 Predictability Limits

Seasonal forecast skill varies by:

- Region
- Season
- Climate pattern
- Variable

Temperature outlooks often have more skill than precipitation outlooks.

ENSO can improve predictability in regions with strong historical relationships.

Local rainfall remains difficult because a few storms can strongly change seasonal totals.

---

# Climate Change and Weather

## 14.61 Climate Change Versus Natural Variability

Natural variability includes patterns such as:

- ENSO
- NAO
- AO
- PDO
- MJO

Climate change alters the background state over longer periods.

Both can operate simultaneously.

For example, a strong El Niño may temporarily raise global temperature above an already warming baseline.

A La Niña may temporarily slow the increase in global surface temperature without reversing the long-term trend.

---

## 14.62 A Shifted Distribution

Imagine a range of summer temperatures represented by a bell-shaped curve.

As the climate warms, the entire distribution shifts toward higher values.

```text id="1orwjz"
PAST

cold      average       hot
  ___----^^^^----___


WARMER CLIMATE

      cold      average       hot
        ___----^^^^----___
                   → shift
```

Consequences include:

- Fewer unusually cold events
- More unusually hot events
- Higher extreme temperatures
- Formerly rare heat becoming more common

Cold weather still occurs, but against a warmer background.

---

## 14.63 Moisture in a Warmer Atmosphere

Warmer air can contain more water vapor before reaching saturation.

This can contribute to heavier precipitation when storms have adequate moisture and lift.

But warming does not make every place wetter.

Changes in circulation, evaporation, soil moisture, and storm tracks can produce:

- Heavier downpours
- Longer dry periods
- Regional drought
- Changing snowpack
- More rain instead of snow

A warmer atmosphere can intensify both water excess and water shortage, depending on the region and season.

---

## 14.64 Attribution

**Event attribution** examines how human-caused climate change may have altered the probability or intensity of a specific weather event.

Scientists compare:

- The observed climate
- Model simulations with current greenhouse-gas levels
- Simulations of a climate with less human influence

They may conclude that an event became:

- More likely
- More intense
- Longer lasting
- Largely unchanged
- Difficult to assess

Attribution usually does not claim that climate change created an event from nothing.

It asks how the odds or severity changed.

---

## 14.65 Weather Still Matters

Even in a changing climate, individual events depend on immediate weather patterns.

A heat wave may require:

- Persistent ridge
- Dry soil
- Clear sky
- Weak wind

Climate change can raise the baseline and increase the probability, while the ridge provides the daily mechanism.

The climate loads the environment.

The weather pulls the particular lever.

---

# Regional Climate Clues

## 14.66 Köppen Climate Classification

The **Köppen climate classification** organizes climates using temperature and precipitation patterns.

Broad categories include:

- Tropical
- Dry
- Temperate
- Continental
- Polar

Subcategories describe features such as:

- Dry summer
- Dry winter
- No dry season
- Hot summer
- Warm summer
- Severe winter

The system links climate with vegetation zones.

It is widely used because it condenses complex regional patterns into a manageable code.

---

## 14.67 Microclimates

A **microclimate** is a local climate that differs from the surrounding area.

Microclimates occur around:

- Cities
- Valleys
- Lakes
- Forests
- Slopes
- Buildings
- Gardens
- Coastlines

Examples include:

- Frost collecting in a low field
- A south-facing slope warming early
- A city remaining warmer at night
- A lakeshore staying cooler in spring
- A courtyard sheltering plants from wind

The climate map may say one thing while the tomato plant beside the brick wall has arranged a private agreement.

---

## 14.68 Urban Microclimates

Urban neighborhoods differ according to:

- Tree cover
- Pavement
- Building density
- Roof material
- Traffic
- Elevation
- Access to parks
- Proximity to water

One part of a city may remain several degrees warmer at night than another.

These differences affect:

- Heat exposure
- Energy use
- Air quality
- Stormwater runoff
- Human health

Regional climate statistics can therefore hide important neighborhood-scale risks.

---

## 14.69 Coastal Microclimates

Near coasts, wind direction can change temperature sharply.

### Onshore flow

May bring:

- Cooler daytime air
- Higher humidity
- Fog
- Low clouds

### Offshore flow

May bring:

- Warmer daytime temperatures
- Drier air
- Clearer skies
- Increased wildfire risk in some regions

A beach and a community only a few miles inland can experience different weather and even different seasonal timing.

---

# Reading Climate Graphs

## 14.70 Climographs

A **climograph** displays average monthly temperature and precipitation.

It helps reveal:

- Wet and dry seasons
- Seasonal temperature range
- Monsoon patterns
- Maritime versus continental influence

### Illustration 7: Simplified climograph

```text id="znnigz"
temperature line:
      /^^^^^^\
_____/        \_____

precipitation bars:
▂ ▂ ▃ ▅ █ █ ▇ ▅ ▃ ▂ ▂ ▂
J F M A M J J A S O N D
```

A tropical climate may show little annual temperature change but strong rainfall variation.

A continental climate may show a large temperature range.

---

## 14.71 Anomaly Maps

Climate maps often display anomalies rather than actual values.

A temperature anomaly map shows how much warmer or colder a period was than average.

A precipitation anomaly may be shown as:

- Inches above or below average
- Percentage of normal
- Percentile
- Standardized index

Always check the legend.

A red color may mean warmth on one map and drought on another.

Maps are visually persuasive and have been known to exploit that.

---

## 14.72 Percent of Normal

Suppose normal monthly rainfall is four inches.

If two inches falls:

```text id="g13x9o"
2 ÷ 4 × 100
=
50% of normal
```

If six inches falls:

```text id="rvur10"
6 ÷ 4 × 100
=
150% of normal
```

Percent of normal can become misleading in very dry regions or seasons, where a tiny absolute amount produces a large percentage.

Always consider both the percentage and actual quantity.

---

## 14.73 Percentiles

Percentiles show how a value ranks compared with historical observations.

Example:

```text id="j5v06m"
95th percentile temperature
```

means the value exceeds about 95% of values in the comparison record.

Percentiles are useful because they account for local climate.

A 90°F day may be ordinary in one region and extreme in another.

---

# Field Observation  
## Build a Monthly Climate Log

For one month, record each day’s:

- High temperature
- Low temperature
- Rainfall
- Cloud cover
- Main weather event

At month’s end, calculate:

- Average high
- Average low
- Total rainfall
- Warmest day
- Coldest day
- Number of rainy days

Then compare with local climate normals.

Ask:

- Was the month consistently unusual?
- Or did a few extreme days shift the average?

This is the beginning of climate analysis using weather you actually experienced.

---

# Try It  
## Demonstrate Sun Angle

You will need:

- Flashlight
- White paper
- Darkened room

Shine the flashlight directly down onto the paper.

Observe the size and brightness of the illuminated area.

Then tilt the flashlight so the beam strikes at a shallow angle.

The light spreads across a larger area and becomes less intense.

This models the seasonal effect of Sun angle.

Do not use an actual Sun and paper setup. The Sun has declined participation in indoor demonstrations.

---

# Try It  
## Compare Land and Water Heating

Place equal amounts of dry soil and water in separate containers.

Measure their starting temperatures.

Place both in sunlight or beneath the same safe lamp.

Record temperature every five minutes.

Then remove the heat source and continue measuring.

You should observe that:

- Soil warms faster
- Soil cools faster
- Water changes temperature more slowly

This helps explain:

- Sea breezes
- Coastal moderation
- Seasonal lag
- Continental temperature extremes

---

# Try It  
## Track ENSO Headlines Carefully

When El Niño or La Niña is discussed, write down:

- Current phase
- Expected duration
- Strength
- Season
- Regional tendencies
- Forecast confidence

Then separate statements into two columns:

```text id="19clvt"
CLIMATE TENDENCY        DAILY WEATHER PREDICTION
```

Example:

```text id="pstbii"
Tendency:
Southern winter may be wetter than average

Not a daily prediction:
It will rain on January 14
```

This exercise prevents seasonal outlooks from being treated as long-range weather calendars.

---

# Field Exercise  
## Read a Seasonal Outlook

Choose a seasonal temperature or precipitation outlook.

Identify:

1. Time period
2. Above-normal region
3. Below-normal region
4. Equal-chances region
5. Probability values
6. Main climate pattern discussed
7. Forecast uncertainty

Then rewrite the outlook in plain language.

Example:

> The forecast favors a warmer-than-normal three-month average, but individual cold spells remain possible. There is no strong signal for total precipitation.

That is more accurate than:

> Winter will be warm and dry.

Certainty often sneaks into summaries during translation. Keep an eye on it.

---

# Weather Safety  
## Climate Patterns Are Not Warnings

El Niño, La Niña, a negative Arctic Oscillation, or a seasonal drought outlook may raise the probability of certain hazards.

They do not replace:

- Tornado warnings
- Flood warnings
- Hurricane forecasts
- Heat alerts
- Winter-storm warnings
- Evacuation orders

Climate patterns help with preparation over weeks or months.

Weather warnings guide immediate action.

Do not remain in danger because the event seems inconsistent with the seasonal pattern.

The atmosphere is under no obligation to remain on brand.

---

# Chapter Summary

Climate describes the long-term statistics of weather, including averages, variability, seasons, and extremes.

Climate normals provide reference values but do not predict exact daily conditions.

Earth’s axial tilt causes seasons by changing Sun angle and day length.

Land heats and cools faster than water, producing continental and maritime climate differences.

Oceans, currents, mountains, elevation, and latitude strongly influence regional climate.

Uneven solar heating drives global atmospheric circulation.

The Hadley, Ferrel, and polar cells provide a simplified model of major wind and pressure belts.

The Intertropical Convergence Zone shifts seasonally and helps create tropical wet and dry seasons.

Jet streams shift and change strength with seasonal temperature contrasts.

Zonal flow favors faster-moving weather, while amplified meridional flow can produce persistent extremes.

ENSO is a coupled ocean–atmosphere pattern in the tropical Pacific.

El Niño features unusually warm central or eastern tropical Pacific water and a corresponding atmospheric response.

La Niña features unusually cool central or eastern Pacific water and stronger trade-wind circulation.

Neutral conditions mean neither phase strongly dominates; they do not guarantee average weather.

ENSO shifts the probabilities of rainfall, temperature, storm tracks, and tropical cyclone activity but does not determine individual storms.

The NAO, AO, PNA, PDO, MJO, QBO, and monsoon circulations also influence weather across multiple time scales.

Teleconnections connect distant regions through large atmospheric wave patterns.

Seasonal outlooks describe probabilities of broad average conditions, not daily forecasts.

Equal chances means no category is strongly favored, not that normal weather is expected.

Natural climate variability and long-term climate change operate together.

Climate change shifts the background distribution of weather, altering the frequency or intensity of some extremes.

Event attribution estimates how climate change affected the probability or severity of a particular event.

Microclimates can differ greatly from surrounding regional conditions.

Climate patterns load the dice.

Daily weather still rolls them.

---

## Weather-Watcher’s Vocabulary

**Climate:** Long-term statistical description of weather.  
**Climate normal:** A multi-decade average used as a reference.  
**Climate variability:** Natural climate fluctuations over months, years, or decades.  
**Seasonal lag:** Delay between maximum or minimum solar energy and seasonal temperature extremes.  
**Continental climate:** Climate with relatively large daily and seasonal temperature ranges.  
**Maritime climate:** Climate moderated by nearby ocean or large water bodies.  
**Hadley cell:** Tropical circulation with rising air near the equator and sinking air in the subtropics.  
**Ferrel cell:** Middle-latitude circulation associated with prevailing westerlies and weather systems.  
**Polar cell:** High-latitude circulation involving sinking polar air and surface easterlies.  
**Intertropical Convergence Zone:** Tropical belt of converging trade winds and rising air.  
**Trade winds:** Tropical easterly surface winds flowing toward the equator.  
**Zonal flow:** Predominantly west-to-east atmospheric flow.  
**Meridional flow:** Strongly north–south atmospheric flow.  
**Blocking:** Persistent circulation that slows or redirects weather systems.  
**Coupling:** Two-way interaction between the ocean and atmosphere.  
**ENSO:** El Niño–Southern Oscillation.  
**El Niño:** Warm phase of ENSO involving tropical Pacific warming and atmospheric change.  
**La Niña:** Cool phase of ENSO involving tropical Pacific cooling and atmospheric change.  
**Southern Oscillation:** Atmospheric-pressure component of ENSO.  
**Walker circulation:** East–west tropical atmospheric circulation across the Pacific.  
**Upwelling:** Rise of deeper ocean water toward the surface.  
**Anomaly:** Difference from a long-term average.  
**Teleconnection:** Recurring climate relationship between distant regions.  
**North Atlantic Oscillation:** Pressure pattern influencing North Atlantic and adjacent climates.  
**Arctic Oscillation:** Large-scale pressure and wind pattern surrounding the Arctic.  
**Pacific–North American pattern:** Atmospheric wave pattern affecting North American climate.  
**Pacific Decadal Oscillation:** Long-lived North Pacific sea-temperature variability pattern.  
**Madden–Julian Oscillation:** Eastward-moving tropical rainfall pattern lasting several weeks.  
**Quasi-Biennial Oscillation:** Reversal of tropical stratospheric winds over roughly two to three years.  
**Monsoon:** Seasonal wind reversal associated with major rainfall changes.  
**Climate attribution:** Analysis of how climate change affected an event’s probability or severity.  
**Microclimate:** Local climate differing from its surroundings.  
**Climograph:** Graph showing average monthly temperature and precipitation.  
**Percentile:** Rank of a value relative to a historical distribution.

**Next: Chapter 15 — Optical Weather: Rainbows, Halos, Sundogs, Mirages, and the Colors of the Sky**

<!-- End of source chapter -->

---

# Chapter 15  
## Optical Weather: Rainbows, Halos, Sundogs, Mirages, and the Colors of the Sky

Weather is not only something the atmosphere does.

It is also something the atmosphere shows.

Sunlight passing through air, water droplets, ice crystals, dust, smoke, and temperature layers can produce some of the sky’s most striking displays:

- Rainbows
- Halos
- Sundogs
- Coronas
- Glories
- Mirages
- Green flashes
- Red sunsets
- Blue skies
- Crepuscular rays
- Iridescent clouds

These effects are forms of **atmospheric optics**.

They occur because light can be:

- Scattered
- Reflected
- Refracted
- Diffracted
- Absorbed
- Split into colors

The sky is not a passive blue ceiling. It is an enormous optical laboratory with poor signage and excellent lighting.

---

# Light in the Atmosphere

## 15.1 White Light

Sunlight looks white, but it contains many wavelengths of visible light.

These wavelengths appear to us as colors:

```text
red
orange
yellow
green
blue
violet
```

Red light has a longer wavelength.

Blue and violet light have shorter wavelengths.

When light interacts with matter, different wavelengths may behave differently. This separation creates many atmospheric colors.

---

## 15.2 Reflection

**Reflection** occurs when light bounces from a surface.

Examples include:

- Sunlight reflected from water
- Light reflected by ice crystals
- Bright cloud edges
- Moonlight on snow

A smooth surface produces more orderly reflection.

A rough surface scatters reflected light in many directions.

This is why calm water may show a sharp reflection while choppy water creates a broad path of glitter.

---

## 15.3 Refraction

**Refraction** is the bending of light as it passes between materials or air layers where its speed differs.

Light bends when it moves through:

- Air into water
- Air into ice
- Warm air into colder air
- Dense air into less dense air

Refraction is essential to:

- Rainbows
- Halos
- Sundogs
- Mirages
- Green flashes
- Apparent sunrise and sunset positions

### Illustration 1: Refraction

```text
air
       incoming ray
             \
              \
---------------\----------- water
                \
                 \ bent ray
```

---

## 15.4 Dispersion

Different wavelengths bend by slightly different amounts.

This is called **dispersion**.

Violet and blue light generally refract more than red light in water.

As white sunlight enters and leaves a raindrop, the colors separate.

Dispersion creates the color order in rainbows and some ice-crystal displays.

---

## 15.5 Scattering

**Scattering** occurs when light is redirected by particles or molecules.

The type of scattering depends on particle size.

### Very small particles

Air molecules scatter shorter wavelengths more efficiently.

This produces the blue sky.

### Larger particles

Cloud droplets, haze, dust, and smoke scatter a broader range of wavelengths.

This may produce:

- White clouds
- Gray haze
- Pale skies
- Red sunsets
- Milky light

---

## 15.6 Diffraction

**Diffraction** occurs when light bends and spreads around small particles or through narrow gaps.

It is important in:

- Coronas
- Iridescent clouds
- Glories
- Some fogbow effects

Diffraction patterns depend on particle size.

Uniform droplets produce clearer rings and colors.

A cloud with wildly mixed droplet sizes produces a blurrier display, which is one of the few cases where atmospheric organization improves the décor.

---

# Why the Sky Is Blue

## 15.7 Rayleigh Scattering

Air molecules are much smaller than visible-light wavelengths.

They scatter short wavelengths more efficiently than long wavelengths.

Blue and violet light are therefore scattered strongly across the sky.

The sky appears blue rather than violet because:

- The Sun emits less violet than some nearby wavelengths
- Human eyes are less sensitive to violet
- Some violet light is absorbed in the upper atmosphere
- Blue light contributes more strongly to ordinary visual perception

### Illustration 2: Blue-sky scattering

```text
SUNLIGHT → → → atmosphere

red light travels more directly

blue light scatters:
      ↗
←   ↙  ↘   →
      ↖
```

When you look away from the Sun, much of the blue light you see has been scattered into your line of sight.

---

## 15.8 Why the Sky Looks Paler Near the Horizon

Near the horizon, you look through a much longer path of atmosphere.

That path contains more:

- Air molecules
- Dust
- Haze
- Water droplets
- Pollution

Repeated scattering mixes colors and reduces the deep blue appearance.

The horizon often looks:

- Pale blue
- White
- Gray
- Yellowish

A very clear, dry atmosphere usually produces deeper blue.

---

## 15.9 Why Mountains Look Blue

Distant mountains often appear blue or blue-gray.

This happens because the long air path between the observer and mountain scatters blue light into the view.

At the same time, light from the mountain is weakened by haze.

The more distant the ridge, the stronger the atmospheric veil.

This effect is called **aerial perspective**.

Artists used it long before anyone explained the molecular physics.

---

# Sunrises and Sunsets

## 15.10 Why Sunsets Are Red

Near sunrise and sunset, sunlight travels through a long path of atmosphere.

Much of the blue and violet light is scattered out of the direct beam.

The remaining light is richer in:

- Red
- Orange
- Yellow

```text
MIDDAY

short path through atmosphere
Sun appears white-yellow


SUNSET

long path through atmosphere
blue light scattered away
red and orange remain
```

The exact color depends on:

- Clouds
- Dust
- Smoke
- Pollution
- Humidity
- Volcanic aerosols
- Viewing angle

---

## 15.11 Red Sky and Weather

The old saying goes:

> Red sky at night, sailor’s delight.  
> Red sky in morning, sailor’s warning.

There is some meteorological logic behind it in middle latitudes where weather systems usually move west to east.

### Red evening sky

May indicate:

- Clear air to the west
- Setting sunlight illuminating clouds to the east
- Drier weather approaching

### Red morning sky

May indicate:

- Clear air to the east
- Sunrise illuminating clouds to the west
- An approaching weather system

This is only a rough regional clue.

It works poorly when:

- Weather moves from another direction
- Tropical systems are involved
- Local terrain dominates
- Smoke or pollution creates the redness
- The observer lives where prevailing flow differs

The proverb is atmospheric shorthand, not an oceanic legal agreement.

---

## 15.12 Afterglow

The sky may remain red, pink, or purple after the Sun drops below the horizon.

This **afterglow** occurs because sunlight still illuminates high clouds and particles above the observer.

High cirrus clouds may glow long after the lower landscape darkens.

Aerosols from volcanic eruptions or large fires can intensify twilight colors across broad regions.

---

## 15.13 The Belt of Venus

Just after sunset—or before sunrise—a pink band may appear above the horizon opposite the Sun.

This is called the **Belt of Venus**.

Below it lies a darker blue-gray region: Earth’s shadow projected into the atmosphere.

### Illustration 3: Earth’s shadow

```text
opposite sunset

pink band
BELT OF VENUS
----------------

dark blue-gray band
EARTH'S SHADOW
----------------

horizon
```

The pink color comes from reddened sunlight illuminating the atmosphere above the shadow.

Despite the romantic name, Venus is not responsible.

It was merely standing nearby when humans began naming things.

---

# Clouds and Color

## 15.14 Why Clouds Are White

Cloud droplets are much larger than air molecules.

They scatter visible wavelengths more evenly.

Because red, green, and blue light are all scattered strongly, the cloud appears white.

Thin clouds remain bright because sunlight passes through and scatters toward the observer.

---

## 15.15 Why Clouds Turn Gray

A thick cloud scatters and absorbs light through many layers.

Less direct sunlight reaches its base.

The underside may therefore appear:

- Gray
- Blue-gray
- Nearly black

The cloud is not necessarily made of darker water.

It is simply receiving and transmitting less light.

Cloud thickness, shadowing, and surrounding brightness all affect appearance.

---

## 15.16 Why Storm Clouds Can Look Green

Some severe storms develop a green or turquoise tint.

The precise visual effect depends on:

- Deep cloud thickness
- Large amounts of water or ice
- Red or orange sunlight
- Scattering within the storm
- Observer position

Green skies are often associated with intense thunderstorms and hail, but they do not guarantee:

- Tornadoes
- Giant hail
- Severe weather at the observer’s exact location

They indicate an unusual and optically deep storm environment.

A green sky is worth taking seriously, but it is not a certification label attached by the hail department.

---

## 15.17 Why Snow Clouds Look Bright at Night

Low clouds may glow over cities at night because they reflect artificial light back toward the ground.

Snow on the surface adds more reflection.

The combination can make a winter night appear surprisingly bright.

In rural areas, low clouds may remain much darker because fewer lights shine upward.

Clouds are excellent at returning lighting pollution to sender.

---

# Rainbows

## 15.18 How a Rainbow Forms

A primary rainbow forms when sunlight enters raindrops.

Inside each drop, the light:

1. Refracts
2. Separates into colors
3. Reflects from the back of the drop
4. Refracts again as it exits

### Illustration 4: Light inside a raindrop

```text
sunlight →
             \ enters and refracts
              \
             (  raindrop  )
              \          /
               \ reflects
                \______/
                    \
                     \ exits as colors
```

Different colors leave at slightly different angles.

An observer sees red light from one set of drops, orange from another, and so on.

The rainbow is not located at the raindrops themselves as one fixed object.

It depends on the positions of:

- Sun
- Observer
- Drops

Move the observer, and the rainbow moves too.

---

## 15.19 Where to Look

To see a rainbow:

- The Sun must be behind you.
- Rain or water droplets must be ahead.
- The Sun should usually be fairly low.

The center of the rainbow lies opposite the Sun at the **antisolar point**.

A morning rainbow generally appears in the western sky.

An evening rainbow generally appears in the eastern sky.

---

## 15.20 Rainbow Color Order

In a primary rainbow, the color order from outer edge to inner edge is:

```text
red
orange
yellow
green
blue
violet
```

Red appears on the outside.

Violet appears on the inside.

The colors blend continuously rather than existing as exactly six or seven sharp bands.

The familiar list is a useful simplification imposed on a continuous spectrum.

Humans do enjoy forcing gradients into filing cabinets.

---

## 15.21 Why Rainbows Are Circular

The light reaches the observer at a particular angle around the antisolar point.

This creates a circle.

From the ground, the horizon usually blocks the lower part, so we see an arc.

From an aircraft or high mountain, a nearly full circular rainbow may be visible if droplets surround the antisolar point.

The rainbow was a circle all along. The landscape was editing it.

---

## 15.22 Rainbow Height and Sun Angle

The lower the Sun, the higher the rainbow appears.

At sunrise or sunset, the arc may rise high into the sky.

As the Sun climbs, the rainbow becomes lower.

When the Sun is more than about 42 degrees above the horizon, an ordinary primary rainbow falls mostly below the horizon for a ground observer.

This is why rainbows are common in morning and late afternoon but less common near midday.

---

## 15.23 Secondary Rainbows

A **secondary rainbow** forms when light reflects twice inside the raindrop.

It appears:

- Outside the primary bow
- Fainter
- Wider
- With reversed color order

### Primary rainbow

Red outside, violet inside.

### Secondary rainbow

Violet outside, red inside.

### Illustration 5: Double rainbow

```text
SECONDARY
violet on outer edge
red on inner edge

dark region

PRIMARY
red on outer edge
violet on inner edge
```

The dark region between the bows is called **Alexander’s band**.

Fewer light rays emerge toward the observer from that angular region.

---

## 15.24 Supernumerary Bows

Sometimes faint pastel bands appear just inside the primary rainbow.

These are called **supernumerary bows**.

They form through wave interference and diffraction.

They are more likely when raindrops are small and similar in size.

Supernumerary bows show that a complete explanation of rainbows requires both:

- Ray optics
- Wave optics

The atmosphere occasionally adds advanced physics beneath the main feature, like footnotes nobody asked for but everyone admires.

---

## 15.25 Red Rainbows

Near sunrise or sunset, the blue and green portions of sunlight may be strongly scattered away before the light reaches the raindrops.

The resulting rainbow may appear mostly red or orange.

These **red rainbows** are uncommon and often occur when the Sun is very low.

---

## 15.26 Reflection Rainbows

Sunlight may reflect from a smooth water surface before entering raindrops.

This can produce a reflected bow with a different center and orientation.

A **reflection rainbow** can appear above the ordinary primary rainbow.

It is uncommon because it requires:

- A broad reflective water surface
- Favorable geometry
- Rain in the correct location
- Low Sun

Atmospheric optics is generally generous with beauty and rather stingy with scheduling convenience.

---

# Fogbows

## 15.27 The White Rainbow

A **fogbow** forms in tiny fog droplets.

It resembles a broad, pale rainbow.

Because the droplets are very small, diffraction spreads and overlaps the colors.

The bow therefore appears:

- White
- Cream-colored
- Faintly red outside
- Faintly blue inside

Fogbows are sometimes called white rainbows.

They may appear when the Sun shines behind the observer into a fog bank.

---

## 15.28 Cloudbows

A similar pale bow may form in cloud droplets when viewed from an aircraft or mountain.

These are sometimes called cloudbows.

They share the same basic optics as fogbows.

---

# Halos

## 15.29 Ice-Crystal Optics

Halos form when light passes through ice crystals in high clouds.

The crystals act like tiny prisms and mirrors.

Common halo-producing clouds include:

- Cirrostratus
- Thin cirrus
- Ice fog
- Diamond dust

The shape and orientation of the crystals determine the display.

---

## 15.30 The 22-Degree Halo

The most common halo is a ring about 22 degrees from the Sun or Moon.

It forms when light passes through hexagonal ice crystals.

The inner edge is often relatively sharp.

The outer edge may fade gradually.

A faint reddish color may appear on the inside, with bluish tones outside.

### Illustration 6: The 22-degree halo

```text
             halo ring
        .----------------.
      /                    \
     |          ☀           |
      \                    /
        '----------------'
```

A halo indicates ice crystals aloft.

It may accompany an approaching frontal system, but it does not guarantee rain or snow.

---

## 15.31 Estimating 22 Degrees

Extend your arm and spread your thumb and little finger.

For many people, the span is close to 20–25 degrees.

A 22-degree halo has a radius roughly comparable to that hand span.

Never stare directly at the Sun while checking.

Block it with:

- Building edge
- Tree
- Pole
- Your hand

The halo is worth viewing.

Retinal damage is not an atmospheric-optics souvenir.

---

## 15.32 Why There Is No Ordinary 10-Degree Halo

The common minimum deflection through a typical hexagonal ice prism is near 22 degrees.

Light is not directed into the region inside the halo in the same way.

This creates the relatively dark interior.

Other rarer crystal forms can produce features at different distances, but the 22-degree halo dominates because ordinary hexagonal ice crystals are wonderfully repetitive little prisms.

---

## 15.33 The 46-Degree Halo

A much larger and rarer halo can form about 46 degrees from the Sun.

It is usually:

- Fainter
- Less complete
- Harder to distinguish from other arcs

Its formation involves light passing through different faces of ice crystals than in the 22-degree halo.

---

# Sundogs and Related Arcs

## 15.34 Sundogs

**Sundogs**, or parhelia, are bright colored spots appearing to the left and right of the Sun.

They commonly lie on the 22-degree halo.

They form when sunlight passes through flat, plate-shaped ice crystals oriented nearly horizontally.

Sundogs may show:

- Red toward the Sun
- Yellow farther outward
- Faint blue on the outer edge
- Bright white tails

### Illustration 7: Sundogs

```text
sundog        Sun        sundog
   ✦           ☀            ✦

approximately level with the Sun
```

They are often brightest when the Sun is low.

---

## 15.35 Why Sundogs Stay Level With the Sun

Plate-shaped crystals fall with their broad faces nearly horizontal, much like leaves.

This preferred orientation directs refracted light into bright spots at the same elevation as the Sun.

If crystal orientations were completely random, the light would be spread into a full halo rather than concentrated into sundogs.

---

## 15.36 The Parhelic Circle

A **parhelic circle** is a white horizontal line passing through the Sun and sundogs.

It forms mainly through reflection from vertical faces of ice crystals.

The circle may extend:

- Partway around the sky
- Through sundogs
- Occasionally opposite the Sun

It is usually white because reflection separates colors very little.

---

## 15.37 Sun Pillars

A **sun pillar** is a vertical column of light extending above or below the Sun.

It forms when sunlight reflects from nearly horizontal ice-crystal surfaces.

Sun pillars are most common:

- Near sunrise
- Near sunset
- In cold air
- Beneath high ice clouds
- In ice fog

The pillar is not a physical beam rising from the Sun.

Each crystal reflects light toward the observer from a different height.

Together they create the apparent column.

---

## 15.38 Light Pillars

At night, ice crystals near the ground may reflect artificial lights into vertical pillars.

Possible sources include:

- Streetlights
- Buildings
- Vehicle lots
- Industrial lights

These **light pillars** are most common in very cold, calm conditions with suspended ice crystals.

They may appear in several colors matching the lights below.

The effect can look supernatural, though it is mostly parking-lot illumination meeting cooperative frozen water.

---

## 15.39 Upper Tangent Arcs

An **upper tangent arc** appears above a 22-degree halo.

Its shape changes with Sun angle.

When the Sun is low, it may resemble a sharp V or pair of wings.

As the Sun rises, it broadens and may merge with related arcs.

It forms in horizontally oriented column-shaped ice crystals.

---

## 15.40 Circumzenithal Arc

The **circumzenithal arc** is sometimes called an upside-down rainbow.

It appears high above the Sun near the top of the sky.

Its colors are vivid, with red on the lower side and violet above.

It forms when sunlight enters the top face of horizontal plate crystals and exits through a side face.

The Sun must be relatively low for it to appear.

### Illustration 8: Circumzenithal arc

```text
          curved colored arc
             ∪
        high near zenith

              ☀
          lower in sky
```

It does not form a complete circle around the zenith.

It is one of the brightest ice-crystal displays and one of the easiest to miss because people generally fail to inspect the region directly above their heads.

---

## 15.41 Circumhorizontal Arc

A **circumhorizontal arc** appears as a bright horizontal band of color well below a high Sun.

It is sometimes nicknamed a fire rainbow, though it is neither fire nor a rainbow.

It forms through plate-shaped ice crystals when the Sun is high enough in the sky.

Because the required Sun angle cannot occur during winter or at high latitudes, the arc is geographically and seasonally limited.

It may appear as vivid colored fragments within thin cirrus.

---

## 15.42 Moon Halos and Moon Dogs

The Moon can produce many of the same ice-crystal effects as the Sun.

Possible displays include:

- 22-degree halo
- Moon dogs
- Moon pillars
- Tangent arcs

Moon halos often appear nearly white because moonlight is dim and human color vision is less effective at low light levels.

A photograph may reveal colors that the eye barely detects.

---

# Coronas and Iridescence

## 15.43 Coronas

A **corona** is a small ring or series of rings close to the Sun or Moon.

It forms by diffraction through small water droplets or ice particles.

A corona is much smaller than a 22-degree halo.

### Corona

- Close to Sun or Moon
- Often colored
- Caused by diffraction
- Usually associated with small droplets

### Halo

- Much larger
- Often about 22 degrees away
- Caused mainly by refraction through ice crystals

### Illustration 9: Corona versus halo

```text
CORONA

small rings close to Moon
      ( ◯ )


HALO

large ring far from Moon
   (        ◯        )
```

---

## 15.44 Lunar Coronas

Coronas are often easier and safer to observe around the Moon.

They may appear as:

- Blue-white inner glow
- Yellow ring
- Red outer ring
- Additional faint bands

Ring size depends on droplet size.

Smaller droplets produce larger coronas.

Uniform droplets produce more distinct rings.

---

## 15.45 Iridescent Clouds

**Cloud iridescence** appears as pastel colors near cloud edges.

It is caused by diffraction through small, similarly sized droplets or ice particles.

Common colors include:

- Pink
- Green
- Blue
- Orange
- Violet

Iridescence is often seen in:

- Altocumulus
- Lenticular clouds
- Pileus
- Young cloud edges

It usually appears close to the Sun.

Block the Sun before observing.

---

## 15.46 Why Iridescence Fades Quickly

As a cloud ages:

- Droplets grow
- Droplet sizes become less uniform
- The cloud thickens
- Multiple scattering increases

The clean diffraction colors blur.

Iridescence is therefore common in newly formed, thin cloud regions.

The cloud begins with a tidy optical arrangement and then becomes organizationally realistic.

---

# Glories and Brocken Spectres

## 15.47 The Glory

A **glory** is a set of colored rings surrounding the observer’s shadow on cloud or fog.

It is commonly seen:

- From aircraft
- From mountains
- In fog
- Around the antisolar point

The rings are produced through a complex combination of diffraction, reflection, and wave interference involving tiny droplets.

### Illustration 10: Glory from an aircraft

```text
aircraft observer
        ↓
cloud deck below

      colored rings
       ( ( ● ) )

● = shadow of aircraft
```

Each observer sees their own glory centered on their own antisolar point.

Two people standing together do not see precisely the same ring geometry.

---

## 15.48 The Brocken Spectre

A **Brocken spectre** is an enlarged shadow of an observer cast onto fog or cloud below.

It may appear:

- Huge
- Distant
- Surrounded by a glory
- Moving as the observer moves

The effect is named after the Brocken, a mountain in Germany where it was famously observed.

The shadow appears enormous because the fog provides no clear distance reference.

You have not suddenly become a mountain-sized supernatural being.

Disappointing, perhaps, but meteorologically reassuring.

---

# Crepuscular Rays

## 15.49 Sunbeams Through Clouds

**Crepuscular rays** are alternating bands of sunlight and shadow.

They often appear when:

- The Sun is low
- Clouds block parts of the light
- Haze, dust, or moisture makes the beams visible

The rays seem to spread outward from the Sun.

They are actually nearly parallel.

Perspective makes parallel lines appear to converge in the distance, like railroad tracks.

### Illustration 11: Crepuscular rays

```text
           Sun behind cloud
                ☀
             ☁☁☁☁
            \ | | /
             \| |/
              \ /
             beams
```

---

## 15.50 Anticrepuscular Rays

Sometimes rays appear opposite the Sun and seem to converge near the antisolar point.

These are **anticrepuscular rays**.

They are the same nearly parallel bands seen across the entire sky.

Perspective makes them appear to meet in the east at sunset or west at sunrise.

This is the sky performing a full-room demonstration of vanishing-point geometry.

---

# Mirages

## 15.51 Refraction Through Air Layers

Air density changes with temperature.

Cold air is generally denser than warm air.

When light passes through layers with different temperatures, it bends gradually.

If the temperature gradient is strong enough, the light path curves.

This produces a **mirage**.

A mirage is not a hallucination.

It is a real image formed by refracted light.

---

## 15.52 Inferior Mirages

An **inferior mirage** commonly appears over hot roads, deserts, or beaches.

The ground heats the air directly above it.

Light from the sky bends upward through the hot layer into the observer’s eyes.

The brain traces the light backward in a straight line and interprets it as coming from the ground.

The result resembles reflected water.

### Illustration 12: Road mirage

```text
cooler air above

light from sky
        \
         \
          \ curves upward
-----------\---------------- hot road
             \ → observer

observer perceives image below road
```

The apparent puddle retreats as you approach because the geometry keeps changing.

The road is not repeatedly drinking the water.

---

## 15.53 Why Mirages Shimmer

Hot air near the surface is turbulent.

Temperature and density change constantly.

Light paths shift from moment to moment.

This makes the image:

- Waver
- Stretch
- Break apart
- Shimmer

The effect is strongest near the surface where temperature gradients are greatest.

---

## 15.54 Superior Mirages

A **superior mirage** occurs when cold air lies beneath warmer air.

This temperature inversion bends light downward.

Objects may appear:

- Higher than they really are
- Stretched vertically
- Floating above the horizon
- Visible beyond the normal horizon

Superior mirages commonly occur over:

- Cold ocean water
- Ice
- Snow
- Polar regions
- Stable winter air

---

## 15.55 Looming

**Looming** is a type of superior mirage in which distant objects appear elevated.

A ship may seem to float above the horizon.

A distant coastline may become visible beyond its normal geometric range.

The object is real.

Its apparent position is displaced by refraction.

---

## 15.56 Fata Morgana

A **Fata Morgana** is a complex, rapidly changing superior mirage.

It can produce stacked or distorted images that appear:

- Towered
- Stretched
- Compressed
- Inverted
- Repeated

It occurs when strong temperature inversions create complicated light paths.

Ships, islands, or ice may resemble castles or cliffs.

The name comes from Morgan le Fay of Arthurian legend, because medieval observers reasonably concluded sorcery before atmospheric thermodynamics had proper publicity.

---

## 15.57 The Novaya Zemlya Effect
