In polar regions, strong refraction can make the Sun appear above the horizon when it is geometrically below it.

This is called the **Novaya Zemlya effect**.

It occurs when sunlight travels through a long, strongly refracting atmospheric duct.

The Sun may appear as a flattened line or distorted rectangle during polar night.

---

# The Apparent Sun

## 15.58 Atmospheric Refraction at the Horizon

The atmosphere bends sunlight downward.

As a result, the Sun appears slightly higher than its true geometric position.

At apparent sunrise, the Sun is still geometrically below the horizon.

At apparent sunset, it has already moved below the horizon.

Atmospheric refraction therefore lengthens daylight slightly.

---

## 15.59 Flattened Sun

Near the horizon, light from the Sun’s lower edge passes through denser air than light from its upper edge.

The lower part is refracted more strongly upward.

This compresses the vertical image.

The Sun may therefore look:

- Flattened
- Oval
- Distorted
- Layered

Temperature inversions can create even stranger shapes.

The Sun itself remains round.

The atmosphere has simply applied an aggressive vertical resize.

---

# Green Flashes

## 15.60 What Is a Green Flash?

A **green flash** is a brief green appearance at the upper edge of the Sun near sunrise or sunset.

It forms through:

- Atmospheric refraction
- Dispersion
- Mirage effects
- Removal of blue and violet light by scattering

The red portion of the solar image sets first.

The green portion may remain visible for a moment.

---

## 15.61 Best Viewing Conditions

Green flashes are easiest to see when:

- The horizon is distant and unobstructed
- The air is clear
- A temperature gradient or inversion exists
- The observer looks over ocean or flat terrain
- The Sun is setting or rising cleanly

Never stare continuously at the bright Sun.

Observe only when it is very close to the horizon and use safe solar-viewing practices.

Optical curiosity does not grant immunity from eye injury.

---

## 15.62 Blue Flashes

A blue flash is possible but much rarer.

Blue light is scattered more strongly by the atmosphere, so little remains in the direct solar image near the horizon.

Under exceptionally clear conditions, a brief blue edge or flash may appear.

---

# Twinkling Stars and Distorted Planets

## 15.63 Why Stars Twinkle

Starlight travels through turbulent layers of air with changing density.

The light path shifts rapidly.

A star appears to change:

- Brightness
- Position
- Color

This is called **scintillation**.

Stars twinkle strongly because they appear as nearly point-like sources.

---

## 15.64 Why Planets Twinkle Less

Planets appear as tiny disks rather than perfect points.

Light arrives from many points across the disk.

Atmospheric fluctuations affecting one part are partly averaged by the others.

Planets may still shimmer near the horizon, where the light path through the atmosphere is longest.

---

## 15.65 Seeing and Astronomy

Astronomers use the term **seeing** to describe atmospheric steadiness.

Poor seeing occurs with strong turbulence.

Stars and planets appear blurry or unstable.

Good seeing often occurs when atmospheric layers are smooth and stable.

Clear skies do not always mean good seeing.

A crystal-clear night with strong upper-air turbulence can produce terrible telescopic images.

The stars are not malfunctioning. The air is freelancing between you and them.

---

# Moon Illusions and Color

## 15.66 Why the Moon Looks Large Near the Horizon

The **Moon illusion** makes the Moon appear larger near the horizon than high in the sky.

Photographs show that its angular size changes very little.

The effect is mainly perceptual.

Possible explanations involve:

- Comparison with landscape objects
- Perceived distance
- Horizon geometry
- Visual context

Atmospheric magnification is not the main cause.

A mirage may distort the Moon slightly, but it does not explain the ordinary giant-looking horizon Moon.

---

## 15.67 Why the Moon Looks Orange

Near the horizon, moonlight travels through a long atmospheric path.

Blue light is scattered away.

The Moon may appear:

- Yellow
- Orange
- Red

Dust, smoke, haze, and pollution can deepen the color.

The same process reddens the Sun.

---

# Unusual Arcs and Rings

## 15.68 Bishop’s Ring

A **Bishop’s ring** is a broad, pale ring around the Sun caused by diffraction through very small particles.

It has been associated with:

- Volcanic aerosols
- Fine dust
- High-altitude particles

It is much larger and more diffuse than an ordinary corona.

---

## 15.69 Heiligenschein

The **heiligenschein** is a bright glow around the observer’s head shadow on dew-covered grass.

It forms through reflection and focusing of sunlight by dew droplets.

Each observer sees the glow around their own shadow.

The word means “holy light” or halo.

It is atmospheric optics providing everyone with a personal special effect, though only on sufficiently wet lawns.

---

## 15.70 Opposition Effect

Surfaces may look brighter directly opposite the Sun.

At that angle:

- Shadows hide behind objects
- Some surfaces reflect more light back toward the observer

This is called the **opposition effect**.

It helps explain brightness around:

- The antisolar point
- Shadows on rough ground
- Lunar surfaces near full Moon

---

# Aircraft and Mountain Optics

## 15.71 Aircraft Shadows

From an aircraft, the airplane’s shadow may appear on:

- Clouds
- Fog
- Ground
- Water

A glory may surround the shadow when small droplets are present.

The shadow always appears near the antisolar point.

It may seem to race across the surface far below.

Its apparent speed can be enormous because the shadow is projected over great distances.

---

## 15.72 Mountain Shadows

At sunrise or sunset, a mountain may cast a long triangular shadow across the atmosphere.

Even irregular mountains can produce a triangular-looking shadow because:

- The shadow extends toward a distant vanishing point
- Perspective makes its sides converge

The triangle does not prove the mountain is secretly a pyramid.

This is disappointing news for several internet channels.

---

## 15.73 Alpine Glow

**Alpenglow** is the pink, red, or orange illumination seen on mountains near sunrise or sunset.

True alpenglow may occur when the Sun is below the horizon and reddened light is scattered onto the landscape.

Direct low-angle sunlight can also produce vivid warm coloration just before sunset.

Snow-covered peaks make the effect especially striking.

---

# Optical Clues to Weather

## 15.74 Halo Before a Front

A 22-degree halo often appears in cirrostratus ahead of a warm front.

A possible sequence is:

```text
cirrus
   ↓
cirrostratus and halo
   ↓
altostratus
   ↓
nimbostratus
   ↓
steady precipitation
```

This progression may unfold over many hours.

The halo is evidence of high ice cloud and increasing moisture aloft.

It is not a precise rain timer.

---

## 15.75 Coronas and Cloud Droplets

A distinct corona indicates small, relatively uniform droplets or ice particles.

As the cloud thickens or droplets grow, the corona may fade.

This can reveal changes in cloud microphysics even before precipitation begins.

---

## 15.76 Sharp Shadows and Haze

A deep blue sky with sharp shadows often indicates relatively clear, dry air.

A milky sky with weak shadows may indicate:

- Haze
- Smoke
- Humidity
- Thin cloud
- Dust

These clues help estimate visibility and atmospheric clarity.

They do not identify the exact particles without additional observations.

---

## 15.77 Red Sun Through Smoke

Wildfire smoke can make the Sun appear deep red or orange, even when it is well above the horizon.

Fine particles scatter and absorb shorter wavelengths.

A red Sun during the day may indicate substantial smoke aloft or near the ground.

Check air-quality information rather than assuming the effect is merely scenic.

The atmosphere can make a pollution problem look like an album cover, which is unhelpful branding.

---

# Photographing Atmospheric Optics

## 15.78 Protect the Eyes and Camera

Never look directly at the bright Sun through:

- Camera viewfinder
- Binoculars
- Telescope
- Unfiltered optical device

Use a building or landscape object to block the Sun when photographing halos, sundogs, or iridescence.

Digital screens reduce direct viewing risk, but the camera sensor and your eyes still require sensible handling.

---

## 15.79 Use Wide-Angle Views

Large displays such as:

- Halos
- Rainbows
- Crepuscular rays
- Circumzenithal arcs

may require a wide-angle lens or panorama.

Include landscape for:

- Scale
- Orientation
- Sun position
- Visual context

---

## 15.80 Reduce Exposure

Bright clouds and halos can be overexposed.

Reducing exposure slightly may reveal:

- Colors
- Ring boundaries
- Cloud texture
- Faint arcs

Photograph in a lossless or high-quality format when possible.

Subtle optical features may become easier to identify after careful contrast adjustment.

Do not adjust them into existence.

Cloud enhancement should not become optical fan fiction.

---

## 15.81 Record Geometry

When documenting a display, note:

- Time
- Location
- Direction
- Sun or Moon elevation
- Weather
- Cloud type
- Duration
- Changes in shape

These details help distinguish similar phenomena.

A colored arc above the Sun might be:

- Circumzenithal arc
- Upper tangent arc
- Corona fragment
- Iridescence

Geometry is often the deciding clue.

---

# A Practical Identification Method

## 15.82 Step 1: Find the Sun

Ask where the feature appears relative to the Sun.

### Opposite the Sun

Possible:

- Rainbow
- Glory
- Brocken spectre
- Anticrepuscular rays

### Around the Sun

Possible:

- Halo
- Corona
- Sundog
- Tangent arc
- Sun pillar

### Near the horizon

Possible:

- Mirage
- Green flash
- Flattened Sun
- Belt of Venus

---

## 15.83 Step 2: Estimate Distance From the Sun

### Very close

Likely:

- Corona
- Iridescence

### About one hand span

Likely:

- 22-degree halo
- Sundogs

### Much farther away

Possible:

- 46-degree halo
- Circumhorizontal arc
- Large parhelic features

---

## 15.84 Step 3: Note Cloud Type

### High thin ice cloud

Likely:

- Halo
- Sundog
- Pillar
- Tangent arc

### Small water droplets

Likely:

- Corona
- Iridescence
- Fogbow
- Glory

### Rain ahead, Sun behind

Likely:

- Rainbow

Cloud composition is one of the best clues.

---

## 15.85 Step 4: Check Color Order

### Primary rainbow

Red outside, violet inside.

### Secondary rainbow

Violet outside, red inside.

### Halo or sundog

Red usually toward the Sun.

### Corona

Blue or white near the center, red farther out.

Color order helps distinguish features that otherwise look similar.

---

## 15.86 Step 5: Watch Movement

Some optical features move with:

- Clouds
- Observer
- Sun angle
- Droplet field
- Temperature layers

A glory remains centered on the observer’s antisolar point.

A sundog remains linked to the Sun’s elevation.

A mirage changes rapidly as temperature gradients fluctuate.

Movement can identify the geometry.

---

# Field Observation  
## Find Earth’s Shadow

On a clear evening:

1. Face away from the setting Sun.
2. Look low above the opposite horizon.
3. Find a dark blue-gray band.
4. Look for a pink band above it.

The dark band is Earth’s shadow.

The pink band is the Belt of Venus.

Repeat before sunrise by facing west.

The effect is easiest with:

- Clear horizon
- Clean air
- Open landscape
- Elevated viewpoint

---

# Try It  
## Make a Small Rainbow

You will need:

- Sunlight
- Fine water spray
- Dark background

Stand with the Sun behind you.

Spray a fine mist ahead.

Move the spray and your viewing angle until a small rainbow appears.

Notice that:

- The bow moves when you move.
- The Sun remains behind you.
- The colored light comes from different droplets at different moments.

Avoid spraying near traffic, electrical equipment, or anyone who did not request participation in atmospheric optics.

---

# Try It  
## Split White Light

Use:

- Glass prism
- Sunlight or strong white light
- White wall or paper

Pass the light through the prism.

Adjust the angle until a spectrum appears.

Observe the color order.

This demonstrates dispersion similar to the process occurring in raindrops and ice crystals.

---

# Try It  
## Create a Road Mirage Model

You will need:

- Transparent container
- Water
- Sugar or salt
- Small object behind the container

Create layers with different concentrations carefully so density changes from bottom to top.

Look through the side.

The object may appear distorted or displaced because light bends through the density gradient.

This is not identical to a hot-road mirage, but it demonstrates gradual refraction through layered media.

---

# Field Observation  
## Keep an Optical Sky Log

For one month, record sightings of:

- Rainbows
- Halos
- Sundogs
- Coronas
- Iridescence
- Crepuscular rays
- Red sunsets
- Mirages

For each, note:

- Date and time
- Direction
- Sun position
- Cloud type
- Weather before and after

Patterns will emerge.

You may discover that a halo often precedes thicker clouds, or that the best mirages occur over one particular road on hot afternoons.

Once you begin watching for atmospheric optics, the sky becomes almost irresponsibly busy.

---

# Weather Safety  
## Beautiful Does Not Mean Safe

Atmospheric displays can accompany dangerous conditions.

Examples:

- Rainbow near an active thunderstorm
- Halo ahead of a winter storm
- Green sky beneath severe convection
- Red Sun in wildfire smoke
- Light pillars during dangerous cold
- Mirages during extreme heat

Observe from a safe location.

Do not:

- Remain outside during lightning
- Stop on a highway shoulder
- Look directly at the Sun
- Enter smoke for a photograph
- Walk onto ice to improve the angle

A rare optical event is not improved by becoming the emergency described beneath the photograph.

---

# Chapter Summary

Atmospheric optics arise when light interacts with air, water droplets, ice crystals, dust, smoke, and temperature layers.

Reflection redirects light from surfaces.

Refraction bends light as it passes through materials or air layers of differing density.

Dispersion separates white light into colors.

Scattering produces the blue sky, red sunsets, haze, and many cloud colors.

Diffraction creates coronas, iridescence, glories, and fogbows.

The sky appears blue because air molecules scatter shorter visible wavelengths strongly.

Sunrises and sunsets appear red because sunlight travels through a longer atmospheric path and loses much of its blue light.

Rainbows form when sunlight refracts, reflects, disperses, and exits raindrops.

Primary rainbows have red on the outside and violet on the inside.

Secondary rainbows are fainter and reverse the color order.

Rainbows are circular and centered on the antisolar point.

Fogbows form in tiny droplets and appear broad and nearly white.

Halos and sundogs form through refraction and reflection in ice crystals.

The common 22-degree halo surrounds the Sun or Moon, while sundogs appear to either side.

Coronas are smaller than halos and form mainly through diffraction.

Iridescent clouds contain small, similarly sized particles that separate light into pastel colors.

Glories surround the observer’s shadow on cloud or fog.

Crepuscular rays are nearly parallel beams that appear to diverge because of perspective.

Mirages are real refracted images produced by strong atmospheric temperature gradients.

Inferior mirages commonly appear over hot surfaces.

Superior mirages form in temperature inversions and may make distant objects appear elevated or distorted.

The atmosphere refracts sunlight near the horizon, flattening the Sun and slightly extending apparent daylight.

Green flashes occur briefly when dispersion and refraction separate the upper edge of the setting or rising Sun.

Stars twinkle because turbulent air continually changes their light path.

Optical effects can provide clues about cloud composition, moisture, temperature layers, smoke, and approaching weather.

The sky is not merely colored by the weather.

Its colors are measurements written in light.

---

## Weather-Watcher’s Vocabulary

**Atmospheric optics:** Study of light interacting with the atmosphere.  
**Reflection:** Bouncing of light from a surface.  
**Refraction:** Bending of light as it moves through materials or layers of different density.  
**Dispersion:** Separation of white light into different wavelengths or colors.  
**Scattering:** Redirection of light by particles or molecules.  
**Diffraction:** Bending and interference of light around small particles or openings.  
**Rayleigh scattering:** Strong scattering of shorter wavelengths by very small particles such as air molecules.  
**Aerial perspective:** Blue-gray appearance and reduced contrast of distant objects caused by atmospheric scattering.  
**Afterglow:** Twilight illumination occurring after sunset or before sunrise.  
**Belt of Venus:** Pink twilight band above Earth’s shadow opposite the Sun.  
**Antisolar point:** Point in the sky directly opposite the Sun.  
**Primary rainbow:** Bright inner rainbow formed by one internal reflection in raindrops.  
**Secondary rainbow:** Fainter outer rainbow formed by two internal reflections.  
**Alexander’s band:** Dark region between primary and secondary rainbows.  
**Supernumerary bow:** Faint diffraction bands inside a primary rainbow.  
**Fogbow:** Broad pale bow formed in tiny fog droplets.  
**Halo:** Ring or arc produced mainly by light interacting with ice crystals.  
**Sundog:** Bright colored spot beside the Sun formed by oriented ice crystals.  
**Parhelic circle:** White horizontal circle passing through the Sun.  
**Sun pillar:** Vertical column of reflected sunlight produced by ice crystals.  
**Circumzenithal arc:** Bright colored ice-crystal arc high above the Sun.  
**Circumhorizontal arc:** Horizontal colored ice-crystal arc below a high Sun.  
**Corona:** Small diffraction rings close to the Sun or Moon.  
**Iridescence:** Pastel cloud colors produced by diffraction.  
**Glory:** Colored rings around the observer’s shadow on cloud or fog.  
**Brocken spectre:** Enlarged shadow cast onto fog or cloud, sometimes surrounded by a glory.  
**Crepuscular rays:** Sunbeams and shadows that appear to diverge from the Sun.  
**Anticrepuscular rays:** Rays appearing to converge opposite the Sun.  
**Mirage:** Refracted image produced by strong atmospheric density gradients.  
**Inferior mirage:** Mirage appearing below the true object, common over hot surfaces.  
**Superior mirage:** Mirage appearing above the true object, common during temperature inversions.  
**Fata Morgana:** Complex, rapidly changing superior mirage.  
**Green flash:** Brief green appearance at the upper edge of the rising or setting Sun.  
**Scintillation:** Rapid changes in a star’s apparent brightness or position caused by turbulence.  
**Heiligenschein:** Bright glow around the observer’s shadow on dew-covered ground.  
**Alpenglow:** Pink or red illumination of mountains near sunrise or sunset.

**Next: Chapter 16 — Weather Records, Extremes, and How Meteorologists Know a Record Is Real**

<!-- End of source chapter -->

---

# Chapter 16  
## Weather Records, Extremes, and How Meteorologists Know a Record Is Real

Weather records sound simple.

Find the highest temperature, the lowest pressure, the strongest wind, or the deepest snowfall, and write down the number.

In practice, it is much harder.

Was the thermometer shaded correctly? Was it too close to pavement? Did wind shake the rain gauge? Did snow drift against the measuring board? Was the station moved? Did the instrument change? Did the observer record the right time? Did the event occur at the station—or merely nearby?

A weather record is not just an impressive number.

It is a measurement that has survived questions.

---

# What Counts as a Weather Record?

## 16.1 A Record Needs a Category

Every weather record must specify exactly what is being measured.

Examples include:

- Highest temperature
- Lowest temperature
- Greatest 24-hour rainfall
- Greatest calendar-day snowfall
- Fastest wind gust
- Lowest sea-level pressure
- Largest hailstone
- Longest dry spell
- Most consecutive days above a threshold

These are not interchangeable.

For example:

- Greatest rainfall in one hour
- Greatest rainfall in 24 hours
- Greatest rainfall during one storm
- Wettest calendar day

may all belong to different events.

The atmosphere is happy to provide several records, provided we are careful enough to label the drawers.

---

## 16.2 Place Matters

A record may apply to:

- One station
- One city
- One state or province
- One country
- One continent
- The entire planet

A station record is not automatically a citywide record.

A city may contain several observing sites with different histories and exposures.

Examples include:

- Downtown station
- Airport station
- University station
- Military airfield
- Agricultural station

If the official station moves, the climate record must be adjusted or carefully documented.

---

## 16.3 Time Matters

Records must also specify the time interval.

Possible periods include:

- Instantaneous
- Five-minute
- Hourly
- Daily
- Monthly
- Seasonal
- Annual
- All-time

“Record rainfall” means very little without knowing the interval.

Three inches in five minutes is extraordinary.

Three inches in three months may be an emergency of an entirely different sort.

---

## 16.4 Record Versus Extreme

A **record** is the highest or lowest verified value within a defined dataset.

An **extreme** is an unusually severe value or event, whether or not it breaks a record.

A temperature may be dangerously hot without becoming the all-time record.

A flood may be catastrophic even if a larger one occurred a century earlier.

Records are useful for comparison.

Impacts depend on vulnerability, duration, location, and timing—not merely rank.

---

# Standard Weather Measurements

## 16.5 Why Standardization Matters

Measurements from different places and years can be compared only when they are collected in reasonably consistent ways.

Standardization addresses:

- Instrument type
- Sensor height
- Exposure
- Observation time
- Calibration
- Maintenance
- Reporting procedure

Without standards, a thermometer in a shaded field and one attached to a sunny brick wall would compete in the same category.

The brick wall would perform magnificently and contribute almost nothing to science.

---

## 16.6 Temperature Exposure

Official air temperature is measured in a ventilated enclosure or shield that protects the sensor from direct sunlight and precipitation.

The sensor should be:

- Above natural ground
- Away from buildings
- Away from pavement
- Well ventilated
- At a standard height

Direct sunlight can heat the instrument above the actual air temperature.

Nearby asphalt, rooftops, walls, and machinery can also bias readings.

### Illustration 1: Good and poor thermometer placement

```text
GOOD EXPOSURE

open grassy area
ventilated shield
away from buildings and pavement


POOR EXPOSURE

sunny wall
air-conditioning exhaust
dark roof
parked vehicles
```

---

## 16.7 The Stevenson Screen

A traditional weather shelter is the **Stevenson screen**.

It is usually:

- White
- Louvered
- Elevated above the ground
- Ventilated
- Protected from direct radiation

It houses thermometers and other instruments.

Modern automated stations often use smaller radiation shields, but the purpose remains the same:

Measure the air rather than the Sun’s opinion of the equipment.

---

## 16.8 Maximum and Minimum Thermometers

Traditional stations used separate instruments to record the day’s highest and lowest temperatures.

A maximum thermometer retained the highest reading.

A minimum thermometer retained the lowest.

Modern electronic sensors record temperature frequently and store the extremes automatically.

The exact sampling method can affect whether very brief temperature spikes are captured.

---

## 16.9 Observation Time Bias

Daily temperature records depend partly on when the observer resets the instruments.

Suppose a station observes at 5 p.m.

A hot afternoon temperature may be counted on two consecutive observation days if it occurs before one reset and remains recorded after the next period begins.

Morning observation times can create different biases.

Climate networks account for these effects when analyzing long-term averages.

This is called **time-of-observation bias**.

Even the clock can meddle with climatology.

---

# Measuring Rain

## 16.10 Rain Gauges

A rain gauge collects precipitation falling into a known opening.

Common types include:

- Standard cylindrical gauge
- Tipping-bucket gauge
- Weighing gauge
- Electronic optical sensor

The amount collected is converted into a depth.

One inch of rainfall means that, if the water were spread evenly and did not run off or soak in, it would cover the surface to a depth of one inch.

---

## 16.11 Gauge Placement

A rain gauge should be placed:

- Away from roofs
- Away from trees
- Away from fences
- Away from splash surfaces
- In a relatively open area
- At an appropriate height

Wind can cause rain to pass over the gauge opening.

Nearby objects can block precipitation or create turbulence.

Trees may drip into the gauge after rainfall ends.

This is known scientifically as “the maple tree has entered the dataset.”

---

## 16.12 Tipping-Bucket Gauges

A tipping-bucket gauge funnels rain into a tiny seesaw mechanism.

Each side tips after collecting a fixed amount.

The number of tips estimates rainfall.

Advantages include:

- Automatic reporting
- High time resolution
- Rain-rate measurement

Limitations include:

- Undermeasurement during very heavy rain
- Mechanical blockage
- Poor performance with snow
- Evaporation or wetting losses
- Debris in the funnel

---

## 16.13 Weighing Gauges

A weighing gauge measures the increasing mass of collected precipitation.

It can record:

- Rain
- Snow water equivalent
- Freezing precipitation

These gauges can be accurate but require maintenance and protection against:

- Wind
- Evaporation
- Mechanical drift
- Accumulated debris

---

## 16.14 Rainfall Duration Definitions

Rainfall records may be defined by:

### Clock period

For example, midnight to midnight.

### Any consecutive period

For example, the greatest rainfall during any 24 consecutive hours.

### Storm total

From the beginning to the end of one weather system.

These values can differ.

A storm producing six inches from noon one day to noon the next may be split between two calendar days.

The rain did not divide itself. The calendar did.

---

# Measuring Snow

## 16.15 Snowfall Is Difficult to Measure

Snow is moved easily by wind.

It:

- Drifts
- Settles
- Melts
- Compacts
- Blows out of gauges
- Sticks unevenly to surfaces

A single yard may contain:

- Bare grass
- Two inches of average snow
- A three-foot drift

This makes snowfall records more uncertain than many temperature records.

---

## 16.16 Snow Boards

A **snow board** is a flat light-colored surface placed in an open area.

Observers measure newly fallen snow on it.

The board may be cleared at standard intervals.

Measurements are taken in several representative locations and averaged.

Avoid:

- Drifts
- Wind-scoured patches
- Roofs
- Deck rails
- Car hoods
- The suspiciously deep pile beside the snowplow

---

## 16.17 Snowfall Versus Snow Depth

**Snowfall** is the total amount that falls during an event or period.

**Snow depth** is the amount lying on the ground at one time.

Suppose:

- Eight inches falls
- It compacts to six
- Two inches melts
- Three more inches falls

Storm-total snowfall may be eleven inches.

Final depth may be seven inches.

A record report must say which one it means.

---

## 16.18 Measuring Blowing Snow

Strong wind makes snowfall measurement especially difficult.

Observers may use:

- Multiple measurements
- Sheltered gauge systems
- Snow stakes
- Water-equivalent measurements
- Radar and nearby reports

During extreme blizzards, exact totals may remain uncertain.

A range may be more honest than a dramatic single number.

---

# Measuring Wind

## 16.19 Anemometers

Wind speed is measured with an **anemometer**.

Common designs include:

- Cup anemometer
- Propeller anemometer
- Sonic anemometer

### Cup anemometer

Rotating cups spin faster as wind increases.

### Propeller anemometer

A propeller aligns with the wind and measures speed.

### Sonic anemometer

Measures how wind changes the travel time of sound pulses.

Sonic instruments have no moving parts and can measure rapid changes in three dimensions.

---

## 16.20 Standard Wind Height

Surface wind is commonly measured about 10 meters, or roughly 33 feet, above open ground.

Nearby obstacles should be sufficiently distant.

Wind measured:

- On a rooftop
- Between buildings
- Beside trees
- On a ridge
- At street level

may differ greatly from a standard exposure.

A rooftop gust can be real and locally important without qualifying as a standard surface record.

---

## 16.21 Sustained Wind and Gust

**Sustained wind** is averaged over a specified period.

A **gust** is a brief increase above the sustained wind.

Different countries and agencies may use different averaging intervals.

For example:

- One-minute sustained wind
- Two-minute sustained wind
- Ten-minute sustained wind
- Three-second gust

A 100-mph three-second gust is not equivalent to a 100-mph ten-minute sustained wind.

The number is the same. The atmospheric commitment is not.

---

## 16.22 Instrument Survival

Extreme wind records are difficult because instruments may:

- Fail
- Break
- Lose power
- Become obstructed
- Be destroyed
- Stop transmitting

A station that records 140 mph before failing does not prove the maximum wind was 140 mph.

It proves the wind reached at least that value while the instrument was still participating.

---

## 16.23 Terrain Acceleration

Wind accelerates over:

- Ridges
- Mountain gaps
- Coastal headlands
- Building corners
- Canyons

A record gust may be valid for that site while unrepresentative of the surrounding area.

Meteorologists distinguish between:

- Standard exposed station records
- Terrain-enhanced observations
- Structural measurements
- Remote mountain instruments

The site description is part of the record.

---

# Measuring Pressure

## 16.24 Barometers

Atmospheric pressure is measured with a **barometer**.

Types include:

- Mercury barometer
- Aneroid barometer
- Electronic pressure sensor

Pressure readings must be carefully calibrated.

Small errors matter because major weather systems may differ by only a few tens of millibars.

---

## 16.25 Station Pressure

**Station pressure** is the actual pressure measured at the station elevation.

Pressure decreases with altitude.

A mountain station naturally records lower pressure than a coastal station even under similar weather.

---

## 16.26 Sea-Level Pressure

To compare locations at different elevations, meteorologists estimate what the pressure would be if the station were at sea level.

This is **sea-level pressure**.

The adjustment uses:

- Station pressure
- Elevation
- Temperature assumptions

At high elevations, sea-level reduction can become uncertain or unrealistic.

For record low pressure, meteorologists must specify whether the value is:

- Direct station pressure
- Adjusted sea-level pressure
- Aircraft estimate
- Hurricane reconnaissance measurement

---

## 16.27 Tropical-Cyclone Pressure

In tropical cyclones, central pressure may be measured by:

- Aircraft dropsonde
- Aircraft flight-level data
- Surface station
- Buoy
- Ship
- Satellite-based estimate

The exact center may pass between observing points.

Aircraft reconnaissance greatly improves accuracy, but even then the lowest pressure may occur between samples.

A reported central pressure is the best analyzed value, not necessarily a tiny permanent number engraved inside the eye.

---

# Measuring Hail and Tornadoes

## 16.28 Hail Size

Hail size may be determined from:

- Direct measurement
- Photographs with scale
- Damage evidence
- Observer reports
- Collected stones
- Radar clues

Problems arise because hail:

- Melts quickly
- Breaks on impact
- Is irregularly shaped
- May be exaggerated through object comparisons
- Can be photographed without a reliable scale

A ruler is preferable to “about the size of a fruit I remember fondly.”

---

## 16.29 Hail Diameter, Weight, and Circumference

A hail record may refer to:

- Greatest diameter
- Greatest circumference
- Greatest weight

One hailstone may not hold all three records.

An irregular stone can have:

- Large circumference
- Moderate diameter
- Unusual weight

The measurement category must be clear.

---

## 16.30 Tornado Wind Records

Direct tornado wind measurements are rare.

Possible sources include:

- Mobile Doppler radar
- Instrumented towers
- Engineering analysis
- Damage assessment
- In situ probes

Radar may measure extreme wind above ground without sampling the exact surface level.

Damage-based EF ratings estimate ranges rather than exact peak wind.

Therefore:

- Highest measured tornado wind
- Strongest damage rating
- Deadliest tornado
- Widest tornado
- Longest path

are entirely different records.

---

## 16.31 Doppler Radar Measurements

Mobile radar can measure winds within a tornado at very short range.

The reading depends on:

- Beam height
- Distance
- Sampling volume
- Debris
- Radar geometry
- Duration

A radar measurement may represent wind tens or hundreds of feet above the surface.

It is a genuine atmospheric observation but not necessarily a direct ground-level wind measurement.

---

# How Records Are Verified

## 16.32 The First Question: Is the Instrument Reliable?

Investigators examine:

- Calibration
- Maintenance history
- Sensor range
- Known malfunctions
- Data continuity
- Power interruptions
- Nearby equipment

A temperature reading outside the instrument’s reliable range requires special scrutiny.

If every neighboring station reports 102°F and one station reports 127°F, the atmosphere may have produced a remarkable local event.

Or a ventilation fan may have stopped.

Science asks before celebrating.

---

## 16.33 The Second Question: Was the Exposure Proper?

A valid instrument can still produce bad data if poorly placed.

Investigators consider:

- Nearby pavement
- Building heat
- Shade changes
- Vegetation growth
- Terrain
- Snow cover
- Station movement
- New construction

A station may have been excellent when installed and compromised years later by a parking lot, warehouse, or enthusiastic hedge.

---

## 16.34 The Third Question: Do Nearby Observations Support It?

Records are compared with:

- Nearby stations
- Radar
- Satellite
- Weather balloons
- Aircraft
- Reanalysis
- Regional weather patterns

A true extreme may be highly localized.

But it should usually fit the broader meteorological situation.

For example, a record low temperature is more credible if:

- Arctic high pressure is present
- Skies are clear
- Snow cover exists
- Nearby valleys are also exceptionally cold

The surrounding atmosphere acts as corroborating evidence.

---

## 16.35 Metadata

**Metadata** is information about the observation itself.

It may include:

- Station location
- Elevation
- Instrument type
- Observation time
- Site photographs
- Maintenance
- Station moves
- Observer notes

Weather data without metadata can be difficult to interpret.

A number says what was recorded.

Metadata explains what the number means.

---

## 16.36 Original Forms and Observer Notes

Historical records may be checked against:

- Handwritten forms
- Daily logs
- Telegraph reports
- Newspaper accounts
- Ship logs
- Government reports
- Instrument charts

Observer remarks can be extremely valuable.

Examples include:

- “Thermometer exposed to afternoon Sun”
- “Gauge overflowed”
- “Snow badly drifted”
- “Instrument damaged after 3 p.m.”
- “Station moved 200 yards east”

These notes are climatological detective work left by people who did not know anyone would still be reading their handwriting a century later.

---

## 16.37 Quality-Control Flags

Modern weather datasets use automated checks to flag suspicious readings.

Possible tests include:

- Impossible value
- Sudden jump
- Sensor stuck at one number
- Conflict with nearby stations
- Internal inconsistency
- Exceeding instrument limits
- Repeated missing data

A flagged reading is not automatically wrong.

It means the value requires review.

Extremes naturally trigger quality-control systems because they resemble errors.

That is inconvenient, but also exactly what quality control is for.

---

## 16.38 Internal Consistency

Weather variables should make physical sense together.

Examples of suspicious combinations include:

- Heavy rain with zero cloud cover
- Dew point above temperature
- Calm wind paired with repeated extreme gusts
- Sudden pressure jump without any supporting weather change
- Maximum temperature lower than minimum temperature for the same valid period

Some apparent inconsistencies result from timing or coding conventions rather than sensor failure.

Investigators must understand the reporting system.

---

## 16.39 Independent Review

Major records are often reviewed by committees or climate specialists.

They may examine:

- Instrument documentation
- Station history
- Meteorological setup
- Neighboring data
- Photographs
- Original observation forms
- Measurement uncertainty

A proposed national or world record may remain provisional for months or years.

The record is not being ignored.

It is undergoing the atmospheric equivalent of a background check.

---

# Historical Weather Records

## 16.40 Early Instruments

Early thermometers and barometers varied in:

- Design
- Scale
- Accuracy
- Exposure
- Calibration

Observers sometimes placed instruments:

- Indoors
- On walls
- In windows
- On roofs
- In courtyards

Historical measurements can still be valuable, but comparisons with modern records require care.

---

## 16.41 Instrument Shelters Changed

Temperature shelters changed over time.

A station may have used:

- North-facing wall
- Open stand
- Stevenson screen
- Cotton-region shelter
- Modern electronic shield

Each exposure has characteristic biases.

Climate researchers study overlapping periods and station histories to estimate discontinuities.

---

## 16.42 Station Moves

A weather station may move because:

- Airport expands
- Building closes
- Observer changes
- Equipment upgrades
- Site becomes obstructed
- Security rules change

Even a short move can change:

- Elevation
- Wind exposure
- Nighttime cooling
- Urban influence
- Snowfall catch

A station record can be continued across moves, but the changes must be documented.

---

## 16.43 Urbanization

Growing cities can warm local nighttime temperatures through the urban heat-island effect.

Long-term station records may reflect both:

- Regional climate change
- Local urban development

Scientists compare:

- Rural and urban stations
- Station metadata
- Neighboring trends
- Land-use changes
- Large networks

Urban influence is real, but it does not explain broad warming measured across oceans, rural stations, satellites, glaciers, and many independent systems.

One parking lot cannot warm a continent, however ambitious it may be.

---

# Homogenization

## 16.44 What Is Homogenization?

**Homogenization** is the statistical process of identifying and correcting nonclimatic shifts in a long-term record.

Possible causes include:

- Station moves
- Instrument changes
- Observation-time changes
- Shelter changes
- Local construction

The goal is not to make the record smoother or more convenient.

It is to estimate what the series would have looked like under consistent observing conditions.

---

## 16.45 Comparing Neighboring Stations

Nearby stations often experience similar regional climate changes.

If one station suddenly shifts warmer while surrounding stations do not, investigators look for:

- Site move
- Instrument change
- Urban development
- Exposure problem

A correction may be estimated from the difference between the station and its neighbors.

This works best with dense, reliable networks.

---

## 16.46 Raw Versus Adjusted Data

**Raw data** are the observations as originally recorded.

**Adjusted data** include corrections for known or detected nonclimatic changes.

Both are useful.

Raw data preserve the original record.

Adjusted data are often better for studying long-term climate trends.

Adjustments should be:

- Documented
- Reproducible
- Scientifically justified
- Evaluated for uncertainty

Changing data secretly would be misconduct.

Correcting a known station move transparently is ordinary measurement science.

---

# Reanalysis

## 16.47 Reconstructing Past Weather

A **reanalysis** uses a modern weather model and data-assimilation system to reconstruct the atmosphere over many years.

It combines historical observations such as:

- Surface pressure
- Weather balloons
- Aircraft
- Ships
- Satellites
- Buoys

The same model framework is used throughout the period, creating a physically consistent dataset.

---

## 16.48 What Reanalysis Provides

Reanalysis can estimate:

- Wind
- Temperature
- Pressure
- Moisture
- Jet-stream position
- Storm tracks
- Upper-air conditions

It is especially useful where direct observations are sparse.

However, reanalysis is not a direct measurement at every point.

It is a model-based reconstruction constrained by observations.

---

## 16.49 Earlier Periods Are Less Certain

Older reanalysis periods contain fewer observations.

Before satellites, oceans and upper air were sampled less completely.

Confidence generally improves during periods with:

- More weather balloons
- More aircraft
- More satellites
- Better ocean observations
- Denser station networks

A beautifully detailed historical map does not mean every contour was directly observed.

The software can draw a smooth line through uncertainty with alarming confidence.

---

# Return Periods and Probability

## 16.50 The “Hundred-Year Flood”

A **100-year flood** does not mean a flood occurs only once every hundred years.

It means the event has approximately a 1% chance of being equaled or exceeded in any given year under the statistical assumptions used.

```text
annual probability = 1%
```

Two such floods can occur in consecutive years.

They could even occur within the same year.

Probability does not maintain a waiting list.

---

## 16.51 Return Period

The **return period** is the inverse of annual exceedance probability.

```text
return period = 1 ÷ annual probability
```

Examples:

```text
10% annual chance → 10-year return period

2% annual chance → 50-year return period

1% annual chance → 100-year return period
```

This is a long-term statistical average, not a schedule.

---

## 16.52 Chance During a Lifetime

The chance of experiencing at least one 1%-annual-probability event over many years is greater than 1%.

For a 30-year period:

```text
chance of no event in one year = 0.99

chance of no event in 30 years =
0.99³⁰ ≈ 0.74

chance of at least one event =
1 − 0.74 ≈ 0.26
```

So there is roughly a 26% chance of at least one such event during 30 years, assuming the annual risk remains constant and events are independent.

The phrase “hundred-year event” has done considerable public-relations damage to probability.

---

## 16.53 Stationarity

Traditional frequency analysis often assumes **stationarity**—that the statistical behavior of the system remains stable over time.

But risk may change because of:

- Climate change
- Urbanization
- Land development
- River engineering
- Deforestation
- Sea-level rise
- Changing observation networks

A historical 1% annual flood level may no longer represent the same future probability.

Records are most useful when the background conditions have not quietly moved the goalposts.

---

# Record Heat and Cold

## 16.54 Daily Records

A daily record compares one calendar date across years.

For example:

- Highest temperature ever observed on July 20
- Lowest temperature ever observed on January 5

A daily record may be easier to break than an all-time record because it competes with a smaller subset of observations.

---

## 16.55 Monthly Records

A monthly record compares all days within one month across the station history.

Examples:

- Warmest March temperature
- Coldest August temperature
- Wettest January
- Snowiest December

These provide a broader seasonal context.

---

## 16.56 All-Time Records

An all-time record is the most extreme value in the complete recognized period of record.

Its significance depends on:

- Length of record
- Station continuity
- Measurement quality
- Exposure
- Missing years

An all-time high at a station operating for 12 years is not equivalent to one at a station operating continuously for 140 years.

Both may be valid.

They answer different questions.

---

## 16.57 Record High Minimum

A **record high minimum temperature** is the warmest daily low temperature on record.

This often indicates an unusually warm night.

Warm nighttime records matter because they can increase:

- Heat stress
- Energy demand
- Crop stress
- Health impacts

Extreme heat is not only about afternoon highs.

A city that never cools overnight may be more dangerous than one reaching a slightly higher daytime temperature followed by relief.

---

## 16.58 Record Low Maximum

A **record low maximum temperature** is the coldest daily high.

It describes a day that remained unusually cold from morning through afternoon.

This can occur during:

- Arctic outbreaks
- Persistent clouds
- Freezing rain
- Snow cover
- Strong cold-air damming

These records often better describe daytime cold severity than the overnight minimum.

---

# Apparent Temperature Records

## 16.59 Heat Index Records

Heat index is calculated from temperature and humidity.

It is not directly measured.

Extreme values can become unreliable outside the range for which a particular formula was designed.

Claims of extraordinary heat index should specify:

- Formula
- Temperature
- Humidity or dew point
- Exposure assumptions

A calculated value of 160°F does not mean the air itself reached 160°F.

It describes estimated heat stress under defined conditions.

---

## 16.60 Wind-Chill Records

Wind chill is also calculated.

It depends on:

- Air temperature
- Wind speed
- Formula used

Different historical formulas can produce different values.

When comparing events, use the same modern calculation method where possible.

The cold air did not change retroactively.

The mathematics did.

---

# Rainfall Extremes

## 16.61 Short-Duration Rainfall

Very short rainfall records may involve:

- One minute
- Five minutes
- Fifteen minutes
- One hour

These are difficult to verify because:

- Gauges may overflow
- Tipping mechanisms may miss intense flow
- Wind affects catch
- The event may be extremely localized

Radar and surrounding observations help determine whether the value is physically plausible.

---

## 16.62 Tropical Rainfall

Tropical cyclones and tropical moisture can produce extreme multi-hour or multi-day rainfall.

The greatest totals often occur where:

- Storms move slowly
- Rainbands train
- Mountains force uplift
- Moisture is exceptionally deep

The storm center need not pass directly over the wettest location.

Rainfall records and wind records from the same cyclone may occur hundreds of miles apart.

---

## 16.63 Orographic Records

Mountains can produce extreme precipitation when moist air is forced upward.

Windward slopes may receive extraordinary totals while nearby valleys remain much drier.

A mountain rainfall record may be valid but highly specific to:

- Elevation
- Slope orientation
- Wind direction
- Gauge exposure

The record belongs to the site, not necessarily the nearest town printed in the headline.

---

# Snowfall Extremes

## 16.64 Lake-Effect Records

Lake-effect snow can produce extreme local totals.

Narrow bands may remain over one area for many hours.

Challenges include:

- Strong drifting
- Rapid settling
- Measurement frequency
- Sharp spatial variation

Two observers a few miles apart may report dramatically different but equally valid totals.

---

## 16.65 Mountain Snowfall Records

Mountain stations may receive enormous seasonal snowfall.

Measurements can be affected by:

- Wind scour
- Drifts
- Avalanche terrain
- Access problems
- Changing station sites

Snow-water equivalent is often more reliable than depth for water-resource analysis.

---

## 16.66 One-Day Versus One-Storm Snowfall

A storm may span several calendar days.

Therefore:

- Greatest 24-hour snowfall
- Greatest calendar-day snowfall
- Greatest storm-total snowfall

may belong to different events.

Record books must define the interval precisely.

Snow is difficult enough without allowing midnight to move the ruler.

---

# Wind Extremes

## 16.67 Tropical Cyclones

Extreme surface wind records may occur in tropical cyclones.

Possible measurement platforms include:

- Coastal station
- Island station
- Mountain station
- Buoy
- Ship
- Aircraft
- Portable instrument

Wind exposure and averaging period must be documented.

A mountain-top gust in a cyclone may be much stronger than sea-level wind nearby.

---

## 16.68 Mountain-Wave Winds

Mountain waves and downslope windstorms can produce extreme gusts.

Air flowing over terrain may:

- Accelerate down slopes
- Form standing waves
- Create rotors
- Concentrate through passes

These gusts may rival hurricane wind even under clear skies.

The lack of rain does not make a roof feel more secure.

---

## 16.69 Thunderstorm Gusts

Downbursts can produce extreme winds over small areas.

Reliable records may come from:

- Airport stations
- Mesonets
- Research towers
- Portable instruments

Damage estimates alone are less precise.

A severe gust that misses the station may leave major damage without an exact measured speed.

---

# Long-Duration Records

## 16.70 Consecutive Hot Days

Heat records may count consecutive days:

- At or above 90°F
- At or above 100°F
- Above a local percentile
- With minimum temperature above a threshold

Threshold records describe persistence.

A moderately extreme temperature repeated for weeks may cause more impact than one isolated all-time maximum.

---

## 16.71 Consecutive Dry Days

A dry-spell record requires a definition of measurable precipitation.

For example:

- Less than 0.01 inch
- No measurable rainfall
- No rainfall above a regional threshold

Trace precipitation may occur without ending the official dry spell.

The clouds may contribute three drops and still fail the paperwork.

---

## 16.72 Wettest and Driest Years

Annual precipitation records depend on:

- Complete data
- Gauge continuity
- Observation time
- Snow measurement
- Station moves

A year with missing observations cannot be compared fairly without careful reconstruction.

---

# Records in a Changing Climate

## 16.73 Record Ratios

In a stable climate, record highs and record lows would still occur because of natural variability.

Over a long period, their rates would tend toward balance.

In a warming climate, record highs generally become more frequent relative to record lows.

The exact ratio varies by:

- Region
- Season
- Length of record
- Urban influence
- Natural variability

One cold record does not cancel a long-term increase in heat records.

A single coin landing tails does not prove the coin has never landed heads.

---

## 16.74 Records Become Harder to Break

As a station’s period of record grows, new records generally become less frequent because more previous extremes already exist.

A station in its tenth year has had fewer opportunities to establish large extremes than a station in its hundredth year.

This makes unusually frequent new records in a long-running network especially meaningful.

---

## 16.75 Moving Baselines

Climate normals are updated periodically.

A temperature that was far above a 1951–1980 average may be less unusual relative to a more recent average.

Updating normals helps describe current climate conditions.

Older baselines remain useful for measuring long-term change.

The reference period changes.

The historical thermometer reading does not.

---

# Famous Record Problems

## 16.76 Records Can Be Rejected

A claimed record may be rejected because of:

- Instrument error
- Poor siting
- Inconsistent neighboring observations
- Documentation failure
- Misread scale
- Unofficial equipment
- Impossible exposure
- Transcription error

A rejected record is not necessarily fraud.

Many arise from sincere observation under inadequate conditions.

---

## 16.77 Records Can Be Decertified Later

A record accepted decades ago may be reexamined using:

- Better metadata
- Newly found documents
- Improved physical analysis
- Comparison with regional data
- Instrument studies

If serious problems emerge, the record may be removed.

Scientific records are provisional in the best sense:

They remain open to better evidence.

---

## 16.78 Newspaper Numbers

Historical newspapers are valuable but may contain:

- Rounded values
- Unofficial observations
- Exaggerated claims
- Unit conversion errors
- Repeated wire-service mistakes

A headline is evidence that a number was reported.

It is not proof that the number was measured correctly.

Newspapers document history wonderfully and occasionally measure it with jazz hands.

---

## 16.79 Social-Media Records

Modern false records spread rapidly through:

- Cropped screenshots
- Misread app displays
- Home sensors
- Vehicle thermometers
- Unofficial weather stations
- Forecast values mistaken for observations

Before sharing a record claim, check:

- Official source
- Station
- Time
- Measurement type
- Verification status
- Whether the value was observed or forecast

A car dashboard reading in a sunny parking lot is not the new national temperature record.

It is the temperature of a small machine having a difficult afternoon.

---

# How to Evaluate a Record Claim

## 16.80 Step 1: Identify the Exact Claim

Ask:

- What variable?
- Which location?
- What interval?
- What category?
- What period of record?

Rewrite the claim precisely.

Instead of:

> The wettest storm ever

use:

> Greatest verified 24-hour rainfall at this station since records began in 1948.

Precision removes much of the drama and nearly all of the confusion.

---

## 16.81 Step 2: Find the Original Source

Prefer:

- National meteorological agency
- Official climate center
- Recognized record archive
- Peer-reviewed paper
- Verified station report

Be cautious with:

- Unsourced graphics
- News summaries
- Personal weather pages
- Viral posts
- AI-generated lists

Secondary sources may be useful, but the original source should define the record.

---

## 16.82 Step 3: Check Measurement Method

Ask:

- Which instrument?
- Where was it installed?
- What averaging period?
- Was it calibrated?
- Was the site standard?
- Was the reading direct or estimated?

For wind, determine whether it was:

- Gust
- Sustained
- Radar-derived
- Surface measurement

For rainfall, determine whether it was:

- Clock-day
- Rolling 24-hour
- Storm total

---

## 16.83 Step 4: Check Verification Status

Record claims may be labeled:

- Preliminary
- Provisional
- Pending review
- Certified
- Rejected

A preliminary number can change.

During active disasters, reports are often released before full quality control.

Speed is valuable for warning and response.

Certification is slower because it has a different job.

---

## 16.84 Step 5: Check Whether the Comparison Is Fair

Do not compare:

- One-minute hurricane wind with ten-minute typhoon wind
- Calendar-day rain with rolling 24-hour rain
- Snow depth with snowfall
- Heat index with air temperature
- Station pressure with sea-level pressure
- Radar wind aloft with surface anemometer wind

Many “record conflicts” are merely different categories wearing similar labels.

---

# Build Your Own Reliable Weather Record

## 16.85 Choose a Good Site

For a home climate log, place sensors thoughtfully.

Temperature sensor:

- In shade
- Well ventilated
- Away from walls and pavement
- At a consistent height

Rain gauge:

- Open exposure
- Away from trees and roof edges
- Level
- Easy to inspect

Snow board:

- Flat
- Open
- Representative
- Away from drifts

Your observations need not meet national standards to be useful.

Consistency is the most important beginning.

---

## 16.86 Keep Metadata

Record:

- Sensor model
- Installation date
- Exact location
- Height
- Nearby surfaces
- Station moves
- Maintenance
- Battery replacement
- Calibration checks

Take photographs of the site.

Years later, these details may matter more than the numbers themselves.

Metadata is a gift to your future self, who will otherwise stare at a 103°F reading and wonder whether the grill was nearby.

---

## 16.87 Observe at a Consistent Time

Choose a standard daily observation time.

Record:

- Maximum temperature
- Minimum temperature
- Rainfall
- Snowfall
- Snow depth
- Weather notes

If the time changes, document it.

Consistent routine makes the record much easier to interpret.

---

## 16.88 Compare With Nearby Stations

Occasionally compare your observations with official or trusted nearby stations.

Small differences are normal.

Large persistent differences may reveal:

- Poor sensor placement
- Calibration issue
- Genuine microclimate
- Elevation difference
- Urban influence

Do not adjust every value merely to match the airport.

Your backyard may truly differ.

Investigate before correcting.

---

# Field Exercise  
## Investigate a Suspected Record

Choose a local weather record claim.

Find:

1. Exact value
2. Date
3. Station
4. Measurement interval
5. Instrument or observing method
6. Period of record
7. Verification source
8. Nearby weather pattern

Then write one paragraph explaining why the record is credible—or why it remains uncertain.

This turns record hunting into meteorological investigation instead of number collecting.

---

# Try It  
## Test Sensor Placement

Place two thermometers outdoors for one afternoon.

Position one:

- Over grass
- In shade
- Well ventilated

Position the other:

- Near pavement or a sunlit wall

Record both every fifteen minutes.

The poorly exposed thermometer may read substantially warmer.

This demonstrates why official exposure standards matter.

Do not leave a household thermometer in weather beyond its rated limits.

The experiment should end before the instrument contributes a record of its own destruction.

---

# Try It  
## Measure Rainfall Variability

Place two identical rain gauges in separate open parts of a yard.

After several storms, compare the totals.

Differences may result from:

- Wind
- Trees
- Splash
- Gauge leveling
- Local rainfall variation

If the gauges disagree consistently, inspect their exposure.

This exercise reveals how difficult even “simple” rainfall measurement can be.

---

# Try It  
## Calculate a Return Probability

Suppose an event has a 2% annual probability.

The chance of no event in one year is:

```text
1 − 0.02 = 0.98
```

Over 20 years:

```text
0.98²⁰ ≈ 0.67
```

Chance of at least one event:

```text
1 − 0.67 ≈ 0.33
```

So there is roughly a 33% chance of at least one such event during 20 years, assuming constant independent annual risk.

The event still does not know when you purchased the house.

---

# Field Observation  
## Start a Personal Extremes Table

Create a table containing:

| Variable | Value | Date | Notes |
|---|---:|---|---|
| Highest temperature |  |  |  |
| Lowest temperature |  |  |  |
| Greatest daily rain |  |  |  |
| Strongest gust |  |  |  |
| Deepest snow |  |  |  |
| Lowest pressure |  |  |  |

Add only values measured under consistent conditions.

Mark equipment changes.

Over time, you will build a small but meaningful local climate record.

---

# Weather Safety  
## Do Not Pursue the Record

Extreme weather observations should never require dangerous exposure.

Do not:

- Stand outside during lightning to measure hail
- Enter floodwater to read a gauge
- Climb an icy roof to measure snow
- Approach tornado damage before clearance
- Remain on a beach for a hurricane wind reading
- Handle instruments near downed lines
- Drive into a blizzard to verify visibility

A missing observation is preferable to a missing observer.

The atmosphere will produce another number.

Your family would strongly prefer it not replace you.

---

# Chapter Summary

Weather records require precise definitions of variable, location, time interval, and measurement method.

A record may apply to a station, city, region, country, continent, or the world.

Standardized exposure and calibrated instruments allow observations from different places and years to be compared.

Air temperature must be measured in shade with ventilation and away from artificial heat sources.

Rainfall measurement is affected by wind, obstacles, gauge design, and the chosen time interval.

Snowfall is especially difficult to measure because snow drifts, settles, melts, and blows.

Wind records must distinguish sustained wind from gusts and specify the averaging period and instrument exposure.

Pressure records must distinguish actual station pressure from sea-level-adjusted pressure.

Hail records may refer to diameter, circumference, or weight.

Tornado records may describe wind, rating, width, path, duration, or fatalities—each a separate category.

Record verification examines instrument performance, exposure, nearby observations, metadata, original documents, and physical plausibility.

Quality-control flags identify suspicious observations but do not automatically prove they are wrong.

Historical records require careful treatment because instruments, shelters, observation times, and station locations changed.

Homogenization adjusts long-term records for documented or detected nonclimatic changes.

Reanalysis reconstructs past atmospheric conditions using models constrained by observations.

A 100-year event has roughly a 1% annual probability; it is not limited to occurring once per century.

Return-period calculations often assume stationarity, which may fail when climate, land use, or infrastructure changes.

Record highs and lows can still occur in any climate, but their relative frequency changes as the underlying temperature distribution shifts.

Claims on social media, vehicle displays, and unofficial sensors require verification before being treated as records.

A trustworthy weather record is more than a number.

It is a number with a location, a clock, an instrument, a history, and enough evidence to survive doubt.

---

## Weather-Watcher’s Vocabulary

**Weather record:** Highest, lowest, greatest, or longest verified value within a defined dataset.  
**Extreme:** An unusually severe weather value or event.  
**Period of record:** Span of time covered by an observing station or dataset.  
**Stevenson screen:** Ventilated shelter protecting weather instruments from direct radiation and precipitation.  
**Time-of-observation bias:** Statistical bias caused by the time daily instruments are read or reset.  
**Rain gauge:** Instrument measuring precipitation depth.  
**Tipping-bucket gauge:** Automated gauge recording rainfall through repeated small bucket tips.  
**Snow board:** Flat surface used to measure new snowfall.  
**Snow depth:** Amount of snow lying on the ground at a given time.  
**Anemometer:** Instrument measuring wind speed.  
**Sustained wind:** Wind averaged over a specified period.  
**Wind gust:** Brief increase above sustained wind.  
**Station pressure:** Actual atmospheric pressure at station elevation.  
**Sea-level pressure:** Pressure adjusted to an equivalent sea-level value.  
**Metadata:** Information describing how, where, and when an observation was made.  
**Quality control:** Process of identifying and reviewing suspicious or erroneous data.  
**Homogenization:** Adjustment of climate records for nonclimatic changes in observing conditions.  
**Raw data:** Observations as originally recorded.  
**Adjusted data:** Observations corrected for documented or detected nonclimatic changes.  
**Reanalysis:** Model-based reconstruction of past atmospheric conditions using historical observations.  
**Return period:** Average recurrence interval associated with an annual exceedance probability.  
**Annual exceedance probability:** Chance that an event will be equaled or exceeded in a given year.  
**Stationarity:** Assumption that statistical behavior remains constant through time.  
**Record high minimum:** Warmest daily minimum temperature in a record.  
**Record low maximum:** Coldest daily maximum temperature in a record.  
**Provisional record:** Possible record awaiting full verification.  
**Certified record:** Record accepted after official review.  
**Instrument exposure:** Physical environment surrounding a weather sensor.  
**Climate network:** Group of observing stations maintained for long-term climate monitoring.

**Next: Chapter 17 — Weather Folklore, Animal Signs, and Which Old Sayings Actually Work**

<!-- End of source chapter -->

---

# Chapter 17  
## Weather Folklore, Animal Signs, and Which Old Sayings Actually Work

Long before radar, satellites, weather balloons, and numerical models, people forecast weather by watching the world around them.

They noticed:

- Clouds thickening before rain
- Wind changing ahead of fronts
- Smoke settling beneath stable air
- Animals reacting to pressure, humidity, temperature, and sound
- Plants opening or closing with moisture
- Halos appearing before widespread cloud systems

Some observations were genuinely useful.

Others were coincidences repeated until they acquired the authority of furniture.

Weather folklore is therefore a mixture of:

- Careful observation
- Local experience
- Partial physical truth
- Memory bias
- Regional misunderstanding
- Rhyming confidence

The goal is not to mock old sayings. Many encode real atmospheric clues. The useful question is:

> What physical process, if any, lies beneath the saying?

---

# How Folklore Develops

## 17.1 Pattern Recognition

Humans are excellent at recognizing patterns.

This ability helped people anticipate:

- Seasonal changes
- Storms
- Floods
- Frost
- Drought
- Animal movement

But humans also detect patterns that are not really there.

Suppose a person sees ants moving before rain on three occasions. The behavior may become memorable.

The person may forget:

- Times ants moved without rain
- Times rain came without ant movement
- Times no one looked at the ants

This uneven memory creates apparent relationships.

---

## 17.2 Confirmation Bias

**Confirmation bias** is the tendency to notice and remember evidence that supports an existing belief while overlooking contrary evidence.

Example:

> Cows lie down before rain.

After seeing reclining cows followed by rain, the saying feels confirmed.

But to test it properly, one must record:

- Every period when cows lie down
- Every period when they remain standing
- Whether rain follows
- Temperature
- Time of day
- Feeding and resting behavior

The cows may be predicting rain.

They may also be digesting lunch.

A fair investigation allows both possibilities.

---

## 17.3 Local Knowledge

Some folklore works only in a particular region.

A saying may depend on:

- Prevailing wind
- Mountain orientation
- Nearby water
- Seasonal storm tracks
- Local cloud behavior
- Agricultural calendar

A red evening sky can be useful where weather usually approaches from the west.

The same clue may fail where tropical weather arrives from the east or local mountains dominate conditions.

Folklore often belongs to a landscape.

Removing it from that landscape can remove the reason it worked.

---

## 17.4 Rhymes Survive

Rhyming sayings are easy to remember:

> Mackerel sky and mares’ tails  
> make tall ships carry low sails.

The rhyme survives even when the explanation is forgotten.

This gives traditional forecasting an advantage over modern technical communication.

“Differential positive vorticity advection is increasing” has yet to become a beloved nursery verse.

---

# Red Sky Sayings

## 17.5 “Red Sky at Night, Sailor’s Delight”

The full saying is commonly given as:

> Red sky at night, sailor’s delight.  
> Red sky in morning, sailor’s warning.

This has a reasonable physical basis in many middle-latitude regions.

Weather systems often move from west to east.

### Red sky in the evening

The setting Sun is in the west.

For red sunlight to illuminate clouds farther east, the western sky may be relatively clear.

This can suggest that:

- Drier air lies to the west
- A weather system is departing
- Improving conditions may approach

### Red sky in the morning

The rising Sun is in the east.

If it illuminates clouds to the west, a weather system may be approaching from that direction.

### Verdict

**Sometimes useful, especially in regions dominated by west-to-east weather movement.**

It is less reliable when:

- Smoke causes the redness
- Tropical weather approaches from the east
- Local terrain controls weather
- The circulation pattern is blocked
- Clouds are unrelated to an approaching system

### Illustration 1: Red-sky logic

```text
EVENING

clear air to west       clouds to east
Sunset light  → → →     red clouds

possible improving weather


MORNING

Sunrise light → → →     clouds to west

possible approaching weather
```

---

## 17.6 Unusually Red Sunsets

A vivid red sunset may result from:

- Dry air
- Dust
- Smoke
- Pollution
- High clouds
- Volcanic aerosols

The color alone does not identify the cause.

A brilliant sunset during wildfire season may say more about suspended smoke than tomorrow’s rain.

### Verdict

**A clue about particles and cloud placement, but not a dependable forecast by itself.**

---

# Cloud Folklore

## 17.7 “Mackerel Sky and Mares’ Tails”

A **mackerel sky** refers to rippled patches of cirrocumulus or altocumulus resembling fish scales.

**Mares’ tails** refers to wispy cirrus shaped like streaming horse tails.

These clouds may appear as moisture and wind increase high in the atmosphere ahead of a frontal system.

A common progression is:

```text
wispy cirrus
      ↓
rippling high cloud
      ↓
thickening cirrostratus
      ↓
lower clouds
      ↓
precipitation
```

### Verdict

**Often meteorologically meaningful.**

It suggests changing upper-level moisture and wind, but it does not guarantee a storm or establish exact timing.

---

## 17.8 “A Ring Around the Moon Means Rain Soon”

A ring around the Moon is usually a 22-degree halo formed by ice crystals in high cirrostratus clouds.

Cirrostratus often arrives ahead of:

- Warm fronts
- Midlatitude cyclones
- Widespread precipitation

The halo therefore may precede rain or snow by many hours.

However, high ice cloud can occur without a major storm reaching the observer.

### Verdict

**One of the more useful traditional signs.**

It indicates moisture and ice crystals aloft, not a guaranteed rainfall deadline.

---

## 17.9 Counting Stars Inside the Halo

Some traditions claim that the number of stars visible inside a lunar halo predicts how many days remain before rain.

There is no reliable physical basis for this.

Star visibility depends on:

- Cloud thickness
- Moon brightness
- Haze
- Human eyesight
- Light pollution
- Which stars happen to be present

### Verdict

**Not useful.**

The stars are background objects, not a celestial countdown display.

---

## 17.10 “When Clouds Look Like Towers, Expect Showers”

Rapidly growing cumulus clouds indicate rising unstable air.

Clouds with strong vertical development may become:

- Towering cumulus
- Cumulonimbus
- Thunderstorms

Useful visual clues include:

- Crisp cauliflower edges
- Dark flat bases
- Rapid upward growth
- Repeated towers
- Anvil development

### Verdict

**Strongly useful.**

This is direct observation of convective development.

It becomes less predictive if:

- Dry air erodes the clouds
- A cap stops growth
- Sunset removes surface heating
- The towers remain shallow

---

## 17.11 “The Higher the Clouds, the Finer the Weather”

High clouds alone often produce little or no precipitation at the ground.

But they can also signal an approaching storm.

Low clouds may accompany rain, fog, or stable conditions.

The saying is therefore too broad.

### Verdict

**Unreliable without considering cloud type and trend.**

Thin isolated cirrus may coexist with fair weather.

A rapidly thickening veil of cirrostratus may indicate deterioration.

Cloud motion matters more than cloud altitude alone.

---

## 17.12 Lenticular Clouds Mean Strong Wind

Lens-shaped lenticular clouds form in standing waves created when stable air flows across mountains.

They indicate strong airflow and wave motion near mountain level.

Conditions near them may include:

- Turbulence
- Strong ridge-top wind
- Mountain waves
- Rotor circulation

They do not necessarily mean strong wind at the observer’s surface location.

### Verdict

**Useful, particularly in mountain regions and aviation.**

The cloud is evidence of strong flow aloft, not proof that every nearby valley is windy.

---

# Wind Sayings

## 17.13 “Wind From the East, Weather Not Fit for Beast”

In many middle-latitude locations, easterly wind can occur when low pressure or a coastal storm approaches from the south or west.

It may bring:

- Moisture
- Clouds
- Rain
- Snow
- Persistent cool conditions

But wind meaning is highly regional.

An east wind may be:

- A sea breeze
- Dry continental flow
- Tropical moisture
- Downslope wind
- Ordinary trade wind

### Verdict

**Potentially useful only in the region where the saying developed.**

A compass direction has no universal personality.

---

## 17.14 “Wind Before Rain, Soon Fine Again; Rain Before Wind, Long Rain Within”

This saying attempts to distinguish fast-moving frontal rain from a strengthening storm.

### Wind before rain

Increasing wind may signal a fast-approaching front.

Rain may pass quickly with the boundary.

### Rain before wind

Precipitation arriving before strong wind may indicate a larger, deepening low-pressure system with a broader rain shield.

The interpretation is plausible in some synoptic setups but far from universal.

### Verdict

**Occasionally insightful, but too inconsistent for dependable use.**

Tropical cyclones, thunderstorms, warm fronts, and mountain effects can produce many exceptions.

---

## 17.15 A Sudden Cool Breeze Means Rain

On a hot day, a sudden cool gust may signal a thunderstorm outflow boundary.

The gust front can arrive before:

- Rain
- Lightning
- Stronger wind
- Dust
- Rapid temperature drop

You may also smell rain or wet soil.

### Verdict

**Very useful for nearby thunderstorms.**

The rain may still miss your exact location, but the boundary shows that a storm is close enough to affect the air around you.

Treat thunder or lightning as the real safety signal.

---

## 17.16 Calm Before the Storm

Some storms are preceded by a temporary lull.

This can occur when:

- Wind patterns shift near a boundary
- Pressure falls
- Storm inflow changes direction
- A gust front has not yet arrived

But many storms arrive during already windy conditions.

A calm period can also mean nothing more than weak local pressure gradients.

### Verdict

**Possible, but not a dependable warning sign.**

Do not wait for ceremonial calm before taking shelter.

Storms have no obligation to create suspense properly.

---

# Smoke, Chimneys, and Campfires

## 17.17 “Smoke Goes High, No Rain Comes By”

When the lower atmosphere is unstable, smoke may rise and disperse vertically.

Unstable air often accompanies:

- Daytime heating
- Turbulent mixing
- Fair-weather cumulus
- Gusty wind

But instability can also support showers or thunderstorms.

### Verdict

**The smoke reveals stability, not a simple rain forecast.**

---

## 17.18 “Smoke Hangs Low, Rain or Snow”

Smoke trapped near the ground often indicates a stable layer or inversion.

Stable air may occur:

- At night
- Beneath high pressure
- Ahead of some warm fronts
- In valleys
- During fog formation

An approaching storm may strengthen low-level moisture and stability, but low smoke can also occur during clear calm weather.

### Verdict

**Useful for identifying stable air and poor dispersion, but weak as a direct precipitation forecast.**

It may also warn of:

- Fog
- Smoke accumulation
- Poor air quality

---

## 17.19 Campfire Smoke Changes Direction

Smoke that repeatedly shifts direction may indicate:

- Turbulent wind
- Nearby terrain effects
- Converging air
- Passing thermals
- Unsteady breeze

It does not necessarily mean rain.

During wildfire conditions, changing wind is a genuine safety concern.

### Verdict

**Useful as a local wind indicator, not a general forecast method.**

---

# Dew, Frost, and Morning Signs

## 17.20 “When Dew Is on the Grass, Rain Will Never Come to Pass”

Heavy dew usually forms during a clear, calm night.

These conditions often accompany:

- High pressure
- Weak wind
- Radiational cooling

Therefore, morning dew can indicate that the overnight period was settled.

But weather may change later in the day.

A front can arrive after sunrise.

Afternoon thunderstorms may form despite morning dew.

### Verdict

**Reasonable for describing the previous night, weak for forecasting the full day.**

Dew is a weather report written after the event.

---

## 17.21 No Dew Means Rain

Lack of dew may occur because:

- Clouds limited cooling
- Wind stayed strong
- Air was too dry
- The ground was warm
- Rain is approaching
- The surface was sheltered

Clouds and wind ahead of a storm can prevent dew, giving the saying some basis.

But dry air also prevents dew during fine weather.

### Verdict

**Not dependable without checking cloud, wind, and humidity.**

---

## 17.22 Heavy Frost Means Fair Weather

Frost commonly forms under:

- Clear sky
- Light wind
- Dry or moderately moist air
- Strong nighttime cooling

These conditions are often associated with high pressure and fair weather.

But frost can also occur shortly before clouds increase or a winter storm approaches.

### Verdict

**Useful as evidence of a clear calm night, not a guarantee about the following day.**

---

# Sound and Smell

## 17.23 “Sound Travels Far Before Rain”

People sometimes notice distant trains, highways, bells, or voices becoming unusually audible before rain.

This can happen during a temperature inversion.

Sound waves bend toward the ground when sound speed changes with height.

Stable air ahead of some weather systems may therefore allow sound to travel farther.

Wind direction also matters greatly.

### Verdict

**Physically plausible and sometimes useful.**

It indicates atmospheric layering and favorable sound propagation, not necessarily approaching rain.

---

## 17.24 You Can Smell Rain Coming

Several real processes may contribute.

### Petrichor

Dry soil and plants accumulate oils and organic compounds.

Rain releases these into the air.

### Geosmin

Soil microorganisms produce geosmin, which has a strong earthy odor.

Raindrops can aerosolize it.

### Ozone-like odor

Thunderstorm downdrafts may transport air containing reactive compounds from aloft, though the familiar pre-rain smell usually involves several sources.
