Wind can carry these odors ahead of the rain shaft.

### Verdict

**Often real for nearby rain, especially after a dry period.**

The smell usually indicates that rain is already falling somewhere upwind.

It is less useful for forecasting a storm many hours away.

---

## 17.25 Flowers Smell Stronger Before Rain

High humidity can affect how scents linger and travel.

Some plants may also release more aromatic compounds under certain moisture and temperature conditions.

Still air and stable layers may keep scent concentrated near the ground.

### Verdict

**Plausible as a humidity and air-movement clue, not a reliable rain predictor.**

The flowers may know the air is humid.

They have not necessarily reviewed the model guidance.

---

# Animal Behavior

## 17.26 Can Animals Sense Weather Changes?

Animals may detect environmental changes that humans overlook.

Possible cues include:

- Pressure changes
- Humidity
- Temperature
- Wind
- Low-frequency sound
- Static electricity
- Ground vibration
- Light changes
- Odors

The question is not whether animals respond to weather—they clearly do.

The difficult question is whether a particular behavior reliably predicts a particular event.

---

## 17.27 Birds Flying Low Means Rain

Birds and flying insects may remain lower when:

- Air is humid
- Wind is stronger aloft
- Insects concentrate near the ground
- Pressure changes
- Low clouds limit flight height

Swallows feeding low may therefore be following insects rather than directly forecasting rain.

The insects themselves may change altitude as humidity and wind alter flight.

### Verdict

**Sometimes meaningful, but indirect.**

Low-flying insect-eating birds may reflect changing atmospheric conditions associated with approaching weather.

It is not reliable enough to replace a forecast.

---

## 17.28 Birds Become Quiet Before a Storm

Bird activity may decrease when:

- Wind increases
- Pressure falls
- Light dims
- Rain approaches
- Thunder or infrasound is detected
- Shelter becomes preferable

Some species may feed intensely before a storm instead.

### Verdict

**Possible and species-dependent.**

Sudden widespread quiet can be an environmental clue, but it has many causes.

---

## 17.29 Seabirds Coming Inland

Seabirds may move inland or seek sheltered areas before severe coastal weather.

They may respond to:

- Wind
- Waves
- Pressure
- Food movement
- Storm-related turbulence

### Verdict

**Potentially useful near coasts, especially when observed by experienced local residents.**

Individual sightings are weak evidence.

Large unusual movements are more meaningful.

---

## 17.30 Cows Lying Down Means Rain

Cows lie down for many reasons:

- Rest
- Rumination
- Heat management
- Ground condition
- Herd behavior
- Comfort

Some claims suggest they keep a dry patch beneath them before rain, but cattle do not consistently demonstrate reliable meteorological forecasting.

### Verdict

**Mostly folklore.**

A field of reclining cattle indicates that cattle are reclining.

This is one of the more stable conclusions available in rural meteorology.

---

## 17.31 Horses and Livestock Face Away From Storms

Animals often orient themselves relative to wind and precipitation.

They may stand with their backs or hindquarters toward:

- Strong wind
- Rain
- Snow

This reduces exposure of the head and face.

### Verdict

**Useful as evidence of current wind and discomfort, not necessarily future weather.**

Livestock orientation may tell you which way the weather is coming from once it has already arrived.

---

## 17.32 Dogs Act Nervous Before Thunderstorms

Dogs may detect:

- Distant thunder
- Low-frequency sound
- Pressure change
- Static electricity
- Wind-borne odors
- Changes in household behavior

Some dogs become anxious before humans hear thunder.

### Verdict

**Often genuine for nearby thunderstorms, but individual animals vary.**

A nervous dog may provide early notice.

It may also be reacting to fireworks, a vacuum cleaner, or a bag whose movements are apparently suspicious.

---

## 17.33 Cats Wash Behind Their Ears Before Rain

Cats groom routinely.

Humidity may alter fur or static conditions, but evidence for this specific forecasting behavior is weak.

### Verdict

**Not reliable.**

The cat is conducting cat business and has declined to submit methodology.

---

## 17.34 Frogs Croak Before Rain

Frogs often become more active in humid or rainy conditions.

Moisture helps protect their skin and supports movement and breeding.

Increasing humidity before rain may trigger calling.

### Verdict

**Often connected with moist conditions and seasonal rainfall.**

Frog activity can indicate a favorable local environment, but it does not provide precise timing.

---

## 17.35 Crickets and Temperature

Cricket chirping rate often changes with temperature.

Within a suitable range, warmer conditions generally produce faster chirping.

Several informal formulas estimate temperature from chirp counts.

Accuracy depends on:

- Species
- Age
- Time of day
- Moisture
- Individual variation

### Verdict

**One of the better animal-based weather relationships, but it measures current temperature rather than forecasting future weather.**

The cricket is more thermometer than prophet.

---

## 17.36 Ants Build Higher Mounds Before Rain

Ants respond to moisture and may alter nest entrances or move brood when flooding threatens.

Some species may change activity before rain.

However, behavior varies enormously among species and environments.

### Verdict

**Plausible locally, but poorly suited to general forecasting.**

An experienced observer of one colony may notice meaningful changes.

A random anthill does not come with a standardized rain probability.

---

## 17.37 Spiders Build Larger Webs Before Fair Weather

Spider web-building depends on:

- Food availability
- Wind
- Humidity
- Web damage
- Reproductive stage
- Species

Calm conditions may favor larger intact webs.

Some spiders may rebuild after storms rather than before fair weather.

### Verdict

**Weak and inconsistent.**

A fresh web is better evidence of spider motivation than atmospheric stability.

---

## 17.38 Fish Bite Before a Storm

Changes in:

- Pressure
- Light
- Wind
- Water mixing
- Temperature
- Food movement

may affect fish behavior.

Anglers often report increased or decreased activity before fronts.

Different species respond differently, and fishing success also depends on technique and memory.

### Verdict

**Possible but highly variable.**

Barometric pressure may influence aquatic behavior, but there is no universal “falling pressure equals excellent fishing” rule.

The fish have continued refusing peer review.

---

# Insects and Weather

## 17.39 Mosquitoes Before Rain

Mosquito activity depends on:

- Temperature
- Humidity
- Wind
- Light
- Standing water

High humidity and light wind often favor activity.

Strong wind suppresses flight.

Warm humid evenings before a front may therefore produce more mosquitoes.

### Verdict

**Useful as an indicator of current favorable insect conditions, not a direct storm forecast.**

---

## 17.40 Bees Return Early to the Hive

Bees may reduce foraging before rain or strong wind.

They respond to:

- Light
- Temperature
- Wind
- Humidity
- Pressure
- Flower availability

A sudden return of many bees may indicate deteriorating local conditions.

### Verdict

**Potentially meaningful to experienced beekeepers.**

It is still not a substitute for radar when thunderstorms threaten.

---

## 17.41 Fireflies and Weather

Firefly activity depends strongly on:

- Temperature
- Moisture
- Wind
- Season
- Habitat

Warm humid calm evenings often produce greater activity.

### Verdict

**A good indicator of suitable current conditions, not future rainfall.**

Their performance schedule is climatic and ecological rather than prophetic.

---

# Plants and Weather Signs

## 17.42 Pinecones Close Before Rain

Pinecone scales respond to humidity.

In dry conditions, they tend to open and release seeds.

In humid conditions, they close.

This protects seeds from being released when they are less likely to travel effectively.

### Verdict

**Physically real as a humidity indicator.**

A closing pinecone suggests increasing moisture but does not guarantee rain.

It is a simple natural hygrometer.

---

## 17.43 Flowers Close Before Rain

Some flowers open and close in response to:

- Light
- Humidity
- Temperature
- Time of day
- Pollinator activity

Species differ greatly.

Examples may include:

- Dandelions
- Tulips
- Clover
- Morning glories

Closure during humid cloudy weather may precede rain, but the behavior may also follow the daily light cycle.

### Verdict

**Potentially useful for particular species once their ordinary rhythm is understood.**

Without that knowledge, the flower may simply be going to bed.

---

## 17.44 Leaves Turn Over Before a Storm

Some trees show the pale undersides of leaves when wind strengthens before storms.

This is common where leaves are:

- Flexible
- Light underneath
- Easily twisted by gusts

Pressure change itself is not usually flipping them.

Wind is.

### Verdict

**A useful sign of increasing or turbulent wind, often ahead of thunderstorms.**

It indicates the storm’s airflow rather than an independent biological forecast.

---

## 17.45 Seaweed Becomes Damp Before Rain

Traditional “weather houses” and seaweed displays respond to humidity.

Many organic materials absorb moisture from the air and change:

- Texture
- Shape
- Flexibility
- Weight

### Verdict

**A real humidity response.**

Humidity may increase before rain, but it can also rise overnight or near water without precipitation.

---

# Human Body Folklore

## 17.46 Aching Joints Predict Rain

Many people report increased joint pain before changes in weather.

Possible contributors include:

- Pressure change
- Humidity
- Temperature
- Reduced activity
- Individual sensitivity
- Expectations

Research has produced mixed results, and responses vary.

A small pressure change might affect tissues around an injured or arthritic joint, but the mechanism and predictability remain uncertain.

### Verdict

**Possibly real for some individuals, but unreliable as a general forecast tool.**

A personal symptom diary may reveal whether one person has a consistent relationship.

---

## 17.47 Headaches Before Storms

Some people report headaches or migraines related to:

- Pressure changes
- Heat
- Humidity
- Bright sunlight
- Dehydration
- Sleep disruption
- Storm-related allergens

Weather may be one trigger among several.

### Verdict

**Plausible and individually variable.**

Symptoms are not a safe substitute for checking warnings, and recurring headaches deserve appropriate medical attention rather than assignment to a dramatic cloud.

---

## 17.48 Hair Becomes Frizzy Before Rain

Human hair absorbs moisture.

High humidity changes hydrogen bonding within hair fibers, causing some hair types to curl, swell, or frizz.

### Verdict

**A real humidity indicator.**

It does not prove rain will occur, but it can reveal increasing atmospheric moisture.

Hair is an imperfect hygrometer with unusually complicated maintenance requirements.

---

# Seasonal Folklore

## 17.49 Groundhog Day

Groundhog Day tradition claims that a groundhog seeing its shadow predicts six more weeks of winter.

A visible shadow merely indicates sunshine at the observation site.

It has no demonstrated relationship with weather six weeks later.

The date falls near the midpoint between the winter solstice and spring equinox, when seasonal change is already underway.

### Verdict

**Ceremonial, not meteorological.**

The groundhog has no verified seasonal forecasting advantage over ordinary climate statistics.

It does, however, have an excellent communications department.

---

## 17.50 Woolly Bear Caterpillars

Folklore claims the width of colored bands on woolly bear caterpillars predicts winter severity.

Band appearance varies with:

- Species
- Age
- Growth
- Molting
- Genetics
- Local conditions

It does not reliably forecast the coming winter.

### Verdict

**Not useful for seasonal prediction.**

The caterpillar’s markings describe the caterpillar.

---

## 17.51 Persimmon Seeds

Some traditions split persimmon seeds and interpret the shape inside as:

- Spoon: much snow
- Knife: cutting cold
- Fork: mild winter

The internal seed structure reflects plant development, not a forecast of future regional circulation.

### Verdict

**Not meteorologically useful.**

Also considerably more work than reading a seasonal outlook.

---

## 17.52 Onion Skins and Corn Husks

Thick onion skins or corn husks are sometimes said to predict severe winter.

Plant growth responds to conditions during the growing season, including:

- Moisture
- Temperature
- Genetics
- Soil
- Pests

These cannot reliably predict atmospheric patterns months later.

### Verdict

**Not useful for forecasting the next winter.**

They may reveal something about the previous growing season.

---

## 17.53 Abundant Acorns Mean a Hard Winter

Oak trees produce highly variable acorn crops.

Large crops, called **mast years**, may result from:

- Previous weather
- Reproductive cycles
- Predator relationships
- Tree health
- Synchronization among trees

They do not reliably predict future winter severity.

### Verdict

**Ecologically interesting, meteorologically weak.**

The oaks are planning reproduction, not publishing an outlook.

---

## 17.54 Early Animal Coats Mean Severe Winter

Animal coat growth is influenced mainly by:

- Day length
- Genetics
- Temperature
- Health
- Nutrition

Seasonal coat development begins as daylight changes, before the specific winter weather is known.

### Verdict

**Not a reliable long-range forecast.**

A thick coat shows biological preparation for the season, not knowledge of January’s pressure pattern.

---

# Calendar Sayings

## 17.55 “March Comes In Like a Lion and Goes Out Like a Lamb”

March often begins with winter-like weather and ends with milder conditions in the Northern Hemisphere.

This reflects the normal seasonal warming trend.

But individual years may reverse the sequence.

### Verdict

**A colorful description of climatology, not a forecast.**

March can arrive as a lamb, depart as a lion, and spend the middle pretending to be July.

---

## 17.56 “April Showers Bring May Flowers”

In many temperate climates, spring rainfall supports plant growth.

The saying describes a broad seasonal relationship.

But:

- April may be dry
- May flowers depend on earlier weather
- Excess rain can damage plants
- Timing differs by region

### Verdict

**Broadly true as seasonal ecology, not specific forecasting.**

---

## 17.57 “Thunder in Winter Means Snow in Seven Days”

Winter thunderstorms sometimes occur in dynamic storm systems.

Another weather system may arrive within a week because active patterns often produce repeated storms.

But there is no consistent seven-day physical rule.

### Verdict

**Mostly folklore.**

Thunder in winter does indicate instability and a vigorous current system.

It does not schedule next week’s snow.

---

## 17.58 “A Green Christmas Means a White Easter”

This suggests that mild early winter conditions will be balanced by late snow.

There is no dependable atmospheric mechanism requiring such compensation.

Weather does not maintain a seasonal fairness ledger.

### Verdict

**Not useful.**

---

# Pressure-Based Folk Instruments

## 17.59 Weather Glasses

A traditional weather glass contains liquid in a sealed or partly open vessel.

Liquid level changes may respond to:

- Atmospheric pressure
- Temperature
- Evaporation

Some designs function as crude barometers.

Others are strongly affected by indoor temperature and are difficult to interpret.

### Verdict

**Potentially useful if properly designed and calibrated.**

A decorative bottle with colored water may be more ornament than instrument.

---

## 17.60 Storm Glasses

A storm glass contains chemicals that form changing crystals.

Claims associate different crystal patterns with future weather.

Changes are influenced strongly by:

- Temperature
- Placement
- Solution composition
- Time

There is little evidence that storm glasses provide reliable forecasts beyond responding to their immediate thermal environment.

### Verdict

**Attractive but not dependable.**

It is a chemistry display with an ambitious job title.

---

## 17.61 Weather Ropes

A natural-fiber rope may tighten or loosen as humidity changes.

Materials such as:

- Hair
- Gut
- Wood
- Plant fiber

have historically been used in hygrometers.

### Verdict

**Real as a humidity indicator when properly calibrated.**

It forecasts rain only indirectly because high humidity has many causes.

---

# Which Folklore Signs Are Most Useful?

## 17.62 Strong Physical Signs

These have a clear and immediate meteorological basis:

- Rapidly growing towering cumulus
- Sudden cool thunderstorm outflow
- Thickening high clouds ahead of a front
- Halo around Sun or Moon
- Leaves flipping in strengthening gusts
- Smoke trapped beneath an inversion
- Rainbow with Sun behind and rain ahead
- Pinecones responding to humidity
- Distant sound carrying during stable layering
- Rain smell arriving from precipitation upwind

These observations do not replace instruments, but they reveal real atmospheric processes.

---

## 17.63 Moderately Useful Local Signs

These may work for experienced observers in particular environments:

- Seabirds seeking shelter
- Bees returning early
- Frogs becoming active
- Specific mountain clouds
- Local wind-direction sayings
- Particular valley fog signs
- Livestock reacting to wind
- Familiar cloud sequences

Their value depends on:

- Species
- Geography
- Season
- Observer experience

Local knowledge becomes useful when it is systematic rather than merely memorable.

---

## 17.64 Weak or Unsupported Signs

These include:

- Groundhog shadows
- Woolly bear bands
- Persimmon-seed shapes
- Acorn abundance
- Cows lying down
- Cats washing behind their ears
- Exact star counts inside lunar halos
- Thunder guaranteeing snow seven days later
- Plant thickness predicting the coming winter

They may remain culturally enjoyable.

A saying does not need scientific accuracy to be part of human tradition.

It simply should not be used to decide whether to evacuate.

---

# Testing a Weather Saying

## 17.65 Turn It Into a Testable Claim

Suppose the saying is:

> A halo around the Moon means rain within 24 hours.

Define:

- What counts as a halo?
- What counts as rain?
- At which location?
- Within exactly how many hours?
- How many observations are needed?

A testable version might be:

> After a clearly observed 22-degree lunar halo, measurable precipitation will occur at the observing site within 24 hours.

Now the claim can be evaluated.

---

## 17.66 Record Every Observation

Keep a table:

| Date | Sign observed? | Weather afterward | Success? |
|---|---|---|---|
| Jan. 8 | Halo | Rain after 14 hours | Yes |
| Jan. 21 | Halo | No precipitation | No |
| Feb. 4 | No halo | Rain after 10 hours | Missed event |

Record failures as carefully as successes.

Without failures, the test becomes a scrapbook.

---

## 17.67 Compare With the Base Rate

Suppose rain occurs on 40% of days during the season.

A sign predicting rain correctly 45% of the time may sound useful but barely improves on ordinary climatology.

A useful sign should outperform simple alternatives such as:

- Rain usually does not occur
- Tomorrow resembles today
- Seasonal rain frequency
- Official forecast

This is called comparing with a **baseline**.

---

## 17.68 Hits, Misses, and False Alarms

Forecast verification uses categories such as:

### Hit

The sign occurs and the weather follows.

### Miss

The weather occurs without the sign.

### False alarm

The sign occurs but the weather does not.

### Correct rejection

The sign does not occur and neither does the weather.

```text
                    WEATHER OCCURS   WEATHER DOES NOT
SIGN OCCURS              hit          false alarm
SIGN ABSENT              miss         correct rejection
```

A good sign needs more than memorable hits.

It also needs reasonably few misses and false alarms.

---

## 17.69 Sample Size

Ten observations are rarely enough.

Weather varies naturally.

A meaningful test may require:

- Dozens of cases
- Multiple seasons
- Consistent observation
- Similar definitions
- Different weather patterns

A saying can look perfect after three successes and collapse by observation twenty.

Small samples are where coincidences dress most convincingly.

---

## 17.70 Separate Forecasting From Detection

Some sayings do not predict future weather.

They detect weather already nearby.

Examples:

- Smelling rain
- Sudden cool outflow
- Dogs hearing distant thunder
- Leaves flipping in gusts
- Darkening cloud bases

These signs can still be useful.

But their lead time may be minutes rather than hours or days.

A detector should not be advertised as a long-range forecast.

---

# Building Modern Folklore

## 17.71 Reliable Personal Signs

You may discover local relationships such as:

- Clouds over a particular ridge precede rain
- A certain wind direction brings sea fog
- Dew points above a threshold produce morning fog in your valley
- Thunderstorms split around nearby terrain
- A north wind clears smoke
- One road freezes before others

These can become a form of modern local folklore.

Their value improves when supported by:

- Records
- Maps
- Repeated observation
- Physical explanation

---

## 17.72 Combine Old and New

A field observer might notice:

- Halo around the Moon
- Pressure falling
- Southerly wind increasing
- Cirrus thickening
- Official forecast showing a warm front

Together, these provide stronger evidence than the halo alone.

Traditional signs become most useful when connected to modern observations.

The old saying supplies the clue.

Meteorology supplies the mechanism.

---

# Field Observation  
## Test the Moon-Halo Saying

For every lunar halo you observe:

1. Record the time.
2. Describe the cloud.
3. Note pressure and wind.
4. Check whether precipitation occurs within:
   - 12 hours
   - 24 hours
   - 36 hours
5. Record the amount.

After at least ten cases, calculate how often the sign succeeded.

Also record rainy periods that occurred without a previous halo.

This prevents the halo from receiving credit for forecasts it never made.

---

# Try It  
## Build a Natural Hygrometer

Collect a dry pinecone.

Place it indoors in a dry area and observe the scale position.

Then place it in a humid protected environment—not submerged in water.

Watch how the scales gradually close.

Return it to dry air and observe reopening.

This demonstrates how plant material responds to humidity.

Do not expect the pinecone to distinguish between:

- Approaching rain
- A steamy bathroom
- Coastal humidity
- Someone boiling pasta

It is detecting moisture, not context.

---

# Field Observation  
## Follow Bird and Insect Height

On warm days, record:

- Wind
- Humidity
- Cloud cover
- Bird flight height
- Insect activity
- Later precipitation

Use the same location and time of day when possible.

After several weeks, examine whether low bird flight consistently precedes rain or merely occurs during humid calm periods.

---

# Try It  
## Test the Red-Sky Rule

For each vivid red sunrise or sunset, record:

- Direction of cloud
- Wind direction
- Weather-system movement
- Weather during the next 24 hours
- Presence of smoke or haze

Separate:

- Redness caused by illuminated clouds
- Redness caused mainly by smoke or particles

The saying should perform better when the weather pattern moves west to east.

---

# Field Exercise  
## Create a Folklore Scorecard

Choose ten sayings and classify them:

| Saying | Physical basis | Local or universal? | Reliability |
|---|---|---|---|
| Moon halo before rain | Ice cloud ahead of front | Broad but imperfect | Moderate |
| Cows lie down before rain | Unclear | Not reliable | Low |
| Towering clouds bring showers | Convective growth | Broad | High |
| Pinecones close before rain | Humidity response | Broad but indirect | Moderate |

Use these reliability levels:

- High
- Moderate
- Low
- Unsupported

The purpose is not to ruin folklore.

It is to separate observation from decoration.

---

# Weather Safety  
## Never Let Folklore Override a Warning

Do not ignore an official warning because:

- Animals seem calm
- The sky is not green
- There was no red sunrise
- Cows remain standing
- The storm is approaching from an unusual direction
- A seasonal tradition predicted a mild winter

Dangerous weather does not require traditional signs.

Modern warnings use:

- Radar
- Satellite
- Surface observations
- Weather balloons
- Computer models
- Trained spotters
- Emergency reports

Folklore can deepen awareness.

It must not delay action.

A tornado is not required to consult the livestock before forming.

---

# Chapter Summary

Weather folklore developed from generations of close observation, local experience, memory, and cultural storytelling.

Some sayings contain real meteorological information.

Others survive mainly because they rhyme, entertain, or occasionally appear correct.

Confirmation bias causes people to remember successful predictions more readily than failures.

Local sayings may work only where prevailing wind, terrain, and storm tracks match the setting in which they developed.

Red-sky sayings can be useful in middle latitudes where weather commonly moves from west to east.

Mackerel skies, mares’ tails, and lunar halos may indicate increasing moisture ahead of a frontal system.

Rapidly growing towering cumulus is a strong sign of developing showers or thunderstorms.

A sudden cool breeze on a hot day may be thunderstorm outflow and can provide useful short-term warning.

Smoke behavior reveals atmospheric stability and wind but does not directly guarantee rain.

Dew and frost usually describe clear calm overnight conditions more reliably than the coming day.

Unusually distant sound may result from temperature inversions and favorable wind.

The smell of approaching rain can be real when wind carries aerosols and odors from rainfall upwind.

Animals can respond to pressure, sound, humidity, wind, and temperature.

However, many specific animal sayings are inconsistent or unsupported.

Dogs may detect distant thunderstorms, frogs respond to moisture, and cricket chirping reflects temperature.

Cows lying down, cat grooming, groundhog shadows, woolly bear bands, persimmon seeds, and abundant acorns do not provide reliable weather forecasts.

Plants such as pinecones can respond directly to humidity and serve as crude natural hygrometers.

A weather saying should be tested by recording hits, misses, false alarms, and correct rejections.

Its performance should be compared with the ordinary frequency of the predicted weather.

Many apparent forecasts are actually detections of weather already nearby.

Traditional observations become more useful when combined with pressure trends, maps, radar, forecasts, and a physical explanation.

Folklore preserves generations of attention to the natural world.

Science does not require abandoning that attention.

It asks us to record it more carefully.

---

## Weather-Watcher’s Vocabulary

**Weather folklore:** Traditional sayings or beliefs used to anticipate weather.  
**Confirmation bias:** Tendency to notice evidence supporting a belief and overlook contrary evidence.  
**Local knowledge:** Understanding developed through repeated observation of one particular environment.  
**Baseline:** Simple reference forecast used to judge whether another method adds skill.  
**Petrichor:** Earthy scent associated with rain on dry ground.  
**Geosmin:** Strong-smelling compound produced by soil microorganisms.  
**Natural hygrometer:** Material or organism that changes predictably with humidity.  
**Mackerel sky:** Rippled high or middle cloud pattern resembling fish scales.  
**Mares’ tails:** Wispy cirrus clouds resembling streaming horse tails.  
**Weather sign:** Observable condition associated with current or future weather.  
**Hit:** Forecast sign occurs and the predicted weather follows.  
**Miss:** Predicted weather occurs without the sign.  
**False alarm:** Sign occurs but predicted weather does not.  
**Correct rejection:** Sign is absent and predicted weather does not occur.  
**Base rate:** Ordinary frequency of an event without using a special forecast sign.  
**Detection:** Recognition of weather already occurring or nearby.  
**Forecast lead time:** Time between a prediction and the event.  
**Sample size:** Number of observations used to evaluate a claim.  
**Verification:** Comparison between a forecast and observed outcome.

**Next: Chapter 18 — Weather Instruments: Building and Using a Home Observing Station**

<!-- End of source chapter -->

---

# Chapter 18  
## Weather Instruments: Building and Using a Home Observing Station

A weather station turns casual noticing into measurement.

Instead of saying:

> It felt unusually humid.

you can record:

- Temperature: 84°F
- Dew point: 73°F
- Relative humidity: 69%
- Pressure: 29.78 inches and falling
- Wind: south at 12 mph, gusting to 21
- Rainfall: 0.42 inch

The sky has not become less interesting. It has simply begun leaving receipts.

A home weather station can range from a thermometer on a shaded porch to a carefully installed electronic system measuring temperature, humidity, pressure, wind, rainfall, solar radiation, and soil conditions.

The instrument matters.

Its placement matters more.

An expensive sensor installed beside an air-conditioning exhaust vent is an elegant way to measure the air-conditioning exhaust vent.

---

# What a Weather Station Measures

## 18.1 The Core Observations

A basic station may measure:

- Temperature
- Humidity
- Atmospheric pressure
- Wind direction
- Wind speed
- Rainfall

More advanced systems may also measure:

- Solar radiation
- Ultraviolet radiation
- Soil temperature
- Soil moisture
- Leaf wetness
- Lightning
- Visibility
- Air quality

Not every station needs every sensor.

The most useful system is one you can install properly, maintain consistently, and understand.

---

## 18.2 Measurement Versus Estimate

Some station values are directly measured.

Others are calculated.

### Directly measured

- Air temperature
- Relative humidity
- Pressure
- Wind speed
- Rainfall

### Calculated or derived

- Dew point
- Heat index
- Wind chill
- Rain rate
- Apparent temperature
- Pressure tendency
- Evapotranspiration

A calculated value can be useful, but its quality depends on the measurements and formula used.

If the humidity sensor is wrong, the dew point will be wrong with admirable mathematical precision.

---

# Temperature

## 18.3 Thermometers

Temperature sensors may use:

- Liquid expansion
- Bimetallic strips
- Electrical resistance
- Thermistors
- Semiconductor devices

Most modern home stations use electronic sensors.

The sensor converts a temperature-dependent electrical property into a digital reading.

---

## 18.4 What Air Temperature Means

Air temperature is intended to describe the thermal state of the surrounding air.

It is not the same as:

- Surface temperature
- Temperature in direct sunlight
- Car-dashboard temperature
- Roof temperature
- Soil temperature
- “Feels like” temperature

A black driveway may exceed 120°F while the shaded air temperature is 90°F.

Both readings may be valid.

They measure different things.

---

## 18.5 Radiation Shields

A temperature sensor should be protected by a **radiation shield**.

The shield:

- Blocks direct sunlight
- Reduces reflected radiation
- Allows air circulation
- Protects from precipitation

A common design uses stacked white plates with gaps between them.

### Illustration 1: Radiation shield

```text
      sunlight
        ↓↓↓

    ┌─────────┐
    ├─────────┤
air → sensor  → air
    ├─────────┤
    └─────────┘

white plates block radiation
while allowing ventilation
```

Without a shield, sunlight can heat the sensor above the true air temperature.

---

## 18.6 Aspirated Shields

An **aspirated shield** uses a fan to pull air past the temperature sensor.

This improves ventilation during:

- Calm wind
- Strong sunshine
- Hot conditions

Naturally ventilated shields depend on ambient airflow.

Fan-assisted shields may be more accurate but require:

- Power
- Cleaning
- Fan maintenance

A failed fan can create its own bias, so more complicated equipment is not automatically better.

---

## 18.7 Temperature-Sensor Placement

For representative outdoor air temperature, place the sensor:

- Over natural ground when possible
- In an open area
- Away from pavement
- Away from walls
- Away from rooftops
- Away from exhaust vents
- Away from direct artificial heat

A common climate-observing height is roughly 4–6 feet above the ground.

Exact professional standards depend on the network and purpose, but consistency and good exposure are essential.

---

## 18.8 Common Temperature Errors

### Too close to a wall

The wall stores heat and alters airflow.

### Above pavement

Pavement heats strongly during the day and releases heat at night.

### Beneath dense trees

Shade is helpful, but trees reduce airflow and create a distinct microclimate.

### Near an air conditioner

Exhaust air produces artificial warmth.

### Too close to the house

The building modifies temperature, radiation, and wind.

### Inside an enclosed porch

The station measures the porch environment rather than free outdoor air.

---

# Humidity and Dew Point

## 18.9 Hygrometers

A **hygrometer** measures humidity.

Modern stations commonly use electronic sensors whose electrical properties change as they absorb water vapor.

Older or mechanical hygrometers used materials such as:

- Human or animal hair
- Plant fibers
- Organic membranes

These materials expand or contract with humidity.

A strand of hair can therefore become a weather instrument, though not usually one with wireless syncing.

---

## 18.10 Relative Humidity

Relative humidity compares the water vapor present with the amount required for saturation at the current temperature.

It changes when:

- Moisture changes
- Temperature changes
- Both change

A cool morning may have high relative humidity even when the actual moisture content is moderate.

A hot afternoon may have lower relative humidity while containing the same amount of water vapor.

---

## 18.11 Dew Point

Dew point is calculated from temperature and humidity.

It describes the temperature to which air must cool to become saturated.

Because dew point reflects actual moisture more directly than relative humidity, it is especially useful for describing how humid the air feels.

Approximate comfort categories vary by person, but a simple warm-season guide is:

| Dew point | General feel |
|---:|---|
| Below 50°F | Dry |
| 50–59°F | Comfortable to mildly humid |
| 60–64°F | Noticeably humid |
| 65–69°F | Muggy |
| 70°F and above | Very humid |

These are human-comfort descriptions, not universal scientific thresholds.

Someone from coastal South Carolina and someone from Arizona may react quite differently to the same number.

---

## 18.12 Humidity-Sensor Problems

Humidity sensors may drift because of:

- Age
- Dust
- Salt
- Condensation
- Pollution
- Insects
- Chemical exposure

A sensor may become stuck near:

- 99%
- 1%
- One repeated value

Compare with nearby stations and inspect for persistent unrealistic behavior.

Humidity sensors are among the more temperamental members of the home-station household.

---

# Pressure

## 18.13 Barometers

A barometer measures atmospheric pressure.

Modern home stations usually use small electronic pressure sensors.

Because pressure changes with elevation, software may display:

- Station pressure
- Sea-level pressure
- Relative pressure
- Corrected pressure

The labels vary among manufacturers.

Know which one you are reading.

---

## 18.14 Station Pressure

Station pressure is the actual pressure at the sensor’s elevation.

A high-elevation station naturally records a lower value than a station near sea level.

Station pressure is useful for scientific calculations but less familiar to the public.

---

## 18.15 Sea-Level Pressure

Sea-level pressure adjusts the observed value so stations at different elevations can be compared.

Weather maps usually use sea-level pressure.

A home station may require you to enter:

- Elevation
- Calibration offset
- Local reference pressure

Incorrect elevation can create a persistent error.

---

## 18.16 Calibrating Pressure

A practical method is to compare your station with a nearby reliable official observation during calm, stable weather.

Avoid calibration during:

- Thunderstorm passage
- Rapidly deepening low pressure
- Strong fronts
- Large elevation differences
- Major local pressure gradients

Pressure should not be adjusted repeatedly to chase every nearby station.

Small differences are normal.

Calibrate the offset, then watch the trend.

---

## 18.17 Pressure Trend

Pressure tendency is often more useful than the exact value.

### Falling pressure

May indicate:

- Approaching low
- Front
- Increasing wind
- Storm development

### Rising pressure

May indicate:

- Departing storm
- Building high
- Cold-air arrival
- Clearing

### Rapid change

Signals a more active weather pattern.

A pressure graph turns the invisible movement of weather systems into a visible curve.

---

## 18.18 The Barometer Indoors

Unlike temperature and wind sensors, a barometer can usually remain indoors.

Ordinary buildings are not airtight enough to prevent atmospheric pressure changes from reaching the sensor.

Keep it away from:

- Direct heating
- Rapid temperature changes
- Powerful fans
- Sealed pressurized rooms

The house can block rain.

It does not generally block the weight of the atmosphere.

---

# Wind

## 18.19 Anemometers

An **anemometer** measures wind speed.

Common home designs include:

- Rotating cups
- Propeller
- Ultrasonic sensors

### Cup anemometer

Three or four cups rotate around a vertical axis.

Rotation speed is converted to wind speed.

### Propeller anemometer

A small propeller faces the wind.

It often works with a wind vane.

### Ultrasonic anemometer

Measures differences in sound-pulse travel time.

It has no moving parts.

---

## 18.20 Wind Vanes

A **wind vane** shows wind direction.

The vane aligns so its pointed end faces into the wind.

Wind direction is named for the direction from which the wind comes.

A north wind blows from north toward south.

This convention exists mainly so beginners may confidently reverse it at least once.

---

## 18.21 Standard Wind Exposure

Professional wind measurements are commonly taken about 10 meters above open ground.

Most homes cannot provide perfect exposure.

Nearby obstacles create:

- Turbulence
- Wind shadows
- Channeling
- Acceleration

A practical home station may still provide useful local wind information, but it should be interpreted as a site-specific measurement.

---

## 18.22 Obstacle Effects

Wind disturbance can extend many times the height of an obstacle.

Buildings and trees can:

- Reduce average wind
- Produce false gusts
- Change direction
- Create eddies
- Shelter one sector more than another

A sensor mounted two feet above a roof may experience roof-induced turbulence.

A taller mast improves exposure but introduces:

- Structural load
- Lightning risk
- Installation difficulty
- Maintenance problems

The perfect wind site and the convenient wind site are rarely on speaking terms.

---

## 18.23 Compromise Wind Placement

When a 10-meter tower is impractical:

- Choose the highest safe open location
- Avoid immediate roof edges
- Keep distance from trees
- Document the height
- Treat results as local rather than official-standard wind

Consistency matters.

A lower sensor in a known location can still reveal:

- Front passages
- Gust trends
- Sea breezes
- Storm outflow
- Daily wind cycles

---

## 18.24 Wind Direction Errors

Common problems include:

- Vane installed backward
- North alignment entered incorrectly
- Magnetic compass interference
- Mast rotation
- Ice or debris
- Stiff bearings
- Trees blocking one direction

Test the vane before final installation.

Rotate it manually and confirm that the display matches the physical direction.

Discovering during a hurricane that east and west have been swapped is late-stage quality control.

---

## 18.25 Gust Measurement

Electronic stations sample wind repeatedly and calculate gusts over short periods.

Different devices may use different:

- Sampling intervals
- Averaging periods
- Gust definitions

Two nearby stations can report different maximum gusts even in similar exposure.

The difference may reflect both real turbulence and measurement design.

---

# Rainfall

## 18.26 Manual Rain Gauges

A manual rain gauge is one of the most useful and understandable weather instruments.

A standard-style gauge often contains:

- Outer cylinder
- Funnel
- Narrow measuring tube
- Overflow container

The narrow tube enlarges the apparent water depth, allowing finer measurement.

---

## 18.27 Reading a Rain Gauge

Read the bottom of the curved water surface, called the **meniscus**, at eye level.

Record rainfall to the gauge’s marked precision.

Avoid:

- Tilting the gauge
- Reading from above
- Leaving accumulated water for days
- Counting sprinkler water as a thunderstorm

Empty the gauge after each scheduled observation.

---

## 18.28 Tipping-Bucket Gauges

Automatic stations often use a tipping bucket.

Rain flows through a funnel into one of two small cups.

When the cup fills to a set amount, it tips and the other moves into place.

Each tip is counted.

### Illustration 2: Tipping bucket

```text
 rain
  ↓
funnel
  ↓
   \ cup A
    \____
         \ pivot
    ____/
   / cup B

each tip records
a fixed rainfall amount
```

---

## 18.29 Tipping-Bucket Limitations

Tipping buckets may undercount during very heavy rain because water continues entering while the mechanism tips.

Other problems include:

- Leaves
- Bird droppings
- Insects
- Spider webs
- Misleveling
- Splash
- Frozen precipitation

The funnel should be inspected regularly.

A surprisingly large percentage of home meteorology consists of asking whether a spider has sabotaged the equipment.

---

## 18.30 Rain-Gauge Placement

Place a rain gauge:

- Level
- In an open area
- Away from roof drips
- Away from trees
- Away from sprinkler spray
- Away from walls and fences
- Where it can be maintained safely

Nearby obstacles should not create strong wind turbulence or block rainfall.

Completely open windy sites can also undercatch rain, so ideal placement balances exposure and shelter.

---

## 18.31 Comparing Automatic and Manual Gauges

A manual gauge is valuable for checking an electronic tipping bucket.

After each storm, compare totals.

Possible causes of disagreement include:

- Heavy-rain undercatch
- Debris
- Evaporation
- Wind
- Miscalibration
- Unequal placement
- Different observation periods

A small difference is normal.

A repeated large difference requires investigation.

---

## 18.32 Rain Rate

Rain rate describes how fast rain is falling.

An automatic gauge estimates rate from the timing of bucket tips.

A brief intense burst can produce a very high calculated hourly rate even if it lasts only a few minutes.

Example:

A station may display:

```text
rain rate: 6 inches per hour
```

This does not mean six inches will fall.

It means the current short interval, if sustained for an hour, would produce six inches.

Rain-rate displays are enthusiastic extrapolators.

---

# Snow and Ice

## 18.33 Snow Boards

A snow board is a flat, light-colored board placed over representative ground.

It should be:

- Away from major drifts
- Away from buildings
- Away from trees
- Easy to locate after snowfall
- Level

A small marker or flag can help you find it.

The board should not become so well hidden that measuring snow turns into winter archaeology.

---

## 18.34 Measuring New Snow

Use a ruler held vertically.

Take several measurements and average them.

Report to an appropriate precision, commonly the nearest tenth of an inch for small amounts and practical increments for deeper snow.

Measure representative accumulation, not the deepest drift.

---

## 18.35 Measuring Snow Depth

Snow depth is measured at several representative ground locations.

Avoid:

- Plowed piles
- Drifts
- Bare patches
- Roof slides
- Areas beneath trees

Average the readings.

Snow depth may differ from new snowfall because of:

- Settling
- Melting
- Compaction
- Blowing

---

## 18.36 Snow Water Equivalent

Collect a representative core of snow or use a precipitation gauge designed for frozen precipitation.

Melt the sample and measure the water.

Then calculate:

```text
snow depth ÷ melted water depth
=
snow-to-liquid ratio
```

A sample 10 inches deep yielding 0.8 inch of water has a ratio of:

```text
10 ÷ 0.8 = 12.5:1
```

---

## 18.37 Ice Accretion

Freezing-rain accumulation is difficult to measure accurately.

Ice may coat:

- Branches
- Wires
- Railings
- Flat surfaces

The thickness on one side of a cylindrical object is not the same as the total diameter increase.

For a simple estimate:

1. Measure the object’s diameter before icing.
2. Measure it after icing.
3. Subtract the original diameter.
4. Divide by two.

```text
ice thickness =
(new diameter − original diameter) ÷ 2
```

Do not approach ice-coated trees or power lines to improve the measurement.

The weather station is not entitled to a heroic ending.

---

# Solar and Ultraviolet Radiation

## 18.38 Solar-Radiation Sensors

A solar-radiation sensor measures incoming sunlight, often in watts per square meter.

It can help track:

- Cloud cover
- Solar heating
- Day length
- Storm shadows
- Energy available for evaporation

The sensor requires:

- Unobstructed sky
- Level mounting
- Clean surface

Dust, snow, bird activity, and shade create errors.

---

## 18.39 Pyranometers

A **pyranometer** is a more specialized instrument measuring solar irradiance from the sky above.

High-quality versions use carefully calibrated sensing surfaces and domes.

Home-station solar sensors are often simpler.

They remain useful for relative changes even when not research-grade.

---

## 18.40 UV Sensors

Ultraviolet sensors estimate UV exposure.

Readings depend on:

- Sun angle
- Ozone
- Clouds
- Elevation
- Reflection
- Aerosols

Clouds do not always eliminate UV.

Thin clouds may allow substantial radiation through.

Snow and water can reflect UV and increase exposure.

A UV sensor is useful, but sunscreen should not await formal instrumentation approval.

---

# Soil and Garden Measurements

## 18.41 Soil Temperature

Soil-temperature sensors may be placed at standard depths such as:

- Surface
- 2 inches
- 4 inches
- 8 inches

The appropriate depth depends on the purpose.

Soil temperature affects:

- Seed germination
- Root growth
- Frost
- Insects
- Microbial activity

Record the depth and surface type.

A sensor beneath mulch cannot be compared directly with one beneath bare soil.

---

## 18.42 Soil Moisture

Soil-moisture sensors estimate water content using electrical properties.

They may be influenced by:

- Soil type
- Salinity
- Temperature
- Installation contact
- Sensor design

Readings are most useful after local calibration.

Instead of assuming the displayed percentage is universally exact, track how it changes between:

- Saturated soil
- Normal moisture
- Plant stress
- Dry soil

---

## 18.43 Leaf-Wetness Sensors

A leaf-wetness sensor imitates a leaf surface and detects moisture from:

- Dew
- Rain
- Fog
- Irrigation

These observations are useful in agriculture because prolonged leaf wetness can encourage plant disease.

Sensor angle and placement should resemble the crop environment.

---

# Lightning Sensors

## 18.44 Home Lightning Detection

Some consumer devices detect radio-frequency signals produced by lightning.

They may estimate:

- Flash occurrence
- Approximate distance
- Storm approach or departure

Limitations include:

- Electrical interference
- False detections
- Missed flashes
- Poor distance estimates
- Indoor shielding

They should supplement, not replace:

- Official alerts
- Radar
- Thunder
- Safe shelter rules

If thunder is audible, lightning is close enough to be dangerous regardless of what the gadget says.

---

## 18.45 Electric-Field Mills

Professional systems may use an **electric-field mill** to measure atmospheric electrical fields.

These can help monitor:

- Thunderstorm charge
- Lightning risk
- Rapid electric-field changes

Such equipment is more specialized than most home stations require.

A nearby storm already offers a free and effective electrical-field announcement called thunder.

---

# Visibility and Present Weather

## 18.46 Estimating Visibility

Visibility is the greatest distance at which an object can be identified against the background.

A home observer can create landmarks at known distances:

- Quarter mile
- Half mile
- One mile
- Five miles
- Ten miles

During fog, rain, snow, smoke, or haze, record the farthest visible landmark.

This is approximate but useful.

---

## 18.47 Visibility Is Directional

Visibility may differ by direction because of:

- Fog banks
- Rain shafts
- Smoke plumes
- Sun glare
- Terrain
- Urban haze

Record the lowest significant visibility when appropriate and note the direction.

---

## 18.48 Present-Weather Codes

A personal weather log may use simple codes:

| Code | Meaning |
|---|---|
| R | Rain |
| RW | Rain shower |
| SN | Snow |
| PL | Sleet or ice pellets |
| FZRA | Freezing rain |
| FG | Fog |
| TS | Thunderstorm |
| HZ | Haze |
| FU | Smoke |

You do not need formal aviation coding, but consistent shorthand makes records easier to review.

---

# Clouds and Sky Cover

## 18.49 Estimating Cloud Cover

Cloud cover is often described in eighths, called **oktas**.

| Oktas | Approximate cover |
|---:|---|
| 0 | Clear |
| 1–2 | Few |
| 3–4 | Scattered |
| 5–7 | Broken |
| 8 | Overcast |

Divide the sky mentally into sections and estimate how much is covered.

This becomes easier with practice.

---

## 18.50 Thin Clouds

Thin cirrus may cover the entire sky while sunlight remains visible.

Cloud amount and cloud opacity are different.

Record both when useful:

- Overcast thin cirrus
- Broken opaque cumulus
- Scattered low stratus

A sky can be 100% covered and still look bright.

---

## 18.51 Cloud-Base Estimation

For convective clouds, an approximate cloud-base height can be estimated from temperature and dew-point difference.

A rough rule is:

```text
cloud base in feet ≈
temperature–dew-point spread in °F × 220
```

Example:

```text
temperature: 82°F
dew point:   72°F
spread:      10°F

estimated base:
10 × 220 = 2,200 feet
```

This is only an approximation and works best for well-mixed daytime cumulus.

It is less useful for:

- Frontal clouds
- Elevated clouds
- Fog
- Complex moisture layers

---

# Choosing a Home Weather Station

## 18.52 Integrated Stations

An integrated station combines several sensors into one outdoor unit.

Advantages:

- Easier installation
- Wireless transmission
- Compact design
- Fewer cables

Disadvantages:

- Temperature and wind may require different ideal heights
- Rain gauge may be hard to clean
- One failed component may affect the whole unit
- Compromise placement

Integrated stations are convenient because every sensor agrees to be installed in the same imperfect location.

---

## 18.53 Separate Sensors

A modular station allows sensors to be placed independently.

For example:

- Temperature at 5 feet over grass
- Wind on a taller mast
- Rain gauge in an open lower area
- Pressure sensor indoors

Advantages:

- Better exposure
- Easier sensor replacement
- More flexible expansion

Disadvantages:

- More installation work
- Additional transmitters
- More batteries
- Greater cost

---

## 18.54 Wired Versus Wireless

### Wireless systems

Advantages:

- Easier installation
- No long cable runs
- Flexible placement

Disadvantages:

- Signal loss
- Battery replacement
- Radio interference
- Range limitations

### Wired systems

Advantages:

- Reliable data connection
- Continuous power options
- Less radio interference

Disadvantages:

- Cable routing
- Lightning pathways
- Installation difficulty
- Weatherproofing

---

## 18.55 Display Console Versus Internet Connection

Some stations use a dedicated console.

Others connect through:

- Wi-Fi
- Gateway device
- Phone application
- Cloud service
- Local computer

Consider:

- Can the station operate without internet?
- Is local data storage available?
- Can data be exported?
- Does the service require a subscription?
- What happens if the company closes?
- Can the system integrate with other software?

A weather station should ideally remain a weather station even when the cloud service experiences weather of its own.

---

## 18.56 Accuracy Specifications

Manufacturers may list accuracy such as:

```text
temperature: ±0.5°F
humidity: ±3%
pressure: ±1 hPa
```

These specifications may apply only within certain ranges.

Also consider:

- Resolution
- Sampling interval
- Update frequency
- Calibration ability
- Sensor replacement
- Long-term drift

A display showing temperature to 0.01°F does not mean the sensor is accurate to 0.01°F.

More digits are not more truth.

---

# Designing the Station Site

## 18.57 Survey the Property

Before installation, identify:

- Open grassy areas
- Trees
- Buildings
- Pavement
- Roof drainage
- Power lines
- Sprinklers
- Wi-Fi range
- Maintenance access
- Lightning exposure

Sketch a site map.

The best location often emerges from comparing several imperfect options.

---

## 18.58 Prioritize by Purpose

If your main goal is:

### Temperature and humidity

Prioritize shaded ventilation and distance from artificial heat.

### Rainfall

Prioritize open sky and level mounting.

### Wind

Prioritize height and distance from obstacles.

### Gardening

Prioritize soil and plant conditions near the garden.

### Local storm monitoring

Prioritize dependable power, frequent updates, and data logging.

No single mounting position is ideal for every variable.

---

## 18.59 A Practical Split Setup

A strong home setup might use:

```text
TEMPERATURE/HUMIDITY
5 feet above grass in a radiation shield

RAIN GAUGE
open level site several feet above ground

WIND
highest safe unobstructed mast

PRESSURE
indoors in stable conditions

SOIL SENSORS
fixed documented depths
```

This creates better observations than mounting everything beneath the porch eave because the porch was already available.

---

## 18.60 Mounting Masts

A mast should be:

- Strong
- Vertical
- Secure
- Corrosion-resistant
- Properly guyed when necessary
- Safely separated from power lines

Account for:

- Wind load
- Ice load
- Vibration
- Maintenance access
- Local building rules

Never install a mast where it could fall onto electrical lines.

Weather measurement is not improved by becoming an electrical incident.

---

## 18.61 Lightning Protection

Outdoor equipment can attract or conduct lightning.

Risk reduction may involve:

- Proper grounding
- Surge protection
- Safe cable routing
- Separation from structures
- Professional electrical guidance

No system guarantees protection from a direct strike.

Do not work on outdoor masts when thunderstorms are possible.

A lightning sensor does not need to be installed during lightning for thematic completeness.

---

# Installation

## 18.62 Level the Rain Gauge

Tipping-bucket and manual gauges must be level.

An unlevel tipping bucket may favor one side and mismeasure rain.

Use:

- Built-in bubble level
- Small carpenter’s level
- Stable mounting base

Check again after strong wind or soil movement.

---

## 18.63 Align Wind Direction

Use true north or the direction required by the station instructions.

Magnetic north and true north differ by location.

Some systems allow electronic correction.

Confirm orientation through:

- Compass adjusted for local magnetic declination
- Map
- Known building alignment
- Sun-shadow method

Then test the displayed direction physically.

---

## 18.64 Pair Sensors Before Climbing

Before mounting equipment high:

- Insert batteries
- Pair transmitters
- Confirm signal
- Test wind direction
- Tip the rain bucket manually
- Verify temperature and humidity
- Update firmware when appropriate

Testing on the ground is vastly easier than discovering an unpaired transmitter at the top of a ladder.

---

## 18.65 Record Installation Metadata

Write down:

- Installation date
- Sensor serial numbers
- Heights
- Coordinates or site description
- Distance from obstacles
- Calibration offsets
- Battery types
- Photographs

Update the record whenever something changes.

The metadata transforms a gadget history into a climate record.

---

# Calibration and Quality Control

## 18.66 Side-by-Side Testing

Before final installation, place temperature and humidity sensors together in a shaded ventilated location.

Compare readings over:

- Day
- Night
- Dry conditions
- Humid conditions

Small differences are normal.

Large persistent differences suggest calibration or sensor problems.

Do not compare one sensor in sunlight with another in shade and conclude that one has betrayed you.

---

## 18.67 Ice-Water Check

A temperature sensor can be tested near 32°F using a properly prepared ice-water mixture.

The mixture should contain:

- Crushed ice
- Enough clean water to fill gaps
- Continuous stirring
- Minimal contact with the container wall

The sensor should approach 32°F.

Do not submerge a sensor unless it is designed to be waterproof.

---

## 18.68 Boiling-Water Check

Boiling water is sometimes used to test thermometers, but boiling temperature changes with:

- Elevation
- Atmospheric pressure
- Water purity

It is also unsafe for sensors not designed for immersion.

For most home stations, comparison with a reliable reference is safer and more practical.

---

## 18.69 Humidity Checks

Humidity sensors can be checked in sealed environments using saturated salt solutions, which create known approximate humidity levels at stable temperatures.

This requires careful procedure.

For casual use, compare with:

- Trusted reference sensor
- Nearby official observations
- Repeated dew-point consistency

Avoid adjusting humidity based on a single comparison.

---

## 18.70 Rain-Gauge Calibration

A tipping bucket can be tested by slowly adding a known volume of water.

Because the funnel opening area is known, a given water volume corresponds to a rainfall depth.

Pour slowly enough to avoid overwhelming the tipping mechanism.

Compare expected and recorded tips.

Fast pouring tests the bucket’s panic response rather than ordinary calibration.

---

## 18.71 Wind-Sensor Checks

Simple checks include:

- Cups rotate freely
- Vane turns without sticking
- Bearings are quiet
- Direction aligns correctly
- No insect nests
- Mast remains vertical

Precise wind calibration requires specialized equipment, but mechanical inspection catches many failures.

---

## 18.72 Plausibility Checks

Ask whether observations fit together.

Examples:

### Suspicious

- 104°F at midnight during cool rain
- 100% humidity with temperature far above dew point
- Rainfall increasing under clear skies
- Wind direction fixed for weeks
- Pressure jumping 20 millibars in minutes

### Plausible

- Pressure drops steadily before a front
- Dew point rises with southerly wind
- Temperature falls during thunderstorm outflow
- Rain rate spikes beneath a heavy cell

The weather can behave strangely.

Sensors behave strangely more often.

---

# Maintenance

## 18.73 Monthly Inspection

Check:

- Rain-gauge funnel
- Moving wind parts
- Radiation shield
- Solar sensor
- Mounting hardware
- Batteries
- Cable condition
- Mast alignment

Clean gently according to manufacturer instructions.

Avoid chemicals that may damage sensor coatings.

---

## 18.74 Insects

Common station residents include:

- Spiders
- Wasps
- Ants
- Beetles

They may block:

- Rain funnels
- Drain holes
- Wind parts
- Radiation shields

Inspect carefully.

Do not spray pesticides directly onto sensors unless approved.

The objective is to evict the spider, not chemically recalibrate humidity.

---

## 18.75 Birds

Birds may:

- Perch on equipment
- Block solar sensors
- Contaminate rain gauges
- Damage wind vanes
- Build nests nearby

Bird deterrents should not obstruct airflow or precipitation.

Any modification may affect measurement, so document it.

---

## 18.76 Snow and Ice

Snow can cover:

- Solar sensors
- Rain-gauge funnels
- Radiation shields
- Wind equipment

Ice can freeze moving parts.

Do not climb during dangerous conditions to clear equipment.

Mark data as affected or missing.

An imperfect dataset is preferable to an observer sliding from the roof while holding a broom.

---

## 18.77 Battery Changes

Replace batteries before severe seasons when practical.

Record the date.

Cold weather reduces battery performance.

A failing battery may cause:

- Missing data
- Intermittent transmission
- Incorrect values
- Clock resets

Use recommended battery chemistry.

---

# Data Logging

## 18.78 Why Save the Data?

Long-term records reveal:

- Seasonal patterns
- Local extremes
- Microclimate
- Storm behavior
- Equipment drift
- Climate trends

A display showing current conditions is useful.

A database containing years of observations is a personal weather archive.

---

## 18.79 Observation Intervals

Possible logging intervals include:

- Every minute
- Every five minutes
- Every fifteen minutes
- Hourly
- Daily

Short intervals capture:

- Gusts
- Thunderstorm changes
- Front passages
- Rain rates

Longer intervals reduce storage and noise.

A useful system may store frequent raw observations and summarize them daily.

---

## 18.80 Daily Climate Summary

A daily record may include:

- Maximum temperature
- Minimum temperature
- Average temperature
- Maximum dew point
- Minimum pressure
- Maximum gust
- Total rainfall
- Dominant wind
- Weather notes

This is more manageable than reviewing thousands of individual readings.

---

## 18.81 Missing Data

Mark missing values clearly.

Do not replace them automatically with:

- Zero
- Previous reading
- Nearby station value

A missing rainfall reading does not mean no rain occurred.

Use a distinct missing-data code.

Document the cause when known.

---

## 18.82 Time Zones and Daylight Saving Time

Choose whether to store timestamps in:

- Local standard time
- Local civil time
- Coordinated Universal Time

Daylight-saving changes can create:

- Duplicate hours
- Missing hours
- Confusing daily totals

UTC avoids seasonal clock shifts.

Local time is easier for ordinary interpretation.

Whichever you choose, document it and remain consistent.

Time should not become the station’s least understood weather variable.

---

## 18.83 Data Export

Prefer systems that can export data in common formats such as:

- CSV
- JSON
- Text
- Database tables

Data trapped inside one application may become inaccessible later.

Regular backups protect against:

- Device failure
- Account loss
- Service closure
- Accidental deletion

---

# Sharing Weather Data

## 18.84 Online Networks

Many station owners share observations with:

- Public weather networks
- Citizen-science programs
- Agricultural systems
- Neighborhood applications

Shared data can improve local coverage.

But network users should understand that home-station quality varies.

A map may include:

- Excellent installations
- Rooftop heat sensors
- Rain gauges beneath trees
- Stations that have reported the same wind direction since 2019

Use many observations, not blind trust in one colored dot.

---

## 18.85 Citizen Science

Volunteer observers contribute valuable data on:

- Rain
- Snow
- Hail
- Storm damage
- Phenology
- Drought

Well-designed citizen-science programs provide:

- Training
- Standard equipment
- Observation protocols
- Quality control

Consistency transforms individual reports into a useful network.

---

## 18.86 Storm Reports

During severe weather, useful reports include:

- Hail measured with a ruler
- Tree damage
- Flood depth at a safe reference point
- Measured wind
- Tornado or funnel observation
- Snow amount

Include:

- Exact location
- Time
- Measurement method
- Direction of movement
- Photograph when safe

Do not exaggerate.

Emergency decisions may depend on the report.

“Very windy” is less useful than “large healthy branches down across the road.”

---

# Building Simple Instruments

## 18.87 Jar Rain Gauge

You need:

- Straight-sided clear container
- Ruler
- Stable open location

Mark a scale or place the ruler beside the container.

Limitations include:

- Evaporation
- Splash
- Irregular opening
- Wind
- Tipping

A straight-sided container provides a reasonable educational gauge.

A flowerpot shaped like a trumpet does not.

---

## 18.88 Simple Wind Vane

You need:

- Straw
- Pin
- Pencil with eraser
- Cardboard arrow and tail
- Compass
- Stable base

The broad tail catches the wind and turns the arrow into the wind.

Mark:

```text
N
E
S
W
```

Align the base using a compass.

This shows wind direction but not speed.

---

## 18.89 Cup Anemometer

You need:

- Four small cups
- Cross-shaped arms
- Vertical axle
- Stable base
- Marker on one cup

Count rotations during a fixed period.

Without calibration, the device shows relative wind speed rather than accurate miles per hour.

It can still reveal:

- Gusts
- Daily cycles
- Shelter effects
- Wind changes

---

## 18.90 Simple Barometer

You need:

- Jar
- Balloon membrane
- Rubber band
- Straw
- Card scale

Stretch the balloon material tightly over the jar opening.

Attach the straw as a pointer.

Pressure changes flex the membrane and move the pointer.

### Illustration 3: Jar barometer

```text
        straw pointer ─────→ scale
             /
     _______/
    / stretched membrane \
   |                      |
   |         jar          |
   |______________________|
```

This shows pressure trends rather than calibrated pressure.

---

## 18.91 Sling Psychrometer

A **sling psychrometer** uses two thermometers:

- Dry-bulb thermometer
- Wet-bulb thermometer with a damp wick

As air flows over the wet bulb, evaporation cools it.

The difference between dry- and wet-bulb temperatures indicates humidity.

### Small difference

Air is humid.

### Large difference

Air is dry.

This instrument demonstrates evaporative cooling directly.

Use only safe non-mercury thermometers.

---

## 18.92 Cloud Mirror

A dark reflective surface can make bright cloud detail easier to see without looking directly toward the Sun.

Historically, dark glass was sometimes used for cloud observation.

Modern safer options include:

- Reflections in dark water
- Properly designed viewing aids
- Photographs with reduced exposure

Never improvise solar filters.

Darkness alone does not make a material safe for looking at the Sun.

---

# A Home Station Observation Routine

## 18.93 Morning Observation

Record:

- Minimum temperature
- Current temperature
- Dew point
- Pressure
- Wind
- Cloud cover
- Fog
- Overnight rainfall

Note:

- Dew
- Frost
- Wet ground
- Visibility

---

## 18.94 Afternoon Observation

Record:

- Maximum temperature so far
- Humidity
- Wind and gusts
- Cloud growth
- Rainfall
- Solar conditions

Watch for:

- Sea breeze
- Cumulus development
- Heat peak
- Frontal shift

---

## 18.95 Evening Observation

Record:

- Daily maximum
- Rain total
- Maximum gust
- Pressure trend
- Cloud cover
- Sunset optics
- Expected overnight change

Review whether the day’s forecast verified.

A short written note often becomes more valuable than another decimal value.

---

# Creating a Station Log

## 18.96 Suggested Daily Table

| Date | High | Low | Rain | Peak gust | Pressure trend | Main weather |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---|---|
| 8/12 | 91°F | 72°F | 0.36 in | 28 mph | Falling, then rising | Afternoon thunderstorm |
| 8/13 | 84°F | 66°F | 0.00 in | 16 mph | Rising | Cooler and clear |

---

## 18.97 Event Notes

For significant weather, record a timeline.

Example:

```text
2:10 p.m. Towering cumulus west
2:32 p.m. Thunder first heard
2:41 p.m. Wind shifted southwest to northwest
2:43 p.m. Temperature fell from 88°F to 80°F
2:47 p.m. Heavy rain began
3:05 p.m. Rain ended
Storm total: 0.58 inch
Peak gust: 31 mph
```

This captures the storm as a process.

---

## 18.98 Monthly Summary

At month’s end, calculate:

- Average high
- Average low
- Highest temperature
- Lowest temperature
- Total rainfall
- Wettest day
- Number of rainy days
- Strongest gust
- Average pressure
- Notable events

Compare with local climate normals if available.

---

# Diagnosing Strange Readings

## 18.99 Temperature Too High

Check for:

- Sun exposure
- Failed fan
- Dirty shield
- Nearby hot surface
- Building reflection
- Sensor drift

Compare day and night.

Radiation errors are often largest on sunny calm afternoons.

---

## 18.100 Temperature Too Low

Check for:

- Evaporative wetting
- Sensor in deep shade
- Cold-air drainage
- Irrigation
- Radiative cooling
- Calibration error

The reading may also reveal a real local frost pocket.

Do not correct a genuine microclimate merely because the airport is warmer.

---

## 18.101 No Rain Recorded

Check:

- Blocked funnel
- Insect web
- Loose cable
- Unlevel gauge
- Frozen bucket
- Dead transmitter
- Manual gauge comparison

---

## 18.102 Excessive Rain Recorded

Check:

- Sprinklers
- Roof runoff
- Condensation
- Vibrating bucket
- Bird activity
- False electronic tips
- Incorrect calibration factor

A daily 0.01-inch reading at the exact time the lawn irrigation starts is not a mysterious nocturnal shower pattern.

---

## 18.103 Wind Stuck at Zero

Check:

- Frozen cups
- Bearing failure
- Broken cable
- Dead battery
- Obstruction
- Lost wireless signal

---

## 18.104 Wind Direction Stuck

Check:

- Vane trapped
- Nest or debris
- Mast rotation
- Sensor failure
- Extremely steady real wind

The final possibility exists, though it becomes less convincing after several weeks.

---

## 18.105 Pressure Jump

Check:

- Console reset
- Calibration change
- Temperature shock
- Firmware issue
- Elevation setting
- Sensor failure

Real pressure usually changes smoothly, except for small rapid disturbances such as thunderstorm pressure jumps.

A sudden permanent shift strongly suggests equipment or settings.

---

# Field Exercise  
## Survey a Weather-Station Site

Sketch your property.

Mark:

- North
- Buildings
- Trees
- Pavement
- Grass
- Roof edges
- Power lines
- Sprinklers
- Possible mounting points

Choose separate ideal locations for:

1. Temperature and humidity
2. Wind
3. Rain
4. Pressure
5. Soil measurements

Then choose the best practical compromise.

Write down what each location will measure well—and what bias may remain.

---

# Try It  
## Compare Microclimates

Place portable thermometers temporarily at:

- Open grass
- Beneath a tree
- Near pavement
- Beside the house
- Low point in the yard

Measure at:

- Sunrise
- Midafternoon
- Evening

Compare the differences.

This demonstrates why “the temperature in my yard” is not always one single value.

---

# Try It  
## Track a Front

Before a forecast cold front arrives, record every 15–30 minutes:

- Temperature
- Dew point
- Pressure
- Wind direction
- Wind speed
- Rainfall

Look for:

```text
wind shift
temperature drop
dew-point drop
pressure rise
rain ending
```

Graph the results.

The front becomes visible as a coordinated change across several measurements.

---

# Try It  
## Measure a Thunderstorm Outflow

From safe indoor shelter, record:

- Temperature before gust front
- Wind direction before and after
- Maximum gust
- Pressure change
- Time rain begins
- Rain total

Do not remain outdoors to improve the dataset.

The gust front has not agreed to laboratory conditions.

---

# Field Observation  
## Build a One-Year Climate Record

For one year, maintain daily records of:

- High
- Low
- Rain
- Snow
- Main weather
- Extreme gust
- Notable event

At year’s end, summarize:

- Hottest day
- Coldest day
- Wettest month
- Driest month
- Longest dry period
- Largest storm
- First frost
- Last frost
- Thunderstorm days
- Snow days

A full year reveals the seasonal rhythm of one place.

Several years begin to reveal its personality.

---

# Weather Safety  
## Instruments Are Replaceable

Do not maintain, install, or inspect outdoor equipment during:

- Thunderstorms
- High wind
- Ice
- Flooding
- Extreme heat
- Wildfire smoke
- Dangerous cold

Avoid:

- Ladders in wind
- Roofs during precipitation
- Masts near power lines
- Flooded gauges
- Ice-coated trees
- Lightning-exposed fields

Allow the instrument to miss the observation.

A gap in the graph is not an emergency.

---

# Chapter Summary

A home weather station converts casual impressions into repeatable measurements.

The core observations are temperature, humidity, pressure, wind, and precipitation.

Some displayed values, such as dew point, heat index, and wind chill, are calculated from direct measurements.

Temperature sensors require shade, ventilation, and distance from artificial heat.

Radiation shields prevent sunlight and reflected energy from warming the sensor.

Humidity sensors can drift and should be checked for persistent unrealistic readings.

Pressure sensors may display station pressure or sea-level-adjusted pressure.

Pressure trend is often more useful than the exact value.

Wind measurements are strongly affected by sensor height, buildings, trees, roofs, and terrain.

A home wind observation may be locally useful even when it does not meet professional exposure standards.

Rain gauges must be level, open, clean, and protected from roof runoff and sprinklers.

Tipping-bucket gauges provide automatic rain rates but may undercount intense rainfall or fail when obstructed.

Manual gauges provide a valuable comparison.

Snowfall, snow depth, snow-water equivalent, and ice accretion are separate measurements.

Solar, UV, soil, leaf-wetness, and lightning sensors can extend a station for specialized purposes.

Integrated stations are convenient but require compromise placement.

Modular stations allow better exposure for individual sensors.

Installation metadata should include sensor heights, locations, equipment, photographs, and calibration changes.

Quality control requires comparison, plausibility checks, maintenance, and careful handling of missing data.

A good observing log includes both measurements and written weather notes.

Simple instruments such as jar gauges, wind vanes, barometers, and sling psychrometers can demonstrate the physical principles behind modern sensors.

The best home station is not necessarily the most expensive.

It is the one whose location, limitations, and history are understood.

A weather reading becomes valuable when you know what measured it, where it stood, and why you trust it.

---

## Weather-Watcher’s Vocabulary

**Weather station:** A site containing instruments used to observe atmospheric conditions.  
**Radiation shield:** Ventilated enclosure protecting a temperature sensor from direct and reflected radiation.  
**Aspirated shield:** Radiation shield using a fan to move air past the sensor.  
**Thermistor:** Electronic component whose resistance changes with temperature.  
**Hygrometer:** Instrument measuring humidity.  
**Dew point:** Temperature to which air must cool to become saturated.  
**Barometer:** Instrument measuring atmospheric pressure.  
**Station pressure:** Actual pressure measured at station elevation.  
**Sea-level pressure:** Pressure adjusted for comparison at sea level.  
**Anemometer:** Instrument measuring wind speed.  
**Wind vane:** Instrument measuring wind direction.  
**Tipping-bucket gauge:** Automatic rain gauge using alternating small collection buckets.  
**Rain rate:** Speed at which rainfall is occurring.  
**Meniscus:** Curved liquid surface used when reading a gauge.  
**Snow board:** Flat surface used to measure new snowfall.  
**Snow water equivalent:** Liquid-water content of a snowpack.  
**Pyranometer:** Instrument measuring incoming solar radiation.  
**Leaf-wetness sensor:** Sensor estimating moisture present on plant-like surfaces.  
**Sling psychrometer:** Humidity instrument using wet- and dry-bulb thermometers.  
**Cloud cover:** Fraction of the sky covered by clouds.  
**Okta:** One-eighth of the sky used in cloud-cover estimates.  
**Metadata:** Information describing an instrument, site, method, and observation history.  
**Calibration:** Comparison and adjustment of an instrument against a reference.  
**Sensor drift:** Gradual change in sensor accuracy over time.  
**Sampling interval:** Time between individual measurements.  
**Logging interval:** Time between stored observations.  
**Microclimate:** Local atmospheric conditions differing from the surrounding area.  
**Plausibility check:** Test of whether measurements are physically and meteorologically reasonable.

**Next: Chapter 19 — The Field Guide: Identifying Clouds, Fronts, Storms, and Sky Signs at a Glance**

<!-- End of source chapter -->

---

# Chapter 19  
## The Field Guide: Identifying Clouds, Fronts, Storms, and Sky Signs at a Glance

A field guide should help you answer one practical question:

> What am I looking at?

The previous chapters explained the machinery of weather. This chapter turns that knowledge into a recognition system.

You will not always have:

- A radar loop
- A weather map
- A sounding
- A forecast discussion
- A conveniently labeled diagram

You may have only:

- The sky
- The wind
- The temperature
- A distant cloud
- A sense that something has changed

The goal is not to diagnose every atmospheric detail from one glance. It is to identify the most likely feature, understand what it suggests, and know when it deserves closer attention.

---

# How to Use This Field Guide

## 19.1 Begin With Shape

Ask:

- Is the cloud flat or vertically growing?
- Is it layered or cellular?
- Is it smooth, fibrous, lumpy, or turbulent?
- Does it cover the whole sky?
- Does it have a dark base?
- Is it producing precipitation?
- Does it appear attached to a storm?

Shape usually narrows the possibilities quickly.

---

## 19.2 Then Check Height

A rough cloud-height guide:

| Cloud level | Typical appearance |
|---|---|
| High | Thin, fibrous, icy, often delicate |
| Middle | Layered or patchy, larger elements |
| Low | Broad sheets, rolls, or large lumpy masses |
| Vertical | Towers extending through several levels |

Cloud height is judged by:

- Apparent size
- Movement
- Shadows
- Relation to terrain
- Precipitation
- Familiarity

Small-looking cloud elements are often high.

Large, low-looking elements are usually closer.

This is not foolproof. A massive thunderstorm thirty miles away can look like a polite little cloud sitting on the horizon.

---

## 19.3 Check the Trend

A single observation tells you what exists.

A sequence tells you what is happening.

Ask:

- Are clouds increasing or decreasing?
- Are bases lowering?
- Are towers growing?
- Is the wind shifting?
- Is visibility worsening?
- Is precipitation approaching?
- Are cloud elements moving in different directions?

Trend often matters more than exact classification.

---

## 19.4 Use the Whole Sky
