Do not focus only on the most dramatic cloud.

Look:

- Upwind
- Near the horizon
- Overhead
- Opposite the Sun
- Behind the main storm
- Along boundaries

The clue explaining the weather may be far from the cloud that first caught your attention.

---

# High Clouds

## 19.5 Cirrus

### Appearance

- Thin
- Fibrous
- Wispy
- Featherlike
- Hooked or streaked
- Often bright white

### Meaning

Cirrus consists mainly of ice crystals.

It may indicate:

- Fair weather
- Strong upper-level wind
- Moisture increasing aloft
- An approaching frontal system
- Outflow from distant thunderstorms

### Quick identification

```text
thin + fibrous + high
=
cirrus
```

### Watch for change

Scattered cirrus alone may mean little.

Cirrus that steadily increases, thickens, and spreads across the sky may signal an approaching storm system.

---

## 19.6 Cirrus Uncinus

**Cirrus uncinus** has hooked or comma-shaped ends.

It is sometimes called mares’ tails.

### Appearance

```text
     __
   _/  \__
 _/
```

The curved head contains thicker ice cloud.

The trailing streak shows crystals falling into different winds below.

### Meaning

May indicate:

- Strong wind shear aloft
- Moisture spreading ahead of a system
- Changing upper-level conditions

---

## 19.7 Cirrostratus

### Appearance

- Thin milky veil
- Covers much or all of the sky
- Sun or Moon remains visible
- Often produces a halo
- Shadows may remain faintly visible

### Meaning

Cirrostratus often appears ahead of:

- Warm fronts
- Large midlatitude cyclones
- Widespread precipitation

### Quick test

If you can see the Sun through a smooth whitish veil and a halo is present, cirrostratus is likely.

### Typical progression

```text
cirrus
  ↓
cirrostratus
  ↓
altostratus
  ↓
nimbostratus
  ↓
steady precipitation
```

---

## 19.8 Cirrocumulus

### Appearance

- Tiny ripples
- Small white grains
- Fish-scale pattern
- Little or no shadowing
- Often called a mackerel sky

### Meaning

May indicate:

- Moisture aloft
- Wave motion
- Instability at high levels
- A changing weather pattern

### Distinguishing feature

Cirrocumulus elements are very small.

If the individual cloud pieces appear smaller than the width of your little finger held at arm’s length, the cloud may be cirrocumulus.

This is a rough field estimate, not an atmospheric fingerprint scan.

---

# Middle Clouds

## 19.9 Altostratus

### Appearance

- Gray or blue-gray sheet
- Covers much of the sky
- Sun appears dim, like through frosted glass
- Few distinct cloud elements
- May thicken gradually

### Meaning

Altostratus often indicates:

- Broad rising motion
- Approaching widespread precipitation
- Moisture deepening through the middle atmosphere

### Distinguishing feature

The Sun may be visible, but no distinct halo usually appears.

Shadows are weak or absent.

---

## 19.10 Altocumulus

### Appearance

- Rounded patches
- Rolls
- Bands
- Larger than cirrocumulus elements
- May show shaded sides
- Often arranged in rows or waves

### Meaning

Altocumulus may indicate:

- Midlevel moisture
- Atmospheric waves
- Instability aloft
- Approaching weather change

It can occur during fair weather as well.

---

## 19.11 Altocumulus Castellanus

**Altocumulus castellanus** has small turrets rising from a shared cloud base.

### Appearance

```text
  /\   /\    /\
_/  \_/  \__/  \_
```

The tops resemble castle towers.

### Meaning

It indicates instability in the middle atmosphere.

When seen in the morning during warm, humid weather, it may suggest an increased chance of thunderstorms later.

### Verdict

A useful storm-development clue, especially when:

- Towers are growing
- Humidity is high
- Winds are strong aloft
- A front or disturbance is approaching

---

## 19.12 Altocumulus Lenticularis

### Appearance

- Smooth
- Lens-shaped
- Saucerlike
- Often stacked
- Nearly stationary
- Common near mountains

### Meaning

Lenticular clouds indicate atmospheric waves and strong airflow across terrain.

Possible associated conditions:

- Mountain-wave turbulence
- Strong wind aloft
- Rotor circulation
- Downslope wind

They are sometimes mistaken for unusual aircraft because apparently clouds are not allowed to be too symmetrical without causing rumors.

---

# Low Clouds

## 19.13 Stratus

### Appearance

- Uniform gray layer
- Very low cloud base
- May cover hills or buildings
- Resembles fog lifted above the ground
- May produce drizzle or mist

### Meaning

Stratus often accompanies:

- Stable moist air
- Weak lift
- Coastal onshore flow
- Cold-air damming
- Morning fog lifting

### Fog versus stratus

Fog touches the ground.

Stratus does not.

They can transform into each other as the cloud base rises or lowers.

---

## 19.14 Stratocumulus

### Appearance

- Large low rolls or patches
- Gray and white elements
- Broad gaps may exist
- Rounded but not strongly vertical
- Often covers much of the sky

### Meaning

Stratocumulus commonly appears:

- Behind cold fronts
- Beneath stable layers
- Over cool oceans
- During shallow convection

It usually produces little precipitation, though light showers are possible.

### Distinguishing feature

The cloud elements are larger and lower than altocumulus.

They often look heavy enough to have opinions.

---

## 19.15 Nimbostratus

### Appearance

- Thick dark gray layer
- Covers the sky
- Produces widespread steady rain or snow
- Sun completely hidden
- Cloud base often ragged

### Meaning

Nimbostratus is the classic cloud of prolonged stratiform precipitation.

Associated weather may include:

- Steady rain
- Snow
- Sleet
- Freezing rain
- Poor visibility

### Distinguishing feature

Little vertical structure is visible from below.

If the sky is one broad dark layer producing continuous precipitation, nimbostratus is likely.

---

## 19.16 Scud

**Scud** consists of small ragged cloud fragments beneath a larger cloud deck or storm.

### Appearance

- Torn
- Fast-moving
- Irregular
- Low
- Often dark against a brighter background

### Meaning

Scud forms in humid air near:

- Rain
- Outflow
- Storm inflow
- Low cloud bases

It may appear to rise or rotate because of turbulent wind.

### Safety note

Scud is frequently mistaken for:

- Funnel clouds
- Wall clouds
- Tornadoes

Look for organized, persistent rotation before identifying a severe feature.

Raggedness alone is not rotation.

---

# Clouds With Vertical Growth

## 19.17 Cumulus Humilis

### Appearance

- Small puffy clouds
- Flat bases
- Limited vertical growth
- Bright tops
- Fairly widely spaced

### Meaning

Often called fair-weather cumulus.

They indicate shallow convection caused by surface heating.

### Typical behavior

- Form late morning
- Grow slightly in afternoon
- Dissipate toward sunset

They usually produce no precipitation.

---

## 19.18 Cumulus Mediocris

### Appearance

- Taller than fair-weather cumulus
- Noticeable cauliflower growth
- Vertical depth similar to width
- Darker bases

### Meaning

The atmosphere is becoming more unstable.

Showers may develop if growth continues.

This is a transitional form between harmless small cumulus and stronger towers.

---

## 19.19 Towering Cumulus

### Appearance

- Tall cauliflower towers
- Crisp bright edges
- Dark flat base
- Rapid upward growth
- No full anvil yet

### Meaning

Towering cumulus is a developing thunderstorm cloud.

It may produce:

- Heavy showers
- Lightning soon
- Strong gusts
- Small hail

### Field clue

Watch the top for five minutes.

If it grows rapidly and remains sharp, the updraft is strong.

If it frays and collapses, dry air or weak instability may be limiting development.

---

## 19.20 Cumulonimbus

### Appearance

- Very tall storm cloud
- Dark base
- Heavy precipitation
- Anvil top
- Lightning
- Turbulent edges
- Sometimes overshooting top

### Meaning

Cumulonimbus produces thunderstorms.

Possible hazards include:

- Lightning
- Heavy rain
- Hail
- Damaging wind
- Tornadoes
- Flash flooding

### Illustration 1: Mature thunderstorm

```text
                   anvil
             _________→
            /          
      overshooting top
             ↑
          strong updraft
             ↑
        dark rain-free base

 rain and hail ↓      ↓ downdraft
```

---

# Thunderstorm Features

## 19.21 Anvil

The anvil is the flat spreading cloud at the top of a mature thunderstorm.

### Meaning

Rising air has reached a stable layer near the tropopause and spreads horizontally.

### Wind clue

The anvil points generally downwind at upper levels.

Lightning may occur far beneath or even beyond the anvil.

Do not stand outside under a seemingly rain-free anvil because the storm core appears distant.

---

## 19.22 Overshooting Top

### Appearance

- Dome or bubble above the anvil
- Briefly rises, collapses, and reforms
- Bright in visible imagery

### Meaning

The updraft is strong enough to penetrate temporarily above the stable tropopause.

Persistent overshooting tops may indicate:

- Powerful updraft
- Large hail potential
- Severe storm
- Strong convection

It is an important warning sign but does not identify the exact hazard by itself.

---

## 19.23 Rain Shaft

### Appearance

- Dark or gray curtain extending from cloud to ground
- May have sharply defined edges
- May hide terrain

### Meaning

The cloud is producing precipitation reaching the ground.

A rain shaft may contain:

- Heavy rain
- Hail
- Strong downdrafts
- Reduced visibility

If the shaft appears white or opaque beneath a severe storm, hail may be present.

---

## 19.24 Virga

### Appearance

- Precipitation streaks fall from cloud
- Streaks fade before reaching the ground
- Often curved by wind

### Meaning

Rain or snow is evaporating or sublimating into dry air.

Possible effects:

- Cooling
- Gusty downdrafts
- Dry microbursts
- Little surface rainfall

### Illustration 2: Virga

```text
cloud base
────────────

rain streaks
  ↓ ↓ ↓
   ↓ ↓
    ↓
   fade
before ground
```

---

## 19.25 Shelf Cloud

### Appearance

- Long horizontal wedge
- Attached to the storm’s leading edge
- Low and turbulent
- Often spans much of the horizon
- May have rising cloud ahead and rain behind

### Meaning

A shelf cloud marks a thunderstorm gust front.

Expect:

- Sudden strong wind
- Temperature drop
- Heavy rain
- Blowing dust
- Possible damaging gusts

### Movement

Shelf clouds usually move with the storm line.

They do not normally show persistent rotation around a vertical axis.

### Safety

Move indoors immediately.

The cloud may look spectacular because it is attached to weather attempting to arrive all at once.

---

## 19.26 Roll Cloud

### Appearance

- Long horizontal tube
- Detached from the storm base
- May appear to roll
- Often smooth or layered

### Meaning

A roll cloud may form along an outflow boundary.

It can mark:

- Wind shift
- Gust front
- Cooler air
- Turbulence

### Shelf versus roll cloud

| Feature | Shelf cloud | Roll cloud |
|---|---|---|
| Attached to storm | Usually yes | Usually no |
| Position | Leading edge | Detached ahead |
| Main clue | Wedge shape | Tube shape |

---

## 19.27 Wall Cloud

### Appearance

- Localized lowering beneath a thunderstorm
- Often beneath the rain-free base
- Broader than a funnel
- May have inflow tail
- May rotate

### Meaning

A wall cloud forms where moist inflow rises into the storm’s updraft.

A rotating wall cloud can precede tornado formation.

### What to watch

- Persistent organized rotation
- Rapid vertical motion
- Funnel development
- Strong inflow
- Nearby clear slot
- Tornado warning

### Safety

Do not remain outside trying to decide whether rotation is “good enough.”

Use official warnings and move to shelter.

---

## 19.28 Inflow Tail

### Appearance

- Low cloud band extending toward a wall cloud
- Often points from the storm’s rain-cooled region toward the updraft

### Meaning

It shows humid air being drawn into the storm.

It may help identify the inflow side of a supercell.

Not every tail cloud indicates a tornado.

---

## 19.29 Funnel Cloud

### Appearance

- Narrow rotating condensation column
- Extends downward from a cloud base
- Does not visibly reach the ground

### Meaning

A funnel cloud indicates concentrated rotation.

A tornado exists when the circulation reaches the ground, whether or not condensation connects fully from cloud to surface.

### Look below

Possible evidence of a tornado:

- Debris
- Dust circulation
- Tree movement
- Power flashes
- Ground-level spray

Do not wait for a perfect white tube.

The dangerous circulation may be nearly invisible.

---

## 19.30 Tornado

### Appearance

May appear as:

- Rope
- Cone
- Stovepipe
- Wedge
- Dark column
- Rain-wrapped circulation
- Debris cloud with little visible funnel

### Meaning

A violently rotating column of air is in contact with the ground.

### Immediate action

Move to:

- Basement
- Interior small room
- Lowest safe floor
- Away from windows

Protect the head and neck.

Visual identification is not required when a warning is issued.

---

## 19.31 Mammatus

### Appearance

- Rounded pouches hanging beneath an anvil
- Smooth or ragged
- Often dramatic after severe storms

### Meaning

Mammatus forms through sinking and mixing beneath cloud layers.

It indicates strong atmospheric structure nearby but does not prove:

- Tornado
- Hail at your location
- Severe weather is about to begin

It is often seen after the strongest storm has passed.

### Verdict

Interesting, meaningful, and widely accused of crimes it did not commit.

---

## 19.32 Pileus

### Appearance

- Smooth cap cloud above a rapidly growing tower
- Thin and short-lived
- Resembles a scarf or hat

### Meaning

A strong updraft is lifting a moist stable layer above it.

Pileus may indicate rapid convective growth.

The growing cumulus often rises through and absorbs it.

---

# Storm Types at a Glance

## 19.33 Single-Cell Storm

### Appearance

- One main tower
- Short life
- Local rain shaft
- Weak or brief anvil

### Common hazards

- Lightning
- Heavy rain
- Local downburst
- Small hail

### Typical environment

- Strong heating
- Modest instability
- Weak wind shear

---

## 19.34 Multicell Cluster

### Appearance

- Several towers at different stages
- New cells forming near older ones
- Broad irregular cloud mass
- Several rain cores

### Common hazards

- Heavy rain
- Hail
- Strong wind
- Repeated lightning

One cell may weaken while another strengthens.

The cluster survives longer than any individual member.

---

## 19.35 Squall Line

### Appearance

- Long line of thunderstorms
- Shelf cloud along leading edge
- Continuous or broken rain curtain
- Strong wind shift

### Common hazards

- Widespread damaging wind
- Lightning
- Heavy rain
- Embedded tornadoes
- Rapid arrival

### Field clue

If the horizon becomes a continuous dark wall with a low wedge-shaped leading cloud, a storm line may be approaching.

---

## 19.36 Bow Echo

### Appearance on radar

- Curved outward bulge in a storm line
- Bow-shaped reflectivity pattern

### Visual clues

- Fast-moving shelf cloud
- Strong straight-line wind
- Widespread turbulent outflow

### Common hazard

Damaging wind concentrated near the bow’s apex.

A bow echo is mainly a radar identification, though its ground-level entrance tends to be memorable.

---

## 19.37 Supercell

### Appearance

Possible visual features include:

- Isolated large storm
- Broad rotating updraft
- Wall cloud
- Overshooting top
- Large anvil
- Striated or stacked appearance
- Separate precipitation core

### Common hazards

- Large hail
- Damaging wind
- Tornadoes
- Flash flooding
- Frequent lightning

### Classic structure

```text
inflow → rain-free base → rotating updraft
                     ↓
                 wall cloud

hail and heavy rain farther downwind
```

Visual appearance varies greatly.

Many supercells are partly or fully obscured by rain.

---

## 19.38 High-Precipitation Supercell

### Appearance

- Large rain shield
- Rotation hidden in precipitation
- Dark storm base
- Hail and rain wrapping around center

### Hazard

Tornadoes may be rain-wrapped and difficult to see.

Visual spotting is especially dangerous.

Radar and warnings are critical.

---

## 19.39 Low-Precipitation Supercell

### Appearance

- Sculpted updraft
- Limited rain
- Striated cloud structure
- Hail core may be small but intense
- Clear view of base

### Hazard

Can produce:

- Large hail
- Tornadoes
- Strong wind

The clear appearance can tempt observers to approach too closely.

The storm is not safer because it photographs well.

---

# Fronts in the Field

## 19.40 Approaching Warm Front

### Possible signs

- Cirrus increasing
- Halo around Sun or Moon
- Cirrostratus thickening
- Altostratus lowering
- Steady precipitation developing
- Wind becoming easterly or southeasterly
- Pressure falling slowly

### Typical sequence

```text
high thin cloud
      ↓
thick middle cloud
      ↓
low overcast
      ↓
steady rain or snow
      ↓
warmer air
```

The exact sequence depends on storm track and season.

---

## 19.41 Cold-Front Passage

### Before

- Warm or humid air
- Southerly wind
- Falling pressure
- Growing cumulus
- Showers or storms

### During

- Wind shift
- Gusts
- Rain or thunder
- Pressure bottoms out
- Temperature may fall rapidly

### After

- Rising pressure
- Cooler drier air
- West or northwest wind
- Clearing or broken stratocumulus

### Illustration 3: Field signs of a cold front

```text
BEFORE       PASSAGE        AFTER

warm         gusty rain     cooler
humid   →    wind shift →   drier
S wind       pressure low   NW wind
```

---

## 19.42 Stationary Front

### Possible signs

- Clouds persist for days
- Wind differs across the region
- Temperature changes little
- Repeated showers follow the same corridor
- Fog or low cloud lingers

A stationary front may drift back and forth.

It can produce prolonged rainfall and flooding.

The word stationary refers to the boundary’s general movement, not the precipitation’s willingness to stop.

---

## 19.43 Occluded Front

An occluded front is difficult to identify visually because it is part of a mature cyclone.

Possible signs include:

- Broad cloud shield
- Mixed precipitation
- Wind shifts
- Wraparound rain or snow
- Low pressure nearby

Surface maps are usually more useful than sky appearance for identifying occlusion.

---

## 19.44 Dryline

### Possible field signs

- Sharp humidity drop
- Wind shift
- Temperature may rise
- Dust or haze boundary
- Cumulus line forms along the boundary
- Thunderstorms develop on the humid side

Drylines are especially important in the Plains.

One side may feel muggy.

The other may feel dramatically drier within a short distance.

---

## 19.45 Sea-Breeze Front

### Possible signs

- Wind shifts onshore
- Temperature falls near the coast
- Humidity rises
- Cumulus line develops inland
- Haze or cloud boundary moves inland

Sea-breeze fronts may trigger thunderstorms when they collide with:

- Outflow boundaries
- Other sea breezes
- Terrain
- Larger fronts

---

## 19.46 Outflow Boundary

### Signs

- Sudden cool gust
- Wind shift
- Dust rising
- Arc of low cloud
- Pressure jump
- Temperature drop
- New thunderstorms forming along it

An outflow boundary may travel far from its parent storm.

It can arrive under blue sky before clouds build along it.

---

# Precipitation Identification

## 19.47 Rain

### Appearance

- Liquid drops
- Darkened surfaces
- Splash
- Smooth streaks

### Light rain

Small drops, limited splash.

### Heavy rain

Large drops or dense sheets, rapid runoff, poor visibility.

---

## 19.48 Drizzle

### Appearance

- Very small droplets
- Slow settling
- Often from low stratus
- Minimal splash

Drizzle can reduce visibility and create a steady dampness without large rainfall totals.

Freezing drizzle is much more dangerous because it forms glaze ice.

---

## 19.49 Snow

### Appearance

- Ice crystals or aggregates
- Soft flakes
- Slow fall
- May swirl or drift

Large flakes usually indicate:

- Aggregation
- Temperature near freezing
- Moist cloud conditions

Tiny dry flakes often occur in colder air.

---

## 19.50 Sleet

### Appearance

- Hard translucent pellets
- Bounces from surfaces
- Taps against windows
- Accumulates like coarse grains

Sleet indicates a warm layer aloft and a sufficiently deep cold layer below.

---

## 19.51 Freezing Rain

### Appearance

- Looks like ordinary rain
- Freezes on contact
- Produces smooth glaze
- Coats branches, wires, and vehicles

The danger may be invisible at first.

A wet-looking surface can be solid ice.

---

## 19.52 Graupel

### Appearance

- White opaque pellets
- Soft
- Crushes easily
- Bounces lightly

Graupel forms through riming in clouds.

It is not sleet and not true hail.

---

## 19.53 Hail

### Appearance

- Hard ice stones
- Usually associated with thunderstorms
- Often irregular or layered
- May range from tiny pellets to very large stones

Seek shelter immediately.

Do not run outside with a ruler during the storm.

Measure hail afterward, if safe, before it melts.

---

# Visibility Hazards

## 19.54 Fog

### Appearance

- Cloud at ground level
- Visibility reduced
- Moist air
- Diffuse light

### Common types by setting

| Setting | Likely type |
|---|---|
| Clear calm night | Radiation fog |
| Moist air over cold water | Advection fog |
| Hill or mountain slope | Upslope fog |
| Rain falling into cold air | Precipitation fog |
| Cold air over warmer water | Steam fog |

---

## 19.55 Mist

Mist is similar to fog but generally produces less severe visibility reduction.

The distinction is based more on visibility than appearance.

Both consist of suspended water droplets.

---

## 19.56 Haze

### Appearance

- Milky or washed-out sky
- Distant objects lose contrast
- Horizon appears pale
- Air may remain dry

Haze consists of very small particles.

Possible sources include:

- Pollution
- Dust
- Smoke
- Salt

---

## 19.57 Smoke

### Appearance

- Blue-gray, brown, or white veil
- Reduced visibility
- Distinct odor possible
- Sun may appear red or orange

Smoke may be:

- Near the ground
- Elevated aloft
- Trapped beneath an inversion

Check air-quality information.

A beautiful red Sun is not proof the smoke is safely above you.

---

## 19.58 Blowing Dust

### Appearance

- Brown or tan cloud
- Reduced visibility
- Sharp advancing wall possible
- Gusty wind
- Dry conditions

A dense dust wall is sometimes called a **haboob**.

It often forms from thunderstorm outflow in dry regions.

### Safety

Avoid driving into dense dust.

Visibility may fall nearly to zero.

---

## 19.59 Blowing Snow

### Appearance

- Snow lifted from the ground
- Horizontal streaks
- Drifts forming
- Visibility reduced

Blowing snow may occur:

- During snowfall
- After snowfall ends
- During a ground blizzard

---

# Wind Clues From the Landscape

## 19.60 Leaves and Small Branches

### Light breeze

Leaves move.

### Moderate breeze

Small branches sway.

### Strong breeze

Large branches move.

### Gale

Whole trees move, walking becomes difficult.

The Beaufort scale formalizes these visual clues.

---

## 19.61 Flags

Flags provide a rough wind estimate.

### Hanging

Very light wind.

### Partly extended

Moderate breeze.

### Fully extended and snapping

Strong wind.

Flag size, fabric, and mounting affect behavior, so this is approximate.

---

## 19.62 Water Surfaces

### Calm

Mirrorlike surface.

### Light breeze

Small ripples.

### Moderate wind

Frequent wavelets and whitecaps.

### Strong wind

Larger waves, spray, organized whitecaps.

Water offers a wide visible wind sensor with no digital display and a worrying lack of warranty.

---

## 19.63 Smoke

### Rises vertically

Very light wind.

### Drifts gently

Light wind.

### Flattens rapidly

Stronger wind or stable layering.

Smoke also shows turbulence and wind-direction changes.

---

# Sky Color and Light

## 19.64 Deep Blue Sky

May indicate:

- Clean dry air
- Low aerosol concentration
- Good visibility

It does not guarantee fair weather.

Cold dry air can exist beneath rapidly approaching cirrus.

---

## 19.65 Milky White Sky

May indicate:

- Haze
- High humidity
- Thin cloud
- Smoke
- Dust

Check:

- Sun appearance
- Visibility
- Odor
- Satellite imagery
- Air quality

---

## 19.66 Red or Orange Sun

May result from:

- Sunrise or sunset
- Smoke
- Dust
- Haze
- Volcanic aerosols

A deeply colored daytime Sun often suggests substantial particles in the light path.

---

## 19.67 Halo

### Identification

- Large ring around Sun or Moon
- Approximately one hand span in radius
- Often reddish inner edge
- High thin cloud present

### Meaning

Ice crystals aloft.

Possible approaching frontal system.

---

## 19.68 Corona

### Identification

- Small colored rings close to Sun or Moon
- Much smaller than a halo
- Often seen through thin water cloud

### Meaning

Small similarly sized droplets or ice particles.

---

## 19.69 Sundogs

### Identification

- Bright spots left and right of Sun
- Same height as Sun
- Often red toward Sun

### Meaning

Plate-shaped ice crystals in high cloud or ice fog.

---

## 19.70 Rainbow

### Identification

- Sun behind observer
- Rain ahead
- Red outer edge
- Violet inner edge

### Meaning

Water droplets and sunlight in the correct geometry.

A rainbow may sit near an active shower or thunderstorm.

Remain lightning-aware.

---

# Pressure and Instrument Clues

## 19.71 Slowly Falling Pressure

May indicate:

- Approaching broad low
- Warm front
- Increasing cloud
- Gradual deterioration

---

## 19.72 Rapidly Falling Pressure

May indicate:

- Strengthening storm
- Strong front
- Tropical cyclone
- Increasing wind

Look for support from:

- Wind
- Clouds
- Radar
- Forecasts

---

## 19.73 Sharp Pressure Rise

May mark:

- Cold-front passage
- Thunderstorm outflow
- Strong high pressure arriving
- Rapid clearing

A pressure jump with a cool gust often indicates a gust front.

---

## 19.74 Dew Point Rising

May indicate:

- Moisture return
- Southerly flow
- Increasing fog potential
- Greater thunderstorm fuel
- Warm-front approach

---

## 19.75 Dew Point Falling

May indicate:

- Cold front
- Dryline passage
- Drier air intrusion
- Improved visibility
- Reduced fog risk

---

# Quick Identification Keys

## 19.76 Cloud Key

```text
Is it fibrous and high?
    yes → cirrus

Is it a thin veil with halo?
    yes → cirrostratus

Is it tiny ripples high in the sky?
    yes → cirrocumulus

Is it a gray middle-level sheet with dim Sun?
    yes → altostratus

Is it patchy with rounded middle-level elements?
    yes → altocumulus

Is it a low uniform gray layer?
    yes → stratus

Is it a low lumpy sheet?
    yes → stratocumulus

Is it a dark widespread rain layer?
    yes → nimbostratus

Is it puffy with a flat base?
    yes → cumulus

Is it towering with an anvil?
    yes → cumulonimbus
```

---

## 19.77 Storm-Feature Key

```text
Long wedge attached to storm front?
    shelf cloud

Long tube detached from storm?
    roll cloud

Localized lowering beneath rain-free base?
    wall cloud

Narrow rotating condensation column?
    funnel cloud

Rotation reaches ground or debris visible?
    tornado

Pouches beneath anvil?
    mammatus

Smooth cap above growing tower?
    pileus
```

---

## 19.78 Winter-Precipitation Key

```text
soft flakes
    snow

hard pellets that bounce
    sleet

liquid rain freezing on contact
    freezing rain

soft white pellets that crush
    graupel

hard stones from thunderstorm
    hail
```

---

## 19.79 Visibility Key

```text
cloud touching ground
    fog

small water droplets with lighter restriction
    mist

dry-looking milky air
    haze

distinct smoke plume or odor
    smoke

brown wind-driven cloud
    dust

snow lifted from ground
    blowing snow
```

---

# Common Misidentifications

## 19.80 Funnel Cloud Versus Scud

### Scud

- Ragged
- Changes shape rapidly
- May rise into cloud base
- No organized persistent rotation

### Funnel

- Smooth or concentrated
- Attached to rotating base
- Persistent rotation
- More coherent shape

When uncertain during a warned storm, take shelter.

Field identification should never become a courtroom standard for personal safety.

---

## 19.81 Wall Cloud Versus Shelf Cloud

### Wall cloud

- Localized
- Beneath updraft
- Often near rain-free base
- May rotate

### Shelf cloud

- Long and horizontal
- Leading edge of outflow
- Moves outward with gust front
- Usually associated with widespread wind

A shelf cloud can look ominous but is mainly an outflow feature.

A rotating wall cloud is associated with the storm’s inflow and possible tornado development.

---

## 19.82 Hail Shaft Versus Tornado

A bright or dark narrow precipitation shaft beneath a storm can resemble a tornado from a distance.

Check for:

- Connection to cloud base
- Organized rotation
- Debris at ground
- Shape persistence
- Movement relative to rain

Do not approach to clarify.

Distance is an excellent safety feature and should not be discarded casually.

---

## 19.83 Contrail Versus Cirrus

### Contrail

- Straight line initially
- Often traces aircraft path
- May widen with time
- Can spread into cirruslike cloud

### Natural cirrus

- More irregular
- Fibrous
- Often part of broad upper pattern

Persistent contrails can evolve until the distinction becomes difficult.

Both consist largely of ice crystals.

---

## 19.84 Fog Versus Low Stratus

Fog reduces visibility at the ground.

Low stratus has a base above the observer.

On hills, the same cloud may be stratus in a valley and fog at higher elevation.

Classification depends partly on where you are standing, which seems unfair but is meteorologically standard.

---

## 19.85 Sleet Versus Graupel

### Sleet

- Hard
- Translucent
- Bounces sharply
- Forms from refrozen melted precipitation

### Graupel

- White
- Opaque
- Soft
- Crushes
- Forms through riming

---

## 19.86 Halo Versus Corona

### Halo

- Large
- Far from Sun or Moon
- Ice-crystal refraction

### Corona

- Small
- Close to Sun or Moon
- Diffraction by small droplets

---

# A One-Minute Sky Assessment

## 19.87 First 15 Seconds

Look at:

- Cloud amount
- Lowest cloud
- Darkest region
- Precipitation
- Visibility

---

## 19.88 Next 15 Seconds

Check:

- Wind direction
- Wind change
- Temperature feel
- Humidity
- Smell of rain or smoke

---

## 19.89 Next 15 Seconds

Look upwind for:

- Growing towers
- Thickening layer
- Shelf cloud
- Rain shaft
- Clearing line

---

## 19.90 Final 15 Seconds

Ask:

- Is weather improving or worsening?
- Is a hazard nearby?
- Do I need radar or alerts?
- Should I move indoors?

A one-minute assessment will not replace forecasting.

It will often tell you what to investigate next.

---

# Field Exercise  
## Cloud Identification Walk

Choose a day with varied cloud.

Every thirty minutes, record:

- Cloud genus
- Estimated height family
- Amount
- Movement
- Weather
- Trend

Take photographs facing the same directions.

At day’s end, reconstruct the cloud sequence.

You may observe a progression such as:

```text
morning stratus
    ↓
broken stratocumulus
    ↓
afternoon cumulus
    ↓
towering cumulus
    ↓
evening thunderstorm
```

The sequence teaches more than a folder of isolated cloud portraits.

---

# Try It  
## Make a Sky Compass

Draw a circle divided into:

- North
- East
- South
- West

At three times during one day, sketch:

- Cloud type
- Cloud motion
- Darkest sky
- Rain location
- Sun position

This helps reveal that different cloud levels may move in different directions.

It also encourages observation of the entire sky rather than the photogenic bit near the sunset.

---

# Try It  
## Estimate Cloud Motion

Choose one cloud feature and note its position relative to:

- Tree
- Roof
- Utility pole
- Mountain
- Compass direction

Time how long it takes to cross a fixed angle.

Compare low and high cloud motion.

Remember that apparent speed depends on distance.

A low cloud moving slowly can appear faster than a high cloud carried by powerful winds.

---

# Field Exercise  
## Build a Storm Timeline

From safe shelter during a thunderstorm, record:

- First visible tower
- First thunder
- Wind shift
- Shelf cloud arrival
- Rain onset
- Peak rain
- Pressure jump
- Temperature drop
- Rain ending
- Clearing

This creates a complete lifecycle record.

Do not step outside to improve the visual record once lightning begins.

The storm already has adequate documentation staff.

---

# Quick Safety Reference

## 19.91 Lightning

When thunder is heard:

- Go indoors
- Avoid open shelters
- Stay away from water
- Wait until the storm has safely moved away

---

## 19.92 Tornado

When warned:

- Move to lowest safe floor
- Use an interior room
- Avoid windows
- Protect head and neck
- Wear shoes

---

## 19.93 Flash Flood

- Move uphill
- Avoid channels
- Never drive through water
- Do not wait to see how deep it becomes

---

## 19.94 Hail

- Move indoors
- Protect windows
- Keep away from skylights
- Do not shelter beneath isolated trees

---

## 19.95 Damaging Wind

- Move away from windows
- Avoid rooms beneath large trees
- Secure loose objects before storms
- Expect power lines and branches down afterward

---

## 19.96 Freezing Rain

- Avoid travel
- Stay clear of trees and wires
- Prepare for power outages
- Watch for delayed branch failure

---

## 19.97 Extreme Heat

- Use air conditioning
- Reduce exertion
- Drink regularly
- Check vulnerable people
- Treat confusion as an emergency

---

# Field Guide Summary

High fibrous clouds are usually cirrus.

A thin milky veil producing a halo is usually cirrostratus.

Tiny high ripples indicate cirrocumulus.

A gray middle-level sheet with a dim Sun is altostratus.

Rounded midlevel patches are altocumulus.

A low uniform gray layer is stratus.

A low lumpy layer is stratocumulus.

A thick dark widespread precipitation layer is nimbostratus.

Small puffy flat-based clouds are fair-weather cumulus.

Rapidly growing towers are towering cumulus.

An anvil-topped storm cloud is cumulonimbus.

A shelf cloud marks thunderstorm outflow and often precedes strong wind.

A wall cloud is a localized lowering beneath the updraft and may rotate.

A funnel cloud is a rotating condensation column.

A tornado exists when the circulation reaches the ground, whether or not the funnel is fully visible.

Mammatus consists of pouches beneath a cloud and does not by itself indicate an approaching tornado.

Virga is precipitation evaporating before reaching the ground.

A cold front often produces a coordinated wind shift, pressure change, temperature drop, and clearing.

A warm front often announces itself through a sequence of lowering and thickening layered clouds.

A dryline separates humid and dry air and may trigger thunderstorms.

Snow is soft and crystalline.

Sleet is hard and bounces.

Freezing rain looks liquid but freezes on contact.

Graupel is soft and opaque.

Hail is hard ice produced by thunderstorms.

Fog is cloud touching the ground.

Haze, smoke, dust, and precipitation each reduce visibility differently.

Cloud identification becomes much more reliable when shape, height, trend, wind, and precipitation are considered together.

The field observer’s most important skill is not naming every cloud correctly.

It is noticing when the sky is changing—and knowing what that change may mean.

---

## Weather-Watcher’s Vocabulary

**Cloud genus:** Major classification category based on cloud form and height.  
**Cirrus:** High fibrous ice cloud.  
**Cirrostratus:** High thin cloud sheet often producing halos.  
**Cirrocumulus:** High cloud composed of small rippled elements.  
**Altostratus:** Middle-level widespread gray cloud sheet.  
**Altocumulus:** Middle-level rounded cloud patches or rolls.  
**Stratus:** Low uniform cloud layer.  
**Stratocumulus:** Low lumpy or rolled cloud layer.  
**Nimbostratus:** Thick layered cloud producing widespread steady precipitation.  
**Cumulus humilis:** Small fair-weather cumulus.  
**Towering cumulus:** Strongly growing convective cloud.  
**Cumulonimbus:** Thunderstorm cloud with great vertical development.  
**Overshooting top:** Dome rising above a thunderstorm anvil.  
**Shelf cloud:** Low wedge-shaped outflow cloud at a storm’s leading edge.  
**Roll cloud:** Detached horizontal tube-shaped cloud associated with a boundary.  
**Wall cloud:** Localized cloud-base lowering beneath a thunderstorm updraft.  
**Funnel cloud:** Rotating condensation column extending from a cloud base.  
**Scud:** Ragged low cloud fragments beneath a storm or cloud deck.  
**Mammatus:** Pouch-shaped cloud features hanging beneath a cloud layer.  
**Pileus:** Smooth cap cloud above a growing convective tower.  
**Virga:** Precipitation evaporating or sublimating before reaching the ground.  
**Rain shaft:** Visible precipitation curtain extending toward the surface.  
**Outflow boundary:** Boundary formed by cool air spreading from a thunderstorm.  
**Dryline:** Boundary separating humid and dry air.  
**Sea-breeze front:** Inland-moving boundary created by cooler marine air.  
**Haboob:** Dense wall of windblown dust, often produced by thunderstorm outflow.  
**Visibility:** Greatest distance at which an object can be identified.  
**Cloud trend:** Change in cloud amount, height, thickness, or development over time.

---

# Chapter 20  
## Becoming a Weather Watcher: Final Projects, Safety Rules, and a Lifetime of Reading the Sky

Learning weather changes the way the world looks.

Clouds stop being background decoration.

Wind becomes evidence.

A falling barometer becomes part of a moving pressure system.

A warm humid morning becomes a question about instability, lift, and timing.

A halo around the Moon becomes a layer of ice crystals high above you.

The goal of this book was never to make you memorize every term.

It was to help you notice relationships.

A weather watcher learns to ask:

- What is happening now?
- What changed?
- What is moving toward me?
- Which ingredients are present?
- What could happen next?
- What would make that forecast fail?
- Is there a hazard requiring action?

You do not need to predict every storm correctly.

You need to observe carefully, think physically, and know when curiosity must give way to safety.

---

# The Weather-Watcher’s Method

## 20.1 Observe Before Explaining

Begin with what you can actually see or measure.

Record:

- Cloud type
- Cloud trend
- Wind
- Temperature
- Dew point
- Pressure
- Visibility
- Precipitation

Avoid jumping immediately to conclusions.

Instead of:

> A storm is definitely coming.

write:

> High clouds are thickening, pressure has fallen steadily, and the wind has shifted southeast.

The second statement contains evidence.

The first contains confidence.

Evidence is usually more useful.

---

## 20.2 Look for Change

Weather is movement.

A single observation is helpful.

A sequence is much better.

Watch for:

- Clouds lowering
- Cumulus growing
- Pressure falling
- Dew point rising
- Wind shifting
- Visibility decreasing
- Rain becoming more organized
- Temperature changing rapidly

A cloud name tells you what something is.

A trend tells you what the atmosphere is doing.

---

## 20.3 Think in Ingredients

Use the ingredient method.

### Thunderstorms

```text id="xk11fa"
moisture
+
instability
+
lift
+
wind shear for organization
```

### Winter precipitation

```text id="7vt4kp"
moisture
+
lift
+
vertical temperature profile
```

### Fog

```text id="vfm91d"
moist air
+
cooling or moisture addition
+
light wind
```

### Flooding

```text id="v6bmrc"
heavy or prolonged rain
+
runoff
+
vulnerable terrain or drainage
```

### Fire weather

```text id="61nq7a"
dry fuel
+
low humidity
+
wind
+
ignition
```

Forecasting by ingredients works better than waiting for one dramatic sign.

---

## 20.4 Use More Than One Source

A good weather picture may combine:

- Your own observation
- Official forecast
- Radar
- Satellite
- Surface map
- Forecast discussion
- Local alerts
- Home-station data

Each source answers a different question.

### Observation

What is happening here?

### Radar

Where is precipitation, and how is it moving?

### Satellite

What is developing over the broader region?

### Surface map

Where are the highs, lows, fronts, and air masses?

### Forecast discussion

Why do forecasters expect the weather to behave a certain way?

### Alerts

What action should you take?

No one source does everything.

---

## 20.5 Separate Confidence From Importance

A low-probability event may still deserve preparation if its consequences are severe.

For example:

- Small tornado risk
- Slight chance of flash flooding
- Possible freezing rain
- Uncertain wildfire shift

Ask two questions:

```text id="9fpp6l"
How likely is it?

How serious would it be?
```

A forecast with low confidence can still support an easy precaution.

Bringing outdoor furniture inside is cheap.

Retrieving it during a squall line is less elegantly priced.

---

# Build a Daily Observing Habit

## 20.6 The Morning Check

Spend one minute observing:

- Sky cover
- Visibility
- Wind
- Temperature feel
- Dew or frost
- Ground moisture

Then check:

- Current temperature
- Dew point
- Pressure trend
- Official forecast

Ask:

> What changed overnight?

---

## 20.7 The Afternoon Check

Look for:

- Cumulus growth
- Wind increase
- Sea-breeze arrival
- Haze or smoke
- Temperature peak
- Thunderstorm development

Ask:

> Is the atmosphere becoming more or less active?

---

## 20.8 The Evening Check

Observe:

- Cloud trend
- Wind decrease or shift
- Sunset color
- Dew-point spread
- Pressure trend

Ask:

> What conditions will control the night?

Possible concerns include:

- Fog
- Frost
- Heat retention
- Overnight storms
- Wind
- Freezing roads

---

## 20.9 The Three-Sentence Forecast

Write:

1. What is happening now
2. What is expected next
3. What remains uncertain

Example:

> Moist southerly wind is increasing ahead of a weak cold front. Scattered thunderstorms are likely late this afternoon, with heavy rain and gusty wind in a few locations. The main uncertainty is whether morning clouds will limit instability.

This is enough to train real forecasting judgment.

---

# Final Project 1  
## Keep a Thirty-Day Weather Journal

Record each day:

- High temperature
- Low temperature
- Rainfall
- Main cloud type
- Wind direction
- Pressure trend
- Main weather event
- One-sentence forecast
- What actually happened

Use a table:

| Date | Forecast | Observed weather | Best clue | Main error |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| May 3 | Afternoon storms | Storms formed at 5 p.m. | Growing cumulus | Timing two hours late |
| May 4 | Clear and cooler | Verified | Rising pressure | None |
| May 5 | Morning fog | Fog remained in valley only | Small dew-point spread | Ignored terrain |

At the end of the month, review your errors.

Look for patterns such as:

- Forecasting rain too often
- Missing morning fog
- Underestimating cloud cover
- Trusting one model too much
- Ignoring wind direction
- Predicting storms too early

The journal turns vague experience into learnable evidence.

---

# Final Project 2  
## Follow One Storm From Beginning to End

Choose a non-dangerous weather system or observe a more hazardous one only from safe shelter.

Track:

- Initial forecast
- Surface map
- Cloud sequence
- Pressure trend
- Wind shift
- Radar development
- Precipitation
- Temperature change
- Clearing

### Example timeline

```text id="7r0a1q"
Day 1, evening:
thin cirrus, pressure falling

Day 2, morning:
cirrostratus and halo

Day 2, afternoon:
altostratus, southeast wind

Day 2, night:
steady rain, lowest pressure

Day 3, morning:
cold-front passage and wind shift

Day 3, afternoon:
rising pressure and broken stratocumulus
```

Write a short explanation connecting each observation to the storm structure.

This one project ties together much of the book.

---

# Final Project 3  
## Build a Cloud Portfolio

Photograph or sketch:

- Cirrus
- Cirrostratus
- Cirrocumulus
- Altostratus
- Altocumulus
- Stratus
- Stratocumulus
- Nimbostratus
- Cumulus
- Cumulonimbus

For each image, note:

- Date
- Time
- Direction
- Estimated height family
- Weather before
- Weather after
- Identification clues

Add special features when found:

- Halo
- Sundog
- Virga
- Shelf cloud
- Mammatus
- Pileus
- Lenticular cloud
- Fogbow
- Rainbow

Do not chase dangerous storms to complete the collection.

The sky will offer another cloud.

It has been making them for a while.

---

# Final Project 4  
## Map Your Local Microclimates

Choose several locations:

- Open grass
- Shaded yard
- Pavement
- Hilltop
- Valley
- Lakeshore
- Urban area
- Forest edge

Record temperature at:

- Sunrise
- Midafternoon
- Evening

Also note:

- Wind
- Shade
- Surface type
- Elevation
- Nearby water

Create a simple map showing:

- Warmest daytime location
- Coldest nighttime location
- Windiest site
- Most humid site
- Most frost-prone site

This reveals how local landscape modifies regional weather.

---

# Final Project 5  
## Test a Piece of Weather Folklore

Choose one claim:

- Moon halo means rain
- Red sky predicts weather
- Pinecones close before rain
- Birds fly lower before storms
- Dew means a fair day

Define the claim precisely.

Record:

- Sign present or absent
- Weather afterward
- Hits
- Misses
- False alarms
- Correct rejections

After at least twenty observations, decide whether the sign is:

- Useful
- Weak
- Merely descriptive
- Unsupported

The project teaches both meteorology and skepticism.

A rare two-for-one arrangement.

---

# Final Project 6  
## Build a Home Weather Station

A basic version may include:

- Shaded thermometer
- Manual rain gauge
- Simple barometer
- Wind vane
- Observation notebook

A more advanced version may include:

- Electronic temperature and humidity sensor
- Tipping-bucket rain gauge
- Anemometer
- Pressure sensor
- Data logger
- Online dashboard

Document:

- Sensor locations
- Heights
- Equipment
- Calibration
- Limitations

A weather station becomes valuable through consistency, not decoration.

---

# Final Project 7  
## Make a Hazard Plan for Your Home

List the main hazards in your area.

Possible hazards include:

- Tornado
- Hurricane
- Flood
- Winter storm
- Extreme heat
- Wildfire
- Lightning
- Damaging wind
- Ice storm

For each, identify:

| Hazard | Warning source | Safe place | Supplies | Evacuation trigger |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Tornado | Phone alerts, weather radio | Interior room | Shoes, helmets, flashlight | Warning issued |
| Flood | Local alerts | Higher ground | Documents, medications | Water rising or order issued |
| Hurricane | Official forecast | Shelter or evacuation destination | Water, food, fuel | Local evacuation order |

A plan written during calm weather is easier to follow during dangerous weather.

The atmosphere is a poor meeting facilitator.

---

# Weather Safety Essentials

## 20.10 Lightning

Lightning can strike:

- Beneath a storm
- Outside the rain
- Miles from the main cloud
- Before or after the heaviest weather

When thunder is heard:

- Go inside a substantial building
- Avoid open shelters
- Leave water
- Avoid isolated trees
- Stay away from windows
- Wait until the storm is safely distant

A vehicle with a metal roof can offer protection when no building is available.

The protection comes mainly from the conductive outer shell, not rubber tires.

---

## 20.11 Tornadoes

When a tornado warning is issued:

- Go to the lowest safe floor
- Choose a small interior room
- Avoid windows
- Protect your head and neck
- Wear sturdy shoes
- Bring children and pets immediately

In a mobile home, leave for a sturdier shelter before the storm arrives.

Do not shelter beneath a highway overpass.

Do not wait to see a funnel.

---

## 20.12 Severe Wind

Straight-line wind can damage:

- Roofs
- Trees
- Power lines
- Vehicles
- Weak structures

During a severe thunderstorm warning:

- Move indoors
- Stay away from windows
- Avoid rooms beneath large trees
- Expect power outages
- Do not go outside during a lull

Damaging wind does not become harmless merely because it lacks rotation.

---

## 20.13 Flash Flooding

When flooding threatens:

- Move to higher ground
- Avoid creeks and drainage channels
- Never drive through floodwater
- Do not enter flooded basements
- Follow evacuation orders

Water may hide:

- Missing pavement
- Deep channels
- Debris
- Electrical hazards
- Strong current

The road beneath the water may no longer be participating.

---

## 20.14 Hurricanes and Storm Surge

Know:

- Evacuation zone
- Route
- Shelter destination
- Pet plan
- Medication plan
- Fuel needs

Leave when instructed.

Do not focus only on category.

Consider:

- Storm surge
- Rainfall
- Wind field
- Tornadoes
- Inland flooding
- Power loss

Never go outside during the eye.

The opposite eyewall is still scheduled.

---

## 20.15 Winter Storms

Prepare for:

- Slippery roads
- Power outages
- Frozen pipes
- Carbon monoxide
- Cold exposure
- Falling branches

Avoid travel during freezing rain and blizzards.

Keep generators outdoors and far from openings.

Dress in dry layers.

Treat confusion, severe shivering, and slurred speech as possible hypothermia.

---

## 20.16 Extreme Heat

During dangerous heat:

- Use air conditioning
- Reduce strenuous activity
- Drink regularly
- Seek shade
- Check vulnerable people
- Never leave anyone in a parked vehicle

Heat stroke signs include:

- Confusion
- Collapse
- Seizure
- Abnormal behavior
- Very high body temperature

Call emergency services and begin rapid cooling.

---

## 20.17 Wildfire

When wildfire threatens:

- Monitor official alerts
- Prepare more than one route
- Keep the vehicle ready
- Bring medications and documents
- Leave early
- Do not wait for visible flame

Smoke and embers may arrive before the fire front.

Do not return until officials give clearance.

---

## 20.18 Weather Radio and Alerts

Use more than one warning source when possible:

- Phone emergency alerts
- Weather application
- Weather radio
- Local media
- Community alert system

Keep devices:

- Charged
- Audible
- Updated
- Near the bed during nighttime hazards

Do not rely on outdoor sirens indoors.

Sirens are designed mainly to warn people outside.

Walls, sleep, television, and weather itself are all excellent at hiding them.

---

# Understanding Risk Language

## 20.19 Outlook

An outlook describes a possible hazard over a broad area or future period.

It means:

- Pay attention
- Review plans
- Monitor updates

---

## 20.20 Watch

A watch means conditions are favorable for a hazard.

```text id="o9a0dt"
WATCH
=
be ready
```

Examples:

- Tornado watch
- Flood watch
- Hurricane watch
- Winter storm watch

---

## 20.21 Warning

A warning means the hazard is occurring, imminent, or expected soon.

```text id="4v9zsm"
WARNING
=
take action
```

Do not spend the warning period beginning a research project on whether the warning seems justified.

That work has already been assigned.

---

## 20.22 Emergency Language

Terms such as:

- Flash flood emergency
- Tornado emergency
- Extreme wind warning

indicate an exceptionally dangerous situation.

Treat them as urgent instructions.

---

# Avoiding Common Weather Mistakes

## 20.23 Mistaking Appearance for Safety

A storm can be dangerous even when:

- The sky is not green
- No funnel is visible
- Rain has not started
- The wind is calm
- The storm appears distant

Lightning, wind, and tornadoes can extend outside the most dramatic cloud.

---

## 20.24 Depending on One App Icon

An icon cannot fully communicate:

- Timing
- Duration
- Coverage
- Severity
- Confidence
- Hazard type

Read the detailed forecast and alerts.

A tiny cloud with one raindrop has been given an unreasonable amount of responsibility.

---

## 20.25 Looking Only at the Forecast High

Also check:

- Dew point
- Wind
- Overnight low
- Heat index
- Cloud cover
- Rain timing

The same high temperature can feel and behave very differently under dry, humid, windy, or cloudy conditions.

---

## 20.26 Assuming the Forecast Failed Because Your Yard Was Dry

A forecast may describe a region or probability.

Scattered storms can affect one neighborhood and miss another.

Evaluate:

- Forecast area
- Probability
- Expected coverage
- Timing
- Nearby observations

A missed shower is not proof that the atmosphere violated the forecast.

It may have obeyed it several streets away.

---

## 20.27 Confusing Precision With Certainty

A model may display:

```text id="ifpl02"
3.74 inches rain
```

That does not mean the atmosphere has signed a contract for 3.74 inches.

The value is exact output from an uncertain model.

Use ranges and probabilities.

---

## 20.28 Waiting Too Long

People delay action because:

- Weather still looks calm
- The warning seems early
- They want visual confirmation
- They are finishing one task
- Previous warnings did not affect them

Warnings are issued before the worst conditions precisely because action becomes harder afterward.

The ideal time to enter shelter is while doing so still feels slightly premature.

---

# The Limits of Forecasting

## 20.29 Some Weather Is Predictable

Large-scale patterns can often be forecast several days ahead.

Examples include:

- Major cold fronts
- Broad heat waves
- Large winter storms
- Tropical cyclone environments
- Regional rain events

---

## 20.30 Some Weather Is Highly Local

Exact details remain difficult:

- Which neighborhood receives a thunderstorm
- Where a snow band stalls
- Which road freezes first
- Where fog becomes dense
- Which tree receives the strongest gust

This uncertainty does not make forecasting useless.

It means forecasts should be interpreted at the proper scale.

---

## 20.31 Forecasts Improve as the Event Approaches

At long range, focus on:

- Pattern
- Possibility
- Broad hazard

At medium range, focus on:

- Region
- Time window
- Confidence

At short range, focus on:

- Exact timing
- Radar trends
- Local warnings
- Immediate action

Detail should increase as evidence increases.

---

## 20.32 Uncertainty Is Information

A forecast that says:

> The storm track remains uncertain by about 100 miles.

is telling you something important.

That uncertainty may determine:

- Rain versus snow
- Major impact versus near miss
- Surge direction
- Severe-storm placement

Uncertainty is not failure.

Hidden uncertainty is failure.

---

# A Lifetime of Weather Learning

## 20.33 Repeat the Same Observations

Experts become skilled partly because they have seen similar patterns repeatedly.

Watch:

- The same horizon
- The same valley
- The same cold fronts
- The same summer cumulus
- The same fog season
- The same local wind shifts

Familiarity reveals subtle differences.

A cloud sequence that once looked random begins to tell a story.

---

## 20.34 Learn Your Local Weather

Every region has characteristic patterns.

Possible examples:

- Afternoon sea-breeze storms
- Cold-air damming
- Lake-effect snow
- Mountain waves
- Desert outflow
- Coastal fog
- Nocturnal thunderstorm complexes
- Tropical rainbands

Local expertise often matters more than knowing every rare cloud name.

Learn what weather repeatedly does where you live.

Then notice when it stops behaving normally.

---

## 20.35 Study Events Afterward

After major weather, review:

- Radar
- Satellite
- Surface maps
- Warnings
- Forecast discussions
- Your own observations

Ask:

- What clues appeared first?
- What was forecast well?
- What changed?
- Which hazard mattered most?
- What would you do differently next time?

Post-event study is where disconnected facts become understanding.

---

## 20.36 Keep Wonder and Skepticism Together

Weather invites awe.

It also invites exaggeration.

Maintain both:

### Wonder

- Watch the storm tower grow
- Notice the colors of twilight
- Listen to wind in trees
- Study the first snow crystal
- Follow a front across the map

### Skepticism

- Check the source
- Verify the timestamp
- Question dramatic record claims
- Compare multiple observations
- Separate forecast from certainty

Wonder without skepticism becomes superstition.

Skepticism without wonder becomes a very dull afternoon.

---

# Final Field Exercise  
## Read the Entire Sky

Choose a day when the weather is changing.

Stand in a safe open location.

Observe for ten minutes.

### First: the surface

- Temperature
- Wind
- Humidity
- Visibility
- Ground condition

### Second: low clouds

- Type
- Direction
- Base
- Precipitation

### Third: high clouds

- Type
- Movement
- Thickening or thinning

### Fourth: the horizon

- Dark areas
- Rain shafts
- Clearing
- Haze
- Boundaries

### Fifth: the trend

Ask:

- What changed during the ten minutes?
- Which cloud layer moved fastest?
- Is weather approaching or departing?
- What evidence supports that conclusion?

Then check radar, maps, and the official forecast.

Compare your interpretation.

The point is not to defeat the forecast office.

It is to understand what they are seeing.

---

# Final Project  
## Write a Complete Local Forecast

Use this template:

### Current conditions

Describe what is happening now.

### Atmospheric pattern

Identify the high, low, front, trough, ridge, or air mass.

### Expected weather

Describe temperature, clouds, wind, and precipitation.

### Hazard period

State when the main risk is greatest.

### Confidence

Use:

- High
- Moderate
- Low

### Main uncertainty

Explain what could change the outcome.

### Action

State the practical decision.

Example:

> Warm humid air is moving north ahead of a cold front currently crossing the western part of the state. Clouds should break enough for moderate instability to develop this afternoon. Thunderstorms are most likely between 3 and 8 p.m., with heavy rain, frequent lightning, and isolated damaging wind. Confidence is moderate because lingering morning clouds may reduce storm strength. Outdoor plans should finish by early afternoon, and everyone should have access to indoor shelter.

That is a real forecast.

Not perfect.

Not certain.

Useful.

---

# The Weather-Watcher’s Ten Rules

## 20.37 Rule 1

**Look outside before looking at a model.**

---

## 20.38 Rule 2

**Watch trends, not isolated readings.**

---

## 20.39 Rule 3

**Use dew point to understand moisture.**

---

## 20.40 Rule 4

**Forecast by ingredients.**

---

## 20.41 Rule 5

**One model run is not a forecast.**

---

## 20.42 Rule 6

**A warning outranks your interpretation.**

---

## 20.43 Rule 7

**Never drive through floodwater.**

---

## 20.44 Rule 8

**When thunder is heard, go indoors.**

---

## 20.45 Rule 9

**Record mistakes as carefully as successes.**

---

## 20.46 Rule 10

**Stay curious, but stay alive.**

---

# Final Summary

Weather is the changing state of the atmosphere.

It is driven by unequal heating, pressure differences, water phase changes, Earth’s rotation, terrain, and the exchange of energy between land, ocean, and air.

Temperature, pressure, humidity, wind, clouds, and precipitation are connected parts of one system.

Clouds form when air rises, cools, and reaches saturation.

Different lifting mechanisms produce different cloud and storm patterns.

Air masses acquire the characteristics of their source regions.

Fronts form where contrasting air masses meet.

Midlatitude cyclones develop through interactions among temperature contrasts, surface pressure, and upper-level flow.

Thunderstorms require moisture, instability, and lift.

Wind shear helps organize them.

Tornadoes form through concentrated rotation within certain storms and boundaries.

Tropical cyclones are warm-core systems fueled by warm water, moisture, and latent heat.

Winter precipitation type depends on the entire vertical temperature profile.

Flood, drought, heat, and wildfire weather are linked through water, energy, terrain, and vulnerability.

Radar observes precipitation and motion.

Satellites observe clouds, moisture, temperature, smoke, and broad storm structure.

Weather models calculate possible futures from imperfect starting conditions.

Ensembles describe uncertainty.

Climate describes long-term patterns, while weather describes individual atmospheric events.

Optical phenomena reveal how light interacts with droplets, ice crystals, particles, and temperature layers.

Weather records require standardized instruments, careful exposure, metadata, and verification.

Folklore sometimes preserves real atmospheric clues, but it must be tested against evidence.

A home weather station becomes useful when its location and limitations are understood.

Field identification depends on shape, height, motion, trend, and context.

The most important weather skill is not naming every feature.

It is recognizing change, understanding risk, and acting before conditions become dangerous.

---

## Final Weather-Watcher’s Vocabulary

**Observation:** Direct measurement or description of atmospheric conditions.  
**Forecast:** Prediction of future weather.  
**Trend:** Direction in which a weather variable or pattern is changing.  
**Ingredient method:** Forecasting approach based on the physical requirements for an event.  
**Nowcasting:** Prediction of weather over the next minutes or hours.  
**Hazard:** Weather condition capable of causing harm or damage.  
**Vulnerability:** Degree to which people or property may be harmed by a hazard.  
**Risk:** Combination of hazard likelihood and potential consequence.  
**Outlook:** Broad statement that hazardous weather may develop.  
**Watch:** Alert that conditions are favorable for a hazard.  
**Warning:** Alert that a hazard is imminent, expected, or occurring.  
**Verification:** Comparison of a forecast with observed weather.  
**Confidence:** Degree of certainty in a forecast.  
**Uncertainty:** Range of plausible outcomes.  
**Microclimate:** Local climate differing from nearby surroundings.  
**Weather journal:** Repeated written record of forecasts and observations.  
**Situational awareness:** Understanding current conditions, expected changes, and available actions.  
**Weather watcher:** A person who observes the atmosphere carefully, interprets its patterns, and respects its hazards.

---

# Closing Note

The atmosphere is too complicated to be reduced to a set of rules.

But it is not too complicated to understand.

Begin with one cloud.

Then notice the wind beneath it.

Watch the pressure change.

Follow the front across the map.

Compare the forecast with what happens.

Repeat.

Over time, the sky stops looking random.

It becomes a moving system—never entirely predictable, often surprising, and always worth watching.

**End of the Field Guide**
